# Frame rate

Frame pacing: the host's frame step, engine frame limiters, present intervals and display rates. Each entry: symptom, cause, fix, the games it was found on. The [symptom index](../agents/symptoms.md) lists every symptom.

## Frame step

### Invalid GPU completion times inflate frame diagnostics

- **Symptom:** after a GPU address fault, a frame's diagnostic GPU time is trillions of milliseconds while adjacent frames report ordinary timings.
- **Cause:** `gpu_tag_done` accepted any positive duration with a positive start, and `up_flush` (`g_upload_gpu_ns`) accepted any positive duration. Missing or corrupt completion feedback (for example an unwritten `UINT64_MAX` tick sentinel converted through Apple Silicon's `125/3` timebase, ~`6.15e9` s, on a faulted command buffer) could supply a non-finite or future endpoint, poisoning the summed duration (`mtl_gpu_ms`, `@upload_gpu_ms`), the frame span (`mtl_gpu_span`), and `mtl_gpu_done` (`vfh_vsync`).
- **Fix:** `gpu_time_valid` (`nvn_mtl.m`) accepts only finite, positive, ordered endpoints that have elapsed on Metal's host clock (`CACurrentMediaTime`), shared by `gpu_tag_done` and `up_flush`. Invalid feedback still retires its pending completion, but adds neither duration nor span; valid work in the same frame remains counted. This changes timing accumulation, not GPU execution or fault recovery.
- **Check:** `t_gpu_timing` (missing, negative, reversed, equal, NaN, infinite, future, and #1737-magnitude endpoints; mixed valid/invalid frames across `mtl_gpu_ms`, `mtl_gpu_span`, and `mtl_gpu_done`; ring slot reuse).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), [issue #1737](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1737). The underlying intermittent GPU address fault remains open.

### Frame cap checked and used from the same sample

- **Symptom:** clang reports division by zero in `fstep_tick` (`hle_nvn.c`).
- **Cause:** the present path checked `fps_step_cap()` through `fstep_on`, then `fstep_tick` sampled it again for `60 / cap`. The second sample could be zero even though the first enabled stepping.
- **Fix:** sample the cap once in `nvnQueuePresentTexture`, check it and pass the nonzero value into `fstep_tick`.
- **Found on:** clang static analysis ([issue #424](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/424)); no gameplay failure reported.

### sead's dynamic present interval, `ksys::VFR` and the host's frame step

- **Symptom:** a 30 fps game to run at 60; after the floor was lowered, slow motion on a GPU-bound device (GitHub issue #9).
- **Cause:** find where the present interval comes from and how the logic time step follows it before patching anything. BotW's `sead::GameFrameworkNx` main loop (`main+0x112660c`) runs a dynamic interval: from the last frame's time it computes how many vertical syncs were needed, clamps that to [2, 3] (the maximum from the interval controller built at `main+0x1126c04`), steps up after three overrunning frames and down one level every six frames, and sets the result through `setWaitVsyncInterval` (vtable +0x130, `main+0x11269b8` → `nvnWindowSetPresentInterval`). The game logic follows on its own: `ksys::VFR` is initialized with a constant base interval of 2 (`main+0x127e740`), and every frame `VFR::updateInterval` (`main+0x151d3d0`) takes the framework's current interval, giving a frame ratio of interval / 2, so the engine's variable frame rate scales its time step by the present interval and the speed is right as long as the interval matches the frames. Then check what the controller measures: BotW's measured only the main thread's CPU time (about 3 ms a frame), so a GPU-bound device stayed at interval 1 and played in slow motion.
- **Fix:** now the host's frame step in whole vsyncs: a stub at the framework's `setWaitVsyncInterval` hands the game the host's step, and frames stay on screen exactly that long. The method before it cuts the game's frame-interval logic, and those cuts stay, for the interval the framework keeps while the game's VFR override holds (DM_FRAME_STEP=legacy, which ran this method on its own, was removed in 79d82f7b): five rewriter cuts lower the floor from 2 to 1 (its `patches.py`, build `cd57b23f…`): the candidate floor (two places) and the hysteresis targets. Two more cuts make the controller always take its GPU-time path, and the host's timestamp reports had to carry real GPU times for that path to see anything ([GPU timestamps per submission segment](#gpu-timestamps-per-submission-segment), every game's now).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (1.9.0; its note has the interval controller and the legacy cuts).
- **Check:** probes confirmed the legacy result: the framework presents at interval 1, VFR reports interval 1 with ratio 0.5, and when a frame runs long the game steps back to 2 with ratio 1, so the game speed does not change.
- **Note:** the zeldaret/botw decompilation (`VFR.cpp`) gave the semantics; 1.9.0's addresses were found by the constants (`60 / interval`, the `fmov s11, #2.0` floor).

### A fixed frame step plays in slow motion on a slow Mac

- **Symptom:** on a Mac whose frames take longer than a vsync, the game runs in slow motion rather than at a lower frame rate: movement, animation and the game's clocks fall behind real time, while the frame counter shows 30-45 fps.
- **Cause:** the game advances its time by an amount fixed per frame, set by its own choice of frame rate, not by the time the frame took. On the Switch the game holds its rate, so the two agree; on a slower GPU each frame still counts as one vsync. Two forms: Ocarina of Time divides its base rate by its int frame rate into a frame ratio that its time object steps by; Mario Kart World's framework picks an interval of 1 or 2 vsyncs, which the game steps by, from the frame's CPU time alone, so frames that wait on the GPU keep interval 1.
- **Fix:** the host's frame step (`fps_target 60`): the host decides how many vsyncs each frame stays on screen ([sead's dynamic present interval](#seads-dynamic-present-interval-ksysvfr-and-the-hosts-frame-step), `nvn_fstep.h`), and a stub hands that step to the game where it takes its own: Ocarina's ratio becomes step × base rate / 60, Mario Kart World's interval the step, at most 2 (its own intervals), with its own 30 fps mode published as a step the game fixes. Both stubs are placed by their function on any build that keeps it ([Frame-step stubs placed by their function](rewriter.md#frame-step-stubs-placed-by-their-function)). Until the host has decided a step, and at `fps_target 0`, the game's own code runs.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate) ([issue 1375](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1375)'s M1 Max) and [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#frame-rate) (the 16 GB M1 Pro's 25-28 ms races); slow motion seen by the user on both. Lichii, a GPL emulator, named both places (facts only).
- **Check:** see each game's note. Open: no run on a Mac slow enough to show the slow motion before and after.

### A regress run stops when another app takes the key window

- **Symptom:** `scripts/regress.py` reports a hang (`no frame after … s`, a state dump with every game thread waiting) in some runs of Tears of the Kingdom, baseline and candidate alike; the log's last focus line before it is `oe focus state → 2`, with no `→ 1` after it.
- **Cause:** the game window resigning key is sent to the game as out of focus, and Tears of the Kingdom stops presenting while out of focus. During an unattended run another app (a build, a notification, another session's window) takes the key window now and then. Every hung TotK run in the harness's folder on 2026-11-03/04 had that focus line without a return; the runs that passed either had none or regained focus within a second.
- **Fix:** `regress.py` runs the host with `DM_FOCUS_KEEP=1` (`front_mac.m`): the game stays in focus while its window is up, whichever app is active; minimizing still counts.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (the regress route, 2026-11-03 and 04).

### Benchmark captures perturb the measured frame pacing

- **Symptom:** otherwise stationary gameplay benchmarks show roughly 125–135 ms frame spikes at their verification screenshots; adaptive-cap results vary between runs.
- **Cause:** `scripts/bench.py` requested readback/PNG captures one-quarter and three-quarters through the timing window. The game frame stalls while the image is captured, and the adaptive frame-step controller can react to that stall. It is not evidence of equivalent renderer or pipeline-compilation work.
- **Fix:** request the default verification screenshot half a second after the measurement window, within the existing two-second exit tail. `--shots` remains an explicit diagnostic override; in-window captures are not uncontaminated performance evidence. Game-speed reports also parse the host's current `frame step: game time …` log format.
- **Check:** pre-refresh Mac BOTW 1.9.0 outdoor and TOTK 1.4.3 opening-cave logs put screenshot completion beside 125–135 ms frames, with shorter submit/GPU spans and no new pipelines. Subsequent runs used post-window screenshots; a TOTK launcher smoke report recovered `game_speed: 1.0` with the corrected parser. Those runs used shader chain `fda42ce035db`, not this branch's `b3ca4b7c621f`; no fresh runtime check on this branch is claimed. The six existing benchmark behavior tests are retained, not newly executed here.
- **Found on:** [BOTW](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#gpu-work) and [TOTK](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#frame-rate) on Mac routes.

### A thermally serious iPhone holds 30 fps

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, a game with the frame step at 60 fps drops a frame in most windows after a few minutes, and its GPU time per frame roughly doubles to triples (17-26 ms for the same scenes).
- **Cause:** at thermal state serious (`NSProcessInfo` `thermalState` 2) iOS caps the GPU clock to about a third: the median of the frame's tiny passes, a clock proxy from `DM_NVN_GPUPROF`, rose from about 6 to 22 µs. A frame with thousands of draws no longer fits 16.7 ms, and running the GPU flat out keeps the device hot.
- **Fix:** `thermal_capped` in `hle_nvn.c`: while the device is serious or worse, or in Low Power Mode where the player turned that on ([Low Power Mode holds 30 fps under the frame step](#low-power-mode-holds-30-fps-under-the-frame-step)), `fps_step_cap` holds 30 fps; it goes back to the cap after the device has been fair or better for the hold's calm wait, so it does not flap: 30 s (then `THERMAL_CALM_NS`, now `nvn_heat.h` `r4_wait0`), longer for a heat hold that comes back within 10 min of the last one's end ([The heat hold switched between 60 and 30 fps every few minutes](#the-heat-hold-switched-between-60-and-30-fps-every-few-minutes)). The thermal state is read once a second (`dm_thermal_now`). The log says `thermal state 2: the game holds 30 fps …` (`the frame step holds 30 fps …` before X2.0) and `frame step cap 60 → 30 fps … (thermal state serious)`. Since X2.0 the same hold reaches the games whose engine limiter the host writes at 60 ([Heat and Low Power Mode hold an engine limiter at 30 fps](#heat-and-low-power-mode-hold-an-engine-limiter-at-30-fps)). `DM_THERMAL_CAP=0` turns the heat cause off (a diagnostic switch; Low Power Mode keeps its own setting). The Mac reports no thermal state, so it never applies there.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (iPhone 17 Pro Max, docked at 60 fps: serious after 226 s from a cold start, after 64 s from a warm one; at 30 fps the frames held with no drops).
- **Resolved: movies interrupted thermal recovery, and acquire/present callers raced.** The cap getter short-circuited thermal sampling while a movie or a 30 fps target applied. A serious interval during a movie could leave an earlier calm timer intact and release the cap too soon; cooling during a movie instead added another 30 s hold after playback. Thermal sampling now runs independently of those caps, with a mutex protecting the sample, cached result and recovery timer across acquire/present threads.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_thermal thermal-tsan` drives the production cap getter with a controlled clock and thermal source: serious/critical, recovery interrupted by heat, cooling and heating during movies or a 30 fps target, one-second sampling, disabled/inactive caps, the Mac's absent thermal state, the calm wait's back-off ([below](#the-heat-hold-switched-between-60-and-30-fps-every-few-minutes)), and concurrent readers under ThreadSanitizer. No new device gameplay run or on-device recovery observation is claimed.

### The heat hold switched between 60 and 30 fps every few minutes

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone in a long session, the [30 fps heat hold](#a-thermally-serious-iphone-holds-30-fps) comes on and goes off again and again: `thermal state 1 for 30 s: back to the frame rate cap` exactly 30 s after the front end's `thermal state → 1`, then `thermal state 2: the game holds 30 fps …` a few minutes later. Ocarina of Time's 55-minute run went to state 2 eight times, 50-390 s after each return to 60: a switch between 60 and 30 every 2-6 min, about 38% of the run at 30.
- **Cause:** the hold's calm was a fixed 30 s at fair or better (then `THERMAL_CALM_NS`, now `nvn_heat.h` `r4_wait0`), however often the heat came back. State 1 on a phone that has just heated at 60 is a pause, not a cool phone: in that run the state never went back to 0 after 280 s, and each return to 60 heated it to state 2 again.
- **Fix:** `thermal_capped` in `hle_nvn.c` backs the heat hold's calm wait off: 30 s for the first heat hold, or one that starts 10 min or more after the last one ended (`nvn_heat.h` `rearm`); twice the last one's for a hold that comes back sooner, up to 240 s (then `THERMAL_CALM_MAX_NS`, now `nvn_heat.h` `r4_wait_max`): 30, 60, 120, 240 s. Heat during the calm restarts it at the same wait, as before; only a new heat hold doubles it: one that starts after the last heat hold ended, whether 60 came back in between or [Low Power Mode](#low-power-mode-holds-30-fps-under-the-frame-step) kept 30 all along (a phone that heats again at 30 is hot), so a player in Low Power Mode whose phone moves between states 1 and 2 reaches the 240 s wait too, and keeps it for a hold that comes when Low Power Mode is turned off. The timers count the device's sleep (`now_cont_ns` in `hle_os.c`, `mach_continuous_time`; `now_ns`, `mach_absolute_time`, stops while the device sleeps), so a break with the phone locked counts toward the 10 min. Away (`dm_gfx_suspend`), nothing is sampled, so the heat's calm pauses and goes on from where it was when the game comes back; but a stay of the longest calm wait (240 s) or more that comes back at thermal state 0 ends the heat hold at once, dated to the stay's start, so the 10 min run through the stay (`thermal state 0 after 1800 s away, the longest calm wait (240 s) or more: back to the frame rate cap (the next heat hold waits 30 s)`). Without it a 30-minute break on a 240 s wait came back to 4 min at 30 on a cold phone, where the fixed 30 s had cost at most 30 s; this is the heat ladder's rule for leaving and coming back. Low Power Mode keeps its own 30 s calm: each cause now has its own calm, and the hold ends when both have passed (before, the last sample's cause chose the calm, so Low Power Mode going off just after the heat cut the heat's calm short). Both lines say the wait: `thermal state 2: the game holds 30 fps until the device is fair again for 60 s (the heat hold came back 210 s after the last one ended: calm wait doubled; …)` and `thermal state 1 for 60 s, this heat hold's calm wait: back to the frame rate cap (a heat hold within the next 10 min waits 120 s)`; a heat hold that ends while Low Power Mode still holds says `the heat hold ends, Low Power Mode still holds 30 fps`. `scripts/devlog.py` shows each hold's calm wait in the `heat` row, with the number of heat holds and their time at 30 (`heat holds` and `time at the heat hold (%)` when two logs are compared). No setting; every game the hold reaches (the frame step, an engine limiter the host writes, the 120 → 60 step) gets it, and the Mac, which reports no thermal state, never holds. It is the X2.0 heat ladder's last rung (R4) and its restore wait, taken ahead of the ladder.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (iPhone 18 Pro Max, iOS 27.3, build 431d5d18, 55 minutes of play to Hyrule Field on battery; `devlog.py`: 8 heat holds, 20.6 min at 30 fps, 37.5% of the run). Replayed on that run's own thermal states, the back-off makes 6 holds with waits of 30, 60, 120, 240, 240 and 240 s, and about 33 min at 30 instead of 21; inferred only, since a longer 30 cools the phone and changes the states that follow. Not yet run on the phone: the same route on battery, judged by the `heat` row (holds, time at 30, the waits) and the battery per hour against that run.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_thermal thermal-tsan` (`t_thermal`): waits of 30, 60, 120, 240 and 240 s for holds that come back within 10 min, heat during a calm restarting it at the same wait, 30 s again for a hold 10 min after the last one's end, and after an hour asleep in which `now_ns` moved 5 min; a stay away (`dm_gfx_suspend`): 30 min back at state 0 ending a 240 s wait at once and the next hold waiting 30 s, 49 s away pausing a calm, 20 min back at state 1 going on with the calm from where it was, 239 s back at state 0 starting the calm on return; an engine limiter's hold with the doubled wait, and with `DM_LOW_POWER_CAP=1` Low Power Mode's own 30 s calm against a longer heat wait (both ending together, the heat ending first, heat starting under Low Power Mode, a heat hold doubling the wait while Low Power Mode kept 30 since the last one ended, a long stay ending the heat hold while Low Power Mode holds). With the ceiling at 30 s (no back-off) six of its checks fail. `dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py` reads the lines `thermal_capped` writes (its format strings), a log with three holds and one with holds ended by a stay away.

### The heat ladder above the 30 fps hold

- **Symptom:** a phone at thermal state 1 for most of a session (Mario Kart World from about 3 min, Ocarina of Time's field run from 280 s, Luigi's Mansion 3 at 9 min, Fortune's Weave at 27 min) got nothing from the host until state 2, where the only answer was 30 fps; the plan asks for resolution first, then effects, then the frame rate (60 → 40 → 30), with the 30 fps hold last.
- **Fix:** `nvn_heat.h` (NX18-X2.0 heat-ladder.md): one controller samples the heat once a second for every game, from its own call site once per present (`hle_nvn.c` `nvn_heat_tick`, before the 120 fps and frame-step branches) and the front end's 1 s timer (`dm_heat_timer_tick`, for loads; a thermal-state notification makes the next call sample at once). Its rungs go on in order (85%, 70%, effects, 40 fps, 30 fps). At state 1, 85% first after 10 s where the game can take it, then one at a time, at least 30 s apart and only on 30 s of evidence gathered at state 1 at the rung in force ("60 not holding" from the frame step, `nvn_fstep_nothold`: more than 10% of the last 30 s held past the cap or late, so a held 30 with Hold frames on counts, and a step the game fixes itself, Breath of the Wild's VFR override, does not; its window starts again at each rung or cap change, so a rung's evidence is of frames drawn at it; one-off load hitches, frames that made a pipeline or shader library on the submit thread or packaged a texture the first time (and the frame after each), are left out of it while they are at most 2.5% of its frames, so a first visit's compiles do not count as not holding, from S2Arm 022b03353; a CPU-bound game skips 70% and effects, both GPU work, to 40). State 2 jumps to its target in one change, and the 30 fps hold follows after 15 s behind 70% (D15) or 30 s behind 40 (D1), at once at state 3, when frames at that rung are late once it has been on 10 s (70% or effects pushed over it in the jump do not restart the 10 s), or where neither 70% nor 40 went on (behind 85% or effects alone it does not wait). They come back one at a time, last in first out, after a restore wait at state 0 (60 s doubled to 300 s for a rung back within 10 min; 60 s on at least; 70%, effects and 40 also at state 1 once 60 fits again, `nvn_fstep_fit60`, after 2 min); the 30 fps hold keeps its own back-off (above). `thermal_capped` reads the 30 fps hold's causes from it, and [frame generation](rendering.md#frame-generation) its rungs on (`nvn_heat_rung`, published after each sample: any rung, the 30 fps hold's included, sets it aside until that rung's restore wait has passed; Low Power Mode's cause is no rung, frame generation reads Low Power Mode itself). `DM_HEAT_STEPS=0` is today's behaviour; `DM_HEAT_FORCE` forces a state or a timeline (the Mac's test bench); `DM_HEAT_TUNE` sets the placeholders. Each rung change is a `[heat] rung A → B (…)` line (`devlog.py`: the heat row and the timeline).
- **NX 18:** the resolution rungs act where the game has a live render scale lever ([below](#the-heat-ladders-resolution-rungs-and-the-clock-rescue-share-one-render-scale): Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom once repacked with their +0x14 stubs, the Mario Kart World family through the GPU time stretch); no game has the effects or 40 fps lever yet (`dm_heat_can`, step 5) and nothing dims (`front_heat_dim_ok`, step 3), so elsewhere the ladder comes to the 30 fps hold alone, at once at state 2, as before. A game the host does not pace (no frame step at a target, no engine hold, no 120 fps limiter whose cap applies: Persona 5 Royal's own present interval; on an iPhone an `env.txt` `DM_FPS_TARGET=120`) is sampled too, but nothing holds it: its heat and Low Power Mode holds are `[heat] … nothing goes to 30 fps` and `[heat] … nothing was held in this game` lines, never `[nvn] … the game holds 30 fps` or `back to the frame rate cap` (`devlog.py`'s cap events), and it reports no hold state to StateReporting, as before the ladder, when nothing asked for the hold there.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_heat check_t_thermal` (`t_heat`: debounce, dwell, last-in-first-out restore, dim without a frame step, Ocarina of Time with 70% skipped, a held 30 read as not holding, CPU- and GPU-bound evidence, the evidence exit, Mario Kart World run 2's timeline, square waves and a short flap, a warm start to state 2, a 40 that loses its screen, a long stay away, the doubling waits, `DM_HEAT_STEPS=0`, Low Power Mode, the review's cases: evidence from state 0 not counted (85% first, then 70% or 40 30 s after it; without 85%, as for Ocarina of Time, 40 or 70% 30 s after the first state-1 sample, not at it), 85% first also when `r1` is longer than `evid`, CPU-bound frames skipping effects too, frames late at 70% or 40 counted only once 10 s at it (70% first, then 30 within the 15 s; stale late frames do not skip the 15 or 30 s), timed from that rung and not from the 70% or effects state 2 pushes over it (CPU-bound frames late at 40, GPU-bound ones late at 70%: 30 in the first state-2 sample), 85% and effects alone not delaying 30, a stay pausing the wait from the last restore, each rung event numbered in order, the game's own step not counted, one-off load hitches left out while few and counted when many; the parsers and the values they refuse whole (empty, `nan`, `inf`, over a day, a 0 wait, a wait over its `_max`, a 17th timeline entry); `t_thermal` also with `DM_HEAT_STEPS=0` (in each run frame generation's reader, `nvn_heat_rung`: 1 for the heat hold through its calm wait, 0 after it, with Low Power Mode's hold alone and with `DM_THERMAL_CAP=0`), with `T_THERMAL_LADDER=1` (a game with 85% and 70%, its frames through `nvn_heat_frame`; `nvn_heat_rung` 2 before the hold, 3 with it, 0 once all are back, 2 at state 1) the caller's window started again at a rung change (late frames from before 70% went on do not bring 30 before the 15 s) and at a change of the frame step's fastest hold (70% 20 s later), and in a game the host does not pace, `T_THERMAL_NO_ENGINE=1 DM_PRESENT_INTERVAL=game`, no hold line and no hold state, also from the 1 s timer while the first present works out that nothing paces the game (`T_THERMAL_FIRST=1`: before, the timer could sample there with the default of paced and log and report a hold); `test_devlog`: those `[heat]` lines are no cap event).
- **Not yet run:** any phone; the placeholders are from the round's logs (x2-round-all-on.md), not an A/B.

### The heat ladder's resolution rungs and the clock rescue share one render scale

- **Symptom:** the render scale could change only at game start (the loader's state +0x10), so the heat ladder's 85% and 70% had no lever in any game; and the GPU clock rescue wrote +0x10 itself for its 3 s and put back the value it read at its start, so a heat step under it would have been undone (or a heat step's end overwritten) by the rescue's end. Breath of the Wild's +0x10 below 1.0 also replaces the game's own dynamic scale (0.8-1.0), so a heat step could raise the resolution.
- **Fix:** one live setter (`hle_nvn.c` `scale_set`, state in `nvn_heat.h` `nvn_scale`): the player's Render scale, the heat ladder's factor and the rescue's (0.75 for 3 s), each set by its owner alone, composed by `nvn_heat_scale` and floored at 0.5, into the game's lever, worked out once (`scale_lever`):
  - a build whose render-scale stubs read the host's factor at state +0x14 (`LIVE_SCALE` in its fixups table, `ctx.live_scale`; Breath of the Wild's `main+0x1266880` / `main+0x126686c`, Tears of the Kingdom's view size at `main+0xd5d80` on 1.4.3): +0x14 = the composed scale over the player's. Under a fixed Render scale the stubs multiply it in; at the game's own scale they take the lower of it and the game's own dynamic scale (Breath of the Wild's s8, Tears of the Kingdom's multiplier), so a heat step never raises the resolution; Breath of the Wild's apply block runs every time its dynamic-resolution method does (1.9.0 never leaves its dynamic mode), so a factor back at 1.0 gives the game's own scale at the next call. 0 until the first change: the stubs as before. Only the rungs that lower this player's scale by 0.02 or more are offered (`nvn_heat_can_res`): with the 0.5 floor, Render scale 0.5 has neither, 0.55 and 0.60 85% alone, so at state 2 the 30 fps hold does not wait 15 s behind a 70% that changed nothing (a `[heat] Render scale …: … not offered` line says so);
  - a build whose render-scale stub reads +0x10 alone (no `LIVE_SCALE` in its fixups table: a Breath of the Wild or Tears of the Kingdom build from before it, or a stub with no live factor, such as Hades II's size set once at layer creation): +0x10 = the composed scale, for the rescue alone, as before, which only a stub that reads +0x10 every frame follows (no resolution rung: `dm_heat_can`). The lever line suggests a repack only where the profile's `patches.py` sets `ctx.live_scale` (`game.py` `DM_GAME_LIVE_SCALE_PATCH`: Breath of the Wild, Tears of the Kingdom);
  - the Mario Kart World engine family (profile `heat_gpu_stretch on`: Mario Kart World, Donkey Kong Bananza, Splatoon Raiders), whose own dynamic resolution steers by the GPU time between its counter reports: no game patch; each submission's reports move back from its end by k times their distance (`nvn_heat_stretch_ts`, `nvn_mtl.m` `post_run`), k = 1 / scale² of the heat and rescue factors (cost following the pixel count: 85% ×1.38, 70% ×2.04, the rescue ×1.78, together at most ×4), never before the latest time written for the submissions before it, read once per submission (so the stretch stops at the GPU's idle time before a submission, and a submission's own reports keep the order of their times: its ops are not in time order, a `TIMESTAMP_TOP` after a `BOTTOM` in one segment reads the segment's start), and never later than it was (a record run early with c1 = now, `post_early` or a later fence's handler, can write later than the next submission's own times: those reports then read their own). The submission's end stays, so a frame-rate controller that compares a report with its CPU clock (Bananza's) reads the same frame. `DM_HEAT_GPU_STRETCH=k85,k70[,k_rescue]` sets the stretches for an A/B. Never host-wide: Breath of the Wild's interval controller and Ocarina of Time's load reading read the same reports.
  The rescue covers the family with its trigger as for the stubs (held off also by a step the game fixes itself and at thermal state 2 or worse, [the rescue's entry](#the-gpu-clock-stuck-at-a-third-of-its-speed-with-the-phone-cool-the-clock-rescue)): its target is the frame step's where there is a state page, else the game's own controller's top rate (60 over its lowest interval, `hle_fctl.c` `dm_fctl_top_fps`: a cutscene's fixed 20 is not rescued), and in Bananza a rescue also lets the controller probe back to 60 at once (`dm_fctl_rescue`: its hold-off counted as spent, only where a timed probe may start), since its tries from 30 failed because their frames were late at full size (NX18-X2.0 gpu-clock-collapse.md). Lines: `[heat] render scale lever: …` once, `[heat] render scale 0.85 = player 1.00 × heat 0.85 × clock rescue 1.00 (heat ladder: resolution 85%): state +0x14 0.850` (or `the GPU time its dynamic resolution reads ×1.38`) at each change, the present crop line ends with `; render scale …` (and the stretch) once it changed, and the controller's probe line says `for the GPU clock rescue`.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_heat` (the rungs each Render scale is offered, and the 30 fps hold at state 2 at once where 70% is not; the setter's composition, the +0x14 factor and its float floor (Render scale 0.85 × 70% × the rescue, whose rounded quotient alone draws at 0.49999997, and the whole Render scale grid against each factor, the product taken in float as the stubs do), the stretch for each rung, `DM_HEAT_GPU_STRETCH`'s parser, the report stretch: a span read k times longer, its end kept, held at the submission before it, `TOP, BOTTOM, TOP, BOTTOM` in one segment both read k times longer, a submission after one written later than its own times read at its own, and 20,000 random submissions in order and none later than it was); `check_t_thermal` (one `make` per GAME: a switch of GAME rebuilds every test that compiles the profile, `.last_game` in the tests' Makefiles) with `T_THERMAL_LADDER=1` under `GAME=01007EF00011E000` (+0x14 0.70 at a warm start to state 2, 1.0 once every rung is back, 0.85 then 0.70 at state 1; with `T_THERMAL_PLAYER=0.5`, `0.55` and `0.6` the rungs that player is offered and the hold at once) and `GAME=040074A01BF12000` (the stretch ×2.04, ×1, ×1.38, the same in what `post_run` applies, `mtl_gpu_stretch_now`, and none under the +0x14 GAME; both rungs at any player's scale); `check_t_counter_clock` under either GAME (`post_run` itself: no stretch at k 0, the start 1.5 times its distance back from the kept end, `TOP, BOTTOM, TOP, BOTTOM` both pairs stretched, a submission after a later-written one read at its own times, the floor never lowered, k 1 untouched); `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=040074A01BF12000 run` (`t_clock_rescue`: the rescue's factor under the heat ladder's, its end leaving a rung that came or went meanwhile, Bananza at 30 with the probe at 2.52x stretched ×1.78 for 3 s, the targets, and what holds it off: a step the game fixes itself, thermal state 2 or worse; `t_fctl`: the rescue's probe at once with the hold-off at 32 s, spent at interval 1, a cutscene's top rate 20); `test_patches` (`LiveScaleStubs`: both games' stubs run on a small evaluator for each player's scale, factor and game scale).
- **Not yet run:** the stubs on the game (a repack of Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom carries them), the stretch in Mario Kart World or Bananza, and any phone. Open: whether Tears of the Kingdom sizes its views every frame; whether Mario Kart World's dynamic resolution moves at all on the iPhone at 60 fps (the round's run showed GPU p50 6-9 ms and a crop that never moved), and how far a stretch takes it (heat-ladder.md's Mac screen: `DM_HEAT_FORCE` with the default stretches, then `DM_HEAT_GPU_STRETCH`).

### A saturated GPU caps the performance cores while dynamic resolution waits for late frames

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (handheld Balanced, f5ae72b99, with and without a phone cooler, thermal state 0 throughout) falls to 40-50 fps for minutes: the `power tick` lines show the performance cores at exactly 1.44 GHz (from 4.4) from the second the GPU timeline reaches about 850-960 ms/s (`about B ms/s as the union of the frames' spans`; 780-880 ms/s summed over command buffers), and back at 4.4 GHz only when the GPU's load falls (an area load, a light scene). The `dynamic resolution` line stayed at level 0 through most of the capped stretches.
- **Cause:** the SoC gives a saturated GPU the power budget and caps the CPU, with no thermal state to say so; Ocarina's frames are limited by the CPU (the serial main → replay → encode chain), so the capped clock costs frames, while the GPU itself still keeps its frames on time. The game's own dynamic resolution (its level function, run by the host's stub) steps only on the GPU time of a frame over its threshold (about 16.6 ms): a GPU at 85-95% of the wall clock never asks it to.
- **Fix:** `DM_DRS_GPU_TARGET` (default 75, in percent; `0` or `off` the game's steps alone; `nvn_drs_gpu.h`, `hle_nvn.c` `drs_gpu_tick`): a floor under the game's level. Once a window (250 ms of presents) the host gives the controller the GPU busy share (`mtl_gpu_busy_ns`, every command buffer with overlaps merged, the `power` line's `GPU … ms/s busy`) and the performance cores' clock (`proc_pid_rusage`'s cycles over time there). The floor goes one level past the level the game runs at when busy is over the target in 2 windows in a row (1 s after the last change, once the game has reached the floor), or when the clock sits at 45% or less of the session's highest with busy at 50% or more for 1 s (2 s after the last change; at most 3 such steps while the clock stays there); it comes back one level when busy stays under the target less 15 points for 2 s with the clock off its floor, 3 s after the last change and not sooner than 5 s after the floor last stepped down from that level (doubled up to 60 s each time it trips there again within 60 s). The level stub (Ocarina of Time's `patches.py` `drs_controller`) returns no level above the floor at state +0x290 and writes the game's level count at +0x294; the game's own controller keeps choosing at or below the floor, and the game's upscaler path is unchanged (the floor only changes which of the game's own levels it runs). The target is on the merged busy, not the timeline's union of spans: the spans count the gaps inside a frame and stayed at 780-890 ms/s through the capped stretches while the merged figure read 510-600, so a target there would have held the floor down for good once the CPU was the limit; in GPU-bound play the merged figure runs about 5-8 points under the spans, so 75 sits about where 80 would on the spans, a level's margin under the 83-88% the cap came at. It runs where the game's dynamic resolution runs (`DM_DYNAMIC_RESOLUTION=1`, Balanced and Battery Saver); Quality keeps full size before the floor is read. Each change is a line, `[nvn] dynamic resolution floor 0 → 1 at frame N (the game at level 0 of 10): GPU busy 81% > 75%` (or `CPU clock floor (performance cores 1.44 of 4.40 GHz) with GPU busy 58%`, or `GPU busy 41% < 60% for 8 windows`), and the `dynamic resolution up to frame` line ends with the window's `GPU headroom (target 75%): floor F (lo–hi), GPU busy mean M% max X%, performance cores down to C GHz; D down (B GPU busy, K clock floor), U up, N back for no gain; last: …`. The Mario Kart World family (Donkey Kong Bananza) has no level stub and its own dynamic resolution steers by the GPU time it reads: not covered (a stretch toward a busy target there would be the next step).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_drs_gpu` (Mac, arm64): the level stub run natively with no floor climbs from its lowest level to full size as before, with a floor of 3 climbs to 3 and no further, a floor past its lowest gives the lowest, off returns 0; in a closed loop with that stub, a model of the game's update and a SoC that caps the cores after 1 s over 85%: saturation at 97% steps down 0.47 s after the onset and settles at floor 3 with no change in the last 25 s (capped 3.5 s against 28.0 s without, 2,359 frames against 2,052 in 40 s); 72% at full size for 120 s moves nothing, 82% steps twice and holds; a heavy scene, an area load and a light scene step back to 0 one level at a time; 10 s heavy / 10 s light scenes for 200 s: 25 changes, capped 13.8 s against 105 s; Ocarina's per-second GPU time from the cooler run: two steps down 0.57 s after the first second over 75%, one up after the area load, no cap against 2 s without. Open loop on the phone's logged seconds: the first step at the tick of 81.7 s, three seconds before the clock fell (84.7 s), and 3 clock-floor steps in the capped stretch.
- **What an iPhone run should show:** the same warm route as the cooler run, Balanced. Compare the `power tick` lines' `performance cores X GHz` (fewer seconds at 1.44) and fps, the `dynamic resolution floor` lines and their reasons, the `dynamic resolution up to frame` line's summary (the floor's range, busy mean and max), the `power` line's GPU ms/s and CPU energy per frame, and `on-screen … dropped`. `DM_DRS_GPU_TARGET=0` in `Documents/env.txt` is the off side; `=80` a lighter hand.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, X2.0 build f5ae72b99 (NX18-X2.0 traces/oot-mst-1-findings.md).

### Dynamic resolution stepped down to its floor for no GPU time

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (Balanced, b8a67e1d7, 2026-11-23, `DM_NVN_GPUPROF=every:600`) ran its main scene at 854x480, the game's lowest level, for most of a 4.4-minute run while the `dynamic resolution up to frame` lines read GPU busy 82-87%. Around frames 3779-4200 the floor stepped down 5 times in about 7 s (`GPU busy 78% > 75%` … `87% > 75%`) while the busy rose.
- **Cause:** the [GPU headroom floor](#a-saturated-gpu-caps-the-performance-cores-while-dynamic-resolution-waits-for-late-frames) assumed a smaller picture costs less GPU time. In this game it hardly does on the phone: the profiled frames' scene passes took 228 us each at 854x480 and 224 us at 1440x810 (many small passes, compute and fixed work dominate, as the M4 Pro already showed). Each step down left the busy over the target, so the next step followed 1 s later.
- **Fix:** `nvn_drs_gpu.h` checks each step down. Once the game runs at the new level (its first window skipped), the busy of the next 4 windows is compared with the busy that called the step (the mean of the 2 windows before it). Less than 2 points lower: the floor goes back up at once (`[nvn] dynamic resolution floor 1 → 0 … : no gain: GPU busy 86% at level 1 against 85% at level 0; no step down from it for 20 s`) and no step down from that level is taken for 20 s, doubled up to 120 s when it happens there again within 120 s. No other step down is taken while a check runs; a check whose level the game has not reached in 5 s is dropped. A step on the CPU clock floor is judged by the clock instead: it stands if the performance cores leave their floor in the 12 windows (3 s) after it, whatever the busy, since a clock that comes back raises the frame rate and the busy with it; otherwise it is taken back (`no gain: performance cores still at 1.44 GHz 12 windows after the step from level 2; …`). The holds are per kind of step and cover the level and every smaller picture, since the game also steps down on its own. The first phone run with the check (ea555d697, 2026-11-23) showed both: a busy step taken back at 85% against 86%, then a 37 s stretch with the cores at 1.44 GHz and the game's own threads on about 1.9 cores (`threads` line: 1,400-1,900 ms/s against about 700 before), a CPU cap no picture size could lift, in which the floor went 0 → 4 → 5 → 6 one verdict at a time, each judged on the busy, which rose as the clock steps were taken. The summary counts them (`N back for no gain`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_drs_gpu`: a GPU whose time does not follow the resolution, 80% busy at every level for 120 s: 3 steps down, each taken back (holds of 20, 40, 80 s), the floor never past level 1, the end at full size. Cores held at their floor from 5 s whatever the GPU does: each clock step is taken back after 3 s (holds of 20, 40, 80 s), the floor never past level 1. The earlier cases (saturation, the band, an area load, alternating scenes, Ocarina's phone seconds, the open loop) keep their results.
- **What an iPhone run should show:** the same Balanced play: `no gain` lines where the busy did not fall, the floor's range in the summary near 0-1 instead of 7, the scene passes in a profiled run at the larger sizes, and fps, dropped frames, GPU ms/s and CPU W against the 2026-11-23 run (59.8 fps, 1.93% dropped, GPU p50 10.8 ms).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, b8a67e1d7.

### GPU headroom for the games whose dynamic resolution reads the GPU time

- **Symptom:** none measured yet. Donkey Kong Bananza, Mario Kart World and Splatoon Raiders have no level stub, so the [GPU headroom floor](#a-saturated-gpu-caps-the-performance-cores-while-dynamic-resolution-waits-for-late-frames) did not reach them; their own dynamic resolution steers by the GPU time between their counter reports and steps only on frames over its budget, the same wait that let Ocarina of Time's GPU run 85-95% busy at full size. Breath of the Wild's and Tears of the Kingdom's own dynamic resolution was made for 30 fps (Breath of the Wild steps down only above an average of 29 ms), so at 60 it waits for very late frames; Breath of the Wild settled about 40 fps in heavy scenes with the GPU 83-88% busy (NX18-X2.0 gpu-clock-collapse.md).
- **Fix:** the profile key `drs_gpu_scale on` (needs a live lever: `heat_gpu_stretch on`, or a `patches.py` with `ctx.live_scale = 1`): the same controller runs on levels of its own, level L a factor of 1 - 0.1 L on the picture's side (0.9 to 0.5), handed to the render scale's setter as a fourth owner, `NVN_SCALE_HEADROOM` (`nvn_heat.h`), composed with the player's, the heat ladder's and the clock rescue's factors under the 0.5 floor. In the stretch family the GPU time the game reads is stretched by 1 / factor², so the game lowers its own resolution; in Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom the 3D views' factor at state +0x14 takes it (at Render scale 1.0 the stubs take the lower of it and the game's own dynamic scale). Each check skips 4 windows (1 s, `settle_n`) before it measures; the no-gain rules hold as above. `DM_DRS_GPU_SCALE=0` turns it off (the Quality preset's line), read again each window so the in-game menu's preset change applies at once. Lines: `[nvn] GPU headroom scale floor 0 → 1 at frame N (0.9 of the picture's side): GPU load 81% > 75%` and every 20 s that saw a change or a check `[nvn] GPU headroom scale up to frame N: GPU headroom (target 75%): …`.
- **The load, for every game here and the level stub:** the controller is handed the GPU time per frame over the target's frame time, the busy share times target / fps (fps at most the target; the target as the clock rescue reads it, 30 under Bananza's Battery Saver hold). At the target frame rate it is the busy share; with frames late it is more, and a smaller picture that lets more frames through counts as a gain even where the busy share hardly moves (Breath of the Wild waits for each frame's fence, so its GPU's busy share stays 83-88% whatever its clock). The lines say `GPU load` where they said `GPU busy`.
- **Clock room (Mario Kart World on the iPhone 18 Pro Max, 136433517, Balanced, about 3.7 min):** in races the game held 60 fps with the GPU 84-88% busy and the GPU probe at 2.4x (at times 3.2x) the session's fastest, in fixed steps: iOS held the GPU clock low until the GPU was busy most of the frame. Four steps to 0.9 of the picture's side, each taken back for no gain (78-93% against 76-88%), the hold growing 20 → 120 s. A busy share at a low clock is the clock governor's own target, not a GPU short of time, so a window over the target now counts toward a step only when the fastest of the probe's last two readings is under 1.5x the session's fastest or the frames are late (under 95% of the target; a clock that collapsed and stays down still steps, as in Breath of the Wild). The windows left alone are counted in the `GPU headroom scale` summary (`… windows over the target left alone with the GPU clock low and the frames on time`).
- **Late only when it lasts (Donkey Kong Bananza on the iPhone 18 Pro Max, c2bfbce88, Balanced, about 8 min):** with the clock-room gate in, the steps came from the game's own 60/30 controller instead: it drops to 30 fps for 20-200 frames at a time and comes back. Those windows read as a load of 94-106% with the frames late, which opened the gate; after the blip the load fell back to about 85% and the check called it a gain, so the floor walked to 0.6 of the picture's side (0.7 or less for about 20 s, 0.9 or less for about 2 min in all) with the GPU at a low clock (probe 2.4-3.2x) and no frame gained. The load (`nvn_drs_gpu_load`) now counts frames as late only after 12 windows (3 s) in a row under 95% of the target, before that it is the busy share, and the gate needs the same; a check that spans the switch is dropped. A clock that collapses and stays down still steps after 3 s. A late window counts only with the GPU busy at least 75% of it (`NVN_DRS_LATE_BUSY`): on c8351b8cf Bananza's CPU-bound stretches (48-56 fps, frame time 19-20 ms, GPU p50 10-11 ms, busy 55-62%) read a load of 76-79% and took two steps to 0.9, one held about 75 s, with no frame gained; a collapsed clock (Breath of the Wild, 83-88% busy) still counts.
- **Tears of the Kingdom on the Mac (2026-11-24, build 41656c9d4 plus the measurement, Temple of Time):** the view size function runs once a frame, but its dynamic-multiplier branch (where the +0x14 stub sits, `main+0xd5d80`) only runs when the game hands it its dynamic-resolution object, and that argument was null in every call, so the factor never reached the size. With the factor moved to the extents (a branch, `totk-live-scale`, not in this line) the 3D views did shrink (a screenshot at 0.5 is visibly softer) but the GPU time did not: per-pass profiles (`DM_NVN_GPUPROF=every:300`) at 1.0 and 0.5 show the G-buffer pass at 171 and 185 µs, the attachments' traffic down from about 583 to 172 MB a frame, and the frame's GPU time 9-11 ms either way. Its GPU frame is about 2,300 draws in about 150 render passes, 43-57 of them 64x64 or smaller, and the passes cost what they cost whatever their size: resolution is not where Tears of the Kingdom's GPU time is on Apple GPUs. GPU headroom stays off there. `DM_CTL` `scale <factor>` sets the headroom owner's factor by hand for such a measurement.
- **Breath of the Wild on the Mac (2026-11-24, the Great Plateau's lookout):** its +0x14 stub does reach the size (visibly softer at 0.5), but the GPU time barely follows: GPU p50 11.9 ms at 1.0, 11.0 at 0.5, 11.6 at 0.75; the attachments' traffic fell from about 380 to 130 MB a frame and the passes' summed time stayed 10.1-10.9 ms. About 150 render passes and 2,900 draws a frame; the 56-62 passes of 64x64 or less take 2.7-2.9 ms (26-28%) at about 48 µs each, the largest share, as in Tears of the Kingdom. A smaller picture buys at most about 8% there: GPU headroom stays off in both; the lead for their GPU time is the per-pass cost of the many tiny passes, not resolution.
- **Off in Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom (2026-11-23):** Tears of the Kingdom on the iPhone 18 Pro Max (c8351b8cf, Balanced, about 7 min) held the factor at 0.5-0.6 for about 5 min, most of it from clock-floor steps with the performance cores at 1.44 GHz, and the GPU time per frame stayed 10-11 ms, as at 1.0; the owner saw no softer picture. The +0x14 factor does not reach the views' size on the device (the static trace in its README found the view size function on the views' path, so where the size is actually taken is open), and Breath of the Wild's lever was never measured. `drs_gpu_scale off` in both until a run shows the factor cutting the GPU time; the heat ladder's resolution rungs there use the same lever and are as unproven.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_drs_gpu`: with the game following 0.5 s late and 92% busy at full size, a GPU whose time follows the picture settles two steps down within 3 s and stays; one whose time does not gets each step taken back and ends at full size. Late frames in Breath of the Wild's shape (CPU 4 ms plus GPU 21 ms at full size, 40 fps, busy 84%): by the load 4 steps, none taken back, 60 fps; by the busy share alone 6 steps with 2 taken back. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_heat check_t_thermal` for Bananza, Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom: the heat ladder and the rescue unchanged.
- **What an iPhone run should show:** Balanced play: the `GPU headroom scale` lines and their reasons, `no gain` where the smaller picture did not lower the GPU time per frame, and fps, dropped frames, the GPU clock probe and CPU W against earlier runs of the same route. The stretch moves each submission's reports back from its end and keeps the end, which Bananza's frame-rate controller reads for its 60/30 steps, so those should not follow it (to confirm in the log's interval lines). With a low GPU clock and the frames on time there should be no steps, only the summary's count of windows left alone.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md), [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md), [Splatoon Raiders](../../dm/games/0400380020056000/README.md), [Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) and [Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md) (every game with a live render scale lever): Mario Kart World run once (above), the others not yet run on a device. NieR:Automata's dynamic resolution is not reached: the host only selects its preset minimum (`hle_fpslock.c` `k_nier_dr`) and has no floor it reads.

### Low Power Mode holds 30 fps under the frame step

- **Symptom:** in Low Power Mode, with the launcher's **Low Power Mode at 30 fps** on (off by default, on in the Battery preset; [Settings](../settings.md#low-power-mode-at-30-fps)), the frame-step games (Breath of the Wild, Tears of the Kingdom, Age of Calamity, Paper Mario: The Thousand-Year Door, Astral Chain), the games whose engine limiter the host writes at 60 and a game whose 60 fps cap is the present interval alone (display mode: Red Dead Redemption) run at 30 fps on an iPhone or iPad; the game's speed is unchanged. The log says `[nvn] Low Power Mode: the game holds 30 fps …` and `frame step cap 60 → 30 fps at present N (Low Power Mode)`, `engine frame limit 60 → 30 fps at present N (Low Power Mode)` or `present cap 60 → 30 fps at present N (Low Power Mode)`.
- **Cause:** the player chose Low Power Mode, which already lowers the CPU and GPU clocks, and Apple's energy guide asks apps to lower their frame rate in it. The host now does half the frames there (GPU, game and host work), as for heat.
- **Fix:** `thermal_capped` in `hle_nvn.c` takes Low Power Mode (`dm_low_power_now`, `NSProcessInfo` `lowPowerModeEnabled`, read with the thermal state once a second) as a second cause of the [thermal cap](#a-thermally-serious-iphone-holds-30-fps): the same scope (the frame step's games, an engine limiter the host writes at 60 and a display-mode 60 fps present cap without one; a 120 fps target already runs at 60 on an iPhone or iPad, where 120 fps is not offered), 30 s of calm after it is turned off (then `THERMAL_CALM_NS`, now `nvn_heat.h` `THERMAL_LP_CALM`, its own, whatever the [heat hold's calm wait](#the-heat-hold-switched-between-60-and-30-fps-every-few-minutes)), and the frame-step stubs and the limiters keep game time equal to real time at 30, as for heat and movies. It is the player's choice since X2.0: `DM_LOW_POWER_CAP=1` (the **Low Power Mode at 30 fps** row, `platform:ios`, shown where the Frame rate row is) turns it on, and anything else leaves it off, so Low Power Mode is never read then; before X2.0 it was on unless `DM_LOW_POWER_CAP=0`. The in-game menu sets it while the game runs (`front_ios.m` `dm_setting_live` → `dm_low_power_cap_set`): on takes effect at the next sample, off ends a hold Low Power Mode alone set at once, without the calm interval. `DM_THERMAL_CAP=0` turns the heat cause off only. The Mac reports no Low Power Mode (the weak `dm_low_power_now` returns 0). Since the [heat ladder](#the-heat-ladder-above-the-30-fps-hold) samples every game, a game the host does not pace (Persona 5 Royal's own present interval; on an iPhone an `env.txt` `DM_FPS_TARGET=120`) reads Low Power Mode too, with the thermal state once a second while the setting is on, but nothing holds it: its lines are `[heat] Low Power Mode: its hold is on …: nothing goes to 30 fps` and `[heat] Low Power Mode off for 30 s: its hold is over (nothing was held in this game)`, and it reports no hold state.
- **Found on:** not yet measured on a device: the frame rate, power and game speed in Low Power Mode are to be compared with a run without it (`power up to frame`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_thermal thermal-tsan` runs with the setting off (the default), with `DM_LOW_POWER_CAP=1`, and both with `DM_THERMAL_CAP=0`: Low Power Mode read only with the thermal sample and only with the setting on, the frame-rate cap (`nvn_game_fps`) reading neither it nor the thermal state without an active frame step (the ladder's own sample at each present, `nvn_heat_tick`, reads both in every game; where the host paces nothing it holds nothing: `T_THERMAL_NO_ENGINE=1 DM_PRESENT_INTERVAL=game`), its calm interval, heat and Low Power Mode together, the in-game menu turning it off and on, and the heat switch leaving it alone. `dm/tools/tests/test_settings.py` and `test_launcher_settings.py` check the row (off by default, on in Battery, iOS only), `dm/ios/tests/test_live_menu_wiring.py` its in-game menu wiring.

### Heat and Low Power Mode hold an engine limiter at 30 fps

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, a game whose frame limit the host writes (`hle_fpslock.c`) at 60 fps kept 60 while the device was thermally serious or in Low Power Mode: the hold reached only the frame-step games and the 120 → 60 step of a limiter at 120.
- **Cause:** `thermal_capped` was asked only by the frame step and by `high_cap_tick`; at a 60 fps target the engine limiter and the present cap were fixed at the start (`hle_fpslock.c` `target`, `hle_nvn.c` `g_present_cap`).
- **Fix:** `nvn_engine_hold_tick` in `hle_nvn.c`, once per present before `dm_fps_lock_tick`, for a main with no frame step, not at 120, whose present cap is 60 and whose limiter the host writes (`dm_fps_lock_engine`): while `thermal_capped` holds, `fps_present_cap` returns 30 (two vsyncs, the 30 Hz display link) and `nvn_fps_hold_why` tells `dm_fps_lock_tick` to write 30, so the limiter and the present cap change on the same frame. 30 is the value the launcher's 30 fps choice writes to every one of these limiters, which the Frame rate row offers for each such game, so the speed is that choice's: Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave's target (it steps 60.0 / target a frame), Luigi's Mansion 3's NLG limiter (33.333 ms, steps by the time that passed), NieR:Automata's present interval and interval mode, Monster Hunter Rise's RE Engine limit, Need for Speed: Hot Pursuit Remastered's Criterion step and ticks, EA SPORTS FC 27's regulator and Frostbite's simulation rate, Unity's `vSyncCount` and `targetFrameRate`, Unreal's frame limit, sync interval and fixed frame rate. A 60 fps cap applied by the present interval alone (display mode without a limiter, `present_cap_base`: Red Dead Redemption, which steps by the time that passed) is held the same way, the present cap alone, as its own 30 fps choice; its line says `present cap` instead of `engine frame limit`. Recovery is the thermal cap's: its calm wait (30 s, [longer for a heat hold that comes back within 10 min](#the-heat-hold-switched-between-60-and-30-fps-every-few-minutes)), then 60 for both. Each change is logged: `[nvn] engine frame limit 60 → 30 fps at present N (thermal state serious)` / `(Low Power Mode)`, `engine frame limit 30 → 60 fps at present N (the cause has ended)`, and the limiter's own lines with the cause (`main: target frame rate 60 → 30 fps (Low Power Mode)`, the first 8 per row). Not covered: a game the host does not pace (Persona 5 Royal's own present interval), and on an iPhone a `DM_FPS_TARGET=120` from `env.txt` (120 is offered on the Mac only and runs at 60 there).
- **Found on:** not yet measured on a device: Luigi's Mansion 3 and Fortune's Weave in Low Power Mode with the setting on, against a run with it off (`power up to frame`, game speed by a timed route).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> run` (`t_fpslock`: the hold and its end on the target frame rate, NLG, RE Engine, Unreal, FIFA, Frostbite, Unity and NieR rows; none at a 30 target or at 120) and `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_thermal` (the present cap and `nvn_fps_hold_why` together, heat, Low Power Mode with the setting on and off, the 30 s calm and the heat's doubled wait; with `T_THERMAL_NO_ENGINE=1` a main without a limiter: the display-mode present cap held alone, and in game mode no hold).

### Movies hold 30 fps under the frame step

- **Symptom:** in a game the host steps at 60 fps (`FRAME_STEP`), a prerendered movie plays at twice its speed, its sound falls behind, and the frame counter shows 60 during it. The log has `[movie]` lines with pictures 33 ms apart (`time 0 / 33000 / 67000 µs`) and no `frame step cap 60 → 30` at the movie.
- **Cause:** the game's movie player takes one picture from the decoder per game frame instead of following the pictures' times. On the Switch these games run at 30 fps, the movies' own rate. Breath of the Wild played 34 s of its movie in about 1,250 presents (21 s at 60) and 3,507 pictures in about 3,300 presents. Tears of the Kingdom, with the same `subsdk0` movie library, had rewriter stubs at its own movie start and end (`main+0x2b36100` / `+0x2b33918`) that set the state page's movie flag. Breath of the Wild had none.
- **Fix:** the host's movie decoder (`hle_movie.c`) is the general signal. `dm_movie_playing` holds while a video decoder handed out a picture in the last 0.5 s, cleared at once when the decoder is stopped or destroyed. Audio outputs do not count. `fps_step_cap` (`hle_nvn.c`) then holds 30 fps, logged as `frame step cap 60 → 30 fps at present … (movie playing)`. Each present copies the state into the frame-step state page's movie flag (`+4`), where Tears of the Kingdom's step stub reads it for its first, undecided steps. Its movie start and end stubs are gone. Only frame-step builds (a state page) are affected. A game that paces its movie by the pictures' times also drops to 30 for the movie, which is its Switch rate. The Switch 2's `nn::codec` decoders raise the same signal with their stream's picture rate, and a movie above 30 pictures a second is not held at 30: [A 60 fps movie played at the game's frame rate](#a-60-fps-movie-played-at-the-games-frame-rate). The state page's flag is set only for a movie held at 30.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) 1.9.0 on the Mac ([issue #636](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/636)). It also replaces [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md)'s movie stubs.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 run` (`t_movie_vpx`: a video picture raises the signal for 0.5 s, an audio output does not); `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_thermal thermal-tsan` (movies against the thermal cap); `t_nvn` (the state page's flag is the host's copy). Open: no game run with the fix yet, in Breath of the Wild or Tears of the Kingdom (the movie at its own speed, `frame step cap 60 → 30 … (movie playing)` at its start and back to 60 after it); Tears of the Kingdom's `main` rewrites with 9 frame-step sites instead of 11.

### A 60 fps movie played at the game's frame rate

- **Symptom:** a prerendered movie of a Switch 2 game plays in slow motion whenever the frame rate is below the movie's own rate, and its picture falls behind the game's music and voices, by minutes over a long movie; at a 30 fps setting it takes more than twice its length. The log has `[codec] AV1 decoder …` lines and, before the fix, no `frame step cap … (movie playing)` line at the movie; on an iPhone the frame step moves between holds 1 and 2 during the movie (`frame step: hold 1 → 2 vsyncs … (3 late frames; cost 1.1 …)` and probes back that fail).
- **Cause:** the game's movie player (the SDK's movie library in `subsdk0`, NintendoSDK_movie) takes one decoded picture per game frame instead of following the pictures' times, as the Switch 1 library did ([Movies hold 30 fps under the frame step](#movies-hold-30-fps-under-the-frame-step)), and the host's `nn::codec` decoder (`hle_codec_video.c`) handed out every picture in order. Ocarina of Time's opening, `Event/Movie/Dm_Kokiri_0000.webm`, is AV1 1920x1080 at 60.00 pictures a second, 9,254 pictures, 154.2 s, with no sound of its own: the game plays the soundtrack and voices on game time, which the frame step keeps at real time. On an iPhone 18 Pro Max at about 36 fps it took 251 s (61.5% speed), and about 44% speed at the 30 fps setting. The movie cap of the old path never fired: only `movie::Decoder` (`hle_movie.c`) raised `dm_movie_playing`, and its 30 would halve a 60 fps movie anyway. Each movie frame costs about 16.5 ms of GPU on that phone (the NDVE network an estimated 11–12 ms of it), right at the 60 fps budget, so at thermal state 1 the frame step stepped to hold 2 and its probes back to hold 1 kept failing.
- **Fix:** two parts.
  - Pictures by their times (`hle_codec_video.c`, `CV_PACE`): a released buffer is the player asking for the next picture, and it gets the newest decoded picture whose time is at or before the movie clock; the older ones are freed (they are decoded already, so the codec's reference chain is whole). The clock starts when the player gives back the first picture it was handed (a filled buffer released), at that picture's time, and then runs on the guest clock (`guest_ns`). It does not run while the app is in the background (the guest clock is frozen there), while a released buffer waits for a picture, or across a gap of 0.25 s or more between two asks (`CV_PACE_GAP`, the frame step's `FSTEP_DEBT_MAX`: a paused player, or a load). A new stream, a seek's flush, a stop, or times that start again begin a new clock. When the player asks faster than the pictures' times nothing is held back, and the clock follows the pictures it gives back. So the clock never runs ahead of a player that paces itself by the pictures' times (Donkey Kong Bananza, Mario Tennis Fever, Mario Kart World, Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave hold a few buffers and give each back when the next one is due). In the first version the clock started at the first picture handed out and ran up to 0.25 s across a gap. A player that started 0.2 s after filling its buffers, or came back from a pause, then got pictures from its future, held each until due, and showed 102 of a 30 fps movie's 300 pictures in a simulation (`t_codec_video`). Two decoders at once share the movie signal: it drops when the last one stops. Pictures are skipped only while their times rise, never past an end of stream and never the last one waiting, so end of stream, sync handles and sync point events behave as before; skipped pictures leave the queue of 12 (`CV_HOLD`), so the submission event rises and the player sends more packets. One line per movie: `[codec] AV1 decoder 1: movie paced (end of stream): 4628 pictures handed out, 60.00 pictures/s, 4626 skipped by the pictures' times` (the opening at 30 fps). `DM_CODEC_PACE=0` turns this part off: every picture is handed out in order and none is skipped. It leaves the second part on: the decoder still raises the movie signal, so the frame step still caps or holds for the movie as below (before both parts these decoders never raised it).
  - The frame step knows the movie (`hle_movie.c` `dm_movie_picture`, `dm_movie_fps`; `hle_nvn.c`; `nvn_fstep.h` `pin`): the decoder raises the movie signal with its stream's picture interval (from the decoded pictures' times). A movie above 30 pictures a second holds the fastest hold the cap allows while it plays: hold 1 at 60 fps, hold 2 at a 30 fps target or under the [thermal cap](#a-thermally-serious-iphone-holds-30-fps), which still wins. No step up, trial or probe runs during it, late frames are still paid back (game time stays real time), and its frames do not teach the GPU reference. Its start and end forget what failed probes measured and both hold-offs, as a cap change does. A movie of 30, or of a rate not known (the `movie::Decoder` path), keeps the 30 cap. With the pictures following their times a movie stays in sync at any hold, so holding 60 is for smoothness. Log lines: `frame step: a 60 fps movie plays from present N: hold 1 vsyncs until it ends (no steps up, trials or probes; its pictures follow their times)` and `frame step: the movie ended at present N: steps up and probes from hold 1 again`.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (iOS 27.3), two logs of the title and the opening at the 60 and 30 fps settings.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 build/t_codec_video && dm/host/tests/build/t_codec_video`: a 60 fps stream of 600 pictures against a player that takes a picture a frame. At 30 Hz 303 pictures shown and 297 skipped in 303 frames (10 s), at 36 Hz 363 and 237, at 60 Hz all 600; times rising, the end of stream on the last picture, no frame waiting for its picture with Ocarina of Time's feeder (until the decoder refuses, 12 waiting) or Mario Tennis Fever's (one packet per signal, 4 waiting, also at 15 Hz), with four buffers in flight; 60 frames with the guest clock frozen take the next picture each, a 2 s stall does not run the clock and a 0.2 s hitch does, a flush or times that start again begin a new clock, and a player that paces itself by the pictures' times (4 buffers, started 0.2 s late or after a 1 s pause, at 24, 30 and 60 fps) shows every picture; a decoder finalized while another plays leaves the movie signal up. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 t_fstep check_t_thermal`: a 60 fps movie holds hold 1 through 150 s of frames at 0.98–1.15 vsyncs and through 40 ms frames, with game time equal to real time (without the pin it steps up); under the thermal cap hold 2 and never 3; the hold-off of two failed probes over budget forgotten at its start and end; the cap 60 during a 60 fps movie, 30 during a 30 fps one or one of no known rate, and 30 at a 30 fps target or on a serious device. On b9a87f95 these fail (600 pictures in 600 frames at 30 Hz; hold 2 after one step up; the 60 fps movie capped at 30). On an M4 Max Mac, headless, from an empty save (2026-11-11): at `DM_FPS_TARGET=30` the opening took 154.2 s, 4,628 pictures handed out and 4,626 skipped, at hold 2 throughout; at 60, 154.2 s with all 9,254 handed out, at hold 1 throughout (the shader library there lacked the movie's shaders, so the GPU cost was not measured). Again at 30 with the clock started at the first picture the player gives back: the opening took 154.3 s, 4,553 pictures handed out and 4,701 skipped, at hold 2, while two other hosts ran on that Mac (game time 0.98 of real time in one 60 s window). Not yet run on an iPhone.

### A movie network that fills the iPhone's GPU frame

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, Ocarina of Time's opening movie plays at 60 fps but with the GPU about 95 % busy (GPU p50 16.2-17.6 ms of the 16.7, 2 draws a frame), and the 30 fps heat hold comes on about a minute after it ends (thermal state 0 → 1 during the movie, 2 later).
- **Cause:** `nn::ndve`'s movie network, about 15.7 billion multiply-adds a movie frame even as the host's kernels, estimated at 11-12 ms of the 16.5 ms a movie frame takes on the phone; the 60 pictures a second keep it at the full clock ([the NDVE movie network skipped for MetalFX on the iPhone](shaders.md#the-ndve-movie-network-skipped-for-metalfx-on-the-iphone)).
- **Fix:** on iOS and iPadOS the network is skipped and MetalFX's spatial scaler writes the movie's 2560x1440 canvas (or 2048x1152, should the canvas follow the docked output size there) from the converted 1920x1080 picture (`DM_NDVE_ENGINE=mfx`, the default there; no setting), and the network's 92 MB of tensors are not made unless it runs after all. Efficiency first: the same 60 fps for, by the estimate, about 2-4 ms of GPU a movie frame instead of 16.5, so the clock and the heat can stay low through the movie. The Mac keeps the host's network (`DM_NDVE_ENGINE=1`), which its GPUs run within the frame.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_ndve_mfx`. On the phone, not yet: an on-battery A/B of the opening movie, `DM_NDVE_ENGINE=1` in `Documents/env.txt` against no line, judged by GPU busy, the thermal state through and after the movie, and the look.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (iOS 27.3).

### The frame step after a 30 fps cap ends

- **Symptom:** after a 30 fps cap ends (a prerendered movie, the thermal cap), a GPU-bound game stays at 30 fps in a scene that held 60 before the cap; the log shows `frame step cap 30 → 60 fps` and a few frames later `frame step: hold 1 → 2 vsyncs at present … (3 late frames; …)`, with no probe after it.
- **Cause:** when the cap rose, `nvn_fstep_next` put the hold back to 1 with no grace, while the clocks were still set for 30 fps, so three late frames stepped it up again. After a movie, the cap change had dropped the cost that held hold 1 (`cref`), so the same-cost probe could not fire. After the thermal cap, the GPU reference (`nvn_fgpu`) had been learned during the cap, at the clock it left, so the same work at hold 2 did not look slowed. A cost near `FSTEP_FIT` at either hold kept the fit trial off in both cases.
- **Fix:** `nvn_fstep.h` keeps `cref` and the GPU reference for each kmin. Entering a cap also saves the cost of an outgoing run that held for at least 8 s, even if no earlier hitch had populated `cref`; a shorter run keeps the earlier proven reference. The frames after the cap rises are a counted probe with the usual 30 frames of grace, starting with the first frame at the shorter hold. The completed frame at the old cap consumes none of that grace, and a failed recovery doubles the hold-off as usual.
- **Found on:** reading the code; modelled in `t_fstep` with the 30 fps movie cap ([Movies hold 30 fps under the frame step](#movies-hold-30-fps-under-the-frame-step)) and [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md)'s iPhone GPU times (0.86 vsyncs at hold 1, 1.5 at hold 2). Not yet seen on these paths in a device log; first seen on a Mac with a 30 fps mode in a downstream fork.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 t_fstep && dm/gfx/tests/t_fstep`: movie and thermal recovery, the first movie without a prior hitch, repeated caps after short and sustained runs, all 30 grace frames, failed-probe backoff and game-time debt. The original recovery cases fail on main before [PR #253](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/253); its initial fix still failed the first-movie, repeated-cap and full-grace regressions. All pass with the corrections above.

### A probe's grace lasts while its frames keep getting cheaper

- **Symptom:** on a Mac, a GPU-bound game at 60 fps falls to 30 or 20 fps when the scene gets heavier and stays there until a menu or a lighter scene, while the GPU clock sits at its floor (338–400 MHz on an M5 Pro) and the GPU is powered 60–75% of the time; the log shows probes back to hold 1 that fail after about 33 frames (`frame step: hold 2 → 1 vsyncs … (probe, GPU 1.67× its time at hold 1)` then `the probe failed`), and the host's profiler (`@gpu_mhz`) shows the clock rising during each probe and falling back after it.
- **Cause:** macOS lowers the GPU clock while a vsync-paced game meets its deadline (to the point where the frame's GPU work is about 70% of the frame), and raises it for a late game at 85–100% GPU occupancy slowly, about 100 MHz a second. A heavier scene at that clock makes three late frames, so the frame step steps up within 50 ms, long before the clock could rise; at hold 2 or 3 the GPU idles between frames, the clock falls to its floor, and a probe back to hold 1 (`FSTEP_GRACE` 30 frames, the iPhone controller's settling time) sees the clock rise 338 → 432 MHz and the GPU time fall 24.8 → 18.5 ms, then fails at 22 ms frames; hold 1 needs about 560 MHz there. The GPU's occupancy stays below 100% because the game waits for the fence of the frame it just submitted (as on the Switch; `nvnQueueFenceSync` takes the fence of the last commit and the GPU's own signal wakes the wait), so between the GPU's end and the next frame's first segment the GPU has nothing: the game's own work (about 1 ms), the translation up to the first cut (`DM_NVN_CUT` 64 draws, 0.5 ms) and the segments the GPU drains faster than the host encodes them (1–4 ms a frame).
- **Fix:** `nvn_fstep.h` renews a probe's grace while the probe's frames keep getting cheaper (`nvn_fstep.ramp`, set by `fstep_tick` from `DM_FSTEP_PROBE_RAMP`, on by default; `0` turns it off, `ab` alternates with `DM_AB`): a grace window whose second half cost at most 95% of its first (`FSTEP_RAMP`) and had a late frame starts another, up to `FSTEP_PROBE_MAX` (180) grace frames in all. A cost that does not fall, or falls and stops short of the hold, fails the probe as before; a window with no late frame ends the grace and the probe is judged as a trial. The probe's late frames are paid back as any other.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 t_fstep && dm/gfx/tests/t_fstep`: a cost falling 0.6% a frame fails the probe without the rule and holds with it (three windows); a flat cost, a cost that stops at 1.3 and a cost that falls without reaching the hold last 33, 63 and 183 frames.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#the-frame-step-current) (Mac M5 Pro, fullscreen, present snapshots, the host's profiler); resolved there: in a 470 s run with the rule switched every 30 s, every probe with it held (11 in a row).
- **Note:** what shortens the serial chain itself, so the GPU's occupancy at a GPU-bound hold is higher and the clock rises faster: Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, the profile's `enc_thread on`; the submit thread's translation 6.5 → about 4 ms a frame, measured on an M4 Pro and for Need for Speed), and a smaller first segment. Neither can start the GPU before the game's own work after its wait, and neither was measured on this symptom yet.

### A scene that does not fit the shorter hold probed against another scene's GPU time

- **Symptom:** a GPU-bound game whose GPU time is more than a vsync even at hold 1 keeps probing back to 60 fps and failing: the log repeats `frame step: hold 2 → 1 vsyncs … (probe, GPU …× its time at hold 1)` and `the probe failed` every few seconds, each after a grace of late frames at hold 1, often followed by hold 3 (20 fps). On an M2 Pro, Ocarina of Time standing in Hyrule Field at night: 22 probes in a minute, all failed, 159 frames at 20 fps; a 30 fps cap (`DM_FPS_TARGET=30`) had none.
- **Cause:** the GPU counted as slowed (`nvn_fgpu_slow`) when its time at the longer hold was `FSTEP_GPU_SLOW` (1.3) times its reference, its running mean at hold 1. That reference is another scene's: the frames of hold 1 outside probes, which were the title screen's and a few of the heavy scene's before three late frames stepped it up, moving it 1/32 of the way a frame; it sat at about 0.7 vsyncs. Any scene over a vsync at hold 1 is more than 1.3 times that at any hold, so each wait before a probe (1, 2, 4, then 8 s) ended in one, though hold 1 did not fit even at the clock it raises. A gate on the reference itself (PR [#2611](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2611): no probe while the reference is over hold 1) does not engage after a title screen, and where it does it keeps a later lighter scene at the longer hold: kmin's frames only relearn the reference after a probe.
- **Fix:** `nvn_fstep.h` learns from the failed probes how much the longer hold slows the GPU, for each kmin and hold (`nvn_fgpu.ratio_at`): the GPU time at the longer hold before the probe and again after it (the smaller of the running means of 8 and of 32 frames, so a turn of the camera does not count) against the probe's frames at hold 1 from the second half of its grace on (the clock risen). The ratio can be under 1: on an M4 Pro, whose clock barely moves, Ocarina of Time's Hyrule Field at render scale 1.333 took 0.99–1.00 vsyncs of GPU time at hold 2 and 1.03–1.09 at hold 1 after the grace, and taken as 1 the estimate (1.00) kept starting probes. A probe whose ratio is under `FSTEP_GPU_RMIN` (0.75) gives none: its frames were another scene or a stall (a load failed two probes there at 4.4 and 5.7 vsyncs, a ratio of 0.15). Once two failed probes have given a hold's ratio (`FSTEP_GPU_FAILS`; one may fail before the clock reached its top, as at the iOS thermal cap's end), the GPU counts as slowed there when its time over the largest of their ratios, the scene's time at hold 1, fits hold 1, in place of the reference: the scene that failed is not probed again while it costs what it did, and a lighter one is, even when it is not 1.3 times a reference learned in a heavier scene. A probe that holds gives no ratio. The probe line names the estimate (`GPU … at hold 2, … at hold 1 by the failed probes`), and `the probe failed` names its frames' GPU time at hold 1 after the grace. `nvn_fstep_gpu_slowed` takes the step's state (`nvn_fstep`), as it needs the probe's grace.
