- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 t_fstep && dm/gfx/tests/t_fstep`, with a GPU clock that makes the longer holds 1.6, 2 or 3 times as slow and the first 20 frames back at hold 1 as slow, each after a title screen: Kokiri Forest (1.25 vsyncs at hold 1, 2.05 for 60 frames in every 400 as the camera turns) 2–3 probes in 7,200 frames where the reference made 21–48; Hyrule Field (1.12) 2 where it made 15–21; a scene of 0.6 after one of 1.25 from the start back at hold 1 with one probe at every slowdown, where the reference kept hold 2 for good at 1.6 and 2 times; the GPU as slow at hold 1 as at hold 2: two failed probes, then a lighter scene's probe holds; the M4 Pro's field (1.00 at hold 2, 1.12 at hold 1): two failed probes where a ratio of at least 1 made 23; two probes failed by a stall give no ratio, and the next probe holds.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game): Amost-'s M2 Pro run on PR #2611 (Hyrule Field at night, render scale 1.0, main `e0cf2ac7`), and Kokiri Forest on an Android port that shares `nvn_fstep.h` (DeathKhan/nx2android, Ayn Odin 3: 1.2–2.1 vsyncs by where the camera looks, a probe at each turn). Resolved on an M4 Pro (hosts at 2401b288 with and without the change, the PR #2532 modules, the Hyrule Field save point at night standing, render scale 1.333, 210 s runs): 9 and 13 failed probes a run before, with 325 and 443 late frames and 161 frames at hold 3 in one; 2 failed probes in each of three runs after, then none, with 53–69 late frames. At render scale 1.0 the field holds 60 both ways; the M2 Pro retest is open.
- **NX 18:** ported from upstream b1ba6570f with one difference. NX 18 already holds off probes after two failed probes over budget, for at most 32 s since the last step up (`nvn_fstep_pheld`, `FSTEP_PHOLD_MAX`), and doubles each failed probe's wait up to 32 s (`phold`), so it never made upstream's 22 failed probes a minute. Where the ratio says the scene does not fit hold 1 but the reference still calls the GPU slowed, NX 18 starts one probe each 32 s since the last step up (`nvn_fgpu_slow`'s `late`) instead of none: a ratio learned in one scene is applied to the next, and after a heavy area as slow at either hold (Breath of the Wild on an iPhone: 22–24 ms at hold 2 and as much in each probe, a ratio of 1) a scene whose clock follows the hold (14.4 ms at hold 1, about 25 at hold 2) would stay at 30 fps until a load ([Probes held off while hold 1 measured clearly over budget](#probes-held-off-while-hold-1-measured-clearly-over-budget) has the probe that finds hold 1 again). `DM_FSTEP_GPU_RETRY=0` is upstream's rule (no such probe), `ab` alternates with `DM_AB`; no player setting. In `t_fstep` the scenes where hold 1 is just out of reach make fewer failed probes than before (1.12 at hold 1: 6 in 150 s against 8; the M4 Pro shape: 7 against 9 in 100 s), the GPU-bound ones as many (one each 32 s), and a lighter scene after the failed probes is probed and holds; with `DM_FSTEP_GPU_RETRY=0` they make upstream's 2–3 and the Breath of the Wild case stays at hold 2. Open: a heavy scene of 1.25 at hold 1 that steps to hold 3, then a scene of 0.6 with the GPU twice as slow at the longer holds stays at hold 2 with NX 18's rule, as before the port (the fit trial to hold 2 runs before the probe's 32 s wait, and hold 2 has no ratio of its own); upstream's rule probes and holds. Not run on the iPhone: which rule costs less battery for the fps it gives is an A/B there (`DM_FSTEP_GPU_RETRY=ab`, Ocarina of Time in Hyrule Field at night and Breath of the Wild's GPU-bound areas).


### Probes held off while hold 1 measured clearly over budget

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, a GPU-bound game at 60 fps under the frame step enters a heavier area (GPU 9 → 22–24 ms a frame at 30 fps) and drops to 30 or 20 fps as it should, but stutters every 2–3 s on top of the low frame rate: the log repeats `frame step: hold 2 → 1 vsyncs … (probe, GPU 1.60× its time at hold 1; cost 1.40, …)`, `hold 1 → 2 … (3 late frames; cost 1.6–1.9 …)`, `the probe failed …`, often with `hold 2 → 3` a few presents after the failed probe and `hold 3 → 2 … (trial …)` before the next probe; 7 failed probes in 30 s. Separately, opening a menu stepped 1 → 2 → 3 within 3 presents on frames costing 0.72–0.76 vsyncs.
- **Cause:** the GPU-slowed probe (`nvn_fstep_gpu_slowed`: the GPU at the longer hold at least 1.3 times its hold-1 reference) assumes the GPU clock fell with the hold and rises again at hold 1. In this area it did not: each probe measured hold 1 at 1.3–1.9 vsyncs after its grace, and nothing kept that measurement, so the next probe started as soon as the hold-off allowed. The hold-off doubled to 8 s at most, and a trial from hold 3 back to 2 that held set it back to 1 s. The step to hold 3 came from the screen's verdicts: with present snapshots `fstep_tick` judges the frame 8 presents back, so after a step up the next verdicts are about the late frames made at the old hold, and three of them stepped up again although the frames now fitted (the same two steps on a menu hitch).
- **Fix:** `nvn_fstep.h` keeps what a failed probe measured: the mean cost of its frames after the grace at kmin (`pk`) and the cost at the longer hold it started from (`pfrom`). The cost at the longer hold now, scaled by `pk / pfrom`, estimates kmin's cost (`nvn_fstep_pest`). After two failed probes in a row whose kmin cost was over `FSTEP_OVER` (1.15) times the budget, no probe starts while that estimate stays over it (`nvn_fstep_pheld`), for at most `FSTEP_PHOLD_MAX` (32 s) since the last step up (`nvn_fstep_probe_ok`). It takes two failures because a clock that is slow to come back after a cap fails the first probe and holds the second. The 32 s bound is there because the cost at the longer hold does not tell a scene hold 1 cannot hold from one it can: the GPU clock follows the hold, so this area took 22–24 ms at hold 2 and a scene that held hold 1 at 14.4 ms took about 25 there. Held off for good, a game that left the heavy area for such a scene stayed at 30 fps until a load. Probes that fail within the margin back off on their own hold-off (`phold`), doubled from 1 s up to `FSTEP_PHOLD_MAX` (32 s); trials keep the 8 s cap. An estimate of at most `FSTEP_FIT` of the budget forgets the measurement and both hold-offs, so a fit trial or a probe is not delayed when the scene gets light. A stall of `FSTEP_DEBT_MAX` (a load, or a trip to the background), a cap change, a step the game fixes itself (a cutscene), or a run at kmin that holds (a trial or a probe's 8 s run) also forgets it. For `FSTEP_UP_LAG` (8) frames after a step up, a late verdict from the screen does not count toward another step up when the frame's own cost fits the new hold. Log lines: `the probe failed at present N (CPU …% on performance cores; cost 1.45 at hold 1, 2 in a row over 1.15: no probe for 32 s while hold 2 costs over 1.11)` and, once per such line, `probes back to hold 1 allowed again at present N (cost 0.80 at hold 2, about 0.83 at hold 1)` (or `the measurement forgotten`) when the cost fell or the scene changed; the probe 32 s on is logged as a probe. Both count toward the host's 40 frame-step lines and carry its marker (`scripts/devlog.py` `FSTEP_CUT`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 t_fstep && dm/gfx/tests/t_fstep`. A GPU-bound stretch at 1.4 vsyncs for 60 s, with screen verdicts and judged by cost, makes two probes and a third 32 s later; before the fix it made 15 and 8. After it, a scene that holds hold 1 at 0.86 but costs 1.5 at hold 2 is back at hold 1 within 32 s (held off for good, it stayed at hold 2). It returns to hold 1 within 12 vsyncs once the cost falls to 0.55, and within 56 vsyncs (159 before) when the cost falls just after the second failed probe. Hold 1 just out of reach (1.12) gives 8 probes in 150 s at 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 and 32 s; before, there were 32 probes 3.7 s apart. A hitch of six late frames at a cost of 0.74 steps up once (twice before), and frames that do not fit the new hold still step up again. The three earlier cases that expected a failing probe every 8 s forever (CPU slowed, GPU slow at hold 1 too, same cost at hold 2) now expect two probes, then one every 32 s, and a probe at once after a load, a movie's end, or the cost at hold 2 falling to 0.75. The thermal and movie recovery cases are unchanged.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#the-frame-step-current) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (the 0.5 build, MetalFX, 60 fps cap with `DM_FPS_HOLD=on`). Not yet run on a device with the fix.


### A scene that fits hold 1 kept at 30 fps by the fit's margin

- **Symptom:** on a Mac whose GPU clock barely follows the hold, a scene that costs a little under a vsync stays at 30 fps for good once a heavier moment or a load steps it up, though it held 60 before and would hold it again: `frame step: hold 1 → 2` and then no `hold 2 → 1` line, few or no probes. GPU time is the same at either hold, about 0.86–1.0 vsyncs (14.5–16.7 ms). Upstream: Ocarina of Time on an M4 Pro at full resolution, GPU p50 15.0 ms: 48.1 fps, holding 2 vsyncs much of the time.
- **Cause:** the frame step's two rules disagreed between 0.85 and 1.03 vsyncs. Hold 1 keeps frames up to about 1.03 vsyncs (it steps up on three late frames in a row), but the fit trial back from hold 2 needed the cost at most `FSTEP_FIT` (0.85) of a vsync, 14.2 ms. Where the GPU's clock falls at the longer hold, the cost there overstates hold 1's and the GPU-slowed probe covers the gap; where it does not (a base M5, an M4 Pro), nothing did: a scene that fits never fails a probe, so no ratio is learned.
- **Fix:** `nvn_fstep.h`, on the Mac only (`FSTEP_FIT_WIDE`, from `TARGET_OS_IPHONE`; upstream NXNative 293b1d4db): `FSTEP_FIT` is 0.95, so the fit trial covers the band up to where hold 1 still keeps the frames. Two rules come with it there:
  - A trial that held is judged by its run, as a probe is: a step up before the run at its hold lasts `FSTEP_HOLD_MAX` (8 s) counts as a failed trial and doubles the wait (`nvn_fstep.tpend`); the wait goes back to 1 s, and a failed probe's measurement is forgotten, only once the run has lasted.
  - `nvn_fgpu_slow`'s gate asks whether the GPU time fits kmin, the hold a probe goes to, not k − 1: with 0.95 a slowed GPU at hold 3 (1.87 vsyncs) fitted hold 2, took the fit trial there, where no ratio was learned yet, and stayed at 30 where the probe brought 60.
- **NX 18:** the iPhone keeps 0.85 and the rules as they were. It lowers its GPU clock until the work fills most of the frame, so a GPU-bound cost sits near the fit at either hold ([The frame step after a 30 fps cap ends](#the-frame-step-after-a-30-fps-cap-ends)), and NX 18's probes, back-off and resume rules were tuned on phone logs at 0.85; the wider fit there waits for an iPhone frame-rate and battery A/B.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_fstep check_t_fstep_wide`: `t_fstep` runs the whole suite with the iPhone's rule, `t_fstep_wide` the Mac's: a scene of 0.90 vsyncs (±0.03) at either hold after a heavier moment from a light one spends 5,970 of 6,000 frames at hold 1 with one failed trial (none with 0.85); 1.14 vsyncs stays at 30 fps (208 frames at hold 1, NX 18's 32 s probes); an edge scene that steps up 4 s into each held trial takes 13 step ups in 6,000 frames with the wait at 8 s (about 18 with a 1 s wait); a step up 3 s after a held trial fails it and doubles the wait, one after 8 s does not; a GPU at 1.87 vsyncs at hold 3 against 0.6 is slowed.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game): upstream issue [#2400](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2400) (a base M5 at 30 fps in Kokiri Forest; its walk itself costs 1.06 vsyncs of GPU time, so 30 fps is right there and unchanged) and an M4 Pro's full-resolution run. Not measured in a game in NX 18.

### Three late frames among frames on time raised the hold

- **Symptom:** a game that holds about 60 fps with `DM_FPS_HOLD=off` runs at about 40 with the hold on, stuttering: the `frame step: game time …` lines count many frames held 2 and 3 vsyncs, `up / down` climbs to 30 or more a session, and the long frames come out at 33 and 50 ms with little GPU time in them.
- **Cause:** `nvn_fstep_next` stepped the hold up after three late frames in a row (each more than `FSTEP_LATE`, 1.7 ms, past its hold), whatever the frames around them did. The threshold is a rate (53 fps is not 60) applied to single frames: frames whose cost scatters around one vsync meet three late in a row about once a second, though the step pays them back.
- **Fix:** upstream NXNative 1e3fd031, ported to NX 18. Three late frames in a row raise the hold only when the last `FSTEP_RUN` (30) frames at that hold also fall short of it on average (more than `FSTEP_LATE` a frame, each counted between −1 and `FSTEP_SHORT_MAX`, 2), and the new hold is the ceiling of the cheapest of the three late frames' cost. The screen's verdict (present snapshots: Ocarina of Time and Breath of the Wild) and 120 fps (`no_payback`) keep the old rule.
- **NX 18:** Ocarina of Time's short bursts on the CPU side ([Waits at 30 fps doubled by short bursts on the CPU side](#waits-at-30-fps-doubled-by-short-bursts-on-the-cpu-side)) are judged by the screen there, so `fps_hold_backoff burst` still applies to it; under the average a burst is counted only where the average would step up. `t_fstep` runs the Ocarina of Time replays with the screen's verdict.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#frame-rate) on an M4 Max (upstream issue [#1823](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1823)).
- **Check:** `t_fstep` (near-misses, three of every 20 frames late by 4 ms: hold 1, game time = real time; a three-frame hitch: hold 1; a 50 ms frame after eleven of 22 ms: hold 2, not 3; 120 fps: three late frames still step down). The probe cases use a hitch of seven 27 ms frames (`hitch()`), which still steps up.

### A lone stall among near-misses raised the hold

- **Symptom:** a game that holds 60 fps with the frame step drops to 30 for about a second a few times in a few minutes of play, each time just after one long frame (`[longframe]` at 43 to 68 ms), and the `frame step: hold 1 → 2` line says `3 late frames`.
- **Cause:** the average above counted a stall at `FSTEP_SHORT_MAX` (2 vsyncs) toward the 30 frames' limit of 3; near-misses around it already summed to about 1.3, so one stall and the next three late frames stepped up.
- **Fix:** upstream NXNative edc07d34b: the average leaves out the window's longest frame. Two stalls in 30 frames still count one; steady frames over the hold still go to 30, one late frame later.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#frame-rate) on an M4 Max at 144 Hz (upstream issue [#1823](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1823), the retest).
- **Check:** `t_fstep` (lone stalls of 43 to 68 ms at the report's presents among near-misses: hold 1 throughout, game time = real time).

### Frames held longer than the game's largest step

- **Symptom:** a game plays slightly slow (`game time … 0.977 of real time`) in windows where the frame step held frames 3 vsyncs.
- **Cause:** the host held frames up to `FSTEP_MAX` (3) vsyncs and paid debt back with a step one above the hold, but Mario Kart World's stub hands the game at most 2 vsyncs (its own intervals): at hold 3 the game stepped 2 for every 3 shown, and at hold 2 the paid-back 3 was cut to 2 and lost.
- **Fix:** upstream NXNative 1e3fd031: a frame-step stub declares its largest step (`ctx.step_max`, `STEP_MAX` in `fixups.tsv`), the loader passes it on (`dm_fps_step_max`), and `nvn_fstep_next` keeps the hold, a step the game fixes, a fast movie's hold and the paid-back step within it (`smax`). Stubs that declare nothing keep `FSTEP_MAX`. Mario Kart World needs a repack to carry the key.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#frame-rate) (the 0.977 window on the 16 GB M6).
- **Check:** `t_fstep` (frames of 2.6 vsyncs with a cap of 2: hold 2, no step above 2; a step the game fixes at 3 held at 2); `test_patches` (Mario Kart World's stub declares 2).

### Single frames over the fit keep a longer hold

- **Symptom:** on a Mac, 20 or 30 fps for 10–40 s after a new area loads, then a steady 60 again, with nothing compiling or uploading in between: `frame step: hold 1 → 2` and `hold 2 → 3` at the load, and the hold comes back only much later.
- **Cause:** the fit trial needed `FSTEP_RUN` (30) frames in a row each within `FSTEP_FIT` of k − 1, so one frame over the fit in every 12 or 20 kept the longer hold, though the shorter one would absorb it.
- **Fix:** upstream NXNative 5b0f1372 (PR #2367): a frame fits k − 1 when its own cost or the running mean of the hold's frames on time (`ccur`) does. A late frame still ends the run, and a failed trial doubles the wait as before.
- **NX 18:** on the iPhone a scene at hold 2 whose mean fits hold 1 now goes back by a fit trial after 1 s, where the probes did. A trial the mean started (a frame among the last `FSTEP_RUN` (30) of its fit run, the frames that started it, fitted only through `ccur`, so X1.0's per-frame rule would not have started it on that frame: `nvn_fstep.tbym`) is judged by its run, as every trial is on the Mac: a step up before the run at its hold lasts `FSTEP_HOLD_MAX` (8 s) counts as a failed trial and doubles the wait (`tpend`). Judged as a trial whose every frame fitted (X1.0's iPhone rule, the wait back to 1 s once it held), Ocarina of Time's run B replay switched between 60 and 30 every 2–3 s, its stays at hold 2 all 30 frames, about 15 step ups in 30 s (the X1.0 note's flapping); with the run's judgment it gives the log's stays of 30, 60, 120 and 240 frames again (31, 61, 121 and 240 in the log, brought back by probes that doubled their waits). A trial X1.0's rule would have started on the same frame keeps X1.0's iPhone rule, also when a frame earlier in its run (60–240 frames once the wait has doubled) fitted only through `ccur`: marked by any such frame, those trials failed at the next step up and the wait stayed at 8 s (a scene at hold 2 of 0.70 with one frame of 0.90 early in each stay and a burst at hold 1 every 200 frames: 71% of the time at hold 2, 32% now, 28% with X1.0's header). Such a trial that holds further down also ends the pending run of an earlier one the mean started, which ended in that step down: before, a load to hold 3, a held 3 → 2 trial the mean started and an all-fit 2 → 1 trial that held left the first run pending, and a step up 1 s later failed it and doubled the wait.
- **Cost:** a scene whose mean fits the shorter hold while its frames there step up again within 8 s goes back and forth about once every 8 s: each held trial the mean started fails at that step up and the wait doubles to 8 s (`t_fstep` pins that rate on the iPhone with the screen's verdicts: 33 step ups in 5 minutes of run B's scene, 106 when a held trial reset the wait). A scene with a quarter of its frames late at the shorter hold goes back and forth about once every 15 s whether or not its trials are judged by their run: 70 trials in 36,000 frames (17 minutes of real time, most of it at the longer hold), a rate set by how slowly a run that fits only through `ccur` builds there (its mean sits near the fit), not by the wait, which never binds; judged by their run, each of them fails at the next step up (none did before).
- **Check:** `t_fstep` (OoT on a Mac: a load to hold 3, then 1.3 with a frame of 2.0 in every 12: back to hold 1; a mean of 1.77 makes no trial; issue #2400's 1.56 with every sixth at 1.8 or 1.9: hold 2; run B's replay: stays of 30, 60, 120 and 240 frames, 1,137 of 1,800 at hold 2; run B's scene for 5 minutes with the screen's verdicts: at most one step up each 8 s, each a failed trial; only a frame among the last 30 marks a trial as the mean's: a frame of 0.90 as the 30th of a 60-frame stay at hold 2 keeps X1.0's rule, the wait back to 1 s and the next step up no failed trial, as the 31st the trial fails and the wait doubles; 5 minutes with such a frame early in each stay: 32% of the time at hold 2; a held all-fit trial below a held one the mean started: a step up 1 s later no failed trial, the wait 1 s).

### Waits at 30 fps doubled by short bursts on the CPU side

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, at 60 fps under the frame step, a game drops to 30 fps for 1, then 2, 4 and 8 seconds, with a second or two at 60 between, in an area that holds 60 fine otherwise; the log repeats `frame step: hold 1 → 2 vsyncs … (3 late frames …)`, `hold 2 → 1 vsyncs … (probe, cost 0.83× the cost that held hold 1 …)`, `the probe held`, then `the run at hold 1 after the probe lasted under 8 s, counted as a failed probe`, with the GPU at the step ups under the late line of 1.1 vsyncs.
- **Cause:** the back-off in `nvn_fstep.h`. A probe back to hold 1 that holds its 60 frames counts as held only if the run at hold 1 it starts then lasts `FSTEP_HOLD_MAX` (8 s); a shorter run is a failed probe, and each failure doubles both waits (the trials' up to 8 s, the probes' up to 32 s). That stops a CPU that cannot hold 60 from being probed every second, but a game with a short burst on the CPU side every 1–2 s (three frames of 1.12–1.23 vsyncs, GPU 0.81–1.05, beside 11–21 ms of submit against a usual 5–7) never makes 8 s, so the waits at 30 grow with every burst although 60 holds between them. Nothing in the back-off told such a burst from a scene the GPU cannot hold at 60.
- **Fix:** the profile key `fps_hold_backoff burst` (`nvn_fstep.backoff`, set by `fstep_tick` with each frame's GPU time; `DM_FPS_HOLD_BACKOFF=double` / `burst` overrides it). Late frames at hold 1 whose GPU was under the late line and that ask for one vsync more are a short burst on the CPU side: the hold stays at 1 and their lateness is paid back as debt, as after a stay in the background, so the burst ends no run, starts no wait and forgets nothing the probes measured; the first 8 log `a short burst on the CPU side at hold 1 at present N (3 late frames, GPU at most 0.98, under the late line): paid back, no step up`. A burst is one of at most `FSTEP_BURST_N` (5) late frames in a row, not within 30 frames of the last, in a fit trial or a probe's trial too (kept out of trials, a burst in a probe's trial back to hold 1 failed it and doubled its wait); a longer one, one with the GPU over the late line, one asking for two vsyncs more and one soon after the last step up as before. A held probe's run at hold 1 that ends early that way is a failed probe and doubles the waits, as without the key. A cap of those waits at 2 s in the first version sent a probe every 2 s into the GPU-bound scene that had ended such a run (run A, `log.prev.txt` around presents 6610-6734), each failing with its 30 frames of grace late. Probes that fail within their own 30 frames with late frames that are no burst, fit trials that fail that way and the hold-off of two failed probes over budget (`FSTEP_OVER`) are unchanged, so a GPU-bound area backs off as before. With `DM_FIELD_LOG=1` the window line counts the bursts. The first version stepped up for each burst and let the next probe come after 1 s, forgetting what failed probes had measured: with bursts every 1.3–2.3 s it switched between 60 and 30 about every 2 s (13 step ups in 30 s of run B against 6 when doubling), and after a burst in run A's heavy areas the probe 1 s later went into a GPU-bound scene and failed. The trade now: each burst's 3 late frames are shown at 60 instead of a second or more at 30. Per game in X1.0 (Ocarina of Time); every other game's back-off is unchanged.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 t_fstep && dm/gfx/tests/t_fstep`. A replay of Ocarina of Time's run B (hold 1 at 0.88, a burst at the log's five step ups with their GPU times, runs at hold 1 of 114, 108, 78 and 138 frames): doubling gives the log's stays at hold 2 within a frame (30, 60, 120, 240) and 1,137 of 1,800 frames at hold 2 (fit trials by the mean, each judged by its run, bring hold 1 back where the probes did: 1,245 with the probes); With the bursts on their own clock (one every 114, 108, 78 and 138 vsyncs of real time), doubling / `burst`: 7 / 3 step ups (only the bursts whose GPU was 1.13 step up), 750 / 90 vsyncs at hold 2 of 1,800, and the late frames at hold 1 the bursts' own; over 5 minutes 55 / 33 step ups (the 33 are the bursts over the late line) and 15,242 / 1,980 of 18,000 vsyncs at hold 2, where keeping bursts out of trials gave 41 and 11,662. A burst pays back its lateness at hold 1; a longer one, one with the GPU over the late line, one asking for two vsyncs more and one within 30 frames of the last step up. Run A's held probe whose run a burst ended, then a GPU-bound scene, eight times: the same step ups either way (8, and 9 with 15 s GPU-bound), and one late frame more at hold 1 per burst (the first GPU-bound frame, at hold 1 after the burst); with the 2 s cap 16 and 24 step ups and 296 and 560 late frames. Run A's GPU-bound areas (1.3 vsyncs at hold 1, 1.6 times that at hold 2, a burst every 2 s) and the stretch just out of reach (1.12) make the same probes and step ups either way (3 and 4; 5 and 6). At the edge of the heavy areas (0.95 at hold 1, a burst every 2.5 s) 75% → 0% of the time at hold 2, at 6 → 0 step ups a minute.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0, run B, 90–120 s). Not yet run on the phone with the key.

### A return from the background paid back as lost time

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, after each return to the app the game runs at 30 fps for about a second, and for a quarter of a second its time runs fast; the log has `frame step: hold 1 → 2 vsyncs … (3 late frames; cost 1.57 …)` a few presents after `graphics resumed`, a `[longframe]` as long as the stay (40,454.8 ms for 40 s away), and the `game time` line's dropped vsyncs grow by the stay less 15.
- **Cause:** `nvn_fstep.h` measures each frame from the previous frame's start, and the frame across a stay in the background contains the stay. The guest clock is frozen while the app is away (`front_ios.m`), so the game lost no time, but the frame step counted that frame as late and as a debt, capped at `FSTEP_DEBT_MAX` (15 vsyncs) and paid back by stepping the game a vsync more per frame: 0.25 s of fast game time it never owed (run B: dropped vsyncs 470 → 2,884, the stay less 1 and the 15). With the frame at the focus loss before it (41.3 ms) and the first after it (93.2 ms, likely page-ins: iOS had evicted about 0.6 GB of file pages, and about 160 MB came back in the next seconds), that made three late frames, and the hold stepped up; the GPU frames of 26 ms right after the return kept it there until the next probe.
- **Fix:** the profile key `fps_resume on` (`DM_FPS_RESUME=1` / `0` overrides it). `fstep_tick` takes the time `dm_gfx_suspend` held the graphics suspended between the two frame starts (`away_ns`) and marks that frame (`nvn_fstep.away`): it is on time and owes nothing, it is no cost of the scene (the probes' references leave it out), and for `FSTEP_GRACE` (30) frames after it lateness is paid back but does not count toward a step up (`rgrace`), while the clocks and the evicted pages come back; frames still late after that step up as before. A fit trial or probe in progress at the stay does not count its frames down during that grace, so the cost after it decides the trial (a late one fails and doubles the wait as before), not the grace. The focus changes around the stay (about 1.15 s of that 40.5 s frame) count as away too. The log says `frame step: a frame of 40.5 s across 39.3 s in the background at present N: on time and owing nothing …`. Per game in X1.0 (Ocarina of Time); every other game counts the frame as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 t_fstep && dm/gfx/tests/t_fstep`: a replay of run B's trip (41.3 ms, the 40,454.8 ms frame, 93.2 ms, ten frames of 26 ms) steps up once, pays back 17 vsyncs and drops 2,415 (the log: 2,414) without the mark; with it no step up, 11 vsyncs paid back, the frames' own lateness, and none dropped; 26 ms frames past the grace still step up; a fit trial in progress at the stay, followed by frames hold 1 cannot hold, fails after the grace (the wait doubles to 2 s) instead of ending as held; the mark holds for one call and leaves the cost references alone.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0, run B's 40 s trip, run A's 3 s one). Not yet run on the phone with the key.

### A derived profile drops the frame step its build lacks

- **Symptom:** a game whose profile has a frame step (`FRAME_STEP` in its `patches.py`) runs at double speed when packed from another update; the log has no `frame step in whole vsyncs` line, and the frame counter shows 60.
- **Cause:** the NSP script packs a version the profile was not brought up on from a derived profile (`derive_profile` in `scripts/build_ipa_from_nsp.py`), which copied `fps_target 60` unchanged. The frame step stubs are keyed by the main's build ID (`patch_nso.py`), so the other build's main gets no stubs and no state page. Without the page, `fps_present_cap()` (`hle_nvn.c`) took the profile's 60 as the present cap and presented every vsync, while the game still stepped 1/30 s per frame at its own present interval 2.
- **Fix:** `derive_profile` reads the build IDs of the profile's `FRAME_STEP` table (with `ast`; the file is not run). When the table has entries and none is for the derived main, the derived `game.tsv` gets `fps_target 0` with a comment saying why, so the game keeps its own step and the launcher shows no Frame rate choice. Registered builds, `shader_only` derivations, profiles without `FRAME_STEP` and profiles with a function-matched frame step (`MATCH_FRAME_STEP` for `main`, which may place the stubs on this main; where it does not match, the main has no state page and the host keeps the game's own present interval: [A present cap applies only with a frame step or an engine limit the host writes](#a-present-cap-applies-only-with-a-frame-step-or-an-engine-limit-the-host-writes)) keep the key as it is. Until 2026-11-09 a profile with both tables lost the key: Hyrule Warriors: Age of Calamity v458752 stayed at 30 fps with its matched stubs in place.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md) 1.4.2 (v655360; the profile registers 1.4.3), reported in LNPANDY/NX2Apple#303 and traced by fredchu ([PR #794](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/794)).
- **Check:** `test_nsp_incremental` (`test_derived_main_without_frame_step_stubs_keeps_the_games_step`: stubs for another build only, stubs for the derived main too, no table, `shader_only`). fredchu measured 30 fps at real speed on 1.4.2 on an M1 Max; not packed here from a real 1.4.2 NSP, and not run on an iPhone.

### A present cap applies only with a frame step or an engine limit the host writes

- **Symptom:** a fixed-step game runs at double speed (or slow motion at 30) after `DM_FPS_TARGET` is set, in `Documents/env.txt` or the launcher, on a build its profile's frame step stubs and limiter rows do not know.
- **Cause:** without a frame-step state page, `fps_present_cap()` (`hle_nvn.c`) took `DM_FPS_TARGET` or the profile's `fps_target` as the present cap for any game, on the assumption that an engine limiter in `hle_fpslock.c` makes the game run at that rate ([Engine-native frame caps also govern presentation](#engine-native-frame-caps-also-govern-presentation)). Those limiters are build-gated (fixed addresses) or found by name, so on another build none may apply: the host then presented at the cap while the game stepped by its own present interval. [A derived profile drops the frame step its build lacks](#a-derived-profile-drops-the-frame-step-its-build-lacks) keeps the profile's key from asking for it; the environment could still ask.
- **Fix:** `dm_fps_lock_engine()` (`hle_fpslock.c`) says whether the host writes the target into an engine limiter of the running main: a row for its build, NieR's frame interval, Unreal's variables or Frostbite's simulation rate. The search runs once (`pthread_once`), from the first present or from the acquire's present cap, whichever asks first. Without a state page and without such a limiter, `fps_present_cap()` returns 0 and logs `present cap … not applied`, so the game's own present interval stays. Every registered build with a nonzero `fps_target` has a frame step or a limiter, so none changes. Display mode is the exception: there the cap is the present interval alone ([below](#a-present-interval-that-is-the-games-only-limit-in-display-mode)).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md) 1.4.2 with `DM_FPS_TARGET=60`, from reading the code after [PR #794](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/794); not reproduced in a game run.
- **Check:** `t_fpslock` (`dm_fps_lock_engine` in every case, asked before the first present in half of them: limiter rows, Unreal, Frostbite, NieR's build gate, another build's main, a main without Unreal variables); `t_nvn` (no limiter: the 60 and 30 caps are not applied and the game's interval stays; a state page needs no limiter).

### A present interval that is the game's only limit, in display mode

- **Symptom:** a game holds 30 fps with spare CPU and GPU time; it asks for present interval 2 once (`the game sets present interval 1 → 2`) and nothing else limits it. With `present_interval display` it runs at 60, but `fps_target` logs `present cap … not applied` and the player's **Frame rate** (30 or 60) does nothing: frames stay at one a display tick.
- **Cause:** `fps_present_cap()` (`hle_nvn.c`) applied a cap without a frame-step page only when the host writes an engine limiter of the main ([above](#a-present-cap-applies-only-with-a-frame-step-or-an-engine-limit-the-host-writes)), so that a game stepping by its own fixed rate is not presented at another one. A game whose only limit is its present interval has no limiter to write, and needs none: `present_interval display` already declares that it steps by the time that passed.
- **Fix:** in display mode (`present_interval display`, or `DM_PRESENT_INTERVAL=display`) the cap is applied by the present interval alone, with or without an engine limiter, and logged as `present cap … applied by the present interval alone`: 60 presents one frame a tick, 30 two, and the game reads that interval back. `display_mode()` answers from the environment and the profile before the window's first interval, as `present_cfg` does. Game mode is unchanged. Every other profile with `present_interval display` and `fps_target` has a limiter the host writes (or, Monster Train 2, no `fps_target`), so none changes; on a build whose limiter row is missing, the cap now sets the interval display mode would otherwise hold at 1.
- **Found on:** [Red Dead Redemption](../../dm/games/01007820196A6000/README.md#frame-rate) 1.0.7: the interval 2 is set once (`main+0x38ea0`) and the game steps by the time that passed. 60 fps (16.67 ms frames, GPU p50 about 5.5 ms on an M6) and 30 with the player's choice; the opening cutscene's scene cuts at the same seconds at 30 and 60, and a 4 s walk from Armadillo Station's spawn ends at the same spot.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (display mode without a limiter: the 60 and 30 caps applied, the intervals 1 and 2, the interval read back, the 30 Hz link target, no cap, the cap asked for before the window's first interval).

### Engine-native frame caps also govern presentation

- **Symptom:** selecting 30 FPS changes the engine's interval field, but the screen and frame loop still run near 60 FPS.
- **Cause:** presentation took its cap only from the optional frame-step state page. Games with engine-native frame limiters have no such page, so a profile using display pacing continued to present every tick despite `DM_FPS_TARGET=30`.
- **Fix:** resolve a presentation cap from the launcher environment or profile when no frame-step page exists. Use it for the present interval and display-link target without enabling frame-step logic. Existing state-page, movie and game-override behavior keeps precedence; target zero retains the profile's interval semantics.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` verifies literal 33,333,333 / 16,666,666 ns periods for 30 / 60, display-link targets, disabled frame-step logic for native engines, target zero and movie/state-page precedence. All 134 NVN checks pass with Metal validation.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata iPhone controls](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#iphone-resolution-and-frame-rate-controls).

### Resolution policy is independent of the frame-rate cap

- **Symptom:** the game always renders at full resolution, or turning off dynamic resolution still allows the native controller to lower it.
- **Cause:** fixed-resolution code cuts bypassed native scale clamping and UV publication, while the frame-limit handler also overwrote scale and counters. Restoring only those instructions is insufficient when an additional mode-specific lower bound can override the selected preset.
- **Fix:** the verified build retains native scale/UV publication and makes its lower bound follow the selected preset. Apply the resolution choice independently of the FPS limiter. In dynamic mode the game owns current scale and timing counters; fixed mode selects the full-resolution minimum. Verify the build and required instructions before enabling it. Rewriter `CAPABILITIES` metadata offers the launcher control only for supported rewrites; older fixed-resolution images omit it.
- **Check:** production memory-policy tests cover 0/30/60 FPS, on/off/unset/invalid choices, older code and unmatched builds. An asset-backed fixture executes the actual native controller with only GOT addresses relocated, checking both console modes, scale reduction under load, correct UV publication, full-resolution recovery and the fixed-resolution path.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata iPhone controls](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#iphone-resolution-and-frame-rate-controls).

### GPU timestamps per submission segment

- **Symptom:** slow motion when the GPU is the bottleneck: a game's interval controller that times the GPU never steps up (GitHub issue #9).
- **Cause:** a game that times its GPU work as the difference of two timestamp reports in one submission (`TIMESTAMP_TOP` at the start of the frame's command buffer, `TIMESTAMP` at its end) read 0–6 ticks, because the host wrote all the reports of a submission with one time in its completion handler.
- **Fix:** for every game, each report takes the GPU start (`TIMESTAMP_TOP`) or end (other types) of the `sub_cut` segment ([Submissions handed to the GPU in segments](#submissions-handed-to-the-gpu-in-segments)) it was encoded in (`rep_ticks` in `nvn_mtl.m`: a boundary's counter-heap timestamp, or the MTL4CommitFeedback times of its command buffer, converted to the guest clock, written in the last segment's handler as before). It was the profile key `gpu_timestamps segment` of five games until it became the host's default; that is what hardware records, and games that measured nothing before now see their GPU time ([PR #1229](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1229), [issue #1064](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1064)). A boundary's time is its age on the GPU clock taken back from Metal's paired CPU sample projected onto the guest clock (`guest_ns_at_host_time`), not from a later guest clock read, so a delay after the sample does not enter the report (`t_counter_clock`). `DM_NVN_GPU_TS=submission` gives one time per submission again, `segment_gaps` keeps the gaps below.
- **Gaps between command buffers:** a Switch GPU runs a submission's commands back to back. Here it waits at each of `sub_cut`'s commits while the host translates up to the next one (a commit is made only once the GPU has run out of work), and a span of reports counted that wait as GPU time: about 5–6 ms of a Mario Kart World frame on an M5, and Splatoon Raiders read 16 ms where the GPU worked 8. agl's dynamic resolution then saw its budget exceeded when it was not, stepped its render size down and back up from frame to frame, and thin distant geometry flickered (Mario Kart World's mountains and bridges, on the title screen and in free roam). Lowering the resolution cannot shorten the host's translation, so a report leaves out the GPU's idle time before it in its submission (`seg_idle_before`): the gaps between consecutive command buffers the submission committed (`seg_clk.cm`), each counted once the report's time is past its middle, as a boundary's stamp lands either in the command buffer before a commit or at the start of the next, never inside the gap. A command buffer whose feedback has not arrived gives no gap. `t_counter_clock` checks a submission of three command buffers. Found by [PR #1350](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1350) and [issue #1290](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1290), whose change was written for segments that were all commits and no longer applied. Expected cost: with the gaps counted the game kept a lower render size, which also hid the host's translation; without them it picks a higher size, and where the host's translation is not hidden behind the GPU, frames may miss more often (Splatoon Raiders in issue #1290: render width median 0.57 → 0.94 of full size, dropped frames 4 → 35 per 300). A game whose controller reads real times may also step down where one time per submission kept it at full size with no frame to gain: Mario Kart World on a 16 GB M6, at 60 fps either way, with 9–11 ms of GPU a frame ([its note](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#frame-rate)).
- **Encode thread:** the boundaries' timestamps are recorded with the encoder's other commands (`nvn_rec.h` `RO_R_TS` / `RO_C_TS`), so the counter heap serves the encode thread too. Before, with it (every iPhone game) every boundary stayed a commit, which the default would have spread to every game.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (legacy frame-step method): BotW's frame has 5 reports in one submission (`main+0xe94584`, `main+0xe94664`); with two more cuts the controller always takes the GPU path, and the GPU time then read the frame's GPU span, gaps where the GPU waits for the host's translation included (taken out since; not measured on BotW). Measured on the Mac with two BotW runs sharing the GPU under a heavy CPU load (load average about 75, a shader rebuild running): GPU time p50 16–23 ms; the fixed run stayed at interval 2 (3 while the GPU time passed 1.9 vsyncs, 1 for under 2% of the frames) at 0.93–0.98 of real time, while a run with `DM_NVN_GPU_TS=submission` spent 10–70% of its frames at interval 1 and ran at 0.78–0.95. The same GPU time drives the game's dynamic resolution. [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#frame-rate) times its GPU work the same way (PR #1229): with one time per submission a 16 GB M1 Pro kept 2048x1152 at a GPU p50 of 27 to 28 ms; with segment times (gaps still counted) the first race's scene stepped down to 1328x748 and went from 33.4–33.6 to 42.2–42.3 fps. Also [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md), [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), [NieR:Automata](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md) and [Splatoon Raiders](../../dm/games/0400380020056000/README.md).

### The GPU's wait for the host's translation between submissions

- **Symptom:** a game with dynamic resolution steps down to its floor and stays there while its GPU time does not follow the resolution: Nintendo Switch Sports Resort in a table tennis match at 854x480 for minutes (560 frames of 600 at 854x480), GPU p50 about 12 ms at 854x480 as at full size, 60 fps either way. Whether it falls is chance, and a fall is for good: of four runs of the same route that left the frame's span as it was, one held full size and three fell and stayed down.
- **Cause:** a Switch GPU starts a submission as soon as the previous one ends, or when the game submits it if later. Here the host translates each submission after the game submits it, and the GPU waits for that. Switch Sports Resort submits a frame's three submissions within 0.2 ms (from two threads), and the GPU then waited 1.3-2.6 ms before each. Its frame-start `TIMESTAMP_TOP` (in the first submission) and frame-end `TIMESTAMP` (in the third) spanned about 14.3 ms where the GPU worked about 12. agl's dynamic resolution steers by that span; no resolution shortens the host's translation, so once a long frame took it down, the span stayed over its threshold at every size. [GPU timestamps per submission segment](#gpu-timestamps-per-submission-segment) already left out the same wait between the command buffers of one submission.
- **Fix:** general: the reports read a GPU timeline that places each command buffer where a Switch GPU would have run it, at the later of the timeline's end and its submission's ready time, and each report moves back by how much later the real GPU ran its command buffer (`place_back` in `nvn_mtl.m`). The ready time is the game's `nvnQueueSubmitCommands` (`nvn_queue_submit`), or the passing of a queue wait it was held for (`mtl_sub_ready`). `sub_place` gives a submission's first command buffer its offset, in fence order; each later command buffer adds the gaps before it, as in [GPU timestamps per submission segment](#gpu-timestamps-per-submission-segment), since a submission is ready all at once. Reports never move before the game submitted, so the game's own waits (its CPU work, the present, its pacing) stay in, nor after the real GPU ran them. A submission with no ready time, or whose first command buffer's feedback has not arrived (a SyncWait that woke on the GPU's signal first), is not moved, and the timeline goes on from its end as its reports read it, so no later report reads earlier than one of it (in [PR #2491](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2491) as written, such a submission left the timeline behind and the next one was placed over it). `DM_NVN_GPU_TS=segment_submit_gaps` keeps the wait (the earlier default).
- **Ruled out first:** timestamp reports at their own place in the command stream (a counter-heap stamp at each report instead of the cut segment's time) made agl's per-part reports exact but left the frame's span, and the fall, as it was. Taking out every gap between submissions of one guest frame failed: the game presents a frame after submitting part of the next, so the present count does not bound its frames.
- **Check:** `t_counter_clock`: a submission the game submitted while the one before ran is placed at that one's end and its span is its GPU work; a submission submitted after the one before ended keeps the wait for it; one of two command buffers is placed from the first one's start with its own gap out too; no ready time, a fence out of order, a first command buffer with no feedback and a placement later than the real GPU move nothing, and the next submission after one not moved starts at its end. With `DM_NVN_GPU_TS=segment_submit_gaps` the 11 placement checks fail. In the game (M2 Max, a clone of the app with the old chain's shaders, the table tennis route of the playtest save, 330 s, 1280x720 window): the frame span 14.3 → about 12.1 ms; DM_UPSCALER=game: full size in 92% of the frames, back to full size within 600 presents after a fall (the four runs with the span as it was: 75%, 98%, 33%, 44%); with the player's settings (DM_UPSCALER=metalfx, DM_PRESENT_UPSCALE=metalfx) 99.4% against 61% without, and MetalFX scalers made 13 times in the run against 34 (the game's upscaler sizes come back less often). Frame time p50 16.0-16.3 / p95 20-24 ms and GPU p50 about 12.0 / p95 13.6 ms at full size, as without (the window's 75 Hz screen sets the p95). Avioneta's tutorial, whose GPU takes 27.6 ms a frame: 30 fps at 854x480 with and without, the same frame and GPU times. `regress.py check` on an M4 Pro (2026-11-15, main against the timeline): Mario Kart World and Breath of the Wild PASS, the same fps, frame p99 and screenshots; Mario Kart World's GPU took 7-8 ms a frame and its scene stayed at 2560x1440 with and without (`DM_NVN_GPUPROF=every:600`), so its dynamic resolution was not exercised there.
- **Found on:** Nintendo Switch Sports Resort, upstream (PR #2491; not a profile in this tree). On NX 18 the games whose dynamic resolution reads these reports include Ocarina of Time, Mario Kart World and Donkey Kong Bananza (agl), not yet run with this change.

### Raw NVN timestamp units

- **Symptom:** GPU time reads 32 times too small when a game converts raw NVN counter timestamps itself.
- **Cause:** the host wrote reports in 19.2 MHz CPU ticks, while the raw NVN timestamp field uses 614.4 MHz ticks (384 ticks per 625 ns). Games calling `nvnDeviceGetTimestampInNanoseconds` saw correct time because its inverse conversion had the same wrong rate, but an inline conversion read a 16 ms span as 0.5 ms.
- **Fix:** `nvn_timestamp.h` shares the 614.4 MHz conversions between all report writes (`gpu_ticks`, `rep_ticks`) and `nvnDeviceGetTimestampInNanoseconds`. Wide integer products preserve long uptimes. The guest clock epoch stays the same; CPU system counters stay at 19.2 MHz (31.25 MHz on the Switch 2: [The Switch 2's system tick is 31.25 MHz](#the-switch-2s-system-tick-is-3125-mhz)), and the API still returns nanoseconds. The Switch 2's raw field holds nanoseconds instead: [NVN2 timestamps are nanoseconds](#nvn2-timestamps-are-nanoseconds).
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata The End of YoRHa Edition](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), whose dynamic-resolution controller subtracts two raw report timestamps and converts the difference inline.
- **Check:** `t_timestamp` checks known vectors, long uptimes, round-trip precision and the game's exact inline arithmetic. `t_nvn` checks the API's known units and bounds a completed report between submit and fence wait.

### A storage takeover looked at a spared entry once per 64 KB cell

- **Symptom:** a `sample` of the queue replay thread shows `alias_take_over` → `nvn_hm_get` and `memcmp` under the passes' first draws (`draw_` → `rt_dims` → `tex_mtl_at`): 3% of the thread's samples, 6% of its busy time, in a scene where the game makes its render targets again as new texture objects.
- **Cause:** a new shared texture takes over the storage of the entries it overlaps under another description (`alias_take_over` in `nvn_mtl.m`; `nvn_owner.m` upstream). It finds them through the index of 64 KB cells, in which each entry is listed in every cell its storage reaches, and it looked at each entry in each cell of the overlap. An entry it spares (looked up in the last 16 submissions, or held by a live object) cost a key compare and two hash lookups per cell: a 16 MB render target over storage that others share, about 256 cells, repeated that hundreds of times per new texture.
- **Fix:** general, in the host (`alias_take_over`, upstream NXNative `d664d3172`): an entry is looked at only in the first cell of its overlap with the new texture. The result is the same; `g_alias_looks` counts the entries takeovers looked at (for the test; not on the memory line in NX 18).
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), Mac, the owner's save in the Temple of Time (batch OoT-30, item C-1).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_alias_takeover`: a spared entry that lies in every cell of the new texture is looked at once (fails before the fix).

### NVN2 timestamps are nanoseconds

- **Symptom:** on a Switch 2 game, GPU times measured from raw counter reports read 0.6144 of their length: a 16.7 ms frame reads 10.2 ms, and the timestamps lag the guest clock by a third of its uptime.
- **Cause:** the host wrote every game's reports in the Switch's 614.4 MHz ticks ([Raw NVN timestamp units](#raw-nvn-timestamp-units)), while NVN2's raw field holds nanoseconds. `nn::gfx`'s `GetDuration` shows both: it subtracts two raw timestamps and converts the difference, by 625 / 384 in its NVN backend (ApiVersion 8) and not at all in its NVN2 backend (ApiVersion 200). Upstream's scan of 14 Switch 2 mains found none that converts by 625 / 384.
- **Fix:** general, by the game's NVN (upstream `d6bb5894c`): `nvnDeviceInitialize` sets `nvn_timestamp_ns` from `dm_sdk_ounce`, and `nvn_timestamp.h`'s two conversions, which all report writes (`gpu_ticks`, `rep_ticks`) and `nvnDeviceGetTimestampInNanoseconds` share, are then the identity. Games that read reports through the API see no change (Donkey Kong Bananza's frame-rate controller, `hle_fctl.c`, reads its report through `nvnDeviceGetTimestampInNanoseconds`: unchanged). The heat ladder's GPU time stretch (`nvn_heat_stretch_ts`, `post_run`) is linear in the report time, so the k it applies gives the same span ratio in either unit; NX 18's own GPU measures (Vitals, the devlog GPU p50, the GPU clock probe) read Metal's command-buffer times, not reports, and are unchanged.
- **Found on:** upstream's Nintendo Switch 2 Welcome Tour, whose profiler reads each frame's GPU time through `nn::gfx` and fed dynamic resolution about 39% low. In NX 18 the Mario Kart World family (Mario Kart World, Donkey Kong Bananza) steers its dynamic resolution by report spans: any of their raw readers saw 0.6144 of the GPU time before this, so the stretch's k values, set from readings under the old unit, may need a recheck ([the heat ladder's resolution rungs](#the-heat-ladders-resolution-rungs-and-the-clock-rescue-share-one-render-scale)).
- **Check:** `t_timestamp`: with `nvn_timestamp_ns` set both conversions are the identity and `nn::gfx`'s raw difference reads a 60 fps frame as 16.67 ms; the 614.4 MHz report gave 10.24 ms. `t_counter_clock`: the stretch reads a 2,000 ns submission at x1.5 as 3,000 ns in both units.

### A GPU time scale for one game

- **Symptom:** on the iPhone 18 Pro Max, Donkey Kong Bananza with true NVN2 report times ([NVN2 timestamps are nanoseconds](#nvn2-timestamps-are-nanoseconds)) ran at 60 fps 59% of the time (47.8 fps median, 105 of 112 host probes back to 60 failed), against 83-93% at 60 on the builds before, whose reports read 0.6144 of the GPU time.
- **Cause:** not settled. The reports' spans are the only thing the unit change moved for this game: its frame-rate controller reads a single report through `nvnDeviceGetTimestampInNanoseconds` against its CPU clock (the end of the frame's GPU work, which round-trips to the same nanoseconds in either unit), so its measure and `hle_fctl.c`'s are unchanged; a raw span reader (its dynamic resolution, if read through `nn::gfx`'s NVN2 `GetDuration`) now reads 1 / 0.6144 times longer GPU times than before. The runs also differ in build, route and clocks.
- **Fix (owner's choice, one game):** the profile key `gpu_time_scale` (`dm/games/README.md` Keys table), Donkey Kong Bananza 0.6144: `post_run` moves each submission's reports toward its end by that factor of their distance (`nvn_heat_stretch_ts` with k below 1), so every span inside a submission reads 0.6144 of its length, as before, and the end stays. It rides on the heat ladder's GPU time stretch: k = scale × 1 / render scale² (`nvn_scale_stretch_at`), so 85% reads 0.85 of the GPU time, 70% 1.25, the clock rescue 1.09. With k below 1 a report reads between its own time and its submission's end, never past it, so the order of reports and submissions holds and the floor never binds; a span across submissions shrinks only by its parts inside them. `DM_GPU_TIME_SCALE=1` gives the true times for an A/B; every other game keeps k exactly as before (a product by 1.0). NX 18's own GPU measures (Vitals, the devlog GPU p50, the GPU clock probe) read Metal's command-buffer times and the frame-rate controller gate (`hle_fctl.c`) the game's own end-to-end measure: none reads report spans, so their thresholds stay. One `[nvn] GPU time scale ×0.6144` line at the lever's start.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md), round logs bananza-r3 against bananza-r2-ctl, bananza-r2-game and bananza-grace-A. Whether the scale brings back the earlier share of 60 is not yet measured on the device.
- **Check:** `t_heat` (k below 1 alone and under the heat and rescue k: order, ends, the floor, nothing outside a report's own time and its end, k 1 bit for bit), `t_counter_clock` (0.6144 and 85% through `post_run` on NVN2 reports), `t_thermal` under `GAME=040074A01BF12000` (the wire from `scale_set` carries scale × k).

### A Battery Saver that holds a game's own controller at 30

- **Symptom:** Donkey Kong Bananza drew no Battery Saver chip: a chip is drawn only where the preset moves one of the game's rows, and Battery Saver moves Frame rate and Render scale, which this game has neither of (its own frame-rate controller picks 60 or 30).
- **Cause:** a preset could only set rows; the game's 60 / 30 choice is its controller's, which the host steers through the observation gate (`hle_fctl.c`) with no row of its own.
- **Fix:** a preset may set a switch no row sets (`settings.py PRESET_ENV`, launcher.json `preset_env`; the profile's `preset battery DM_FPS_CTL=hold30`), which counts as a move, so the chip is drawn; the launcher puts it into the environment at every start for that preset and clears it for the others (Custom included), never over an env file's or the launch's value, and the in-game menu does the same under its lock. `DM_FPS_CTL=hold30` hands the controller the middle of interval 2's band every judged frame: it steps up to 30 three frames after it reads it and never comes back, so there are no probes and no failed ones, and a cutscene's own fixed interval is left alone. Under it the GPU clock rescue is off (its target is 30) and the game's counter reports read their true times ([A GPU time scale for one game](#a-gpu-time-scale-for-one-game) off), so its dynamic resolution has the whole 33 ms; the heat ladder's resolution rungs still stretch that reading, and its 30 fps hold changes nothing at 30. The host reads the switch again once a second, so a preset change in the in-game menu applies while the game runs (`now DM_FPS_CTL=… (the in-game menu's preset …)`), and back at the host's rule its probe wait starts from there. No new visible row.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md). Not yet run on a device.
- **Check:** host `t_fctl` (the hold: interval 2's band every frame, no probe through light scenes and rescues, top rate 30, the change back to host read within a second and its probe after); `test_launcher_settings` (Bananza's chip and `preset_env`, nothing else moved); `test_live_menu_wiring` (every start and the menu set it under the lock).

### A feedback time read in its own clock tick counted as in the future

- **Symptom:** `t_counter_clock`'s check "feedback conversion uses the resumed guest epoch" failed in about one run of six (`gpu_s_guest_ns` returned 0, unknown). In a game, a counter report whose completion handler reads the clock in the same tick as the command buffer's `GPUStartTime` or `GPUEndTime` fell back as if that GPU time were unknown (GitHub issue #1896).
- **Cause:** `gpu_s_guest_ns` (`nvn_mtl.m`) took the age of a feedback time as `(double)now - s * 1e9`. clang contracts that line into a fused multiply-subtract, so the product `s * 1e9` is not rounded to a double, and for about half of all times its exact value lies a fraction of a nanosecond above the integer `now` of the same clock tick (mach time steps 41.67 ns at 24 MHz). The age came out just below 0 and the time was rejected as in the future.
- **Fix:** the age is taken in integer nanoseconds: the time rounded to a nanosecond, allowed to lie ahead of `now` by what the double's resolution at that uptime can add (about 0.5 ns a month) plus the rounding; such a time is the read's own. NaN, infinity and negative times stay unknown.
- **Check:** `t_counter_clock` converts 20,000 times read just before the call, none of which may come out unknown (failed in every run before, passes in 100 of 100 after, on an M4 up for 33 hours), and a time one double step ahead of the read, which must be the read's time.
- **Found on:** the unit test, on an M5 Max (3 of 20 runs) and an M4 (6 of 40); no game report.

### The Switch 2's system tick is 31.25 MHz

- **Symptom:** a Switch 2 game holds 20 or 30 fps on the Mac although its frame work fits in one vsync: its frame-rate controller raises the present interval (`the game sets present interval 1 → 3` in the log) and never steps back down.
- **Cause:** the Switch 2's SDK converts the system counter at 31.25 MHz (`nn::os::GetSystemTickFrequency` answers `0x1dcd650`; it reads `cntpct_el0` itself and converts with that constant), while the host served the counter (the `cntpct` gate and `svc GetSystemTick`) at the Switch's 19.2 MHz. Every span the game measured in ticks came out 1.63 times too short, and a nanosecond time it converted to ticks (`nn::os::ConvertToTick`) landed 1.63 times later than a counter read taken at the same moment. A controller that compares a GPU timestamp with a CPU tick stamp then saw a frame that ended long after it started.
- **Fix:** `guest_ticks` (`hle_os.c`) gives the counter on the guest clock at the rate of the game's SDK: 32 ns a tick when the `sdk` module names its spec `Ounce` (`dm_sdk_ounce`), 19.2 MHz otherwise. Both the `cntpct` gate (`hle.c`) and `svc GetSystemTick` (`hle_svc.c`) read it. NVN's report timestamps ([Raw NVN timestamp units](#raw-nvn-timestamp-units), nanoseconds on NVN2: [NVN2 timestamps are nanoseconds](#nvn2-timestamps-are-nanoseconds)) are a separate unit.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md): its controller (`main+0x204c20`, called from `main+0x204a64` and `main+0x203718`) measures `ConvertToTick(nvnDeviceGetTimestampInNanoseconds(report))` minus a tick stamp taken at the frame's start (`main+0x204618`); a probe read 0x11d69767da4 against 0xaf5b673f04, a ratio of 1.6276 = 31.25 / 19.2. It set interval 3 and ran at 20 fps (50 ms frames); with the fix its first level runs at 60 fps (frame time p50 16.7 ms over 200 s, interval 1, raised only for a moment at loading hitches).

### A frame-rate controller timed by clocks that follow its interval

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, a game that picks its own present interval drops to 30 fps in a busy moment and stays there for minutes, until a menu is opened (`the game sets present interval 1 → 2` in the log and no `2 → 1` until the menu), or comes back and drops again a few frames later, 60 ↔ 30 several times a second (power windows of 39-49 fps, `the game sets present interval` lines in pairs); thermal state 0 throughout.
- **Cause:** the game's controller times each frame on the wall clock, from its start to the later of its submission and the GPU's end, and takes the time a frame took at the longer interval as what it would take at the shorter one. On the console the clocks do not follow the interval. An iPhone lowers its GPU and CPU clocks once the game runs at 30 fps, until the work fills most of the 33 ms (GPU 13.6 → 17.7 ms a frame and the host's GPU probe 2.4 → 3.2 times slower than its best with MetalFX drawing the game's upscaler, 25 ms with the game's own network), so the frames measured over the controller's threshold for coming back (1.2 vsyncs) for as long as the scene lasted. When they measured just under it, the controller came back at the lowered clocks, and three late frames stepped it up again before the clocks could rise. A menu's frames (about 1 ms of GPU) fit at any clock, which is why it brought 60 back.
- **Fix:** the host decides when the controller comes back, through the measure the controller judges (`nvn_fctl.h`, `hle_fctl.c`): an observation gate in the controller (`OBS_FPS_CTL`, in the game's `patches.py`; the rewriter puts the callback before the controller's test of its off switch) hands it the host's measure in the same ticks. At the longer interval its own return is held (the measure stays in its interval's band, so its countdown does not move) until a probe: 1 s after the step up, doubled after each failed probe up to 32 s (the frame step's hold-offs, `nvn_fstep.h`), only at thermal state nominal or fair and with Low Power Mode off; or at once after six frames in a row under one vsync at the lowered clocks (a menu), lighter than where the last probe failed. A probe hands the controller interval 1's band until its own countdown brings it down (7 frames a step), then holds the measure in that band for the 30 frames the phone takes to raise its clocks (the frame step's grace), then gives the game's own measure back: a scene that really cannot hold 60 steps up again by the game's own rule, which is a failed probe when it comes within 8 s. 8 s at interval 1 or a load forgets the failures. The game's own steps up are never held, the game still steps by the interval it chose (so its speed is its own), and an interval it fixes itself (a cutscene's 3) is left alone. `DM_FPS_CTL=game` gives the game's own measure back: it is the A/B switch, the only difference between two runs of the same route. Frames late in the grace run the game slow for that moment, as any late frame at an interval does on the console.
- **The probes' gate and early end (`DM_FPS_CTL_PROBE`, on by default, `=0` off):** Bananza's long phone run (NX18-X2.0 bananza-alldata-analysis.md, ranked fix 5) had the CPU capped, frames of about 19 ms at 60 fps: the timed probes failed again and again with the back-off, and frames of 18-20 ms are late at 60 but under the controller's own step at 1.2 vsyncs, so a try could run late for seconds. Two rules on ordinary timed probes: once one has failed in the scene (no load and no 8 s at interval 1 since), the next waits while the 8-frame mean at the longer interval is over 20.8 ms (`DM_FPS_CTL_PROBE=<ms>`, 15 to 40, for another gate) and starts at the first frame under it; and a probe whose frames after its 30-frame grace run 4 of the last 8 over 18.3 ms by the game's own measure is ended at once (interval 2's band handed until the controller steps up: a failed probe, its hold-off doubled). The first probe after a step up is never gated: at 30 the mean reads the lowered clocks (22-29 ms where 60 fits at the full clock, the case above), so only a failed try says the scene has no headroom. A light scene's probe, a GPU clock rescue's or kick's (`dm_fctl_rescue`) and the pin (`DM_CLOCK_RESCUE_S`, `DM_CLOCK_KICK_PIN`) are as before; Battery Saver's `hold30` never probes. A gated wait releases as the scene lightens, so a menu after a failure usually comes back by the mean a frame or two before the light rule's sixth frame. Lines: `the probe waits (DM_FPS_CTL_PROBE, frame N): … over the gate's 20.8 ms since the probe that failed`, `the probe is ended early (DM_FPS_CTL_PROBE, frame N): 4 of the last 8 frames after its grace over 18.3 ms`; the summary line ends `; DM_FPS_CTL_PROBE: probes ended early N, waits the gate held M`. Expected (not yet measured on the phone): fewer late frames in failed tries (the analysis: about 550-700 per 4.5 min) for a steadier 30 in scenes that cannot hold 60.
- **Log (for tuning the rule on measured numbers):** logging only (`nvn_fctl_obs` reads the decision's state through a const pointer; `t_fctl` replays the same frames with and without it and compares every measure handed and every state). A light probe's start line gives its six frames' mean and largest measure and the scene's measure at the longer interval just before them (`the run 5.0 ms mean, 5.0 max, the scene before it 33.3 ms`). Each probe that ends (held 8 s, failed, dropped, or cut short by the game) has a line of its numbers: its frames from the start, the game's own measure in its 30 grace frames (mean and largest: what the controller would have judged while the clocks rose) and after them, its frames at interval 1 that missed their vsync and the time they took over it (not a frame of 15 vsyncs or more: it spans what the gate did not see), and the host's GPU probe (`DM_GPU_PROBE`, every 2 s) at its start and end against the session's fastest. The summary line every 3,600 frames adds the time at interval 1, 2 and 3 or more (frames across a load or a stay in the background at neither) and the late frames failed probes cost; with `DM_FPS_CTL=game` it gives the time alone, so an A/B compares the time at 60 under both rules (`scripts/devlog.py <log> <log>`: `time at interval 1 (%)`, `failed probes: late frames`). The game's [note](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#frame-rate-on-the-iphone) has the formats.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#frame-rate-on-the-iphone) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (three logs, 2026-11-13 to 15; the owner's report: 30 fps until the pause menu). With the game's own upscaler: 30 fps for 35 s at a time (GPU 13.6 ms a frame at 60, 24-26 ms at 30), and 3 minutes of 30-41 fps windows; with MetalFX: two stretches of 60 ↔ 30 flapping, 5 and 12 s. Not yet run on a device with the fix.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=040074A01BF12000 t_fctl && dm/gfx/tests/t_fctl` models the controller as its code does it on a phone whose GPU clock follows the interval: without the host the scene stays at 30 for the minute after a hitch, with it back at 60 in 72 vsyncs and held (clocks 1.3 and 1.84 times slower at 30); a scene that cannot hold 60 stays at 30 with 7 probes in 2 minutes and the hold-off at 32 s; the pause menu brings 60 within 30 vsyncs whatever the hold-off; no timed probe under heat; the game's own step up frame for frame as without the host; a listener that holds the return; a load; the log's numbers (each probe's end seen once, also when the game steps up on a frame long enough to start the next probe at once, its grace and late frames, a 200-vsync frame no late frame, the time at each interval as the frames showed), and 10,000 frames through every kind of probe with and without the log, the same measure handed and state every frame. `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=040074A01BF12000 run` (`t_fctl`: the gate's registers, ticks at 31.25 MHz, frames left alone, `DM_FPS_CTL=game`; a replayed run through the gate with its log lines hands what `nvn_fctl_next` alone decides, frame for frame, and the probe, light-start and summary lines carry their numbers, the summary's time with `DM_FPS_CTL=game` too; a probe that fails on the frame that starts the next has its line, before the next one's start). Both also check `DM_FPS_CTL_PROBE`: the first probe runs, a capped probe ended after its grace (3 late frames in 8 go on, 4 end it; grace frames and the pin do not count), the gate's wait and its release as the mean falls, a rescue's and a light scene's probes and runs as with the switch off, a listener holding the step up; on the model, a heavy scene's probes 7 → 1, a capped scene 99% at 60 with ~7,100 late frames → a steady 30, and the scene that fits 60 at the full clock handed the same measure every frame; through the gate the replay under both settings, the lines, the clock kick's `dm_fctl_rescue` over the gate, and the switch's values.

## Engine frame limiters

### Unreal's dynamic resolution at the target's frame time

- **Symptom:** an Unreal game raised to 60 fps whose device profile turns dynamic resolution on stays at its highest screen percentage while it runs well below 60: FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE at 80 % (the top of its 60-80 % range) at 40 fps on the AYN Odin 3 (nx2android, Adreno 830).
- **Cause:** the engine's dynamic resolution lowers the resolution when the GPU time passes `r.DynamicRes.FrameTimeBudget`, which the engine's Switch device profile sets to 33.33 ms (`BaseSwitchDeviceProfiles.ini`) for the console's 30. At 60 a frame had to take twice the target before the resolution moved.
- **Fix:** `r.DynamicRes.FrameTimeBudget` is one of `ue_cvar.py`'s names, found by its registration like the frame limit ([Unreal's frame limit and present interval, found by name](#unreals-frame-limit-and-present-interval-found-by-name)), and `hle_fpslock.c` writes the frame time at `fps_target` into both copies (16.167 ms at 60, with the 60 fps margin; 33.333 at 30), whenever it differs. A game without dynamic resolution (`r.DynamicRes.OperationMode` 0) does not read it. FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE 1.0.1: the object at `main+0x8be8118`, beside `r.DynamicRes.MinScreenPercentage` and `MaxScreenPercentage`; 33.33 read there on the Mac.
- **Found on:** [FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE](../../dm/games/0100606026F12000/README.md), on Android (nx2android, where the same value was written from a fixed address first).
- **Check:** `t_fpslock` (the budget written at 60 with the frame limit and the present interval); `ue_cvar.py <main>` prints its object.
- **NX 18:** ported from upstream NXNative 62a38b800 (MIT; a side commit not on upstream's main) on 2026-11-22, for the owner's FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE (global, 0100606026F12000). Our Unreal rows also hold GEngine's fixed frame rate, so up to five rows. `t_fpslock` case 8 adds the budget: 16.167 ms at 60, 33.333 under a 30 fps hold, back at 60. Not yet run on a device.

### Unity's integer target frame rate

- **Symptom:** menus reach 60 fps with `present_interval display`, but entering gameplay returns to 30 fps despite spare GPU time.
- **Cause:** the game sets Unity's `Application.targetFrameRate` to 30. Changing NVN's present pacing does not change the engine's own limiter.
- **Fix:** `hle_fpslock.c` supports integer FPS fields alongside float FPS, milliseconds and vsync counts. It keeps the field at the profile's `fps_target` (`DM_FPS_TARGET` overrides it), preserving unset values such as 0 and -1. `present_interval display` removes the separate interval-2 present cap. The rewriter finds the field from the engine's registration of the setter, with no build row ([below](#unitys-vsynccount-and-targetframerate-found-through-the-internal-call-table)); until 2026-11-09 a row gated on Brilliant Diamond 1.3.0's main named it.
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Brilliant Diamond](../../dm/games/0100000011D90000/README.md), 1.3.0; its note records the setter, field and gameplay timing checks.
- **Check:** `t_fpslock` exercises the write path at the found field, a main where nothing was found, disabled override, repeated game writes, unset values and 30 fps fallback, plus the existing float, millisecond and two-copy vsync paths.

### Unity's vSyncCount and targetFrameRate, found through the internal-call table

- **Symptom:** a Unity game holds 30 fps with spare GPU time (about 2 ms a frame) and sets present interval 2 at its title (`the game sets present interval 1 → 2 (from ?+0)`); with `fps_target 60` the log says the present cap is not applied, as the host writes no limit of this main.
- **Cause:** the game sets `QualitySettings.vSyncCount` to 2 and leaves `Application.targetFrameRate` at -1. The count lives in the current quality level's settings; its setter also hands it to the graphics device, whose `SetVSyncCount` (vtable +0x6f8) clamps it to at least 1, stores it in a global and, only when it changed, tail-calls `nvnWindowSetPresentInterval` (hence `?+0`: no return address in a module). So the interval is set once, on a change: writing the level's count or the device's global afterwards leaves it at 2 (both tried, frames stayed 33.3 ms). Unity steps the game by the time that really passed.
- **Fix:** the rewriter finds both settings in any Unity main whose internal calls it can read, with no build ID (`dm/tools/unity_fps.py`, run by `patch_nso.py` on `main`), and names them in the fixups (`UNITY_TARGET_FPS`; `UNITY_QS_PTR`, `_IDX`, `_ARR`, `_SIZE`, `_VSYNC`). IL2CPP registers the engine's internal calls from a table of name strings followed by a table of their native functions in the same order; Unity 2019.4 and 2017.4 register them from code instead, one call per name with the function in `x1` (from a relocated slot) and the name in `x0`, and the rewriter takes a name registered with exactly one function. `set_targetFrameRate` stores w0 into a global (`adrp`; `str w0`, possibly behind a branch); `get_vSyncCount` reads the QualitySettings object from a global or through `GetManagerFromContext(K)` (the managers array; framed in 2019.4 and 2017.4, and 2017.4 adds the index before an offset load), the level index, the levels, the level by `lsl` or `madd` (levels of 0x100 or 0xa8 bytes), and the count. `hle_fpslock.c` writes the count to 60 / `fps_target` (1 at 120) when it is 1 to 4, and the target to `fps_target` when the game has set one (10 to 120; -1 stays). These count as an engine limit the host writes, so the host's present cap governs presentation (`hle_nvn.c` `fps_present_cap`, and the frame step's hold at 120), which is what actually changes the rate here; the written count keeps what the game reads back (`QualitySettings.vSyncCount`) the same as what it shows. A build row for the same `targetFrameRate` field is written once.
- **Found on:** [The Rogue Prince of Persia](../../dm/games/01008D9022462000/README.md) 1.1.0 (Unity 6000.0.64f1): `targetFrameRate` at `main+0x8d33de8`, `vSyncCount` at `[[main+0x90957d8]+0x20] + [[main+0x90957d8]+0x64] * 0x100 + 0x88`. At 60 its frames are 16.6 ms, and a timed walk from the same save (1 s, then 2 s of the stick) ends at the same place at 30 and 60; 120 also needed [the vsync event at 120](#the-displays-vsync-event-follows-120-fps). Checked offline on the Unity mains on the drives (2026-11-08): Donkey Kong Country Returns HD (6000.0.28f1, the same layout) and Super Mario RPG (2021.3.15f1: `GetManagerFromContext(11)`, levels of 0xa8 bytes, the count at +0x68, the target setter behind a branch); nothing in the 71 other mains. [Fire Emblem Engage](../../dm/games/0100A6301214E000/README.md) 2.0.0 (2020.3.18f1, as Super Mario RPG): its logic steps by the count it reads back, so the host's write of 1 replaced a cut that held the setter's argument at 1 (2026-11-09; the title and attract demo at 16.7 ms, the same scenes at the same frames as with the cut). [Pokémon Brilliant Diamond](../../dm/games/0100000011D90000/README.md) 1.3.0 (2019.4, registered from code): `targetFrameRate` at `main+0x4afc868`, the field its fixed host row wrote until 2026-11-09, and the count at `[[main+0x4f3e0e0]+0x30] + [[main+0x4f3e0e0]+0x4c] * 0xa8 + 0x68`. Return of the Obra Dinn (2017.4) registers only `get_vSyncCount` of the two, found (its profile sets no target). On the 45 mains on the drives on 2026-11-09 the code registration changed only those two results. Open: Unity 2022.3 (SUPER ROBOT WARS Y, Super Monkey Ball Banana Rumble, Monster Train 2) keeps no such pair of tables and registers no name from code in a form this reads (its names are in a string pool nothing points to directly), so nothing is found there.
