- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_unity_fps.py` (synthetic images: Unity 6000's layout, 2021's thunk, managers array and `madd`, a getter it cannot read, names with no function table, a main without the table, 2019.4's and 2017.4's registration from code and manager getters, a name registered with two functions); `t_fpslock` cases 0 to 5 (the found `targetFrameRate`: written, repeated, unset, 0, nothing found) and 25 to 29 (60, 120 following the hold, no target, Brilliant Diamond's field, a count of 0 left for `targetFrameRate`); `unity_fps.py <main>...` prints what it finds in each.

### A render-thread limiter with a constant and a fixed time step

- **Symptom:** the game presents at interval 1, yet every frame takes exactly 33.3 ms of "frame work"; after only the limiter was raised, everything ran twice as fast on the iPhone.
- **Cause:** the cap is the render thread's own limiter, `main+0x707090(this, fps)`, which sleeps out 1000 / fps ms since its previous call, called once with the constant 30 (`main+0x706fa4`). Found from the render thread's backtrace in the thread dump (`HGCommandDispatcher`), not from the NVN calls. The frame update takes a fixed step, the float 1/30 at `main+0x167a6ec`, loaded at `main+0x667904` and passed to `main+0x6a337c`.
- **Fix:** two cuts: the limiter's constant, and a second cut that loads the 1/60 at `main+0x167a834` (the community's 60 fps cheat for this build changes the same two words). Some 20 other loads of that 1/30 turn parameters counted in 30 fps frames into seconds or step single systems; they stay.
- **Found on:** [DARK SOULS: REMASTERED](../../dm/games/01004AB00A260000/README.md) (30 on the Switch, 60 now).
- **Check:** before changing the constant, check how the game steps time, and not by eye: a 0.8 s walk that looked the same at 30 and 60 fps on the Mac was taken for a time-based game, and on the iPhone everything ran twice as fast. Probing every site that loads such a constant (`DM_PROBE`, 16 per run) tells per-frame steps from one-off conversions, but which of them sets the game's speed only shows by timing the game.

### A frame-end wait polled in 2 ms sleeps

- **Symptom:** 55 fps with every frame 18.2 ms, the main thread asleep most of the time; every 11th frame stays on screen two vsyncs.
- **Cause:** the game's own code. KTGL's frame end (`main+0x443e8c`) presents, acquires the next texture, then waits until N / 60 s have passed since the frame's start tick, polling `GetSystemTick` and sleeping 2 ms a round (`main+0x4c9f24`). N is the game's vsyncs per frame, a global. The start tick is read after the scene's update, so each frame lasts N / 60 s plus the time from the last poll to the next start, rounded up to the rounds: never exactly one vsync, on any hardware. The host's `SleepThread` and tick are exact; a model of the loop gives 54 fps with the host's acquire and with the Switch's double-buffered one alike. Found by sampling the host (`sample <pid>`: the main thread in `dm_guest_sleep_ns` under `main+0x443d74`, and in `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` under the present wrapper).
- **Fix:** a build cut on the wait's threshold (its `patches.py`): 0 when N = 1, where the game presents at interval 1 and the host's present pacing (the acquire waits) holds the frame one vsync; frame time p50 16.67 ms, wakeups 2,050 → 300 a second. Scenes with N = 2 still present at interval 1, so the wait stays theirs. Skipping the wait for every N, the first cut, presented 60 frames a second in them (28.8 with the wait, N forced to 2), for code that counts 60 / N frames a second.
- **Check:** before cutting a game's frame limiter, find what sets its target and whether anything else holds those frames. A limiter that also enforces a slower mode (here N = 2) has to keep it.
- **Found on:** [Taiko Risshiden V DX](../../dm/games/0100346017304000/README.md), in its menus and town; its movies, paced by their own player, ran at 60.

### A target frame rate the game steps by

- **Symptom:** a Switch 2 game runs its title and menus at 60 fps and its gameplay at 30, asking for present interval 2, with the GPU mostly idle.
- **Cause:** the game's choice. Its graphics object (a global pointer) holds the display rate, a target frame rate and the longest interval; the present asks for interval min(longest, display / min(display, target)). Three stores set the target, 60 for menus and 30 for gameplay, and 59 readers step the game by 60.0 / target, so a frame advances it 2.0 at 30 and 1.0 at 60.
- **Fix:** the rewriter finds the field by the code that reads and sets it, in any main and with no build ID (`dm/tools/fps_target.py`, run by `patch_nso.py`), and names it in the fixups (`FPS_TARGET_PTR`, the global pointer; `FPS_TARGET_FIELD`, the field). A reader loads the field into a float register, converts it and divides 60.0 by it (`ldr sN, [xB, #F]`; `scvtf`; `fdiv` by a 60.0 made by `movz` + `fmov`), with `xB` the global's value (`ldr xB, [xA]`, `xA` made by `adrp` + `add` or read from the global's GOT slot); a store sets it to an immediate 30 or 60. A field with at least 8 readers and a store of 30 is the target, and exactly one must be: two give a red line and no write. These are the semantics writing it relies on, the game's step following the target, so the search names no engine. `hle_fpslock.c` writes the profile's `fps_target` there whenever it differs; the game then asks for interval 1 itself, and its step follows. At `DM_FPS_TARGET=30` the host writes 30 everywhere, menus included, where the game itself chose 60.
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_fps_target.py` (synthetic code: the global and its GOT slot, too few readers, no store of 30, another dividend, two fields); `fps_target.py <main>...` prints what it finds in each. Before giving a game `fps_target` on this field, check that its readers step the game by it: a target the game only presents by, with logic that counts frames, would run the game fast.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md) v131072 (its gxc graphics object, +0x1dc): gameplay at 60 fps, frame time p50 16.7 ms on an M4 Pro. Checked offline on 100 mains on the drives (2026-11-03), both Paper Marios on Intelligent Systems' older engine among them: the search finds this field (55 of its 59 readers have the form it matches) and, in every other main, not a single reader.

### 120 fps follows the frame step

- **Symptom:** with a game's target frame rate written to 120 and a 120 Hz display link, the game holds 120 where frames fit, but a scene whose frames take 14 ms runs in slow motion at 72 fps, and on a 60 Hz screen the whole game runs at half speed.
- **Cause:** the game's choice: it may step by its target whatever the frames really take ([A target frame rate the game steps by](#a-target-frame-rate-the-game-steps-by): 60.0 / target a frame). A target of 120 advances the game half a step per frame, so its speed is frames a second / 120. Whole-vsync frame steps cannot take 120 at all: their stubs hand the game whole 1/60 s vsyncs.
- **Fix:** `fps_max 120` in a profile lets `DM_FPS_TARGET` take 120 (the launcher's **Frame rate** offers it on the Mac only, through the option condition in `settings.tsv`). `fps_high` in `hle_nvn.c` then runs the frame step's controller ([nvn_fstep.h](../../dm/gfx/nvn/nvn_fstep.h)) with no state page, counted in ticks of 1/120 s: hold 1 is 120 fps, hold 2 is 60, and `hle_fpslock.c` writes the target from the hold (`nvn_fps_high_now`), so what the game steps by stays what the screen shows. `nvn_fstep.per_vsync` keeps the waits between trials the same real time. The cap is 60 on a screen below 120 Hz, while a movie plays and under the thermal cap. On iOS it is always 60 (`high_cap_tick`, `TARGET_OS_IPHONE`), with the 60 Hz link: the iOS front end reports its screen's rate (`front_screen_max_fps` in `front_ios.m`, 120 on a ProMotion screen), and an `env.txt` `DM_FPS_TARGET=120` must not start an unchecked 120 fps there; before, the front end reported no rate and the screen check held it at 60. The display link follows the screen (`mtl_link_mode_set`, `DM_PRESENT_LINK` overrides), and on a screen whose rate is not a multiple of 120 it counts virtual ticks ([Virtual 60 Hz ticks](#virtual-60-hz-ticks-on-screens-that-are-not-a-multiple-of-60)): 144 ticks a second would run a game that steps by its target 1.2 times too fast. `fps_hold off` applies at 120 as at any target: the hold stays at the cap's vsyncs (1 tick at 120; still 2 ticks at a 60 cap during movies, on a 60 Hz screen, or under the thermal cap), so each late frame is shown on the next refresh instead of stepping down to 60, avoiding 120 → 60 drops on delta-time games, at the cost of slow motion on games that step by their target, which `fps_hold on` (the default) prevents ([`fps_hold off` was ignored at 120 fps](#fps_hold-off-was-ignored-at-120-fps)). A vsync count such as `rhi.SyncInterval` stays 1 at 120.
- **Check:** `t_fstep` (kmin 1, kmax 2: frames that fit hold 120, late ones step to 60 and stop there, a cap of 60, the return), `t_link_ticks` (the real acquire at each hold on 120 and 60 Hz links, a game without `fps_max`), `t_fpslock` (the target and Unreal's limit following the hold, the interval staying 1, a game without `fps_max` at 60), `test_settings.py` (the option). Before adding `fps_max 120` to a profile, measure the game's speed at 120 against 60 over the same inputs (below): a game whose logic counts frames runs at double speed. Not run: a real screen at 60 or 144 Hz, or an iPhone.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md). Also on, with its speed measured: [Mario & Luigi: Brothership](../../dm/games/01006D0017F7A000/README.md), through Unreal's `t.MaxFPS` and `rhi.SyncInterval` ([found by name](#unreals-frame-limit-and-present-interval-found-by-name)), with no host change, and [Minecraft Dungeons II](../../dm/games/0100A7C01B792000/README.md#frame-rate), by `rhi.SyncInterval` alone (its `t.MaxFPS` is 0 and its fixed frame rate off): the same walk at 30, 60 and 120 ends at the same spot.

### `fps_hold off` was ignored at 120 fps

- **Symptom:** a game at 120 fps with **Hold frames when late** off (`DM_FPS_HOLD=off` or `fps_hold off`) shows the same `frame step: hold 1 → 2` rises, `up / down` counts, and 60 fps stretches as with it on, and never logs `frames are not held longer than the cap's vsyncs`: the setting is silently ignored.
- **Cause:** `fstep_kmax` (`hle_nvn.c`) returned 2 as soon as `fps_high()` was true, before calling `fstep_hold()`, so `DM_FPS_HOLD=off` was never consulted at 120 fps. That prevented players from opting out of 120 → 60 holds even on delta-time 120 fps games where late frames do not cause slow motion.
- **Fix:** `fstep_hold()` decides first in `fstep_kmax`, at every target: off keeps the cap's vsyncs as the longest hold (1 tick at 120; still 2 ticks when capped to 60 on a 60 Hz screen, during a movie, or under the thermal cap), so each late frame is shown on the next refresh instead of stepping down to 60; on (the default) keeps the 120 rule, where three late frames step down to 60 to preserve real-time speed on games that step by their target.
- **Check:** `t_link_ticks` (`fstep_kmax` under `fps_high()`: `DM_FPS_HOLD=on` returns 2 at 120 and 60; `DM_FPS_HOLD=off` returns 1 at 120 and 2 at a 60 cap; NX 18 made `fstep_kmax` out of the inline rule for it, upstream `0a554d79f`) and `t_fstep` (`120 fps, fps_hold off: frames of 9.2 ms hold 120, no step to 60` vs `120 fps, no payback: three frames late by 2 ms step down to 60`). Reported on Batman: Arkham Asylum 1.0.1 (`0100E870163CA000`, [PR #3339](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/3339)) at `DM_FPS_TARGET=120`, two headless 110 s arms: before the fix both `off` and `on` raised the hold 12 times and returned it 11; after it, `off` raised it 0 times while `on` still raised and returned it.
- **Found on:** Batman: Arkham Asylum 1.0.1 ([PR #3339](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/3339)); applies to all `fps_max 120` titles.

### The display's vsync event follows 120 fps

- **Symptom:** with `DM_FPS_TARGET=120`, a 120 Hz screen and the cap at 120 (`120 fps: cap 0 → 120 fps`), the game holds 60: every trial of hold 1 ends in `hold 1 → 2 … (3 late frames; cost 2.0…)` with the CPU mostly idle and the GPU under 1 ms a frame.
- **Cause:** the game waits for each vblank, and the host signalled `vi::GetDisplayVsyncEvent` at the Switch's 60 Hz (every other tick of a 120 Hz display link, or its own 60 Hz clock without one). Unity's NVN device waits each frame until its vblank time, which a thread sets from that event, has passed (`main+0x14f65f0`, from a `sample` of the main thread: 387 of 869 samples in that wait). So no frame could end sooner than the next 60 Hz event, whatever the present cap.
- **Fix:** the event follows the frame step: `nvn_vsync_hz` (`hle_nvn.c`) is 120 while 120 fps is on (`fps_high`), its hold is one tick of 1/120 s and the cap is 120, else 60. `mtl_link_wait_vsync` then waits one tick of a 120 Hz link instead of two, and the event's own clock (`hle_sdk16.c`, no link) sleeps 1/120 s; `DM_VSYNC_HZ` still fixes a rate. The hold's trial at 120 already has the event at 120, so a game that waits for it can pass the trial. The event is 60 Hz in every other case, so a game without `fps_max 120`, or at 60, sees no change.
- **Found on:** [The Rogue Prince of Persia](../../dm/games/01008D9022462000/README.md): 8.33 ms frames through the first level at 120 (60 while the intro movie plays), the speed right by the user's play test. DRAGON BALL: Sparking! ZERO and Pikmin 4 bind the event too and reached 120 before, so they do not wait for it each frame. A/B at 120 (2026-11-08, hosts of `4f47d211` without and with this change, headless, the same saves and presses): the same frame times in every window (8.37 ms, 16.6 while a movie plays) and their scripted scenes (Sparking! ZERO's tutorial battle start, Pikmin 4's prologue narration and first dialogue) at the same seconds, so neither counts the event as time.
- **Check:** none of its own; the hold and the cap are `t_fstep`'s, and the frame times are in the run log (`timing up to frame`).

### Unreal's frame limit and present interval, found by name

- **Symptom:** an Unreal Engine game holds 30 fps with spare CPU and GPU time and asks for present interval 2; `present_interval display` alone does not help, as the engine's game thread sleeps out its own frame limit.
- **Cause:** two console variables the game's config sets: the frame limit `t.MaxFPS` (30), which the game thread sleeps out before stepping the game by the time that really passed, and the present interval `rhi.SyncInterval` (2; `nvn.SyncInterval` in UE 4.18's Switch RHI). Some games set the limit through a variable of their own that their settings apply to `t.MaxFPS` (SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance's `Project.FrameRateLimit`), so writing `t.MaxFPS` alone loses to the next settings change.
- **Fix:** the rewriter finds the variables by their registered names in any Unreal main, with no address per build (`dm/tools/ue_cvar.py`, run by `patch_nso.py` on `main`), and names each one's `TAutoConsoleVariable` in the fixups (`UE_CVAR:<name>`). It finds the name (UTF-16, or UTF-32 in older builds) in main's read-only data and the code that loads it into `x1` (`adrp` + `add`); the first call after that, with the name still in `x1`, decides. A `bl` with `x0` an address made by `adrp` + `add` is the variable's constructor (UE 4.18), and `x0` is the object. A `blr` is the console manager's register call (UE 4.2x and 5), and the object is the address made by `adrp` + `add` that the value pointer is then stored into (`str x0, [xN, #0x10]`; UE 5 calls a base constructor in between). UE 4.18 also inlines that constructor into a long static initializer that addresses every object from one page base made far before the name (OCTOPATH TRAVELER: `str x8, [x22, #k]`, the vtable, then, after the call that returns the value pointer, `str x0, [x22, #k + 0x10]`); the object is then the base plus `k`, the base taken back to its `adrp` + `add` across forward branches over else blocks, provided nothing else writes the register and no branch from outside lands between (otherwise nothing is found). Registers are followed from the last call before the name, as UE 4.18 loads the object first; registers a call or a load overwrites are forgotten, and the next variable's name ends the search. Anything else (a lookup by name, a log line) is no registration, and a name registered at two objects gives none, with a red line. `hle_fpslock.c` writes the profile's `ue_fps_cvar` (default `t.MaxFPS`) to `fps_target` and the present intervals to 60 / `fps_target`, both threads' copies, whenever they differ; an Unreal main without the profile's variable is a red line with a target set.
- **Found on:** [Octopath Traveler 0](../../dm/games/01005270232F2000/README.md), [OCTOPATH TRAVELER II](../../dm/games/0100A3501946E000/README.md), [DRAGON QUEST VII Reimagined](../../dm/games/0100A9D01C446000/README.md), [DRAGON QUEST XI S](../../dm/games/01006C300E9F0000/README.md), [SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance](../../dm/games/010069C01AB82000/README.md), [FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE](../../dm/games/0100606026F12000/README.md) and its [Asian release](../../dm/games/0100B8A026F14000/README.md), which had 13 rows of fixed addresses until 2026-10-30; [Minecraft Dungeons II](../../dm/games/0100A7C01B792000/README.md#frame-rate) (UE 5, `rhi.SyncInterval` alone: 60 and 120, 2026-11-08). Checked offline on 61 mains: it finds exactly those rows' objects in the six on the drives (DRAGON QUEST XI S and SHIN MEGAMI TENSEI V: Vengeance from the archive drive), including DRAGON QUEST XI S's `t.MaxFPS` constructor, which the host's earlier run-time search (`dm_ue_cvar`) passed over to the next variable's registration; nothing in a game that is not Unreal. [OCTOPATH TRAVELER](../../dm/games/010057D006492000/README.md#frame-rate) (UE 4.18, inlined constructors: `t.MaxFPS` and `nvn.SyncInterval`, 60 fps, 2026-11-09); on the 43 mains on the game drive that day, the inlined form changed only its result.
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_ue_cvar.py` (synthetic code: the constructor form with the object loaded first, the register form with a base constructor between, a lookup, a double registration, UTF-32 names); `t_fpslock` (the rows from main's table, a present interval alone, a main without variables); `ue_cvar.py <main>...` prints what it finds in each.

### Unreal's fixed frame rate, found by its reflection entry

- **Symptom:** an Unreal Engine game with `fps_target 60` asks for present interval 1 (`main: Unreal rhi.SyncInterval … 2 → 1 vsyncs`), and its menus reach 60, but its gameplay holds 30.0 fps with spare CPU and GPU time; the game sets no `t.MaxFPS`.
- **Cause:** the engine's fixed frame rate. The game's config turns it on (`bUseFixedFrameRate=True`, `FixedFrameRate=30.000000` in its `DefaultEngine.ini`), and the engine then steps every frame by exactly 1 / `FixedFrameRate` seconds and holds its tick to that rate, whatever the console variables say. `FixedFrameRate` is a `UEngine` property on the engine object `GEngine` points to, not a console variable. Unreal's Switch frame pace sets it with the present interval: Sparking! ZERO's (`main+0x1e1e904`) caches `rhi.SyncInterval`, sets 1 for its 60 fps mode and 2 for the others, and stores {30, 24, 60}[mode] into `GEngine->FixedFrameRate`.
- **Fix:** the rewriter finds the property, its flag and its object in any Unreal 4.22 to 5 main, with no address per build (`dm/tools/ue_fixed_rate.py`, run by `patch_nso.py` on a main whose console variables it found), and names them in the fixups (`UE_FIXED_RATE_PTR`, GEngine's offset in main; `UE_FIXED_RATE_FIELD`, the property's offset in `UEngine`; `UE_FIXED_RATE_FLAG`, `bUseFixedFrameRate` as byte offset << 8 | bit mask).
  - The property comes from its reflection entry: the data word holding the address of its UTF-8 name (the module's relative relocations applied; the linker may share the string with the tail of `bUseFixedFrameRate`), with the generated-property kind Float (0x0A at +24) and array size 1 (+32). Its offset follows the array size (+36, UE 4.22 to 5.0), or a setter and a getter pointer after it (+56, UE 5.1 and later).
  - The flag comes from `bUseFixedFrameRate`'s entry (kind Bool, 0x0C): the function it holds that sets the bit on an object, `ldrb w, [x0, #byte]; orr w, w, #bit; strb; ret`.
  - `GEngine` comes from where `FEngineLoop::Init` makes the engine: after a reference to the UTF-16 config key `GameEngine` (the engine class's name; the linker may share it with the tail of `UGameEngine`), the first store of a register to `[xR, #lo]` that is loaded back into `x0` and followed by a virtual call (`ParseCommandline`). `xR` is resolved back to the global's page (`adrp`), that plus an offset (`adrp` + `add`), or the address a GOT slot holds (`adrp` + `ldr`). Every such store must name the same global, or the line is `[RED]` and nothing is written.
  - The host writes `fps_target` there as a frame rate (`hle_fpslock.c`, a row beside the console variables) only while the object's flag is set, so an engine stepping by the time that passed is left as it is; at 120 it follows the frame step's hold, 120 or 60 ([120 fps follows the frame step](#120-fps-follows-the-frame-step)). The step stays the time one frame stands for, so the game's speed holds as far as its own code steps by the engine's delta time: a game this reaches needs its speed checked at 60 (and at 120 for `fps_max 120`).
- **Earlier versions (2026-11-08):** `GEngine` was first the one global through which code loaded or stored a float at the property's offset. On Pikmin 4 that was a load through `main+0x9350560` (`main+0x1689a70`), whose object held -980 at `+0x7a8` (a gravity), not 30: the offset only coincided, and the host wrote nothing, as -980 is outside the range it accepts. The second version took only a store, which only Sparking! ZERO's frame pace makes (`main+0x1e1ea04`); Pikmin 4 sets the property from its config through the reflection loader and reads it through `this`, so it got nothing. The engine's creation is in every build.
- **Found on:** [DRAGON BALL: Sparking! ZERO](../../dm/games/010035F022078000/README.md) (UE 5.1.1: `GEngine` at `main+0x9aefa30` through the GOT slot `main+0x8dbfb40`, stored at `main+0x789c`; `FixedFrameRate` at `+0x9c8`, the flag bit 0x40 of `+0x9c4`): its battles at 56–60 fps, at 119.3 in most windows at 120. [Pikmin 4](../../dm/games/0100B7C00933A000/README.md) (UE 4.26.2: `GEngine` at `main+0x9fbf880`, stored at `main+0x9e5f68` and `main+0x9e9224`; `FixedFrameRate` at `+0x7a8`, the flag bit 0x40 of `+0x7a4`; the object read in a run: flag byte 0x47, 30.0): the write `30 → 120`, 119.4 fps in most windows, the speed right to the user in the prologue (2026-11-08). Offline: Princess Peach: Showtime! (`GEngine` at `main+0xaeaf988`, the property at `+0x7a8`) runs at 60 by the present interval alone, so its flag is off and the host writes nothing there; Octopath Traveler 0 and the first OCTOPATH TRAVELER have no such entry. Open: [Minecraft Dungeons II](../../dm/games/0100A7C01B792000/README.md#frame-rate)'s entry (UE 5) has a third layout the search does not read, the setter and getter at +32 / +40, a 16-bit array size at +48 and the offset at +52 (`+0xc38`; flag bit 0x40 of `+0xc34`, `GEngine` at `main+0xae26e30`), so it finds nothing there; that game's flag is off, so it needs no write, and no main on the drives with the flag on has that layout yet (47 checked, 2026-11-08).
- **Check:** `dm/tools/tests/test_ue_fixed_rate.py` (synthetic images: both layouts, the shared names, the flag, `GEngine` as a page plus offset and through a GOT slot, a store with no reload, two globals, another kind); `t_fpslock` cases 22 to 24 (60, a 30 the game sets again, 120 following the hold, the flag off); `ue_fixed_rate.py <main>...` prints what it finds in each.

### Frostbite's simulation rate, found by name

- **Symptom:** a Frostbite game holds 30 fps with spare CPU and GPU time. Raising only the frame limit and the present interval gives 60 frames a second with the game at half speed (the match clock at 3.6 game seconds a second instead of 7.5).
- **Cause:** Frostbite's `SimulationTimeSettings` caps the simulation's ticks a second: `ForceSimRate` (int32) and `MaxSimFps` (float), both 30 in EA SPORTS FC 27. The game's other 30s (its own frame regulator, the present interval 2 of `BaseDisplaySettings.PresentInterval`) only pace the frames.
- **Fix:** `hle_fpslock.c` finds the settings by name, so no address is tied to a build. Frostbite reflects its settings classes: a class record (its name, then at +0x40 its field list) and a 0x20-byte record per field (name, hash, offset at +0x10, type). A static constructor stores the record's address into the class's type info (`adrp` / `add` of each, then `str`), and code that reads the settings asks the settings registry for the instance with the type info in `x1` and a cache word in `x2`, which keeps the instance's address; the instance holds its type info at +8, which checks the word. With `fps_target` 30 or 60 the host writes both fields to the target each frame they differ. A game's own frame limit still needs its row (EA SPORTS FC 27's frame regulator, build-gated) and `present_interval display`. Without the row, as on a build not yet registered, the simulation at 60 with the regulator at 30 runs at 30 fps at the right speed (match clock as at 30).
- **Found on:** [EA SPORTS FC 27](../../dm/games/0100C49025D3E000/README.md): 60 fps in a match with the match clock at the 30 fps rate. Its `main` has unwind entries for 405 functions only, so the rewriter's function matching cannot carry a cut of the regulator across updates either.
- **Check:** `t_fpslock` builds a Frostbite module in miniature (strings, class record, field list, registration and lookup code, instance) and checks the writes, a repeated game write and a cache word whose instance has another type info.

## Present interval and display rate

### Present thread rescues a silent display link

- **Symptom:** the app is active and the game keeps running, but the picture freezes while display-link callbacks stop; the log repeatedly drops queued frames.
- **Cause:** dropping frames releases the game's swapchain textures but never displays them. A display link may stop while the app is active, including an occluded Mac window.
- **Fix:** after 500 ms of silence while active, `mtl_present_wait` requests rescue. `link_sync` invalidates the display link on its run loop before releasing the layer to the present thread, which takes its own drawables in mode 0. Retry delays are 3, 6, 12, 24, 48 and then at most 60 seconds. Rescue state changes take `g_pq_mu`; the requested `g_dm_present_link` stays unchanged, and a new runtime mode ends rescue immediately. A retry resets stall timing. Inactive and background time do not count towards the 500 ms threshold, including a focus timestamp newer than the watchdog's clock sample.
- **Found on:** d2r-mobile commit `afad65c` (an iPhone picture froze for 80 seconds), carried through MacNativeGames `cf1e629` and [PR #198](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/198). That upstream title has no profile in this repository. Mac window occlusion also affected [ANIMAL WELL's image validation](../../dm/games/010020D01AD24000/README.md#files): foreground rescue and out-of-focus fallback produced incomparable timings; its note records the controlled comparison. [Xenoblade Chronicles 2's controller motion review](../../dm/games/0100E95004038000/README.md#controller-motion-review) also records stalled-link timing on both hosts and the paired diagnostic runs.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 t_present_rescue` and `dm/gfx/tests/t_present_rescue` exercise the production wait and retry paths with a controlled clock and a silent link double, without a GPU. They cover foreground grace, backoff saturation, runtime mode changes, and layer release only after run-loop invalidation. The test also runs in CPU graphics CI. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 check_t_present_rescue` adds a paused real Metal display link on an offscreen Mac layer: rescue invalidates it, then `nextDrawable` succeeds (47 checks, zero failures with Metal validation). `DM_TEST_LINK_STALL=<start seconds>,<duration seconds>` pauses a real window's display link for manual verification.
- **Not checked:** gameplay during stalled-link recovery or the iPhone; the PR's gameplay runs were headless.

### Display-link rescue during startup can leave the present queue without a consumer

- **Symptom:** at fullscreen startup the display link logs rescue and recovery, then frame timing and screenshot completions stop. The Presentation Thread waits in `mtl_present_wait` while `nvn-present` waits on its queue.
- **Cause:** the link thread can own the layer before an enqueue applies its first pacing mode. Rescue asks for mode 0 while the last applied mode is still 0, so `link_sync` skipped invalidation. Callbacks during rescue still refreshed the watchdog's tick while declining the queued presents. The present thread waited for the layer, and the next acquire waited for its texture's present ticket.
- **Fix:** `link_sync` retires an attached link on a request for mode 0 even during startup (logged as `display link: returning the layer to the present thread during startup`). A callback that enters during rescue returns before it touches the stall clock or the ticks, so an acquire can release its ticket before the next enqueue.
- **Check:** `t_present_rescue`'s `startup_rescue` case drives this ordering with a controlled clock and the production callback, ticket wait and invalidation path; without the fix its callback check fails.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md#found-on-this-game) upstream (fixed 144 Hz external display on a Mac, 5 of 5 cold fullscreen starts froze before the fix, 0 of 5 after). Ported from upstream NXNative `fa549e08c` ([PR #3004](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/3004)); not reproduced in NX 18. The cause of the link's initial silence remains open.

### Long frames a test run makes itself

- **Symptom:** `[longframe]` lines of 40–180 ms with no new pipelines, a short submit and normal GPU time, which read like CPU hitches in the game: single frames of 60–100 ms, or bursts of 5–12 frames, one of 140–180 ms and the rest about 40 ms.
- **Cause:** the run, not the game. Each `shot` line of `DM_CTL` costs the frame it captures 60–100 ms, so a script that takes a shot every few seconds prints a long frame every few seconds. Each retry of a [silent display link](#present-thread-rescues-a-silent-display-link) waits out the silence for about 0.5 s, and those bursts repeat 3, 6, 12, 24, 48 and then every 60 s, with `display link: rescue ended` just before them. The link was silent in runs with no session on the Mac's display and ticked once a remote session had connected (seen across runs, not tested on purpose). Losing focus (`oe focus state → 2`) adds its own long frames and halves the rate.
- **Fix:** no code change. Shots are diagnostics only, and the retries need a silent link; a link that stops while its picture is visible, as on the iPhone in the entry above, would show the retry as a stutter of about 0.5 s every 60 s. To measure frame times, take no shots, and read a long frame's cause from the log before looking for it in the game: the shot or `rescue ended` line before it, and the focus lines. A stall that repeats on a timer is worth timing first: the doubling gaps gave the retry away.
- **Found on:** [Princess Peach: Showtime!](../../dm/games/01007A3009184000/README.md#frame-rate): of 491 long frames in one 18-minute run, 315 were link retries and 53 followed a shot; with neither, 112 s of lobby and play had none.

### The frame step's own holds counted as long frames

- **Symptom:** after each `frame step: hold 1 → 2` (or `2 → 3`) line, a run of 5–9 `[longframe]` lines of 31–36 ms (about 50 ms at hold 3), with no new pipelines, a short submit and normal GPU time, which `scripts/devlog.py` put on the game's side (`the game's side (its code or its waits)`). In a 9.6-minute run of Ocarina of Time at 60 fps on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, 412 of the 486 long frames it gave the game were such runs, of 638 in all. Guessing holds from runs of such frames hides real ones: in a log with no `frame step` line (a game without `fps_target`, or the step off) it would say `not counted: N frames as long as the frame step's hold …` for runs of a game's own 30 or 20 fps dips.
- **Cause:** the long-frame log, not the game. A frame counted as long when it was over 20 ms and 1.4 times the running mean of recent frames, and the mean takes a sixteenth of each frame. After a step from hold 1 to 2 it needs 8–9 frames before 33.3 ms is under 1.4 times it, and each of those frames took the 33.3 ms the step asked for. The frame step lines that would tell them apart stop after 40 changes (`further changes only counted below`); that run had 86 steps up.
- **Fix:** the long-frame log knows each frame's hold: the one the present before it set (`lf_rec.hold`, read from `g_hold_ring` in `hle_nvn.c`), as the acquire before the frame waited out that present's period. `nvn_lf_long` (`nvn_fstep.h`) counts a frame as long only when it also takes more than 1.25 times its hold (frames at hold 2 took 31–36 ms on the iPhone), or its GPU work more than the hold; every frame still feeds the means. A long frame ends with `; hold K × V ms` whenever the frame step runs, hold 1 too, and one made under a hold of 2 or more (the game's own interval) otherwise. `scripts/devlog.py` leaves a frame as long as its hold out of the count and says how many (`not counted: N frames as long as the frame step's hold …`). It reads the hold from the line, and a line without one in a log that has them as made at no hold; in an older log, from the frame step lines, and past the one that ends them (`; further changes only counted below`, on the host's 40th line, which can be a change of hold, the end of a probe or a short run after one) from runs of three or more frames of 2 or 3 vsyncs at most two frames apart. That marker is read from every kind of line: read from the hold changes alone, it was missed when the 40th line was a probe's, and the last logged hold then hid every later frame of the game's own up to 1.25 times that hold. A log with no frame step line and no hold (a game without `fps_target`, or the step off) guesses none: there such runs are the game's own 30 or 20 fps dips and stay counted. A frame with another cause (pipelines, uploads, GPU, submit) keeps it, and the display link's stretches name the held frames before them apart from the long ones.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_fstep`, the long-frame cases: by the mean alone 9 frames of a step to hold 2 are long, against their hold none; at hold 2 a 45 ms frame is still long and a 40 ms one is not, 25 ms of GPU work is not and 40 ms is; hold 1 is judged as before. `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py`. On that run's log devlog gives 226 long frames instead of 638 (submit 137, game 74, GPU 7, pipelines 7, uploads 1) and 412 not counted; of the 74 left, the frames of 2 or 3 vsyncs are single frames or pairs. No run of the new host has been logged on a device yet.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (build 53860d7f).

### A device log that does not say why the frame step held

- **Symptom:** an iPhone log in which a game spends seconds at 30 fps under the frame step but cannot say why. The `frame step: hold 1 → 2` lines give only `3 late frames` and the cost of the last one, and stop for good after 40 changes (`; further changes only counted below`); the counters on the `game time` line run over the whole run, so a window's own failures cannot be read off them; nothing reports the game's dynamic-resolution level; the `thread policy` line says `real-time policy not in effect, current priority 47` in every window without saying which thread it read; and a stay in the background reads as a long frame of its length (`[longframe] frame 3998 … whole frame 40454.8 ms`), which `scripts/devlog.py` listed as the run's longest.
- **Cause:** the log, not the game. The step-up reason did not say on which side of the frame the late frames ran long, so a burst on the CPU side looked like a GPU over budget. The thread policy line reads the policy of the thread that writes it, the one that presents, while the policy is set on the thread that acquires the window texture, which in Ocarina of Time is another thread ('Presentation Thread' presents, the main thread acquires). The frame interval and the long-frame log measure wall time, and a frame across a stay in the background contains the stay.
- **Fix:** logging only, `hle_nvn.c`; `DM_FIELD_LOG=0` writes the lines as X1.0 did. A step up gives how many of the last 30 frames were late and the three late frames' CPU (the time between frame starts without the pacing wait) and GPU at most, in vsyncs, and which side was over the late line (`3 late frames in a row, 5 of the last 30; their CPU at most 1.23, GPU at most 0.98: the CPU side`); a step down by the fit says so (`fit trial: 30 frames in a row costing at most 0.85 of hold 1`). The change lines stop at 40 in each window of 1800 presents (`; further changes in this window only counted in its game time line`) and start again in the next; that window's `game time` line adds its own counts (`; in this window 12 late, 9 vsyncs paid back, 0 dropped, 2 up / 2 down (2 probes, 1 failed trials)`). Where a frame step stub runs the game's dynamic resolution (Ocarina of Time with `DM_DYNAMIC_RESOLUTION=1`), a `dynamic resolution up to frame N` line every 300 frames gives the level, the levels the window saw and the load measured at each level. The `thread policy` line names the thread it was read on and the one the policy was set on ([The thread policy line read the thread that presents](#the-thread-policy-line-read-the-thread-that-presents) then made it read the latter). A long frame across a stay in the background (time `dm_gfx_suspend` held the graphics suspended) ends with `; N ms of it in the background`, and `scripts/devlog.py` names it apart instead of counting it (`not counted: 161 s frame 3998: 40454.8 ms across a stay in the background (39.3 s of it away)`). It still reads X1.0's marker, which only `DM_FIELD_LOG=0` writes.
- **Check:** `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py` (the producer's formats, the window marker that does not end the change lines, the background frame named apart). Not yet in a device log.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0, two runs).

### Long frames whose submit time the line did not explain

- **Symptom:** a `[longframe]` line whose submit and present take most of the frame, with few or no new pipelines and texture uploads that explain only part of it, so `scripts/devlog.py` gave it to `submit+present` with nothing more to say. Mario Kart World's title load had a frame of 474 ms, 416 ms of it submit and present, with 42 first uploads of 131 MB in 83 ms and no pipeline: about 333 ms had no cause in the log (the 2026-11-15 round, `scan/071972d7-Archive/log.txt:370`; about 380 ms at `e140392c:350`). Its sparse textures' tile mapping was ruled out.
- **Cause:** the log, not the game: the line named pipelines and uploads only, while the replay also makes textures and their memory (render targets, an upload's texture and staging buffer, a placement sparse texture), deswizzles on the CPU, adds to and commits the residency set, and waits for the host's locks and the encode thread.
- **Fix:** logging only (`nvn_lft.h`; timers in `nvn_mtl.m`, the line in `hle_nvn.c`). On the replay thread only (`on_sub_thread`), two clock reads (the commpage) each time it makes a texture or its memory (`tex_mtl_`'s texture and fill, an upload's texture and staging buffer), deswizzles an upload on the CPU (with a sparse texture's gather), makes a mip-dropped texture whole again (`md_whole`), adds to the residency set or commits it (the kernel's part), or waits for one of the host's locks (`g_mu`, `g_smu`, `g_rmu`, `g_res_mu`, `g_res_commit_mu`; tried first, so a free lock reads no clock). Each frame the long-frame record takes them (`mtl_lft_take`) with the replay's encode-thread waits (`rec_drain`), and a long frame's line ends, before its hold, with `; replay split: textures made N, T ms (U in uploads); CPU deswizzle …; mip-dropped textures made whole …; residency adds …; residency commits …; lock waits …; encode-thread waits T ms`, each part with a count, when they come to 0.5 ms or more. A part can hold another (a lock wait inside a texture's making counts in both), and `in uploads` is the part inside the upload time the line gives already. `scripts/devlog.py` names a part outside the uploads that took a quarter of the frame (and 2 ms) as a cause (`textures made (N, T ms)`, `lock waits …`; by cause `textures`, `deswizzle`, `mip drop`, `residency`, `locks`, `encode thread`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_mip_drop`: nothing is counted before a submission names the replay thread; a mip-dropped texture made whole for a copy is timed with its new texture inside its upload; an upload deswizzled on the CPU is timed inside the upload, with its texture and staging buffer and their residency adds; the replay's wait for a lock another thread holds is timed, another thread's wait is not; the line's part and its cut in a short buffer. `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py`: the names match the producer's, and a 474 ms frame's causes are its textures and lock waits. Not yet in a device log.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#found-on-this-game)'s title load on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (the 2026-11-15 round).

### Area-load frames whose replay split named under half their time

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (f110160f1): area-load frames of 150-390 ms, `submit+present 378.2 ms`, whose `replay split` named about 160 ms (`textures made 198, 50.9 ms; ... residency commits 16, 76.3 ms; lock waits 13, 29.4 ms`), and the frames just after them (182 and 152 ms) almost nothing.
- **Cause:** the log again: `submit+present` adds up every submit scope, the replay's nodes and the game thread's submits together, plus the present, and the split times only some of the replay's work. Whether the rest was translation on the CPU or a wait (the disk, a lock, the GPU) could not be told.
- **Fix:** logging only (`nvn_queue.inc` `nq_cost_end_role`, `hle_nvn.c` `g_lfs`). Each scope's wall, CPU and kernel CPU time, which the submit-cost probes already read, is also added to the replay's or the game thread's sum for the frame; a long frame's line adds `; submit scopes: replay W ms (CPU C, kernel K), game thread W ms (CPU C, kernel K)` before the replay split. Wall far over CPU is waiting; CPU near wall is work, kernel CPU the system's part of it (page faults among it). What `submit+present` has beyond the two is the present. Then `; page-ins during the replay's scopes P (faults F), the game thread's P (F), the frame's P (F)`: the process's page-ins and faults (`task_info` `TASK_EVENTS_INFO`, read at each scope's ends and at each frame), so a replay waiting on game memory still being read from its file shows page-ins in its own scopes. The counts are the whole process's while a scope ran: the game's loading threads paging in at the same time count too, so few in the replay's scopes against many in the frame clears the replay.
- **Check:** the iOS compile check and `t_nvn` (the long-frame line itself is checked on a device).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, 2026-11-22. First phone run (fc6040b8e): the worst area-load frames were mostly waiting, replay 443 ms wall on 142 ms of CPU (112 of it kernel), 273 on 46, 531 on 103, with the process paging in 2.2 GB (211,000 page-ins) around them; host locks and residency 0-90 ms of each. The page-in counts are not yet run on a device.

### Area loads waited on the replay's own page faults

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (cb8ad7582): area-load frames of 200-530 ms whose `submit scopes` show the replay's wall time three to six times its CPU time, with tens of thousands of page-ins in the frame.
- **Cause:** an Instruments System Trace of 90 s with three area loads (NX18-X2.0 traces, `oot-sys-1`): in each load the replay thread was blocked for most of its busy stretches in its own `File Backed Page In` faults on the guest's files (580 page-ins and 268 ms of a 376 ms stretch; 152 and 147 ms of 165; 686 and 164 ms of 213), about 0.3-0.5 ms each, one 16 KB page at a time, in the game's main pool. Between them it waited for work. Its locks took little (`psynch_mutexwait` 86 ms in all the loads). The replay reads a texture's storage on the CPU at its first use (`tex_cpu_content`, `tex_mem_any`: any non-zero byte) and when it takes or re-uploads it from there, and an area load brings hundreds of new textures to their first use at once; the residency read-ahead (`fwire_advise`) covers only buffers about to join the residency set.
- **Fix:** for every game. `DM_NVN_TEX_READAHEAD` (on by default; `=0` off; `nvn_mtl.m` `tex_readahead`, from `mtl_tex_init` at `nvnTextureInitialize`): a texture of 16 KB or more whose content the host may take from its storage (`tex_storage_ok`: a CPU-readable pool, not sparse, compressible, linear or an uncompressed image) has its storage asked for ahead (F_RDADVISE) on the residency read-ahead's batched queue, with no lock held. A range the game then rewrites is read once either way (a write to a page not in memory reads it), and a hole reads as a clean zero page.
- **Log:** the `[nvn] residency page-ins up to frame F:` line adds `textures read ahead at their setup N, M MB (DM_NVN_TEX_READAHEAD)`. The long-frame line's `page-ins during the replay's scopes` should fall at area loads.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_file_wire`: a BC1 texture in a CPU pool is asked for once on the read-ahead queue at its setup; one in a `CPU_NO_ACCESS` pool, one under 16 KB and one with the switch off are not; the page-in line counts it.
- **Phone run (4344d3ac0):** the read-ahead covered 453 textures (206 MB) in 6 minutes, and area-load frames still showed thousands of page-ins in the replay's scopes (26,179 in a 448 ms frame). The trace says every one of the replay's 1,774 page-ins in the loads (774 ms) came from its own code touching the guest's memory, none inside a system call, so the texture scan is at most part of it.
- **Next:** `DM_NVN_PF_PARTS=1` (a diagnostic, off by default; `nvn_mtl.m` `pf_enter`, `pf_leave`, `mtl_pf_scope`; the line in `hle_nvn.c`): while a replayed node's scope runs, each change between the parts below reads the process's page-in count and gives the difference to the part that ran: textures made (`tex_mtl_`'s making), storage scans (`tex_mem_any`), pool tables (`pool_gpu`), uniform reads (`ubo_read`, the guest's uniform memory read on the CPU), uploads (`pkg_upload`), residency (`res_commit_to`), and other (the rest of the replay's work). Long-frame lines then add `; replay page-ins by part: ...`. `t_file_wire`: pages of a dropped file read inside a part and outside it go to that part and to 'other'; off, nothing is counted.
- **The parts (7cc596635, `DM_NVN_PF_PARTS=1`):** over the long frames, residency 82,006 page-ins (the parallel page-in's own workers, which are fast), other 71,539, pool tables 25,017, uploads 10,494, storage scans 10,467, textures made 3,799. The worst frames were the pool walk's: 485 ms on 58 ms of CPU with 6,600 page-ins in the walk, 319 ms with 4,014. The walk meets each new texture's storage (a stand-in from its smallest levels, a scan, an upload) one page at a time, after memory pressure has sent the game's pages back to its files.
- **Fix 2, withdrawn:** `DM_NVN_POOL_READAHEAD` (off by default; `=1` on; `nvn_mtl.m` `pool_ra`): before a walk or a patch of a texture table resolves its IDs, the storage of each texture of 16 KB or more not made yet (no view) is asked for ahead, all of them together on the read-ahead queue (off `g_rmu`'s hold), each texture once (by serial). The page-in line adds `textures read ahead by the pool walk N, M MB`. `t_pool_standin`: the walk asks for a new texture's storage once, however often it walks past it.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, 2026-11-22. The trace had no call stacks (no symbols for the NX Play build). Fix 2's phone run (0905d6605) asked for 364,775 textures, about 317 GB, in 137 s: the walk passes far more textures it never makes (IDs no shader reads, textures past the budget that get stand-ins) than the 65,536 serials g_pra holds, so they were asked for again and again, and the reads made area loads worse (the worst frame 937 ms against 529 ms). Turned off the same day.
- **Fix 2b (narrow; `DM_NVN_POOL_READAHEAD=1`, still off by default until a phone run):** the walk asks ahead only for what it will read: the whole storage of a texture it will make (no larger than the walk's limit, within the frame's room of 32 MB as it is spent) and, over the budget, only the levels a stand-in reads (from its first level to the end of each of its first layers, up to `STAND_FRAME` a walk), each texture once by serial and kind (`g_pra`, emptied past 1,048,576). The page-in line gives `the pool walk read ahead N textures it makes, M MB, and N stand-ins' levels, M MB`. `t_pool_standin`: a walk with no room asks for nothing; over the budget it asks for the stand-ins' levels, each once however often it walks past. Phone run (639e9ebdb, `=1`): 393,948 stand-in asks, about 281 GB, and none for textures made: Ocarina's table is over its budget nearly always, the levels from a stand-in's first level to the end of its layers were often the whole texture, and the same stand-ins were asked for again and again (the serials the walk sees change far more often than assumed). Area loads were no better (worst frame 463 against 529 ms, 7 against 9 frames over 200 ms, within run-to-run noise). Left off; the pool walk's read-ahead is not pursued further.
- **Fix 3: stand-ins off by default** (`DM_NVN_POOL_STANDIN`, `=1` on; `nvn_mtl.m` `stand_on`). The stand-in counts explained the pool tables' page-ins: 43,000-83,000 stand-ins made a run (1.3-1.8 s of the replay), each reading the texture's storage (its smallest levels), against 55-57 first reads in a run that found one. The game sets up new texture objects over the same storage, and each registration of one drops the ID's stand-in, so the walk made it again. With `DM_NVN_POOL_STANDIN=0` (639e9ebdb, same route): frames over 200 ms 9 -> 5, over 100 ms 21 -> 16, the worst 529 -> 370 ms, the pool tables' page-ins 25,017 -> 7,907, 53.4 -> 55.9 fps; first reads found the dummy 56 times (51 of them textures of 256 KB or more), and the owner saw no dark frame. The stand-ins came from issue #1969 (the cliff beside the castle town wall dark for one frame on a Mac); `=1` brings them back if that shows again.
- **Next: the 'other' part.** With stand-ins off, 'other' was the largest share of the replay's page-ins (53,407 in a run), with uploads (14,636) and storage scans (12,672) next. `DM_NVN_PF_PARTS=1` now also times the replay's commands (`mtl_exec`, `sop_tick`: one clock read a command): a command of 0.2 ms or more counts its time to its type, and long-frame lines add `; slow commands (DM_NVN_PF_PARTS): draw_idx N / T ms, ...` (the five largest). A page-in stall shows as a slow command, so this names the commands the replay waited in. `t_file_wire`: a 1 ms command counts to its type, a quick one and a sub-list's call do not; off, nothing is timed. Run (453bb85fe): the slow commands were nearly all indexed draws, 1,576 of them for 2,733 ms in the long frames (plain draws 156 for 324 ms, dispatches 73 for 105 ms); 'other' was 87,854 page-ins, uploads 25,856. Inside an indexed draw the CPU reads guest memory in the index scans (`vbuf_index_last`, upstream 79555b676: every index of a draw whose stream lookup failed, uncached; `ix_range` with vertex conversion on) and the uniform-block shadows (`ubp_get`): these now have parts of their own, `index scans` and `uniform shadows`. Run (b15ea9119, with the four upstream ports): frames over 100 ms 21 -> 8, over 200 ms 7 -> 3, 55.7 -> 57.3 fps; index scans 0 page-ins and uniform shadows 979, so neither; indexed draws still the slow commands (1,302 for 1.6 s), no pipeline lookups in play. The draw's other phases now have parts too: `pool buffers made` (`pool_buf`, a no-copy buffer over the guest's pages, made at a lookup that finds none), `vertex buffers` (`vbuf_prepare`), `arguments` (`encode_args`), `pipelines` (`pso_get_ptr`), `images` (`image_prepare`) and `buffer-texture copies` (`tba_prepare`, the aligned copy below Metal's alignment). Run (4271ed5b3, Ocarina): `arguments` held the most, 47,844 page-ins against 'other' 41,855 (vertex buffers 4,364, pipelines 2,893, images 5,818, buffer-texture copies 4,436); the run's 36 fps is the diagnostic's own cost (a `task_info` call at each change of part, several a draw now). The binding loop of `encode_args` now moves the part to each binding's kind (`pf_arg_kind`, the `AG_*` groups of `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS`): `arguments: textures and samplers`, `pool and handle tables`, `uniforms and sizes`, `driver banks`, `push constants`, `storage, images, raw memory`, `other bindings`; what is left in `arguments` is the setup and tail. Run (d4624f9e2): `arguments: textures and samplers` 47,713, residency 40,538 (the parallel page-in's workers), other 27,594, storage scans 13,059, arguments 9,659; uniforms 3,453, other bindings 1,031. Two more parts split the texture bindings: `texture lookups` (`tex_mtl_` outside its named parts: the cached-view checks, a packaged texture's change checks) and `edge checks` (`pkg_edges_take`, `pkg_edges_clean`: the shared first and last pages of a packaged texture's storage compared with their copy). What stays: the parallel residency page-in (`DM_NVN_PREWIRE_JOBS`), whose waits are gone, and the read-ahead at texture setup (`DM_NVN_TEX_READAHEAD`, little effect, no harm).
- **Fix 4: read-ahead of pools, buffers and textures at setup** (`DM_NVN_PREREAD`, off by default since the A/B below; `=1` on; `nvn_mtl.m` `mtl_preread`, called from `hle_nvn.c` `nvnMemoryPoolInitialize`, `nvnBufferInitialize` and `nvnTextureInitialize`). Ported from [kid-with-sword-remake](https://github.com/cheemu21/kid-with-sword-remake) (cheemu21, MIT; L7.7, 58a47fbf and f8aea728). Each memory pool the CPU can reach, and each buffer and texture in one (not packaged textures), asks the kernel to read its range ahead (`madvise` `MADV_WILLNEED`, up to 64 MB a range) on a utility queue while the load runs on; a range asked within 2 s is not asked again (the game remakes small buffers every frame). Advice only: nothing is read, written or wired by it. `DM_NVN_TEX_READAHEAD` stays: it asks the file for texture storage, this asks the mapping, and covers buffers and pools, which ours did not. The fork's iPhone 18 Pro A/B on Ocarina of Time (same save and world change): stalls over 100 ms 18 → 8, over 300 ms 9 → 1, worst 1.18 → 0.50 s, 7.0 → 1.9 s stalled in all, peak memory 5.02 → 4.77 GB. Log: `[preread] up to frame F: N ranges, M MB asked ..., skipped`.
- **Fix 5: addressed pools made resident a few at a time** (`DM_NVN_RES_TRICKLE_MB`, off by default since the A/B below; `=16` is the fork's; `nvn_mtl.m` `res_trickle`, from `mtl_sub_begin` after the unwire sweep). Ported from the same fork (L7.8, c8c37258). Once a game has built a raw global memory pool table, the next table puts every pool the game gave an address in into the residency set in one commit: in the fork's log 2,453 pools, 1,176 MB, in the frame that first drew a new area (784 ms). The game gives those addresses while it loads, so each submission now marks up to 16 MB of them resident ahead of the table (as `pool_res` does: marked under the lock, committed after it). The same pools the table would add, so memory ends where it would, only sooner. Pools over 64 MB and large pools' chunks are left to the table. Log: `[trickle] up to frame F: N pools, M MB`.
- **Check (fixes 4 and 5):** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_preread`: a range is asked once, again after 2 s or when longer, nothing over 64 MB or with the switch off; the trickle waits for a pool table, takes 16 MB a frame, the rest the next frame, and leaves out an unaddressed pool and one over 64 MB.
- **Same-route A/B (iPhone 18 Pro Max, 4b081f162, 2026-11-22; on = both on, off = `DM_NVN_PREREAD=0`, `DM_NVN_RES_TRICKLE_MB=0`):** the read-ahead asked 9.8 GB in 74k ranges (7.2 M repeats skipped) and the trickle made 1.8 GB of 5,100 pools resident early. The replay's page-ins in the frames over 100 ms fell (78k on, 113k off), but those frames did not (13 on, 12 off; worst 383 vs 310 ms), and the run with them had twice the long frames (148 vs 86 a minute, most on the game's side, with more lock waits: 210 vs 80), 6.0 vs 4.5% dropped, CPU 227 vs 149% of a core and 3,283 vs 2,283 wakeups a second. Both are off by default since. Not shown: why the game's side slowed (likely the madvise calls contending with the guest's own memory protection changes, and the trickle's walk of every pool under the pool lock each submission). The fork's figures are from its own tree, which has no residency prewire or unwire sweep.
- **Fix 6: edge bytes left out when their pages are out of memory** (`DM_NVN_EDGE_COLD`, on by default; `=0` off; `nvn_mtl.m` `pkg_edges_take_`, `pages_incore`). The 'edge checks' part (22,214 page-ins in the long frames of the b75542bec `DM_NVN_PF_PARTS=1` run, the largest after the residency workers') is `pkg_edges_take`: right after a packaged texture's upload it copies the storage's bytes in its first and last host pages (shared with other data), so a write beside the texture there is not taken for a write into it ([writes beside a packaged texture](nvn-objects.md#writes-beside-a-packaged-texture-in-a-shared-host-page)). The upload deswizzles on the GPU from the pool's buffer, so the copy was the CPU's first read of those pages, up to two page-ins per upload on the submit thread. Now `mincore` is asked first: when either page is out, neither edge is taken. A page the game writes often is in memory, so the case the edges are for keeps working; a cold one written later re-uploads the texture once, and that upload takes the edges. Log: `[edges] up to frame F: N uploads left their edge bytes untaken`. Check: `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_edge_cold` (pages just written are in memory, a file's untouched pages are not, a mixed range is not); `t_readback`'s edge cases, `t_reupload_bands`, `t_idle_pkg` and `t_nvn` pass. Not checked yet: a phone run.
- **'Other' by step (`DM_NVN_PF_PARTS=1`):** the long-frame line now adds `; 'other' page-ins by step: <step> N, ...` (up to six, largest first). Marks (`nvn_mtl.m` `pfm`) at the top-level steps of `draw_` (start, multi-draw records, images, inline uniform updates, index conversion and prescans, tests and order, feedback snapshots, hoists and plans, vertex streams, render pass begin, pipeline and state, pulled vertices, arguments and encoding, after the draw), of `dispatch` and at `set_rt` give each step the 'other' page-ins between its mark and the next, less the named parts inside it; 'the command' is a command's own work before its first mark (every other command type). Off, a mark is a load and a compare. Check: `t_pf_steps`. Like the rest of `DM_NVN_PF_PARTS`, it costs frame rate: read page-ins from such a run, not fps or power. **Caveat and the wait split:** the page-in count is the whole process's (`task_info`), so 'other' also takes what other threads page in (the game's own loads, the residency workers) while the replay is between named parts; in the first run with steps (7990e0419) 'other' was 66,528 page-ins in the frames over 100 ms, 27,291 of them in 'the command' (every command's start, mostly indexed draws, the commonest command), spread much as time is. So the line also gives `the replay's own waits by step (wall less its CPU)`: per step, the replay thread's wall time less its own CPU time (`pfw_tick`, `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`), 1 ms or more, the six largest; a step's figure includes the named parts it calls. That is the replay's own time off the CPU: its page-ins, lock waits and the like. Same run, edge-check fix (99c0ca597): 1,090 uploads left their edges untaken in 3,000 frames, and the edge checks' page-ins in the long frames were 5,361 (22,214 in the b75542bec run).

### Long frames and dropped frames while the in-game menu is open

- **Symptom:** in an iPhone log, clusters of `[longframe]` lines with 19–56 ms of GPU time where the game's frames otherwise take about 7 ms, and on-screen windows with 25–30 dropped frames, with nothing in the game's own lines to explain them; `scripts/devlog.py` gave them to the game (`GPU N ms`, `the game's side`). The player was using the in-game menu (the gear button's) at those moments, and nothing in `log.txt` said so.
- **Cause:** the system, not the game. UIKit draws the in-game menu, its blur and its open and close animations through the render server on the same GPU as the game's command buffers and the host's present. The same happens out of focus (Control Center, a notification pulled down, on the way to the background): the same run had 17–23 ms of GPU time per frame right after it lost focus, against 6.5 ms just before. The front end never told the host its menu was open, so neither the log nor devlog could tell those frames from the game's.
- **Fix:** diagnostics only: the game runs under the menu exactly as before, and no switch changes anything. The front end reports its menu (`dm_front_menu_set`, `front.h`): `DmGamepad.m` `menuOpened` / `menuClosed` on the iPhone and iPad, the menu bar's `NSMenuDidBeginTrackingNotification` / `NSMenuDidEndTrackingNotification` in `front_mac.m`. `hle_oe.c` logs `front end: in-game menu open` and `front end: in-game menu closed after N s` (with `(menu bar)` on the Mac) and keeps the state, open until half a second after the close because the dismissal is still drawn (`FRONT_MENU_TAIL_NS`). The present path reads it and `dm_focus_now()` once a frame (`hle_nvn.c` `front_ui`, a few atomic loads). A long frame made with the menu open or out of focus, when it was made or when it is judged 8 frames later, ends with ` (in-game menu open)`, ` (not in focus)` or ` (in-game menu open, not in focus)`, after the `; N ms of it in the background` of a frame across a stay in the background, which devlog still names as that stay and does not count apart as well ([A device log that does not say why the frame step held](#a-device-log-that-does-not-say-why-the-frame-step-held)); a `timing up to frame` or `on-screen up to frame` line whose window had such frames ends with `in-game menu open N frames, not in focus M frames` (nothing is added when it had none). `scripts/devlog.py` counts tagged long frames apart (`not counted: N frames made with the in-game menu open or out of focus`), lists the menu's opening and closing among the events, marks the worst windows that had such frames, and gives the dropped rate again without those windows (`dropped, menu / focus windows out (%)` when it compares two logs). Comparing a log from before the tags with one after, use `long frames, menu / focus ones too`: the older log's `long frames` counts every long frame. A menu open a minute or more, or never closed in a log that ran a minute past its opening, is flagged among the events: its close may have been missed (the pad reports one from `willEnd`, or from the next touch on the game if that never came, and a player on a controller may not touch the game), and every frame tagged since may be the game's.
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md#found-on-this-game) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max: five clusters of GPU spikes and dropped frames lined up with the player using the in-game menu, and an earlier burst with the same two-cluster signature that nothing in the log could confirm.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> run` runs `t_oe`: the menu state opens, stays open for the tail after a close and then shuts, a repeated report changes nothing, focus is a state of its own and the game is sent no notification. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_nvn && dm/gfx/tests/t_nvn`, section 9: the three tags and the window note, and nothing tagged without a host. `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py`, class `FrontEnd`: the tag and note strings in `hle_nvn.c` read back, tagged long frames are counted apart, the dropped rate without those windows, the like-for-like count with the tagged frames in, a menu open a minute or more or never closed is flagged, and a log without tags reads as before. The Duo harness's `testInGameMenuOpensForAShortcut`: the pad reports the menu's open and close to the host.
- **Not checked:** no device log has the new lines yet, and the Mac menu bar's notifications have not been seen on a real window. The UIKit screens the menu opens once it has closed (the Share log sheet and its alert, other sheets) are drawn over the game too but are not tagged: the menu's state ends half a second after its close, and those screens report nothing to the host. On the Mac, out of focus means another window has the key status: the system is not drawing over the game, but its frames are tagged `(not in focus)` and counted apart all the same, so a hitch in a game played with a controller while another window is in front is not in the game's count.

### A present command allocator is reused only after its own present completes

- **Symptom:** with the display link driving presents, a presented frame comes out black when three presents are on the GPU at once, or two after a dropped frame; on an M6 the same reuse also logs `[RED] display command buffer error` with `kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorPageFault`.
- **Cause:** `present_now` cycles three Metal 4 command allocators, each with its command buffer and UV buffer, by ticket, and before resetting one it waited for `g_pq_done` to reach the ticket that last used it. `g_pq_done` is not GPU completion: every display-link callback sets it to `ticket - 1` (the previous present was committed, not run), and a present that was not submitted sets it to its own ticket. So a display-link present never waited there. With three presents in flight, or two after a dropped frame, the allocator was reset and its command buffer encoded again under a present the GPU had not run, and that present's drawable read back black.
- **Fix:** each allocator keeps the newest ticket its own commit feedback has reported (`g_present_alloc_done`), and its reuse waits for that. The display-link callback can now block in `present_now` until the GPU has run the allocator's previous present. `g_pq_done` keeps its meaning for the game's swapchain acquire.
- **Found on:** no game. Found by reading `NvMetalDisplayLinkDelegate`, then reproduced in `t_present_alloc` (below).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 check_t_present_alloc` holds the Metal 4 queue behind a shared event and calls the display-link callback by hand, each present with a drawable of its own offscreen layer. It covers a dropped frame (tickets 1, 3, then 4 on allocator 1 again) and one frame per tick (5, 6, 7, then 8 on allocator 2 again). Before the fix, in both cases the reusing present returns while the earlier one is held and the earlier drawable reads back 0 in all 64 pixels (4 of 13 checks fail; on an M6 every run also logs the GPU page fault); with it the reusing present waits until the gate opens, and all 13 checks pass with Metal validation.
- **Not checked:** a game run hitting it, the iPhone, and the not-submitted path, which the test does not exercise.

### Frames of a dynamic present interval held on screen and at the acquire

- **Symptom:** "sometimes the speed increases when jumping" (Mario 1.2.1 on the iPhone): in `display` mode interval-2 frames were shown for 16.7 ms and the game visibly jumped ahead. "When pressing B, Link's move pace is speeding up" (Breath of the Wild on the iPhone); `old frames dropped by merging`.
- **Cause:** Mario times every frame (`main+0xcfd24`); after a frame that ran past one vertical sync it sets present interval 2 for the next frame *and* sets its own time step to the interval (a float at `main+0x36ceff0`), so that frame catches up 2/60 s. A user's 2-minute iPhone session switched 123 times, mostly for one frame. On the game side, the acquire waited for one display-link tick per frame whatever the interval, so the game kept producing frames every 16.7 ms while the screen held each interval-2 frame for 33 ms and dropped the one in between. BotW sprinting flaps between interval 1 and 2 for runs of 6 or more frames, and its VFR time step at interval 2 is a whole 30 fps tick, so those runs went at double speed.
- **Fix:** both sides, fixed:
  - the screen: in `game` mode (the default since 2026-10-15) the display-link callback keeps a frame whose period spans two or more link ticks on screen through those ticks before taking the next one (`g_link_hold_until` in `nvn_mtl.m`, [Queued frames wait for the frame's display-link ticks](#queued-frames-wait-for-the-frames-display-link-ticks); the `display link up to tick` log line counts these ticks as `held for the present interval`), and the 30 fps target that drops the display link to 30 Hz (`nvn_game_fps` in `hle_nvn.c`) waits until interval 2 has held for a full second, so one-frame switches no longer flip the display-link mode;
  - the game: `mtl_link_wait_tick` now waits for as many ticks as the game's present interval (times 2 on the 120 Hz link, half rounded up on the 30 Hz link), as the Switch blocks the next acquire for N vertical syncs.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md) (1.2.1, iPhone: the display-link hold); [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (iPhone: the acquire).
- **Check:** `DM_TEST_PRESENT_LAG=90,30` (sleep 30 ms before every 90th present) makes Mario do this on the Mac; run it with `DM_WINDOW=1` to get the display link. `DM_NVN_TSV` writes each frame's interval as `@itv`.
- **Note:** the windowless Mac host was never affected by the acquire: there the acquire sleeps until the interval's slot.

### Present interval 0 waits for no vsync

- **Symptom:** a loading screen that should take seconds runs for minutes at 60 fps with a few long frames of new pipelines; the log has `the game sets present interval 1 → 0` before it and `0 → 1` after it, and the graphics worker thread spends most of its time in `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` → `mtl_link_wait_ticks`.
- **Cause:** at present interval 0 Horizon's swap does not wait for a vsync: the acquire waits only for a texture the display has let go of, the display shows the newest frame at each vsync, and the game paces itself (the Switch's unlocked-frame-rate mods set interval 0 to go past 60). The host took 0 as 1 (`present_itv`) and waited a display tick at every acquire. Ori and the Blind Forest: Definitive Edition sets Unity's `vSyncCount` to 0 only while its `UberShaderPrewarmer` draws every shader variant behind the first loading screen, and back to 1 after it. `UpdateStream` (`main+0x625b40`, 1.0.2) draws shaders until a per-frame time budget runs out, and when it drew 2 or fewer it waits 15 frames. Each draw waits for Unity's graphics worker, which sat in the tick wait, so the budget mostly ran out after one or two shaders.
- **Fix:** at interval 0 the acquire waits for no tick (`present_unpaced` in `hle_nvn.c`), unless a host frame cap (`fps_target`, the frame step) or display mode decides the pacing, which keep their own. The display link still takes one frame per tick and drops the older waiting ones, and the acquire's ticket wait still holds a texture until its frame is taken or dropped.
- **Found on:** [Ori and the Blind Forest: Definitive Edition](../../dm/games/010061D00DB74000/README.md) 1.0.2 on an Apple M6 with the pipeline archive already filled: from boot to the end of the prewarm 187 → 12 s (the first boot, with an empty archive, took about 5 minutes); during the prewarm frames p50 9.7–14.5 ms, 446 frames in its first 315 display ticks, 146 of them dropped. [Portal 2](../../dm/games/0100ABD01785C000/README.md) starts at interval 0 and sets 1 in its first frames: a 60 s boot reaches frame 3,000 with the same frame times with and without the change.
- **Check:** `t_link_ticks` (`game interval 0 waits for no tick`, display mode and the 60 fps frame step still pace it).

### A game's own present pacing reads the interval the window presents at

- **Symptom:** a game the profile upgrades to 60 fps (`fps_target 60`, `present_interval display`) drops to about 25 fps in a window on an M2 Pro, from the first frames and in every scene, with no red line, once `nn::vi::ListFrameHistory` lists the presents ([Layer frame history](services.md#layer-frame-history)). On an M6 it runs at 54–58 fps with 33 ms frames and empty display-link ticks. The `present interval set by the game` in the `interval mode display` line is 2.
- **Cause:** the game paces its presents by the window's interval itself. Luigi's Mansion 3 (30 fps on the Switch at interval 2) reads the newest history entry and `nvnWindowGetPresentInterval` on its render thread before each `nvnQueuePresentTexture` (`main+0xb7a4b8`). While that frame is not shown yet it sleeps 16.67 ms (`os::SleepThread`) per vsync of the interval until it is. Once it is shown, it waits until (interval − 1) vsyncs after its present time. Display mode presents one frame per tick whatever the game set, but the host still answered the game's 2, so at 60 fps the game held itself to its 30 fps pacing. With no history entries it had skipped the whole block.
- **Fix:** in display mode, `nvnWindowGetPresentInterval` answers the interval the window presents at (`window_interval` in `hle_nvn.c`): 1, or the cap's interval (2 under `DM_FPS_TARGET=30`). In game mode it answers the game's own. NieR:Automata (010056B015FE8000), Need for Speed Hot Pursuit Remastered and Monster Train 2 fetch the function but never call it.
- **Found on:** [Luigi's Mansion 3](../../dm/games/0100DCA0064A6000/README.md) (issue #1705): title screen in a window on an M6, 54.3–58.2 → 60.0 fps, frame time p95 24–25 → 18 ms, max 107 → 22.7 ms, no empty display-link ticks after warm-up.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`display mode: the game reads back the interval the window presents at`).

### A game that changes its present interval every frame filled the log

- **Symptom:** a log that is mostly `the game sets present interval 1 → 2` and `2 → 1` lines and `present thread time-constraint policy (…) set` lines, a pair for almost every frame. Upstream: Mario Kart World 1.8.0 on an iPhone, a 7-minute race below 60 fps, 11,614 changes, which with the policy line each brought there were 23,307 of the log's 27,529 lines.
- **Cause:** the game paces itself by its present interval: below its 60 fps it sets 2 for one frame and 1 for the next, about 30 changes a second, and `nvnWindowSetPresentInterval` wrote a line for every change.
- **Fix:** `itv_log` in `hle_nvn.c`, for every game (upstream NXNative 5cf87e305): the first 8 changes are written as before, then each whose count is a power of two, with its count. The 300-frame summary writes `present interval up to frame N: the game changed it X times (Y since the start)` when there was a change. Each change of the period declares the present thread's time-constraint policy again, which wrote a `present thread time-constraint policy (…) set` line as well: that line is now written for the first 20 declarations, then at each power of two (upstream limits it with its own `rt_declare` change, which NX 18 does not have). The policy is still declared at every change, and the 300-frame `thread policy` line says what is in effect.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md) upstream (1.8.0, iPhone); not run here.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`2,000 interval changes counted`, `... written for the first 8 and each power of two`).

### Swap chain textures a game leaves inactive

- **Symptom:** a game that asks NVN for fewer active swap chain textures than it gave the window gets one frame more queued between its rendering and the screen than it chose (input latency), with a log line `WindowBuilderSetNumActiveTextures n (the host rotates through the SetTextures count)`.
- **Cause:** `nvnWindowBuilderSetNumActiveTextures` / `nvnWindowSetNumActiveTextures` limit `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` to the first n textures of the swap chain. The host only logged them and rotated through all of them, and their getters were unimplemented (a red line and 0).
- **Fix:** the window keeps the count (`nactive` in `win_t` and `win_builder_t`) and the acquire rotates through `window_active` textures; the getters answer it. A count outside 1 to the swap chain's own is a red line and keeps all of them. `WindowInitialize`'s log line names the active count.
- **Found on:** no game on the Mac's drives: every one that calls it (Mario vs. Donkey Kong 2 of 2; 11 others, among them Fire Emblem Engage, Super Mario RPG and Super Monkey Ball Banana Rumble, 3 of 3) asks for its whole swap chain. The S2Arm project reports that [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md) sets 2 of its 3; not run here.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`2 of 3 active: textures 0 1 0 1`, the window's own call, a count out of range).

### A present interval past the hardware's maximum

- **Symptom:** with a frame-rate mod on, the picture stops on its first frames while the sound goes on; turning the mod off brings it back. The log has `the game asks for present interval 1000, past the hardware's 0..5`.
- **Cause:** frame-rate mods written for emulators set the present interval to 1000 (an emulator ignores it). The hardware's maximum is 5 (`MAX_PRESENT_INTERVAL`, `[0x46]` in the device table), but `nvnWindowBuilderSetPresentInterval` and `nvnWindowSetPresentInterval` stored the value raw, so every present waited 1000 vsyncs.
- **Fix:** both setters pass the value through `itv_in`: 0 to 5 is kept, anything else (above 5 or negative) counts as 0, with one log line. Interval 0 is the unpaced path (`present_unpaced`): no vsync wait, still capped by the present pacing.
- **Found on:** the S2Arm project reports it with a public frame-rate mod on the iPhone (ported from S2Arm c0c6c19c); not run here.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`builder interval 1000 -> 0`, `window interval 1000 -> 0`, `window interval -1 -> 0`, 5 and 2 kept).

### Odd present intervals on the 30 Hz display link

- **Symptom:** Donkey Kong Bananza ran its heavy areas at 15 fps where its GPU took about 46 ms a frame: the frames came every 66.7 ms, and the presentation thread spent 85% of its time in `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` waiting for display-link ticks.
