- **Cause:** after a second at interval 2 the display link drops to 30 Hz (`nvn_game_fps`). When the game then stepped to interval 3 (20 fps) the link stayed at 30 Hz, where 3 vsyncs are (3 + 1) / 2 = 2 ticks (`mtl_link_wait_tick`): 66.7 ms for the 50 the game asked for. The comment said such intervals "run at 30"; they ran at 15.
- **Fix:** only an even interval counts toward the 30 Hz link; an odd one returns to the 60 Hz link at once, as interval 1 does, and takes its own 3 ticks (`present_period` in `hle_nvn.c`). The one-second test no longer wraps: the run's start is stored with `| 1` and could be 1 ns after the next clock read, which made `now - start` huge and asked for 30 Hz at once.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md): the slow area on an M1, frame time p50 66.7 → 50.2 ms at interval 3 (`DM_NVN_TSV` `@frame_ms`, `@itv`, `@link_gap_ms` 33.3 → 16.7).
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`interval 3: 60 Hz link`, `back to 2 from 3 waits a second again`).

### One frame per tick of a 120 Hz display link

- **Symptom:** with `present_interval display`, `DM_PRESENT_HZ=120` and `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2` (a 120 Hz display link) a game stays at 60 fps although its frame takes 8 ms; the display link logs about two ticks per present. With a requested 90 Hz, truncating the tick conversion instead allows 120 fps (reproduced through the real acquire with simulated link ticks).
- **Cause:** the acquire waits for the present interval's vsyncs on the display link (`mtl_link_wait_tick`), counted in 60 Hz vsyncs, so two ticks of a 120 Hz link per frame; display mode's interval is 1. Dividing the link rate by the requested rate once per acquire loses fractional ticks (120 / 90 becomes 1).
- **Fix:** in display mode above 60 Hz the acquire waits as many link ticks as one tick of the present rate takes (`link_wait` in `hle_nvn.c`, `mtl_link_wait_ticks`): one at 120 Hz. Fractional ticks carry across acquires: at 90 Hz, three frames wait four link ticks (1, 1, 2); a rate or pacing-mode change starts a new phase. Rates at or above the link's rate wait one tick.
- **Found on:** Batman: Arkham Knight (`0100ACD0163D0000`; its profile and note are in a PR of their own): 119.8 fps in the opening cutscene on an M2 Max.
- **Check:** `t_link_ticks` (also run in CPU-only CI): with a controlled clock and one tick delivered per timed wait, a 60 Hz vsync waits two ticks of a 120 Hz link and one tick of a 60 Hz one, and a wait in the link's own ticks takes one tick per frame. The real acquire checks display pacing at 60, 75, 90, 120 and 240 Hz, game intervals and frame-step caps, including the 60 and 30 Hz link modes. Exact tick counts, already elapsed ticks, spurious wakes and silent-link timeouts are checked without depending on OS scheduling: a sleeping ticker could miss the acquire's wall-clock timeout on CI. These checks do not measure a game's frame rate on a screen.

### Queued frames wait for the frame's display-link ticks

- **Symptom:** a queued frame can replace a 60 fps frame after just one 8.33 ms tick of a 120 Hz display link.
- **Cause:** the callback only held frames with periods above 25 ms, a cutoff sized for a 60 Hz link. A 60 fps acquire on the 120 Hz link already waits two ticks, but it can return immediately when those ticks have elapsed, and another frame can be queued while the first is still on screen.
- **Fix:** `NvMetalDisplayLinkDelegate` in `nvn_mtl.m` holds the whole ticks in the submitted frame's period. Virtual ticks use their scheduled times so uneven refresh spacing cannot end the hold early. The acquire distributes fractional ticks across frames (75 fps on the 120 Hz link holds nothing).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (upstream); ported from upstream (`64a1b8f6d`, [PR #1045](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1045)). The early replacement was reproduced in the callback fixture, not on a device.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_link_hold_cb` queues two frames and drives the real callback with offscreen Metal drawables, in both encode-thread modes. At 60 fps on the 120 Hz link the second frame stays queued through the first tick and is taken on the second. It also covers a virtual 120 Hz grid on a 144 Hz screen, 120 and 75 fps on the 120 Hz link, and 30 fps on the 60 and 30 Hz links.

### A frame that stands in the display link's queue

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone 18 Pro Max the `display link up to tick` lines give game enqueue to target display about a tick more than the frame needs, with no frame dropped by merging: p50 65.5 ms against 33.2 ms of submission ahead of target display in two runs of Persona 5 Royal (49.8 against 33.3 in a third), 65.6 against 33.0 in Super Mario Odyssey; about 85 ms in Ocarina of Time at 30 fps, with two frames queued at nearly every tick.
- **Cause:** the display-link callback takes frames in order, at most one behind (`DM_PRESENT_FIFO`, on by default, from PR #31: taking the newest dropped the one before whenever two frames of a 60 fps game landed in one refresh, and the next frame then stayed up 33 ms; in Dark Souls Remastered presents within 14.7-18.7 ms went from 98.2 to 99.98 %). Once two frames wait at a tick, the tick takes the older, and a game that keeps 60 enqueues another before the next tick, so one frame stands in the queue until a refresh comes without a frame: every frame waits a tick more, 16.7 ms at 60 Hz.
- **Fix:** `DM_PRESENT_TRIM=<K>`: after K ticks in a row that found two or more frames waiting, one tick takes the newest, dropping the frame before it, and the in-order rule holds again (`g_link_trim` in the callback, `nvn_mtl.m`). An empty tick ends a run; a tick held for a long present interval neither ends nor counts in one. K must be longer than the runs that frame-time jitter makes, each ended by a refresh without a frame, or the trim drops the frames PR #31 keeps; 60, a second at 60 Hz (two at 30 Hz), lets a standing frame last that long at most, at the cost of one dropped frame each time it forms. The `display link up to tick` line now ends with `frames queued at the callback mean M / max N, two or more on T of 300, standing frames trimmed C`: a mean near 2 is a standing frame (scripts/devlog.py's pattern still matches the line).
- **Fix:** an iOS default (iPhone and iPad; `PRESENT_TRIM_PLATFORM`, `TARGET_OS_IPHONE` without Mac Catalyst): a game whose profile has no `present_trim` gets K = 60 there; on the Mac it stays off unless the profile sets it (`present_trim_k`). A profile's `present_trim` wins over the platform default, an explicit `0` included (`game.py` takes 0, off, or 30 to 600 ticks, and writes `-1` into `DM_GAME_PRESENT_TRIM` without the key), and `DM_PRESENT_TRIM` in the environment or `Documents/env.txt` wins over both: `DM_PRESENT_TRIM=0` in `Documents/env.txt` turns the trim off for any game, `DM_PRESENT_TRIM=<K>` sets K. K counts the ticks of the display link. On iOS that is 60 Hz, ProMotion screens included (the 60 Hz range asks for 60 exactly, and 120 fps is offered on the Mac only: `hle_nvn.c` `high_cap_tick`), 30 Hz at a 30 fps target, and 120 Hz only with `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2` in `Documents/env.txt`. The iOS default's 60 ticks are a second at 60 Hz and stay 60 (two seconds) on the 30 Hz link, as in Ocarina of Time's 30 fps run; on a link faster than 60 Hz it is a second of ticks, 120 at 120 Hz, so a run of frame-time jitter is not judged in half the time (`present_trim_ticks`). A K from the variable or a profile is ticks as written. The callback's first line with a trim says where K came from (`DM_PRESENT_TRIM=60`, `game profile present_trim 60` or `iOS default 60`), and logs again if the link's rate changes the iOS default's K. [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), [13 Sentinels](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md), [Mario Kart 8 Deluxe](../../dm/games/0100152000022000/README.md), [NieR:Automata](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), [Persona 4 Golden](../../dm/games/010062B01525C000/README.md) and [Persona 5 Royal](../../dm/games/01005CA01580E000/README.md) keep `present_trim 60` in their profiles: the Mac still needs it, and each game's README records its measurement.
- **Found on:** [Persona 5 Royal](../../dm/games/01005CA01580E000/README.md) and [Super Mario Odyssey](../../dm/games/0100000000010000/README.md) (iPhone 18 Pro Max logs). [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on the same phone (2026-11-09): at 30 fps a frame stood in the queue nearly all the time, game enqueue to target display about 85 ms. With `DM_PRESENT_TRIM=60` about 53 ms, 9 trims (9 dropped frames) in 2.5 minutes, no measurable cost in smoothness. Its 60 fps run (9.6 minutes, without the switch) had it in part: 33 of its 88 windows of 300 ticks, all in the first 3 minutes, had about one frame queued (enqueue to display p50 41-49 ms); nearly all the others, while the frame step moved between holds 1 and 3, had two or more frames waiting on 12-93% of their ticks (p50 45-82 ms, the run's median window 55 ms), and four on 297-300 of them (p50 60-62 ms). Empty ticks end a run in those partial windows, so whether K = 60 is reached there is not known. The windows after about 7 minutes also ran beside a loader loop on a damaged file ([A damaged file in a NUP or a dump has no hash to fail](packing.md#a-damaged-file-in-a-nup-or-a-dump-has-no-hash-to-fail)), two of the four full ones among them.
- **Note:** the default on iOS since seven games measured with the switch on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (the 60 Hz display link; Ocarina of Time at 30 fps on the 30 Hz one) all improved and none got worse: Ocarina of Time at 30 fps for 2.5 minutes; [13 Sentinels](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md), [Mario Kart 8 Deluxe](../../dm/games/0100152000022000/README.md), [NieR:Automata](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), [Persona 4 Golden](../../dm/games/010062B01525C000/README.md) and [Persona 5 Royal](../../dm/games/01005CA01580E000/README.md) at 60 fps, game enqueue to display about 43 ms → 28.5 ms, 43–47 ms → 25–28 ms, 42–46 ms → 25–29 ms, 45–49 ms → 28–29.5 ms and 46.9–48.4 ms → 32.4–32.8 ms; 3, 3, 20, 4 and 3 frames dropped in play, NieR's over 9 minutes and almost all at loads and movies, its frame interval p99 unchanged with the GPU near its budget; Persona 5 Royal's frame interval p99 16.8–17.0 ms in play against 16.9–18.0 without. Last, [Super Mario Odyssey](../../dm/games/0100000000010000/README.md) (2026-11-11, build `5b302cd0`, Ultrawide on, no edge smoothing): without the trim, game enqueue to target display p50 40–49.5 ms with two frames queued on nearly every tick for the whole 2:42 run; with `DM_PRESENT_TRIM=60` 25.3–29 ms with one frame queued, 6 trims in about 2 minutes (the start, two area loads, a pipeline build and one just before the app went to the background), frame interval p99 17.2–18.8 ms against 17.1–18.6 without, no audio underruns (the run without was a first start, the run with a second start). The cost, a dropped frame each time a standing frame forms again, depends on how often a game's frames land two to a refresh (Ocarina of Time at 30 fps: one trim every 17 s or so; Odyssey's came at loads and pipeline builds). The Mac stays opt-in per game: no game has been run there with it. Ocarina of Time's profile sets it for every rate and platform, so its 60 fps default and the Mac use it untested.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_present_alloc` (and `GAME=0400486024DEC000`): with K = 3 and two frames waiting at every tick, the first two ticks take the older, the third the newest (one frame dropped, one trim), and a single frame starts the run again. K read at the first tick is the Mac's: the profile's without `DM_PRESENT_TRIM`, the variable's with it (the target runs the test again with `DM_PRESENT_TRIM=0`). The graphics tests build for the Mac only, so `present_trim_k` is checked with the platform as a parameter: on iOS without the key or the variable K is 60 (120 on a 120 Hz link, 60 on the 30 Hz one), on the Mac off; the variable's 0 and a profile's 0 turn it off on both, and a profile's K wins over the platform. `python3 -m unittest test_game_profile` (`dm/tools/tests`): the key's values, `-1` without it and that a profile setting it has a README that names it.
- **Not checked:** the case PR #31 fixed, Dark Souls Remastered's 16–17.5 ms frames, with the trim (which K keeps those frames); the iOS default on an iPad or with `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2`; a trim build without a per-game key on a device (the runs above set K through `Documents/env.txt` or the profile, which the default reproduces); any game on the Mac with it.

### Present snapshots: the game's swapchain texture free once its copy has run

- **Symptom:** frames miss the screen while the GPU stays at its lowest clock (444 MHz on an M2 Max); more work (a higher render scale) only makes the frames slower.
- **Cause:** a game with two swapchain textures gets one back only when the display link has taken its previous present, so each frame's CPU work starts on a tick and the GPU idles from the end of one frame to the first work of the next. macOS reads that idle time as room and keeps the clock low.
- **Fix:** present snapshots (`DM_PRESENT_SNAP=<n>`, a ring of n host textures, 2 to 4; off by default on iOS and Mac; `nvn_mtl.m` `snap_take`): each present is copied on the game's queue, right after the frame's work, into the next ring slot; the display link shows the copy, and the game's texture is the game's again as soon as the copy has run on the GPU, without waiting for the screen. The game runs up to a frame ahead of the screen, and a frame whose work runs long is absorbed instead of shown late. Acquire waits for ring capacity and counts it as pacing (`mtl_snap_acquire_wait`). Present never waits for a snapshot slot: if it is still busy (several textures acquired ahead of time, or a copy not finished), the original texture is queued with its usual acquire ticket. Waiting inside present held the game's locks while the screen was stalled.
- **Setting:** the iOS launcher's **Frame buffering** toggle is offered for NVN builds and remembers the choice. Its default is the profile's `present_snap` (off unless a game was tested with it on: Breath of the Wild, a steady 60 fps on an iPhone 17 Pro Max where it fell to 30 without). `Documents/env.txt` overrides it (`DM_PRESENT_SNAP=1` on, `0` off); restart the app after editing the file. The Mac uses the same environment variable and the same profile default.
- **Fix:** the frame step judges lateness by what the screen shows while snapshots are on: the on-screen interval of the frame 8 presents back, against its hold (`fstep_tick`, `nvn_fstep.screen`). Judged by its cost, a frame whose CPU time holds the game's waits on its own GPU work (which now runs a frame later) looked late at a GPU time of 0.75 vsyncs.
- **Note:** the cost is up to a frame more latency.
- **Symptom:** with snapshots on, Xenoblade Chronicles 3's 3D scene is one flat colour in a third to a half of the frames (black, grey, blue, orange, white; a new colour each time), while the HUD drawn over it is right. The more ring slots, the more often: 32 of 80 screen captures with 2 slots, 49 of 80 with 4, none with snapshots off ([LNPANDY/NX2Apple#1117](https://github.com/LNPANDY/NX2Apple/issues/1117)).
- **Cause:** a present that took a snapshot returned ticket 0, so the game's next acquire of that texture waited for nothing; only the next ring slot paced it. The ring slot is counted per present, not per texture, and it is counted when the queue worker replays the present (`nvn_queue.inc`), not when the game calls it. So with more slots than swapchain textures, a game that acquires its next texture before it presents the current frame, or an acquire that comes before the worker has replayed the last present (the ring wait then looks at a slot one present behind), the game ran more frames ahead of the GPU than its swapchain allows. Which of the three Xenoblade Chronicles 3 meets with 2 slots was not measured. A game that keeps its CPU-written per-frame data (uniform blocks read by the GPU from the shared pool when the draw runs) in a ring sized on the swapchain then rewrote the data of a frame still queued: here the composite's block.
- **Fix:** a snapshot present keeps its ticket, and the queue's receipt for it carries its copy (`mtl_present`'s `copy`, the value the copy signals); the acquire of its texture waits on that receipt, in the game's order, until the copy has run on the GPU (`mtl_present_wait_texture`, from `nvn_queue_present_wait`); the frame drawn into the texture has run before it. `nvnQueueFinish` after a snapshot present waits for the copy in the same way, not for the screen: by the ticket alone it would wait for the display link, which paces a game that finishes its queue every frame by the screen again. The game never has more frames in flight than its swapchain holds, and still does not wait for the screen. With the fix, 0 of 80 flat frames with 2 slots and with 4.
- **Check:** `t_present_snap ticket`: with 4 ring slots and 2 window textures, both presents keep their tickets, acquiring the first texture again waits for its copy, and acquiring the second waits while its copy is held behind a GPU gate and returns once the gate opens; `nvnQueueFinish` after a snapshot present returns in about 1 ms while the display is stalled for 400 ms (405 ms when it waits by the ticket). Before the fix the tickets were 0 and the acquire returned at once.
- **Note:** with Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD`, the default on iOS) the copy is a block of the encode thread's own queue (`rec_own_push`), behind everything submitted before the present, so a present from another thread than the submitting one gets a snapshot too; the present waits only until that block is encoded (`snap_commit_wait`). The wait ledger `DM_NVN_WAITS=1` counts every CPU wait for the GPU or the encode thread (`[waits]` every 300 frames): the acquire's wait for a free ring slot as `snapshot ring` (`mtl_snap_acquire_wait`), and the acquire's or `nvnQueueFinish`'s wait for a texture's copy as `snapshot copy` (`mtl_present_wait_texture`; `t_present_snap ticket` checks it). In `hle_nvn.c` the acquire adds that copy wait to its present-queue wait (`g_pq_wait_ns`).
- **Fix:** suspend drains snapshot commits and their GPU event even when their display tickets were discarded (`snap_drain` in `mtl_drain`). Submission fences and upload completion do not cover these copies; previously suspend could return while the encoder still held a copy to submit.
- **Fix:** with snapshots on and the display link ticking, the acquire waits for the link's present-interval ticks like any present (`nvnWindowAcquireTexture` in `hle_nvn.c`); only a silent link falls back to the texture's slot time. The wait for a free ring slot alone did not pace: the link takes frames in order and drops all but the newest two, and a dropped frame frees its slot. Donkey Kong Bananza at the 30 fps cap ran 43-65 fps of game time on an M1 Pro and 45-50 fps on an iPhone 15 Pro Max, each frame still stepping 1/30 s (fast-forward), and about 110 of every 300 frames never reached the screen; after, 30.0 fps and 300 of 300 frames on screen (Mac; measured by the author of PR #1073 on their build). The game still runs up to a frame ahead of the screen: the link wait limits how often a frame starts, not how far ahead of the display it is. `t_link_ticks` checks the acquire with snapshots on: two ticks of a 60 Hz link at interval 2, one at interval 1, none while the link is stalled.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_present_snap` uses a gated encoder and GPU to check suspend, and a delayed present consumer to check nonblocking present, acquire pacing, fallback tickets and copied pixels after ring reuse. Runs with Metal validation on the Mac, with and without the encode thread; does not measure iPhone frame rate or latency.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (Mac; on the iPhone 17 Pro Max with the encode thread a steady 60 fps at docked resolution, present to screen about 74 ms with a ring of 2 against about 46–60 ms without).

### A present snapshot copied the whole window texture for a smaller crop

- **Symptom:** with present snapshots on (`DM_PRESENT_SNAP`, the launcher's Frame buffering; on by default in Ocarina of Time and Breath of the Wild, and with frame generation or the present repeat), a game whose present crop is smaller than its window texture spends GPU time and bandwidth every frame copying pixels the screen never shows: a dynamic resolution that moves its crop inside the texture, or a texture made larger than the picture. MacNativeGames measured its port of Ocarina of Time (3840x2160 window images there, a 1920x1080 crop) at 0.446 ms of GPU time a frame for the copy, 0.095 ms with the crop alone.
- **Cause:** `snap_take` copied the whole presented texture into its ring slot whatever the crop.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game (`DM_PRESENT_SNAP_WHOLE=1` copies the whole texture again). The copy takes the present's crop alone when it lies in the texture and is smaller (`snap_region`), at its own place in a slot of the texture's size, so every reader keeps the game's crop and a crop that moves needs no new texture. Nothing reads a snapshot outside its crop: the present clamps its samples half a texel inside it (`present_encode_m4`), and the scaler, edge smoothing, frame generation, the present repeat and the screenshots take the crop. A crop off the texture copies it whole (the present then refuses that crop by name, as before). The log says `present snapshots: the copy takes the present's crop alone` at the first such copy. Adapted from MacNativeGames 78cf9896 (MIT), whose slot holds the crop alone at its origin (less memory: 33 → 8.3 MB a slot there); here the slot keeps the texture's size, as [edge smoothing's](rendering.md#edge-smoothing-in-the-present) copy of the crop does, so a dynamic resolution's crop never makes a new slot texture. Its other part (a slot marked busy in the section that moves the present queue past its ticket) has no counterpart: `snap_take` reads a slot's ticket against `g_pq_done` under `g_pq_mu`.
- **Measurement:** not measured on NX 18. Ocarina of Time's crop on NX 18 is not known to be smaller than its window texture (its output is 2560x1440 docked with the internal resolution inside the game), so it may gain nothing; a game whose crop moves with dynamic resolution and that runs with Frame buffering does.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) in MacNativeGames' port.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_present_snap` runs `t_present_snap crop` with and without the encode thread: after two whole copies of 113 and a new frame of 150, two copies with an 8x9 crop at 2,3 leave 150 inside the crop and 113 outside in 16x16 slots, counted as cropped; a crop off the texture and one that is all of it copy the whole texture. `crop_whole` (`DM_PRESENT_SNAP_WHOLE=1`) leaves 150 everywhere.

### A replaced snapshot ring texture stays resident until the slot's next use

- **Symptom:** none seen in a game. When the presented texture changes size or format while snapshots are on, a display command buffer still queued could sample the ring slot's old texture outside the residency set, which faults the queue (`kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorPageFault`, then every later command buffer ignored), as in [A present snapshot's copy needs the same residency commit](rendering.md#a-present-snapshots-copy-needs-the-same-residency-commit).
- **Cause:** `snap_take` replaces a slot's texture only once the slot's last present has been processed (`g_pq_done`), and it removed the old one from the snapshot residency set right away. A processed present has been committed, not run: as with [the present allocators](#a-present-command-allocator-is-reused-only-after-its-own-present-completes), the display-link callback sets `g_pq_done` before the GPU runs the present.
- **Fix:** the slot keeps its old texture (`g_snap[k].old`) and removes it from the residency set at the slot's next use. That use already waits for the replacing copy's event, and the queue signals that event only after all the work committed before it, which includes every present that read the old texture. Memory changes only when the presented size or format does: the old texture lasts one more round of the ring.
- **Found on:** no game. Found by reading `snap_take` while checking [GitHub issue #626](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/626) (whose logs show one size throughout).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 check_t_present_snap` runs `t_present_snap resize`, with and without the encode thread. It holds the queue behind a shared event in place of queued display command buffers and presents a 16x16 texture after two 8x8 ones. The old textures stay in `g_snap_res` while the queue is held, also through a present that falls back because its slot's copy is still queued, and each leaves at its own slot's next use. 13 checks pass with Metal validation; with the old removal, 4 fail.
- **Not checked:** a game that changes its presented size with snapshots on, and the iPhone.

### Vulkan acquire and vsync event follow the display link

- **Note:** the Vulkan layer was removed in 0.5 (in git at `93c34371`); the vsync event half below still applies to every game.
- **Symptom:** a steady game whose frame-rate counter wanders between 50 and 60 on the iPhone only; the `display link up to tick` lines show about as many empty ticks as frames dropped by merging: frames arrive two in one tick and none in the next.
- **Cause:** the Vulkan layer's acquire and the `vi::GetDisplayVsyncEvent` thread each kept a 60 Hz clock of their own, and Galaxy runs the Wii's VI retrace on that event, so its frames drifted against the screen.
- **Fix:** while the display link presents, both follow its ticks (the Vulkan layer's acquire in `hle_vk.m`, until 0.5 removed it → `mtl_link_wait_tick`, as NVN's; `hle_sdk16.c` vsync thread → `mtl_link_wait_vsync`), with the old clocks as the fallback.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Galaxy 2](../../dm/games/0100FD8022DAA000/README.md) (Vulkan).
- **Note:** a Mac window measures nothing here unless it is on screen: macOS fires an occluded window's display link about once a second (`display link recovered: stalled 1017 ms`).

### A game window another process starts opens behind other windows

- **Symptom:** a `regress.py` run of an NVN game stays at 20 fps from the start (`frame N not reached`, a NEW-FAIL) in some runs only: `frame step: hold 1 → 2` and `2 → 3 vsyncs` in the first 15 presents, then `display link recovered: stalled 1017 ms` about once a second to the end, and no trial back to a shorter hold.
- **Cause:** the host is started by another process, so macOS does not activate it (`oe focus state → 2` in every run), and its window opens wherever it lands in the window order. Behind another window it is covered, and macOS fires a covered window's display link about once a second. The late frames of the boot step the hold up, which happens in every run, and the stall each second makes a frame late again before the 30 frames in a row that a trial needs. A run whose window happened to be on screen came back to 1 vsync with a probe.
- **Fix:** `DM_WINDOW_TOP=1` orders the window in front without activation and keeps it above other apps' windows (`front_mac.m`). `regress.py` sets it for its runs and captures. The host logs every change of the window's visibility (`front end: window covered`, `window visible`), and `regress.py` runs a route once more when its window was covered (`, window covered` in the report when the second run was covered too). A revision before this change ignores the setting, so its runs can still be covered.
- **Found on:** [Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (2026-10-28 and 29: every one of its 20 fps runs had 21 to 115 stalls, the runs at 60 fps 0 to 7).

### Virtual 60 Hz ticks on screens that are not a multiple of 60

- **Symptom:** asked for 60 Hz, the system fires the display link on every third refresh of a 165 Hz screen, 55 Hz, so games paced by it ran at 55 fps (27.5 at 30).
- **Fix:** on 144 or 165 Hz Mac displays `link_range` (`nvn_mtl.m`) runs the link at the screen's rate and counts as ticks only the refreshes nearest a steady 1/60 s clock: 60 a second on average, each frame shown 12.1 or 18.2 ms at 165 Hz. A tick lies up to half a refresh off its slot, so two ticks can be one refresh nearer each other than their slots: the hold of multi-tick frames ([Frames of a dynamic present interval held on screen and at the acquire](#frames-of-a-dynamic-present-interval-held-on-screen-and-at-the-acquire)) counts the slots' times, not the refreshes' ([Queued frames wait for the frame's display-link ticks](#queued-frames-wait-for-the-frames-display-link-ticks); before it tolerated one refresh more, and in a simulation of the callback without either, 30 fps frames held a third tick: 22.5 fps at 165 Hz, 24 at 144), and the frame step lets a frame on time show one refresh longer than its hold (`shown_slack`, `nvn_fstep.h`; a cheap 50 ms frame shows 54.5 ms at 165 Hz, and counted as late it kept Breath of the Wild at 20 fps for good). `DM_LINK_VTICK=0` keeps the system's choice.
- **Note:** iOS screens run at multiples of 60 and are not affected.
- **Found on:** PR #27 (its author measured at 165 Hz); [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (the 20 fps without `shown_slack`).
- **Not checked:** not yet run on such a screen here.

### Fullscreen switches the screen to a rate the frame rate divides

- **Symptom:** virtual ticks keep the average, but a frame still stays up two or three refreshes in turn (13.9 / 20.8 ms at 144 Hz), and Apple's HUD showed the frame interval jumping between them.
- **Fix:** `front_mac.m` `front_screen_match`, called from `link_sync` with the link's rate (60, 30, or 120 with `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2`): when the window is fullscreen on a screen whose mode is not a multiple of the link's rate, the host switches that screen to the mode of the same size with the highest rate that is (an XB273U at 2560x1440: 144 → 120 Hz for 60 fps), with `kCGConfigureForAppOnly`, so macOS switches it back when the host quits, crashes included (the PR's author checked: 144 Hz again after quitting); leaving fullscreen, or the screen, switches it back at once. A window keeps the screen's rate and the virtual ticks: the switch changes the whole screen for every app, and most external screens go black for a second or more. The link sets its range again whenever the screen's rate changes (`link_sync`; before, a move to another screen kept the first screen's range), with no virtual ticks after a switch. A screen with no such mode keeps the virtual ticks; `DM_SCREEN_MATCH=0` keeps the screen's rate.
- **Found on:** PR #79: the PR's author ran [Hades](../../dm/games/0100535012974000/README.md) at 144 Hz (switched in a window then): the HUD's frame interval line steady at 16.7 ms, where it jumped before.
- **Check:** here (a built-in 120 Hz screen, a multiple of every link rate) a harness drove `front_mac.m` asked for 50 fps: no switch in the window, 120 → 50 Hz in fullscreen, 120 Hz again on leaving it, and 120 Hz after quitting in fullscreen; at 60 fps and with `DM_SCREEN_MATCH=0` nothing switched.
- **Not checked:** the link's new range after a switch has not run here (Hades windowed and fullscreen paced at 60 as before).
- **Later:** a variable-refresh screen in fullscreen switches too, to a fixed-rate mode at a multiple of the game's rate, though its own rate is one: [The 60 Hz link at 40 fps in fullscreen on a ProMotion screen](#the-60-hz-link-at-40-fps-in-fullscreen-on-a-promotion-screen).

### macOS Game Mode holds the GPU at its lowest clock

- **Symptom:** a Mac game in fullscreen holds 60 fps for 45 s to 3 minutes, then the same work takes about twice the GPU time (Tears of the Kingdom 10.5 → 19.7 ms) and the frame step falls to 30 fps. `powermetrics` shows the GPU at its lowest clock (338 MHz on an M4 Pro) while it is 72–98% busy, with thermal pressure nominal. Probes back to 60 fail, because the clock does not rise within a probe. `gamepolicyd` logs `Game mode enabled` and `Set DPS to 2`.
- **Cause:** macOS's Game Mode, not the host. A full-screen Metal app of about 100 lines, with no host code and about 6.5 M GPU cycles a frame, ran at 618 MHz and 9.5 ms for about 160 s in Game Mode, then at 338 MHz, 18 ms and 94% busy to the end of its 5 minutes. With Game Mode off it ran at 924 MHz and about 7 ms and spent no time at 338 MHz (one run each, M4 Pro on AC power; the run with Game Mode off also sat at a flat 56.3 fps from 120 s, unexplained). Why Game Mode does this is open, and no public API is known that keeps the clock up while Game Mode is on.
- **Fix:** a workaround for a platform limit: `dm/mac/pack_app.py` writes `LSSupportsGameMode` false, Apple's documented off switch, and keeps the games category. In the `gamepolicyd` log a new bundle with the key false gets no gaming session. The cost, not measured, is what else Game Mode does: doubled Bluetooth sampling for controllers and AirPods, and lower priority for background tasks.
- **Limit:** macOS remembers an app it has labelled a game. On a bundle ID already launched in Game Mode, with the key true or omitted, re-packing with the key false kept Game Mode on, also with `GCSupportsGameMode` false and after `lsregister -u` (5 launches with the key off, same version number; a version change is not checked). So a re-packed app needs the player's switch once: in full screen, Command-Esc, Game Overlay, Settings, Game Mode; macOS keeps that per game. A reporter rebuilt a known bundle with an `entertainment` category and got no Game Mode; not checked here.
- **Caution:** do not script full-screen launches in a row. After eight in about three minutes on macOS 27.2, `WindowServer` logged `timed out fence` from 12 s after the last app quit, with none in the 85 minutes before, and the Mac panicked (`userspace watchdog timeout: no successful checkins from WindowServer`). Another session's game had exited 18 s before the last app quit, so the launches are the likely trigger, not a proven one.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md), by two reporters on M4 Pro Macs ([GitHub issue #349](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/349)).
- **Check:** `test_nsp_mac_app.py` checks the key. To see a game's state: `/usr/bin/log show --last 10m --predicate 'process == "gamepolicyd"' --style compact | grep -E "Found game|Game mode|DPS"`.
- **Not checked:** a game packed with the key false (the reporter's runs turned Game Mode off with the Game Overlay switch and with the category); other Apple chips; iOS, whose project still declares the key true (the same collapse is only suspected there, PR #314).

### A game opened from a fullscreen app stays a window

- **Symptom:** a Mac game started with `DM_FULLSCREEN=1` from an app that is itself in fullscreen (the launcher's console mode) came up as a window.
- **Cause:** the launcher lives in its own fullscreen Space, so opening the game makes macOS leave that Space. `front_run` asked for fullscreen on the next run-loop turn. A two-app harness showed the app active but its window not yet on the active Space (`isOnActiveSpace` 0), with the Space change arriving 0.3 to 0.5 s later. AppKit can refuse fullscreen during another animation or user gesture, reporting it only to the window delegate (`windowDidFailToEnterFullScreen:`, there is no notification). The original harness accepted the early request, but the reporter later reproduced the startup rollback on four of four Hades II launches from NX2Apple's console mode.
- **Fix:** `front_mac.m` `front_run` waits until the app is active and the window is on the active Space (`NSApplicationDidBecomeActiveNotification`, `NSWorkspaceActiveSpaceDidChangeNotification`, whichever comes last), and repeats a refused startup request once. The failure handler acts only on an outstanding startup attempt; successful entry or a player transition while the request is waiting retires it, so a later manual refusal cannot restart startup. `g_fullscreen` and `screen_match_apply` are unchanged.
- **Found on:** [PR #665](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/665), the NX2Apple launcher's console mode (LNPANDY/NX2Apple), tested by its reporter with [Hades II](../../dm/games/0100A00019DE0000/README.md).
- **Check:** the reporter confirmed that the deferred request entered fullscreen on its first attempt and stayed there, without a refusal. A harness of the startup callbacks checks both readiness orders, bounded refusal recovery, and cancellation before startup or a queued retry, plus later manual failures after successful startup.
- **Not checked:** the revised lifecycle handling in a real game through the launcher's console mode; native refusal recovery (the reporter's deferred request succeeded without it).

### Frame latency 2 for the display link in fullscreen

- **Symptom:** on a ProMotion built-in screen, only in fullscreen, the frame step went 1 → 2 → 3 vsyncs on frames costing 0.1–0.6 vsyncs (`3 late frames; cost 0.32 vsyncs`) and the game presented 15 frames a second; a window, or the screen set to a fixed 60 Hz, did not do it. The `DM_NVN_TSV` rows show presents shown one 120 Hz refresh late (`@pres_at_ms` − `@link_target_ms` = 8.3 ms) with a skipped tick after each (`@link_gap_ms` 33 ms), and `@link_lead_ms` about 16 ms against about 50 ms in a window.
- **Cause:** in fullscreen the system shows the layer directly, and at frame latency 1 the display link calls back only one 60 Hz period before the target display time. There it shows some presents one refresh late and skips the link's next tick, though the callbacks run on time (16.2–16.6 ms ahead), the present takes about 0.1 ms and the misses do not follow GPU load (the late presents had less GPU work in flight than the on-time ones). The frames that wait for the skipped tick start late, and the frame step reads three of them in a row as load. Why the system misses at 16 ms of lead is **open**.
- **Fix (a workaround):** `link_latency` (`nvn_mtl.m`) asks for frame latency 2 only while the window is fullscreen on a variable-refresh screen (`front_mac.m` `front_fullscreen` and `front_screen_variable_refresh`), and 1 in a window or on a fixed-rate screen. `screen_refresh` classifies the current screen mode by its positive `minimumRefreshInterval` being shorter than `maximumRefreshInterval`, rather than its maximum Hz or model; it updates at startup, on screen and display-parameter changes, and on fullscreen transitions. `link_sync` applies the current policy to an existing link too. Latency 2 gives the system 33 ms, for one frame more input lag in variable-refresh fullscreen only. `DM_PRESENT_LATENCY=1` or `2` overrides the policy for every screen and window mode; larger positive values use 2. iOS keeps latency 1. The fixed-rate policy correction ([issue #822](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/822)) narrows this workaround; it does not resolve the separate [fixed-rate display-link delay](#fixed-rate-display-link-delay-remains-at-latency-1).
- **Found on:** issue #347 (and #348, Mario Kart 8 Deluxe on an M4, closed into it); [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md) on an M4 Pro: in fullscreen at latency 1, 68 to 1,640 of about 9,600 presents a refresh late per 180 s run, and in one run the 1 → 2 → 3 steps and 15 presents a second twice; at latency 2, 17 and 18 in two runs, no cheap step-ups. The reporter's M5 Max: 49 skipped ticks in 2,306 frames at latency 1, 4 in 5,328 at latency 2.
- **Note:** a 120 Hz link instead (`DM_PRESENT_LINK=2`, a frame every other tick, latency 1: 16.6 ms of lead) kept the frame step at its cost in three runs, but the system still showed 102–209 of about 9,600 presents one refresh late, against 17–19 at latency 2; so a steady panel rate does not remove the misses.
- **Check (fixed-rate policy correction, #822):** the full graphics suite passed, including `t_present_rescue --metal` (64 checks) with synthetic fixed/variable screen transitions and both explicit overrides; an isolated run of the production screen classifier passed fixed 60/120 Hz, variable refresh, mode-change and unknown-screen cases. The iOS compile check passed all 185 sources, C/Objective-C lint had no new findings, and the documentation tests passed. Regression risk was judged **moderate** because the policy uses shared Mac presentation state. The three selected fullscreen comparisons were Tears of the Kingdom (the original ProMotion workaround), Breath of the Wild (another affected frame-step profile), and Pokémon Scarlet (a different engine on the same presentation path); all three were named by `regress.py impact`. The user stopped the regression during shader/host preparation, before gameplay comparisons, so no game regression result is claimed.
- **Not checked:** Mario Kart 8 Deluxe on the M4 with latency 2; physical fixed-rate displays; moving between screens or changing refresh mode while a game runs (only synthetic state transitions in `t_present_rescue`); the new policy's fullscreen gameplay comparison.
- **Later:** on an M5 Pro with macOS 27.2 the 60 Hz link at latency 2 still showed every present a refresh late, and the game ran at 40 fps. A variable-refresh screen in fullscreen now goes to a fixed-rate mode where it has one, so this latency-2 policy holds only for one that keeps its variable mode: [The 60 Hz link at 40 fps in fullscreen on a ProMotion screen](#the-60-hz-link-at-40-fps-in-fullscreen-on-a-promotion-screen).

### The 60 Hz link at 40 fps in fullscreen on a ProMotion screen

- **Symptom:** in fullscreen on a MacBook Pro's ProMotion screen, a 60 fps game held 40 fps with its GPU at 10 ms a frame: frame times alternated 16.7 and 33.3 ms, and each `on-screen` line counted half its frames as held too long (`dropped 150` of 300, `discarded 0`). The same route in a window held 60.0 fps, and the title screen showed the same 40 fps in fullscreen.
- **Cause:** with the 60 Hz link at frame latency 2 ([Frame latency 2 for the display link in fullscreen](#frame-latency-2-for-the-display-link-in-fullscreen)), the system showed every present one 120 Hz refresh after the link's target (`@pres_at_ms` − `@link_target_ms` = 8.33 ms on every frame of the `DM_NVN_TSV` rows), and the link skipped its next tick after every second present: `@link_gap_ms` ran 16.7, 33.3, 16.7, 33.3 ms, 40 callbacks a second, each `display link up to tick` line with `empty ticks 0`. The host paces the game by the link's ticks, so the game ran at 40 fps. In a window the link called back 49.9 ms ahead (33.3 in fullscreen) and every present landed on its target. At frame latency 1 (`DM_PRESENT_LATENCY=1`) the misses of issue #347 came back and the frame step went to 4 vsyncs, 15 fps. The 120 Hz link (`DM_PRESENT_LINK=2`, a frame every other tick) at latency 2 showed the presents on their targets. Why the system misses a 60 Hz link's targets in variable-refresh fullscreen is **open**.
- **Fix (a workaround for the system's misses, whose cause is open):** in fullscreen, the Mac front end switches a variable-refresh screen to a fixed-rate mode of the same size at a multiple of the game's rate, for this app only, though the screen's own rate is a multiple (`front_mac.m` `screen_match_apply`, the mechanism of [Fullscreen switches the screen to a rate the frame rate divides](#fullscreen-switches-the-screen-to-a-rate-the-frame-rate-divides)): a ProMotion screen goes from 120 Hz to its fixed 60 Hz mode at 60 and 30 fps. The variable mode is told from the fixed ones by the screen's current state (`front_screen_variable_refresh`), and the fixed candidates leave out the current mode and any mode at its rate. The screen is then fixed-rate, so the 60 Hz link runs at latency 1, as on any fixed-rate screen; a 30 fps target takes the 60 Hz link with interval 2 ([The 30 Hz display link in fullscreen on a fixed-rate screen](#the-30-hz-display-link-in-fullscreen-on-a-fixed-rate-screen)). Leaving fullscreen or the screen, or quitting, gives the screen its own mode back, and the host takes its rate and variable refresh back at once rather than waiting for the screen observer. `link_sync` asks the screen for the game's rate (60), not the link's.
- **Fallback:** a variable-refresh screen with no such mode, or with `DM_SCREEN_MATCH=0`, keeps its mode, and `link_vrr_120` (`nvn_mtl.m`) runs the 120 Hz link of `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2` in place of the 60 Hz one at 60 fps when the screen's rate is a multiple of 120 Hz. The acquire waits two of its ticks per vsync (`mtl_link_wait_tick`), the vsync event follows every other tick, and a frame that misses its tick goes up on the next 120 Hz refresh instead of a whole 60 Hz period later. Frame latency stays 2. A window, a fixed-rate screen, a screen of another rate, a 30 fps target (the 30 Hz link) and a set `DM_PRESENT_LINK` keep their link; `DM_PRESENT_LINK=1` keeps the 60 Hz link in fullscreen. The link follows fullscreen in and out, as the latency policy does, and logs `display link: 120 Hz in fullscreen on a variable-refresh screen`.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (Switch 2, v131072) on an M5 Pro MacBook Pro (16-inch, 20-core GPU, ProMotion), macOS 27.2, the `kokiri-deck` route with `scripts/bench.py`, `host=` variants of `main` and the 120 Hz link on the same libraries and pipeline archive, two runs each: in fullscreen 39.7 → 59.2 fps (58.8–59.6), frame p50 33.19 → 16.66 ms, p99 33.53 → 25.27 ms, frames over 1.5 vsyncs 914 → 62 of about 2,680, GPU p50 10.2 / 10.0 ms; in a window 59.9 / 59.95 fps, unchanged (no `120 Hz` line). At the title screen in fullscreen before the change: 40.5 fps on the 60 Hz link at latency 2, 15 fps at latency 1, 59.3 fps with `DM_PRESENT_LINK=0` (frame p99 25.2 ms) and 59.7 with `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2` (p99 19.2 ms). On the 120 Hz link the system still shows each present one refresh after its target, harmlessly, and about 2% of its callbacks come two refreshes apart, so about 2% of frames stay up 25 ms. At frame latency 1 the 120 Hz link did a little worse (59.3 fps, frame p99 29.2 ms, 48 frames over 1.5 vsyncs, against 59.55 fps, 25.2 ms and 35 at latency 2, two runs each), so frame latency stays 2.
- **Check:** `t_present_rescue` (`vrr120`): a window keeps the 60 Hz link; fullscreen on a 120 Hz variable-refresh screen takes the 120 Hz link at latency 2 with the requested mode left at 1 and asks the screen for 60; a fixed-rate screen, a 144 Hz screen, a screen of unknown rate, `DM_PRESENT_LINK=1` and a 30 fps target (asking for 30) keep theirs; 240 Hz takes it; leaving fullscreen goes back to the 60 Hz link; `DM_PRESENT_LINK=2` asks for 120. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_present_rescue` (with `--metal` and the Metal debug layer): 101 checks, 0 failed. The Mac host builds. The fixed-mode switch has not run on a variable-refresh screen: the only screen here is a fixed 120 Hz external one (equal minimum and maximum refresh intervals), where nothing changes.
- **Measured for the fixed mode:** with the screen held at its fixed 60 Hz mode (`CGConfigureDisplayWithDisplayMode` for one app, before this change), the same route in fullscreen held 60.0 fps, frame p99 16.9 ms, 2 frames over 1.5 vsyncs in two runs, on the 60 Hz link at latency 1 ("fullscreen, fixed refresh"), as in a window; against the 120 Hz link's 59.2 fps, p99 25.3 ms and 62. The GPU took 14.0 ms there against 10.0, at the lower clock the system chose. Issue #347's reporters also saw no misses with the screen set to a fixed 60 Hz. The cost: the switch changes the screen's mode while the game is fullscreen, for this app only, as on 144 Hz screens.
- **Not checked:** the host's own switch on a ProMotion screen (the measurement above set the mode by hand), and its log lines; external variable-refresh screens, whose fixed modes may not be listed apart from the variable one (then the 120 Hz link paces them); other ProMotion Macs and macOS versions; input latency; iOS, where nothing changes (`front_fullscreen` is 0 there).

### The fallback pacer slept a lead the display link built up

- **Symptom:** the game freezes for 0.5 s to half a minute, then carries on, at a moment the display link goes silent (a long frame, a screenshot, the window covered); `DM_NVN_TSV` shows `@wait_ms` growing frame by frame to seconds before it.
- **Cause:** the present pacer (`nvn_pace.h`) is a clock only while the acquire waits for the slot time it gives a texture (`avail`). With the display link on, the acquire waits for the link's ticks instead, and the pacer is only the fallback for a silent link (the snapshot branch) or a link wait that timed out. Each present still stepped the pacer by one period. Whenever presents came faster than the period, the slot ran ahead of real time with no bound, and the fallback slept the whole lead in one go. Any link whose ticks average faster than the present period builds it up; the 120 Hz link on a variable-refresh screen ([above](#the-60-hz-link-at-40-fps-in-fullscreen-on-a-promotion-screen)) did.
- **Fix:** the acquire records on the window whether the pacer paced the frame (`win_t.follow`). For a frame that something else paced (a link wait that returned, or present interval 0), present restarts the beat at the present (`nvn_pace_follow`). The fallback's first wait is then at most one period from the last present, and the pacer steps from there. A caller that waits keeps `nvn_pace` as it was, including a lead of several periods when its swap chain queues frames ahead. A cap on the lead (one period ahead of now, proposed in [PR #2590](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2590)) broke that: with three or four textures and 1 ms of work a waiting caller presented at 87 and 116 fps.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) v131072 on an LG TV, fullscreen, the 120 Hz variable-refresh link, M5 Max, macOS 27 (the PR's author): the slot ran up to 27.6 s ahead over 18,000 frames of 12.5, 16.7 and 20.9 ms, and the game froze 29 times for 0.5 to 27.6 s, 120 s of a 55-minute session.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_pace` replays the acquire and present cycle on the header: a waiting caller at 60.0 fps with two, three and four textures; 18,000 link-paced frames 12.5 ms apart, then a silent link, with the first wait within one period (75 s when the link's frames step the beat) and 16.667 ms a frame after it; and the link pacing, going silent and coming back three times, every wait within one period.
- **Not checked:** the freeze on a variable-refresh screen (upstream had none, NX 18 has none); the fix rests on the replay. The iPhone's display link takes the same acquire branches, so a ProMotion iPhone should follow it too; not yet run on one.

### An early display-link wake logs zero lateness

- **Symptom:** `DM_NVN_TSV` reports an `@late_ms` value close to 18.4 trillion ms for an acquire that woke before its texture's slot.
- **Cause:** `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` subtracted the slot time from the wake time as unsigned nanoseconds. A display-link tick can wake the acquire before that slot.
- **Fix:** measure the delay only when the wake is later than the slot. An early wake writes zero.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#found-on-this-game) on the Mac (upstream); ported from upstream (`3984f5aa3`, [PR #1041](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1041)).
- **Check:** `t_link_ticks` drives the real acquire with synthetic display-link ticks and checks the TSV. A wake 1 ms early writes `0.000` and a wake 1.25 ms late writes `1.250`.

### Fixed-rate display-link delay remains at latency 1

- **Symptom:** on a MacBook Air M2's fixed 60 Hz built-in display, the reporter measured about 50 ms from the game's present call to on-screen presentation with the display link, at both requested frame latencies 1 and 2, versus 29 ms with `DM_PRESENT_LINK=0`. After loading, all three runs held 60 fps with 0.3% of on-screen intervals outside 16.7 ± 2 ms and no frame-step changes.
- **Cause:** **open**. The reported callback lead was 32.8 ms at both latency settings. Applying the ProMotion latency-2 workaround to fixed-rate fullscreen was an overly broad policy, but requesting latency 1 did not remove this delay in the reporter's measurements. No local fixed-rate timing reproduction or raw trace is available.
- **Fix:** **open**. Fixed-rate screens now request latency 1 by default, as described [above](#frame-latency-2-for-the-display-link-in-fullscreen); this only corrects the workaround's scope. The display link remains enabled, and the remaining presentation delay needs its own diagnosis.
- **Found on:** [issue #822](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/822), Lollipop Chainsaw RePOP (`0100DD301A686000`, [PR #684](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/684)'s profile), macOS 27, `--platform mac-app`.
- **Note:** the same 33 ms lead is the steady state on an iPhone too, where the system can also step to it in the middle of a run: [On iOS the display link's lead steps from one refresh to two after a GPU spike and stays there](#on-ios-the-display-links-lead-steps-from-one-refresh-to-two-after-a-gpu-spike-and-stays-there).

### On iOS the display link's lead steps from one refresh to two after a GPU spike and stays there

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, a 60 fps game's `display link up to tick` lines give `submission ahead of target display` p50 about 15.6 ms for the first 40 s or so, then about 32.5 ms for the rest of the run, and game enqueue to target display rises by the same refresh (from about 41 ms to about 57 ms, both with a [standing frame](#a-frame-that-stands-in-the-display-links-queue) in the queue; about 46 ms in the later windows without one). Pacing does not change (frame time p50 16.67 ms, on-screen p99 16.67 ms in steady windows) and there is no red line; the only `display link: frame latency` line is the one at the start, `display link: frame latency 1 (window)`.
- **Cause:** the system's, not the host's. `CAMetalDisplayLink` began calling back one refresh earlier, after the game's frames missed their deadlines in a GPU spike, and never went back, although every present was on time for the next 275 s. The host asks for frame latency 1 on iOS and does not change it ([Frame latency 2 for the display link in fullscreen](#frame-latency-2-for-the-display-link-in-fullscreen): `link_latency`, `link_sync`), so the system moved the lead on its own. About 33 ms of lead is also what this phone shows from the start in Persona 5 Royal, Super Mario Odyssey and Ocarina of Time ([A frame that stands in the display link's queue](#a-frame-that-stands-in-the-display-links-queue)), and what the Mac shows in [issue #822](#fixed-rate-display-link-delay-remains-at-latency-1) at frame latency 1 and 2 alike: the short lead at the start of this run is the exception, and the step looks like the system protecting smoothness after a missed present. The cost is input latency only, one refresh (about 17 ms).
- **Fix:** none in the host, and none planned on this evidence. Asking for frame latency 2 and back would probably do nothing (#822 measured the same 32.8 ms lead at both), and forcing the link back to a one-refresh lead would invite the late presents and hitches that a 16 ms lead brought on the Mac (issue #347, [Frame latency 2 for the display link in fullscreen](#frame-latency-2-for-the-display-link-in-fullscreen)). What a player or a test can use today, with no code: `DM_PRESENT_LINK=0` in `Documents/env.txt` bypasses the display link, so there is no display-link lead (the drawable's own present queue still has the system's latency; the present thread takes the drawables itself; on the Mac #822 measured 29 ms from present to screen against 50 ms with the link); and the separate standing frame in the host's own queue, which adds another refresh on top in some windows, is what `DM_PRESENT_TRIM` handles ([A frame that stands in the display link's queue](#a-frame-that-stands-in-the-display-links-queue)), on by default on the iPhone and iPad and set in 13 Sentinels' profile (`present_trim 60`). The two are independent: a trim cannot shorten the system's lead, and bypassing the link does not by itself change how the game lands its frames.
- **Note:** a layer over the game's (the touch controls) holds the lead at two refreshes from the start: [Touch controls over the game's layer cost a refresh of latency](#touch-controls-over-the-games-layer-cost-a-refresh-of-latency). Whether this run's step had anything to do with the controls (a controller, the Tap layout or the menu button) is not known.
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (2026-11-11, a 330 s run at 60 fps with MetalFX upscaling and edge smoothing): `submission ahead of target display` 15.6 → 32.5 ms at about tick 2,450 (somewhere in presents 2,251-2,401), right after the game's frames 2,365-2,368 took 52 and 46 ms of GPU and the present command buffer up to 47.9 ms; it stayed at 32.5 ms for the remaining 275 s. A second spike of the same size (frames 16,224-16,227, GPU 46 ms, present command buffer 48.6 ms) neither raised nor reset it. That build predates the iOS trim default and 13 Sentinels' `present_trim 60`, so its standing-frame windows would now lose that extra refresh; the system's step is something the trim cannot change.
- **Not checked:** the exact frame where the lead steps (`DM_NVN_TSV` with `DM_NVN_TSV_CALLS=0` gives `@link_lead_ms` and `@link_target_ms` per frame), and whether the real time on screen follows the target (`@pres_at_ms` − `@link_target_ms`); `DM_PRESENT_LINK=0` on an iPhone (latency, on-screen p99 and drops against the link); whether a lighter present path (Upscaling and Anti-aliasing off) keeps the lead at one refresh through the same spike; whether pausing the game (Home and back) resets the lead. Any code that resets the lead would need a device run showing it restores about 16 ms without late presents first.

### Touch controls over the game's layer cost a refresh of latency

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, while the touch controls show, every `display link up to tick` line gives `submission ahead of target display` p50 about 33 ms and `game enqueue to target display` about 45-49 ms, against about 16 ms and 28-30 ms while a controller is in use; Ocarina of Time's log went from 33 to 16 ms in the window a GameSir controller connected (which hides the controls). Pacing is the same either way (frame time p50 16.67 ms) and there is no red line.
- **Cause:** the system's compositing. The touch controls were a full-screen `CALayer` of one `CAShapeLayer` and `CATextLayer` per key over the game's `CAMetalLayer` (`DmGamepad.m`, hidden only while a controller is in use). With a visible layer over it, iOS composites the game's layer with the controls instead of showing its drawables directly, and `CAMetalDisplayLink` targets one refresh later. Inferred from the logs, not from a system trace: the Vitals frame-rate label, a small view present in both cases, did not keep the lead at 33 ms, and the controller window shows the lead follows the controls' layer alone.
- **Fix:** the live present draws the controls into the game's frame (`touch_draw.h`; `DmGamepad.m` `touchDraw:`, `touchAtlas`, `touchPublish`; `nvn_mtl.m` `touch_draw`; `present.metal` `vs_touch`, `fs_touch`), so nothing visible sits over the game's layer while they show. The gamepad view stays and takes every touch as before (hit tests, the game's touch screen, the sticks, the double tap); its control layers stay hidden, as with a controller. Its pictures come from the layers themselves: each control rendered by Core Animation's `renderInContext`, normal and pressed, at the drawable's pixels a point, into one atlas (grey and alpha, `RG8Unorm`, 4.4 MB on an iPhone 18 Pro Max in landscape at Large size; BGRA if a control ever has a colour), with the layer's shadow drawn by Core Graphics from its `shadowPath` (a blur of 2.2 times `shadowRadius`), as Core Animation draws it under a translucent fill. The atlas is made again only when a path, the opacity, the layout or the scale changes (`touchLook`: a rotation, a size or opacity pick, 2D / 3D, a pose change), in 10-30 ms on the main thread. Each press, release and stick move hands the present thread a frame of up to 32 quads (where each control goes on the canvas and which look it has) through a sequence lock (`touch_publish`, `touch_read`): no lock, no wait on either side. The present draws them after the picture, in the same render pass, as premultiplied alpha over (`host_msl_rps_over`), one instanced draw. The game view is the whole screen while they show (`DmLayout.h` `dm_touch_canvas`: the view's bounds, or with Fill the picture's rectangle that covers them, on whole pixels), so the drawable holds every control, and the picture keeps its rectangle on it (`dm_touch_picture`, the frame's `pic`; the present clears the rest to black, as the root view's background was). The drawable then keeps the screen's pixels (no `picturePixels`), so the pictures land 1:1. A frame laid out for another drawable size draws no control and keeps the present's own fit (a drawable made just before or after a layout change); replaced atlases leave the residency set once the present that took the new one has completed. The layers hand over to the frame, never leaving the screen without the controls: they hide only once a live present that drew the controls from a frame published since the last change has completed on the GPU (`mtl_present_touch_ack`, `front_touch_drawn`, `touchDrawn:`), and show again at each change of the canvas (a rotation, a picture mode, the controls coming back from Tap or a controller), where the gamepad first hands over a frame that only places the picture (`TOUCH_PLACE`: the pictures and their places are the old canvas's) until its layout has made them for the new one; while the game presents nothing for 100 ms when a press or stick move comes (a load, a hitch, a program hand-off: `mtl_present_touch_idle_ns`), so the press shows as before, the layers over the last frame, which still holds the controls as they were (a resting knob shows twice until the next present); and when 30 live presents in a row had a drawable the frame is not laid out for (`touch_unfit_step`, `front_touch_unfit`, `present: the touch controls' frame is for a …`), which also lays the root out again (the root also lays out at a display scale change, an iPad window moved to a screen of another scale). When the controls stop being drawn, the frame turns off but keeps the canvas, so the drawables made before the game view left it still place the picture there; the first live present with a drawable of another size ends that (`touch_frame_places`), and half a second later no frame is published at all, so a later layout whose drawable happens to have the old canvas's pixels (Picture Stretch after Fit with `DM_DRAWABLE_NATIVE=1`) takes the present's own fit. The atlas is let go then too (`mtl_present_touch_release`: out of the residency set once the presents that sampled it completed; `present: the touch controls are no longer drawn into the frame: their atlas is let go`), and made again when the controls come back. Only the live present draws them: screenshots and captures show the game's picture alone. `front end: touch controls drawn into the game's frame by the present (…)` or `… drawn on their layers over the game's layer (why)` or `… hidden (…)` is logged at the start and at each change, and `present: the touch controls are drawn into the frame (…)` at the first present that drew them. `DM_TOUCH_DRAW=layer` in `Documents/env.txt` draws them on their layers as before, for an A/B.
- **Where the layers stay:** a TV (the picture is on the TV and the phone is all pad), until the game's first picture (the controls show over the black screen before it), while an offline HTML page is shown (the page is a view over the game view and under the pad: drawn into the frame, the controls would be under it; `front_web_shown` lays the view out at each change), and for the rest of the run if the present cannot make its pipelines or the atlas's texture (`front_touch_draw_failed`, a red line). The Tap layout and a controller draw no control at all, and the game view goes back to the picture's rectangle there, as before. Picture modes: Fit (the canvas is the screen, the bars cleared black by the present), Fill (the canvas is the picture's rectangle, larger than the screen and cropped by the root view as before), Stretch and Ultrawide (the screen), a portrait split and an iPhone Duo half open (the screen, the picture above the controls), an iPad in any of these (the window). Over the game's layer during play there remain only the menu button (`DmMenuButton`, shown 5 s at a time, and while VoiceOver or Switch Control runs or an offline page is shown), the in-game menu while open, the Vitals frame-rate label when on, and the transient views: the progress screen of a first start, the controller prompt, an offline HTML page (with the controls on their layers over it), the program hand-off notice and Apple's Metal HUD (`DM_METAL_HUD`); and the control layers themselves for the moments above (a change of canvas until the next drawn present, a stall, a drawable that does not fit).
- **Cost:** the drawable is the screen's size (Fill: the picture's rectangle, larger than the screen) while the controls show, at the screen's pixels, where before it had the picture's render size wherever MetalFX did not run ([The iPhone's drawable at the picture's render size](rendering.md#the-iphones-drawable-at-the-pictures-render-size)). On an iPhone 18 Pro Max (2868x1320 pixels), three drawables, BGRA8, with Upscaling Standard (the default): a game rendering 1280x720 (handheld mode) in landscape Fit or Stretch goes from 1280x720 (3.7 MB each) to 2868x1320 (15.1 MB), about +34 MB; at 1920x1080 (docked) from 8.3 MB, about +20 MB; in Fill from 1280x720 or 1920x1080 to 2868x1614 (18.5 MB), about +44 MB or +31 MB, the part outside the screen drawn and cropped by the root view; in a portrait split from 1280x720 to 1320x2868 (15.1 MB), about +34 MB, the rest cleared black (which the lossless compression of render-target drawables stores cheaply). With MetalFX at the screen's shape the drawable already had the screen's pixels in its height: Fit goes from 2346x1320 (12.4 MB) to 15.1 MB, about +8 MB. The present pass now scales the picture bilinearly into the screen's pixels and clears the bars every frame, where with Standard it drew the picture 1:1 and Core Animation scaled the layer, and it adds one draw of up to 18 quads over the controls' pixels. The atlas is 4.4 MB in landscape at Large size (grey and alpha), only while the controls are drawn. A composited frame also wrote a full-screen frame in the render server and read the game's drawable again, which a frame shown directly does not. Not done: a Fill canvas of the screen's size, the picture's overflow cropped in the present's source rectangle (about 3 MB less per drawable, and a layer no larger than the screen); whether a layer larger than the screen and cropped by its superview is still shown directly is **open** (the `display link` lines of a Fill run tell). Press feedback is drawn in the next presented frame: at 60 fps a refresh as before (and one refresh sooner on screen), at 30 fps every other refresh; while the game presents nothing for 100 ms the press shows on the layers instead, as above.
- **Check:** `dm/host/tests/t_touch_draw.c` (the sequence lock under two threads, also clean under ThreadSanitizer; each frame's sequence and its wrap; the canvas test; an off frame placing nothing once a drawable of another size came, a placing frame kept; the unfit run reported once; the picture's pixels; the atlas's shelves; the grey atlas); `dm/gfx/tests/t_present_touch.m` (on the real Metal 4 queue: the picture on the canvas with black around it, a control premultiplied over a bar and over the picture, nothing for another drawable size, a frame that is off or another atlas, a grey and alpha atlas, a replaced or released atlas kept until its present completed, an off frame after a drawable of another size taking the present's own fit, a placing frame, the frame each present drew from, the unfit run, nothing in a screenshot); the Duo harness's `DuoTouchDrawTests` in the Simulator: the canvas in Fit, Fill, Stretch, portrait, a Duo's tabletop split, an iPad, a screen of a scale that is not whole; the production root after the first picture in Fit, Fill and Stretch (the game view is the canvas, the drawable's pixels, the frame for it, a press and its release, Tap and a controller taking the layers' path back and letting the atlas go); the layers showing until a present drew the controls for the canvas, a rotation placing only the picture until the pad's layout, the off frame cleared half a second on, a press during a stall on the layers until the next drawn present, the unfit answer, and an offline page keeping the controls on their layers; and the composited frames against the same controls drawn on their layers by the render server (`drawViewHierarchyInRect`) over light grey, at rest, with B pressed and with the stick held, in Fit, Fill and Stretch, a portrait split, a Duo's tabletop split and a 4:3 overlay: a mean difference of 0.03-0.11 of 255 per channel, no pixel more than 27 off (antialiasing at a few edges).
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (the controller window), [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md) and [NieR:Automata](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md) (touch runs: ahead p50 32.9 ms, enqueue 47 and 49 ms), on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (2026-11).
- **Not checked:** on a device: that the lead comes back to one refresh with the controls drawn into the frame (the `display link` lines of a touch run against a `DM_TOUCH_DRAW=layer` run), the power the render server no longer spends compositing, and the controls' look on the phone's screen (the harness compares them with the render server in the Simulator).

### The 30 Hz display link in fullscreen on a fixed-rate screen

- **Symptom:** at a 30 fps target, in fullscreen on a fixed 60 Hz screen, frames stay on screen 66.7 ms, often many in a row; in a window a late frame stays 50 ms. The `display link up to tick` lines count 300 ticks per 300 presents, but over 12-18 s instead of 10.
- **Cause:** at a 30 fps target `link_sync` runs the display link at 30 Hz, in a window and in fullscreen alike: on a 60 Hz screen `link_range` takes 30 Hz with no virtual ticks, and `front_screen_match` changes nothing. In fullscreen, at frame latency 1, the system calls back 32.8 ms before the target display time, one tick of that link (about 50 ms in a window). There the link skipped ticks: over two walks it called back 16.5-25.4 times a second in 8 of the 30 windows of 300 presents; in one run also in 48 of the first 58 frames, with 0.3-1.2 ms of GPU each, and in 222 frames in a row with a GPU span of 24-40 ms and no wait for a sync, a texture or the present queue. A skipped tick keeps the frame on screen a whole 33.3 ms more, and the game, paced by the ticks (`mtl_link_wait_tick`), presents 66.7 ms apart too. In a window the same link called back 30.0 times a second. Why the system skips ticks there is **open**; it resembles the late presents of [frame latency 2](#frame-latency-2-for-the-display-link-in-fullscreen).
- **Fix:** `link_30hz` (`nvn_mtl.m`): in fullscreen on a fixed-rate screen (`front_fullscreen`, not `front_screen_variable_refresh`) the link stays at 60 Hz at a 30 fps target. The present interval paces the 30, as it does interval-2 frames on the 60 Hz link: the acquire waits two ticks and the callback keeps each frame on screen for its period (`g_link_hold_until`). A frame that misses its refresh shows 16.7 ms later. Windows, variable-refresh fullscreen and iOS keep the 30 Hz link.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on a MacBook Air M4 (60 Hz built-in screen, macOS 27.2) at `DM_FPS_TARGET=30`, with LeonardoBai12's frame-delta change, which gives `main` the frame-step state page that applies the cap. On its walk in fullscreen (180 s of scripted stick pushes outside Link's house): 24.2 and 27.7 fps, frame p99 71.5 and 69.1 ms, 996 and 388 frames over 50 ms in two runs before; 29.7 fps, p99 38.4 ms and 7 over 50 ms after, with the link at 57-60 callbacks a second, against 29.7 fps, p99 38.4 ms and 3 over 50 ms in a window. The median time from the game's present to the target display time went from 91 to 76 ms (109 in a window).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_present_rescue` (`thirty`: the link's mode and range at 30 fps in a window, in fixed-rate and variable-refresh fullscreen, and back).
- **Not checked:** variable-refresh fullscreen at 30 fps, external fixed-rate screens, other games at a 30 fps target, and the power cost of 60 callbacks a second instead of 30.

### An acquired image comes back once the present after it has run

- **Symptom:** whole frames of one flat colour (blue, purple, green, or near-black) with the HUD or subtitles drawn over them, horizontal or vertical streaks, and a correct frame only now and then, worse where the GPU frame is long. `DM_NVN_MAGSCAN` finds NaN in the scene targets and the 1x1 exposure texture; with `DM_NVN_PRESENT_FINISH=1` there is none.
- **Cause:** a game that paces its CPU-written per-frame data by the acquire sync alone. Horizon gives an image back once the compositor has taken the next present in its place, so the GPU has finished the frame after the image's last one. The host's acquire sync was `mtl_last_fence()` at the acquire (fenced 2): what the replay had committed by then, which trails what the game has submitted while the queue worker is behind (a two-image game then overwrites constants a frame still being drawn reads), and over-waits with three images once the worker has caught up. Xenoblade Chronicles: Definitive Edition – Nintendo Switch 2 Edition has two images, waits only on the acquire sync and keeps its constants in two slots that alternate by frame: it wrote frame N + 1's into the slot frame N - 1 was still drawing with, its 1x1 exposure went NaN, and the tone mapping multiplied the whole picture by it (upstream's diagnosis).
- **Fix:** general, `hle_nvn.c` and `nvn_queue.inc` (from upstream [PR #3372](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/3372), `1999cbd1f`). `win_t` keeps, beside each image's last present ticket (`ticket[4]`), the ticket of the first present after it (`next_ticket[4]`, set by that present through `last_pres`, under `g_win_tk_mu`). `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` takes both: it still waits on the CPU for the image's own last present as before, and the sync becomes that next present's end (`nvn_sync_set_present`): its GPU fence once its job has replayed (fenced 2, still an acquire sync in the wait ledger), else the receipt itself (fenced 3), so `nvnSyncWait` and `nvnQueueWaitSync` wait for its replay first. With two images that is frame N - 1; with three, frame N - 2. An image with no next present yet (the first round, an image newly made active) keeps the old fence. `DM_NVN_ACQ_NEXT=0` restores the old sync, and a player build keeps it for the phone A/B. NX 18 has no deferred acquire (upstream's `acq_wait_t`, fenced 4), so upstream's changes to it are not ported; nvnQueueAcquireTexture (no sync) is unchanged.
- **Note:** a two-image game that waits only for the frame before loses CPU-GPU overlap it never had on the console: upstream measured Xenoblade DE's intro from 17–18 ms (with the race) to about 21 ms a frame on an M2 Pro.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` section 6a (upstream's cases): two-image CPU `nvnSyncWait` (zero and finite timeouts expire while the next present is held), a second queue's `nvnQueueWaitSync` parked until it replays, a three-image swapchain (image 0 passes while present 2 is held, image 1 waits for it), and `nvnWindowSetNumActiveTextures` 3 → 2 and 2 → 3; 10 of them fail with `DM_NVN_ACQ_NEXT=0`. `t_link_ticks`, `t_present_snap`, `t_acq_pace`, `t_present_rescue`, `t_gfx_suspend`, `t_fstep` and `t_nvn_framewg` pass.
- **Found on:** upstream: Xenoblade Chronicles: Definitive Edition – Nintendo Switch 2 Edition (`0400FF500E34A001`, 35 of 46 sampled intro frames flat before, none after) and [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md) ([#3296](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/3296), M4 Mac, Colony 9). Not yet run on a game here or on the phone.

## CPU cost and pacing

### Arena buffer ownership on allocation

- **Symptom:** autorelease work takes a measurable share of replay time, with temporary arena-buffer ownership traffic a few times per draw.
- **Cause:** under ARC an unqualified `id<MTLBuffer> *` out-parameter is `__autoreleasing`, so assigning the arena buffer in `arena_alloc` autoreleases it. Reading `buf.length` also sends an Objective-C message on each allocation and while selecting or trimming arenas.
- **Fix:** both declarations of `arena_alloc` use a `__strong` buffer out-parameter, matching every caller's strong local. `NvArena` caches its buffer length when created; allocation, reuse selection and idle trimming use that value. Buffer ownership and fence-based reuse remain the same.
- **Found on:** MacNativeGames commit `0531f28`, ported in [PR #195](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/195), reports autorelease work falling from 2.63% to 1.73% of replay time; the PR does not identify the game for that measurement. It has not been repeated here.
- **Check:** `t_ub`, `t_vbuf` and `t_geometry` exercise arena-backed uniform shadows, vertex conversion and geometry scratch on the Mac with Metal validation.

### Reference counting on the replay for objects other tables already hold

- **Symptom:** in a Time Profiler trace of the replay (submit) thread, `objc_retain`, `objc_release`, `objc_retainAutoreleaseReturnValue` and `objc_claimAutoreleasedReturnValue` / `objc_unsafeClaimAutoreleasedReturnValue` are spread over `prog_get`, `pso_get`, `tex_by_id`, `arena_take` and their callers in `draw_` and `encode_args_`.
- **Cause:** ARC ignores `__unsafe_unretained` on a return type (`-Wignored-qualifiers`), so `prog_get`, `pso_get` and `pso_get_` still retained what they returned and handed it back through the autorelease-return handshake, at every call; `tex_by_id` returned its view the same way at every binding that missed the fast test of `tex_fast` / `tex_fast_rid`, and `pso_get_` asked the `DM_NVN_MAGSCAN` diagnostic for an override (an object return) at every call, on or off. `arena_take`'s `__strong` out-parameter ([Arena buffer ownership on allocation](#arena-buffer-ownership-on-allocation)) retained the arena block into a strong local at every argument block, several a draw, and released it at the end of the scope.
