- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game; ported from S2Arm `89ab9704`, `cd9e2826` (Apache-2.0), after NXNative `e82aedb8d`, `7c7c45479`. `prog_get_ptr`, `pso_get_ptr` / `pso_get_ptr_` and `tex_by_id_ptr` return a pointer at +0, which the old names, now macros, bridge, so call sites do not change; `arena_take_u` / `arena_alloc_u` lend the block to `encode_args_`'s blocks, `drv_bank`, `cbsize_table`, `ubd_shadow_`, `ubp_get` and `pull_bind` (`arena_take` / `arena_alloc` stay, as retaining wrappers, for every other caller); `ms_fs_override` is called only while `DM_NVN_MAGSCAN` is on; `draw_`'s geometry program is borrowed. Each holder lets go only behind the fence of the submission being encoded: the serial table replaces or drops a program through `defer_obj_release` / `defer_obj_unref` at `g_fence + 1` (a program without a serial is kept by `g_prog_noserial`, its predecessor let go the same way); `g_pso` and `g_pso_hy` are never cleared and only put to after a miss; a texture ID's view leaves `g_treg` only through `defer_obj_unref`; an arena block stays in `g_sub_arenas` until the submission's `arena_commit`, then in `g_arenas` until the GPU has passed its fence. `dm/tools/tests/test_borrow_release.py` (after S2Arm `21f506c2`) fails when one of these holders is released another way. No switch: the behaviour is the same.
- **Check:** in the iOS arm64 `-Os` assembly of `nvn_mtl.m`, `prog_get`, `pso_get` and `tex_by_id` no longer retain and autorelease their result, the 25 call sites of `prog_get` and 16 of the 18 of `tex_by_id` no longer claim it (the 2 left, and `pso_get`'s 3, keep one retain for a strong local or `draw_`'s strong `cur_ps`), and 12 argument-block allocations take no reference: ARC runtime calls in the file 8,530 → 8,432. `t_args_xpass`'s own bench (a full encode of 28 bindings, Mac, test build at `-O1`) is 220-229 ns both before and after, inside its noise. `check_t_nvn` with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=0` and `1`, the argument-block and arena tests and the pipeline and texture-ID tests pass, also under `NSZombieEnabled=YES`.
- **Open:** not measured in a game or on a device; NXNative measured the reference counting of its replay thread in Mario Kart World at 11.4-12.1 % of its samples before both steps and 5.2 % after (M3 Max, about a third of a millisecond a frame), with a wider set of borrowed sites than ported here. `cbuf_slot`, `ub_padded`, `hybrid_get`, `sm_amp_get`, `tf_pso`, `hm_get_id` and `serial_get` still return or hand back objects.

### Dedicated c1 constants as slices of a bank

- **Symptom:** a game runs at about 10 fps with little GPU work: the submit thread waits for a command allocator (`mtl_sub_begin` → `mtl_wait_at`), the GPU sits idle between encoders for hundreds of ms (`DM_NVN_GPUPROF`), the Metal 4 `SubmissionQueue` thread spends nearly all its time in `iokit_user_client_trap` (almost all system time), and the `memory up to frame` line shows a residency set of 10,000 objects or more.
- **Cause:** a shader whose c1 constants must have 64 KiB of their own (every shader of a library without a `c1_full.tsv`, which a partial build never writes, and the shaders that file lists) got its own `MTLBuffer`. Each one is an allocation in the queue's residency set, and the kernel's work for every commit grows with that count: the submission queue fell behind, the game's command allocators stayed in flight and its frames waited for them.
- **Fix:** such a c1 is an exclusive, zero-filled 64 KiB slice of the 4 MiB banks the other c1s share (`c1_bank`, `c1_store` in `nvn_mtl.m`): the same memory and the same zeros past its data, 64 slices to an allocation.
- **Found on:** [Hyrule Warriors: Age of Imprisonment](../../dm/games/04003F40232DE000/README.md) with a partial Mac library of 15,344 shaders: 9,854 dedicated buffers (616 MB) of a residency set of 16,118 in its first field became 142 banks of 989. Frame p50 100 → 16.6 ms, p99 400–580 → 100–114 ms, submit wall clock 41–48 → 5.8 ms a frame. Open: the submission thread is still in the kernel about two thirds of the time, and the p99 follows the commits per frame (52–69 ms with `DM_NVN_CUT=0`). A Metal System Trace of 4 s in the field shows 2.8 s of driver time wiring system memory (56,769 wires, 84,954 unwire requests, mostly 512 KiB to 4 MiB): the game's GPU allocations total 7 GB, its own memory pool alone 4,655 MB (a constant, `main+0x19eb098`), on a 16 GB Mac with 9.7 GB wired system-wide and about 160 MB free, so the driver cannot keep the working set wired and re-wires it for each submission.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> run` passes.

### One command allocator for each of a frame's submissions

- **Symptom:** the submit thread waits for the GPU at the start of a submission (`mtl_sub_begin` → `mtl_wait_at`), many times a frame, in a game that makes many small submissions a frame; the `translation detail` line's `GPU waits so far` climbs with all 8 allocator pools in use.
- **Cause:** each main submission took a command allocator of its own from a pool of 8, and a visibility buffer from a ring of 8, and kept both until the GPU had completed it. Since the queue replays a guest submission in pieces ([GPU event waits order work across queues](nvn-objects.md#gpu-event-waits-order-work-across-queues)), a frame can hold more submissions than the pool: with all 8 in flight the next one waited for the oldest.
- **Fix:** the submissions of one frame share an allocator. Metal lets an allocator serve command buffers one after another and asks only that all of them are complete before its reset, which its fence, the last one's, already says (segments of one submission shared one before). The pool then holds an allocator per frame in flight; 64 submissions is the most one serves, which bounds its memory while no frame ends. A visibility buffer is taken by a submission's first pass that counts samples, so a submission that counts none takes none, and with the ring in flight such a pass takes a buffer of its own without waiting. The pool stays at 8: raising it instead ([PR #2090](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2090), to 32) removes the waits at the cost of the command memory of each allocator kept in flight, 0.4-0.7 GB on Ocarina of Time by one contributor's measurement.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), reported from an iPhone Duo (17,598 waits in an 18-minute run) and an iPhone 18 Pro Max (about 17 a frame in Hyrule Field); on an M4 Pro the pool never ran dry. Open: not run on the game with this fix, on a Mac or a phone.
- **Check:** `t_wait_event` holds the GPU behind a shared event and makes 24 submissions in one frame: all encode, none waits, they share an allocator and none takes a visibility buffer.

### Visibility buffers made and dropped for each counting submission

- **Symptom:** a game that counts samples in many submissions a frame runs far below its GPU time on a phone: frames of 60-120 ms where the GPU needs 25-45 ms, the game's main thread waiting in `nvnSyncWait` (`nvn_sync_wait` → `mtl_wait_at`), the Metal 4 `SubmissionQueue` threads in the kernel's GPU driver (IOGPUFamily) nearly all the time, the `main thread yields per frame` count in the tens of thousands, and the `residency set up to frame` line showing thousands of commits per 300 frames with `line <n>` of `vis_take`'s overflow (`newBufferWithLength:VIS_SLOTS * 8`) at the top of what was added.
- **Cause:** since [one command allocator serves each of a frame's submissions](#one-command-allocator-for-each-of-a-frames-submissions), a submission no longer waits for an allocator, so more counting submissions are in flight than the visibility ring held (8, one per allocator). With the GPU behind, none was free, and each such submission made a 64 KiB buffer of its own, added it to the residency set and removed it after the submission: two residency changes, each committed through the kernel's GPU driver, for every counting submission. The commits delayed the GPU further, so more submissions found the ring in flight.
- **Fix:** the ring holds a buffer for every submission that can be in flight, so it never runs dry. Here that bound is the post ring's: `mtl_sub_commit` waits for the GPU before more than `POST_RING` = 128 submissions stand uncompleted (`SUB_FLIGHT_MAX`, which sizes the post ring too), so the ring holds 129, those and the one being encoded (`VIS_RING` in `nvn_mtl.m`). Upstream ([NXNative](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative) `b5150895`) sizes both from the allocator bound, `ALLOC_MAX * ALLOC_SHARE` = 512; this tree keeps its post ring at 128 and its wait. The ring grows on demand, only to the most counting submissions in flight at once (about 8 MiB at most), each buffer kept resident and reused once its submission's fence passes (`vis_take`), and `vis_in_ring` checks this submission's ring buffer instead of scanning the ring. A submission still takes buffers of its own past `VIS_SLOTS` counting passes, as before.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), reported upstream ([issue #2535](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2535)) from an iPhone 17 (A19, 8 GB) at the launcher's 30 fps cap with dynamic resolution; a spindump showed the main thread in `nvnSyncWait` 5 of 8 samples. In Kakariko Village about 2,600 overflow buffers per 300 frames (about 9 a frame) and 1,700-5,000 residency commits per 300 frames became 51-89 commits on upstream's build; 8-12 fps → 25-26 fps, frame p50 62-118 → 37 ms, GPU 25-45 → 20 ms. In Hyrule Field 10-12 → 29.5 fps, frame p50 69-78 → 33 ms. A Mac never ran dry of the ring. Open: not measured on this tree's builds.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_wait_event`: 24 counting submissions in flight, none complete, each take a distinct ring buffer, and once they complete the next one reuses one without growing the ring (the case fails with a ring of 8).

### The alias flush on every encoder's end barrier

- **Symptom:** CPU-visible Metal memory (`footprint`'s `IOAccelerator (graphics)`) of several hundred MB in thousands of 32-48 KB regions, most of it the main submission's command allocators: `DM_NVN_ALLOCPROF=1`'s `command allocator usage` shows 8 of 36-80 MB each, a few MB each in light scenes. On some builds, frames with many render passes were slow while the GPU was not the limit.
- **Cause:** while overlapping buffers or aliasing textures exist (large-pool chunks, image family heaps: `resource_vis` adds `MTL4VisibilityOptionResourceAlias`), every encoder's end barrier (`barrierAfterStages:beforeQueueStages:`, `m4_end`) carried the alias flush as well as its start barrier. On the end barrier it costs the command allocator about 100 KB per encoder and costs the GPU time; on the start barrier it costs nothing measurable. An allocator keeps the size of the largest command buffer it held after a reset (about twice what one needs), so 600-1,400 encoders a submission left all 8 at that size.
- **Fix:** an encoder's end barrier flushes to the device only. Every encoder the host opens on the main command buffer starts with a barrier carrying `resource_vis()` after all earlier work (draw passes, `enc_r_begin`, `enc_c_begin`, `blit_open`, the scaler's own before MetalFX), so an encoder's writes are flushed for aliases before any later encoder reads them. A draw pass that starts without the full barrier (`draw_barriers game`) has the flush in a start barrier before its fragment stage instead, which comes before every read NVN orders after the previous pass ([Draw passes that wait for each other again while images share memory](#draw-passes-that-wait-for-each-other-again-while-images-share-memory)). A copy encoder that wrote through an aliased buffer address (`g_blit_alias`) keeps the flush on its end barrier, as [Buffer copies through aliased Metal addresses](rendering.md#buffer-copies-through-aliased-metal-addresses) needs. `DM_NVN_END_ALIAS=1` brings the end-barrier flush back (`ab` with `DM_AB` to compare). `DM_NVN_ALLOCPROF=2` gives each submission a fresh allocator and reports its command memory by operation, by encoder and by size.
- **Measured:** a test program on an M3 Pro: 600 render passes of 4 draws took 27.7 MB of command memory with the flush on their start barriers only and 88.5 MB with it on their end barriers too; device-only barriers on both sides read stale data through a second buffer over the same memory. [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on that Mac, on a build before main's Ocarina fixes (#1299 with this session's changes): CPU-visible Metal memory in Kokiri Forest about 800 → 548 MB (allocators 36-80 → 27-64 MB, still sized by a few large submissions of up to 85 MB); in the same run with `DM_NVN_END_ALIAS=ab DM_AB=600`, scenes of about 150 passes a frame took 18-30 ms a frame at p50 without the end flush against 31-45 ms with it (GPU p50 17.5-29.7 against 22-29.3 ms), and the opening movie's 3 passes the same either way. On main at 2eb71820 (about 95 passes a frame there, and commits that wait for the GPU), the same forest route: CPU-visible Metal memory 392 → 298 MB, the 8 allocators 181 → 116 MB in all; frame time showed no difference in a `DM_NVN_END_ALIAS=ab DM_AB=600` run (phase pairs within ±2 ms either way).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_copy_alias` includes a shader writing through one no-copy buffer and the next encoder's shader reading through another over the same memory, with overlapping buffers present: it fails with device-only barriers and passes with the flush on the start barrier alone.

### Registry lock priority inheritance

- **Symptom:** the submit thread waits on the texture / sampler registry lock while a lower-priority registration thread is preempted holding it.
- **Cause:** game threads register textures and samplers under `g_rmu`, which the submit thread also takes for argument encoding. The previous mutex allowed those owners to delay the higher-priority submitter.
- **Fix:** `g_rmu` is an `os_unfair_lock`, lending a waiter's priority to its owner. All registration, lookup, cache, diagnostic and test callers use its lock API; the critical sections and lock order stay the same.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md), measured upstream in MacNativeGames commit `5f5de5e` and ported in [PR #195](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/195): in a shrine, game threads at priority 46 delayed the submit thread at priority 97 by about 1.8 ms a frame. Waiting fell from 155 to about 0 ms/s there, with unchanged frame time. Those game measurements have not been repeated here.
- **Check:** `t_stencil_view`, `t_view_levels` and `t_view_swizzle` exercise production registry lookups and cached views with the new lock on the Mac.

### Hash-table lookups of 8-byte keys

- **Symptom:** in a replay profile, `_platform_memcmp` is most of the time spent in serial lookups (`tex_mtl` through `g_by_serial`).
- **Cause:** `nvn_hm_get` compared each probed key with `memcmp` and the table's run-time key length, a libc call per probe. The serial, live-serial, ghost, view-cache and compute-pipeline tables have 8-byte keys and are looked up several times per draw.
- **Fix:** for 8-byte keys `nvn_hm_get` loads the key as one word and compares words. The hash, probe order and answers are the same; puts and deletes are unchanged.
- **Found on:** MacNativeGames commit `7431eb7` (38 of 55 samples of the serial lookup were `memcmp` in a Tears of the Kingdom profile). Here, a lookup in a 3,000-entry table of 8-byte keys takes 1.5 ns instead of 5.9 ns on an M4 Pro (`clang -O2`, 60 million lookups); no game frame has been measured.
- **Check:** `t_hmap` checks 8- and 20-byte keys against a naive model through random puts, overwrites, deletes and clears.

### GPU event writes found by word

- **Symptom:** the NVN queue replay thread spends CPU time outside the submit CPU figure, and the time grows with the square of a game's GPU event signals in flight (about 0.3 ms a frame for NieR:Automata's 850 signals, 21 ms for 2,000 on fresh words).
- **Cause:** the queue keeps the last GPU write of each event word until that write's fence completes (`nq_event_issued` in `nvn_queue.inc`; see [GPU event waits order work across queues](nvn-objects.md#gpu-event-waits-order-work-across-queues)). It kept them in a list, and each signal walked the whole list to find its word, under `nq_mu`, after the replay's cost probes had stopped. A node with `signal_events node` also scanned its own signals for each new signal (`nq_signal_on`).
- **Fix:** every game. The writes are found by word in an `nvn_hmap` and kept in a second list in fence order, so `nvn_queue_gpu_progress` takes the completed ones off its head. Entries go to a free list of up to 4,096 (48 bytes each) instead of being freed. A node of more than 16 signals marks its words in a map instead of scanning them. The answers are the same: one entry per word holding its last write, dropped once its fence has completed. `DM_NVN_EVENT_MAP=0` keeps the list and the scan.
- **Measured:** a standalone loop that copies both algorithms, from the memory and efficiency audit, on an M4 Max with `clang -O2`. NieR:Automata's 850 signals a frame on the same words with two frames in flight took 0.26 ms a frame to record and 0.09 ms to look up in their node, and now take 0.003 and 0.007 ms. With 850 fresh words and three frames in flight, recording took 1.8 ms a frame and now takes 0.01 ms. With 2,000 fresh words it took 21 ms and now takes 0.03 ms. No game frame has been measured.
- **Log:** every 300 frames the replay thread writes `[nvn] queue up to frame N`. It gives the GPU event writes per frame, the time this bookkeeping takes per frame, and the event words in flight now and at most. The words in flight are counted with `DM_NVN_EVENT_MAP=0` too, so the two settings compare like with like. The line follows the frame of the last replayed job, which goes back when jobs of two queues interleave (a frame-599 job replayed after the frame-600 job it waits on); a frame at or below the last line's is ignored (`nq_report_due`), so each line covers the frames since the one before.
- **Check:** `t_wait_event` `event_bookkeeping` records three rounds of 2,000 fresh words, rewrites the first round, and lets some writes land. Every word holds its last write, the landed ones are dropped, and a write with an older fence goes to its place in the list. It takes under 4 ms (0.4 ms here, 30 to 60 ms with the list). It also checks the words in flight the queue line reports, with the map and with the list. `signal_set` adds 8,000 signals to one node in under 2 ms (0.3 ms, 8 ms with the scan). It then checks a node of 20 signals on words an earlier node marked (none found before each is added, all after), a node of 17,000 words that passes the map's 16,384-word limit in its middle and keeps them all, and the next node, which empties the map and marks its words again. `report_due` checks that a frame sequence going back across a multiple of 300 gives one line per multiple, over the frames since the last. `signals_2000` sends 2,000 signals on 2,000 events in one command buffer, with `node` and `split`. They publish in recorded order with their values, and a wait on another queue for the last one is released. The fixture runs with the encode thread on and off, with `node` and `split`, and once with the earlier code switched back on.

### Queue nodes and receipts from free lists

- **Symptom:** in split mode (`signal_events split`; the default for every game but NieR:Automata until the upstream merge of issue #2112, a fallback since), every GPU event signal costs five allocations on the submitting thread and as many frees on the replay thread.
- **Cause:** each signal ends its command node. That makes a command node, an `NQ_FENCE` node, the fence node's receipt and an array for the one signal (`nq_signal_add`), and it starts a command copy. The four spare command copies (`NQ_SPARES`) cover only the first four nodes of a submission ([32 with `replay_spares`](#too-few-spare-command-copies-and-pool-tables-for-a-frame-of-many-submissions)).
- **Fix:** every game. A node keeps its first signal inline, so a node with one signal needs no array. Nodes and receipts come from free lists of up to 1,024 each (`nq_node_new`, `nq_new`), behind a lock of their own. A node is 296 bytes and a receipt 32, so the lists hold at most about 330 KB. The command copies are unchanged. `DM_NVN_NODE_POOL=0` allocates and frees each one as before.
- **Log:** the `[nvn] queue` line gives the nodes and receipts made per frame and how many came from the free lists. It also counts the command nodes that started without a spare copy, which the spare pool's size would decide.
- **Check:** `t_wait_event` `split_allocations` submits a command buffer of 400 signals twice in split mode and counts the allocations on the submitting thread. The second submission makes 397, 0.99 a signal (the command copies). With `DM_NVN_NODE_POOL=0` it makes 2,000, 5.00 a signal, and the test checks for at least 4.

### Too few spare command copies and pool tables for a frame of many submissions

- **Symptom:** the `[nvn] queue` line counts about 20 `command nodes without a spare copy` a frame, and the `residency set` line adds thousands of pool tables a window at `line <pool_table_take>` (Ocarina of Time's run C on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, X1.0, up to frame 3900: 19.6 a frame without a spare copy; 2,058 adds of 642 MB in 300 frames at line 11111, about 7 tables of about 312 KB a frame), with 0.6–1.3 ms of kernel time a frame on the `translation detail` line.
- **Cause:** both free lists were sized for a frame of a few submissions. The queue keeps 4 spare command copies (`NQ_SPARES`, `nvn_queue.inc`), and Ocarina of Time's ~25 event waits a frame end about 20 command nodes, so the other 16 grow a copy from 4 KB by doubling under `g_sub_mu`. A replaced pool table goes to 16 spares (`POOL_SPARES`, `nvn_mtl.m`) and is reused once its submission completes; about 6 a frame with three frames in flight fill the 16 with tables still in flight, and a full set let the lowest fence go, the next table to come back, so the next walk made a new buffer and added it to the residency set.
- **Fix:** the profile key `replay_spares on` (`DM_REPLAY_SPARES=1` / `0` overrides it; `nvn_replay_spares`). 32 spare command copies, holding at most 16 MB in all (`NQ_SPARE_BYTES`, the most the 4 could hold), each still at most 4 MB; 64 spare pool tables (about 20 MB at Ocarina's size), never more than 24 MB in all (`POOL_SPARE_BYTES`: a table given past it lets the oldest completed spares go, or goes itself while they are all in flight, so pools that grow cannot hold more), and when every spare is still in flight the table given then, the newest, goes instead of the next to come back; with one completed the lowest fence goes as before (a size the pools have left behind). Performance only: the copies and tables hold the same bytes. Every other game keeps 4 and 16.
- **Log:** with the key the `[nvn] queue` line adds `(spare copies kept N of 32, M MB)` after `command nodes without a spare copy`, and the `pool walk: tables made …, reused …` line adds `(spares kept N of 64, let go N)`; one `replay spares:` line at the start. That `pool walk` line is written from the whole-table path every 500 builds, which Ocarina of Time takes 0.00–0.02 times a frame (once a run), so the power lines' `[nvn] pool tables up to frame …` line adds `; tables made N, reused N (spares kept N of M, let go N)`: per frame made and reused, the spares kept now of how many, and let go in the window. It does so for a profile with `replay_spares`, with the key on or turned off by `DM_REPLAY_SPARES=0` (so both sides of the A/B have it; `of 16` when off), and for any game with `DM_REPLAY_SPARES=1`; every other game's line is as before.
- **What an iPhone A/B should test:** `DM_REPLAY_SPARES=0` in `Documents/env.txt` against the default on the same route: `command nodes without a spare copy` (about 20 → about 0), the `residency set` line's adds at the `pool_table_take` line and its commit time (the line records `__LINE__`, which moves with every edit to `nvn_mtl.m`: `grep -n 'res_add(b); g_pool_made' dm/gfx/nvn/nvn_mtl.m` at the build's commit gives it; 11111 in X1.0), the `pool tables up to frame` lines' `tables made` against `reused` and `let go`, and the `translation detail` line's kernel ms; the footprint on the `on-screen` line (up to about 36 MB more).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_wait_event t_feedback`: `t_wait_event` `spare_copies` submits 20 signals in split mode (41 nodes) three times: 0 command nodes without a spare copy in each with the key, 16 with `DM_REPLAY_SPARES=0`; with the spares emptied, four copies of 4 MB are kept and the fifth not (16 MB), a copy over 4 MB never. `t_feedback` `spare_tables_case`: 16 kept and the lowest fence let go though in flight (X1.0), 64 with the key and the table given then let go while every spare is in flight, the lowest fence once one has completed, and a completed spare taken again, and the counts the power line reads (`mtl_pool_spare_stats`).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0, runs C and D). Not yet run on the phone with the key.

### A finalized-texture table too small for a heavy area

- **Symptom:** in one heavy area of an iPhone run of Ocarina of Time, about 800 textures a frame went into the finalized-texture table for 300 frames, then about 364 (the memory line counts them in `finalized textures still referenced (small table, N put in total)`, now `(table of S, N put in total)` with the table's size), where other games and runs stay under about 100 a frame; the pool tables' patch path kept about 465 tables a frame. An estimated 1–4 ms of replay CPU a frame.
- **Cause:** a record or texture pool registration the game made before it finalized a texture object resolves its serial through the finalized-texture table (`g_ghost`, `tex_put` in `nvn_mtl.m`; [Dead texture serials kept in a ghost table](nvn-objects.md#dead-texture-serials-kept-in-a-ghost-table)), then a ring of 512 whose oldest entry went when a new one came. When a frame replays more of them than the table holds, it pushes out textures it wants again a frame or two later. Every put and eviction notes its serial (`ser_note`): each note sends the pool tables down their patch path (`pool_gpu`), and more notes between two syncs than `SER_NOTES` (64) start a new texture ID epoch, in which every texture ID takes the slow path again.
- **Fix:** the table's size is decided once at the start (`ghost_cap`): 512 for every game, the profile key `ghost_table` (64 to 8,192; Ocarina of Time `4096`), or `DM_NVN_GHOST=<n>` (held to 64 to 8,192; a value that is not a number is ignored). The size is a ceiling: the table is kept by use, the least recently used going first, what a submission in flight used kept, idle entries drained back to 512 at each commit, and everything above 512 let go at low headroom to the memory limit ([A finalized-texture table that kept every texture it had held](memory.md#a-finalized-texture-table-that-kept-every-texture-it-had-held)). Each entry holds its texture, so a larger table can hold more textures alive and resident (a retired one waits in `g_retire_held` for its entry): the memory line's `finalized textures still referenced` gives how many. A memory trim at a pressure warning or above takes a table larger than 512 back down to 512, the least recently used first (`ghost_trim` from `mtl_trim_at`; [Nothing answered the system's memory pressure](memory.md#nothing-answered-the-systems-memory-pressure)).
- **Log:** at the start, `finalized-texture table: up to N textures that finalized texture objects still resolve to, the least recently used going when a new one comes, idle ones drained back to 512 (the default | game profile ghost_table | DM_NVN_GHOST …)`. Every 300 frames, beside the memory line (`mtl_ghost_stat`, from the frame hook in `hle_nvn.c`), every game: `finalized-texture table up to frame F (H held, up to N), per frame: P put, T of them pushed out in the last 4 frames, E pushed out; M ms in the texture misses that put, Q ms patching pool tables; texture ID epochs started X; in the window D idle entries drained, L let go at low memory headroom` (the last two: [A finalized-texture table that kept every texture it had held](memory.md#a-finalized-texture-table-that-kept-every-texture-it-had-held)). `T` is the thrash: a put of a serial the table evicted in the last 4 frames (a record of the evicted serials and their frames, 8,192 slots by the serial's low bits, read before the put's own eviction is recorded; a later eviction of a serial 8,192 apart takes the slot, so it can count low). `M` times a texture lookup that missed (`tex_mtl`) from the miss to its texture, when that lookup put into the table; `Q` is the pool tables' patch path, whether it kept, patched or gave up on a table, less the misses of `M` timed inside it: the patch path resolves texture IDs (`pool_entry`, `tex_fast_rid`, `tex_mtl`), where most re-puts come from, so `M + Q` is the time of both. A window with no put, eviction, drain or low-headroom drop writes no line, whatever the patch path and the epochs did (they move in nearly every game, and the `pool tables up to frame` line counts the patch path), so a game that never fills its table writes none. A memory trim's line gives `finalized-texture table entries N (down to 512)`. The counters are a few additions on the paths that already do the work, and one clock read per pool table patch and per texture miss.
- **What an iPhone A/B should test:** `DM_NVN_GHOST=512` in `Documents/env.txt` (kept in a player build) against the default `4096` on the same heavy area: the line's `put`, `pushed out in the last 4 frames` and `pushed out` (thrash should fall to about 0), its two ms figures (they add up), the epochs, the `pool tables up to frame` line's kept and patched tables, the `translation detail` line's replay CPU, and the memory line's `finalized textures still referenced`, `retired but held by a serial`, `residency set`, resident and GPU allocated, with any memory trim's `finalized-texture table entries`. `scripts/devlog.py` reads the table's size and lines (the `ghost table` row, compared as `finalized-texture …` rows).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_ghost`: `t_ghost` checks the start line and the size (the profile's 4096; `DM_NVN_GHOST=1000`, `=20` held to 64, `=100000` held to 8192, `=junk` the profile's), every source of `ghost_cap_pick`, that a table of 512, 4,096, 64 and 8,192 holds that many with the least recently used gone first, puts and evictions counted, and a serial put again while held no new put; that a serial pushed out 3 frames before counts as thrash and one pushed out 4 frames before or never held does not, also when the put's own eviction shares its record slot; a table of 1,000 (no power of two); `ghost_trim` letting nothing go while a submission in flight uses every entry, then taking 3,000 held in 4,096 and a full table of 1,000 down to 512 from the least recently used, the table filling to its ceiling again before the next eviction, and the memory trim at a warning trimming (not when pressure is back to normal); the use order, the in-flight protection, the idle drain and the low-headroom drop ([A finalized-texture table that kept every texture it had held](memory.md#a-finalized-texture-table-that-kept-every-texture-it-had-held)); the patch time less the misses inside it, never below zero; and the line's per-frame figures over 300 frames, with no line for a window without a put or an eviction even when the patch path and the epochs moved. `dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py` `GhostTable` reads both lines from the formats in `nvn_mtl.m`. `t_cpu_tex --metal` (`handover_holders`) evicts through a table of the profile's size.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone. Not yet run on the phone with the key.

### A submit cost that does not say which thread spent it or what it waited for

- **Symptom:** the `translation detail` line's wall clock is 2–4 ms a frame over its CPU time and the gap does not shrink at a high clock (Ocarina of Time's run C on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, X1.0: CPU 5.9 ms / wall 8.4 ms at 3.87 GHz, CPU 12.8 / wall 16.8 ms at 1.72 GHz), and the line cannot say whose time it is: the game thread's copy of its command lists or the replay, or what either waited for.
- **Cause:** the line adds up two threads' scopes (`nq_cost_begin` / `nq_cost_end`, `nvn_queue.inc`): `nvn_queue_submit` on the game thread, which copies the commands under `g_sub_mu`, and each replayed node on 'NVN queue replay', which holds `g_sub_mu` for the whole node, takes the texture registry lock `g_rmu` for each argument encode (`encode_args_`) and waits for the encode thread at the end of each submission (`rec_drain`, about 20 a frame in Ocarina of Time). None of the waits was counted outside `DM_NVN_WAITS=1`'s ledger, which a player build dropped, and `g_sub_mu` and `g_rmu` waits not even there.
- **Fix:** logging only, every game, with `DM_FIELD_LOG` on (the default): the line adds `; of the submit per frame, the game threads' command copy CPU … ms / wall … ms (N submits) and the replay CPU … / wall …; waits per frame: the game's submits for g_sub_mu … ms (N waits), the replay for g_sub_mu …, for g_rmu …, other threads for g_rmu …, the replay for the encode thread … ms (N of M drains blocked), the replay for the residency commit lock … ms (N), other threads for it … ms (N)` (`wait_split`, `hle_nvn.c`; the last two since the port of NXNative 0c078130, [Residency changes waited for another thread's commit](memory.md#residency-changes-waited-for-another-threads-commit)). The copy and the replay add up to the line's own CPU and wall; the waits are wall time and not part of the CPU time. Each lock is tried first (`nq_sub_lock`, `rmu_lock`, `res_commit_lock`), and a drain reads the clock only when it blocks, so an uncontended lock costs nothing more. `DM_FIELD_LOG=0` writes the line as X1.0 did.
- **What to read:** a large `the game's submits for g_sub_mu` is the game thread blocked behind a whole replayed node (the case for holding `g_sub_mu` for less time); `the replay for the encode thread` near the wall-minus-CPU gap is the per-submission drain; `for g_rmu` the registry lock; whatever the gap keeps beyond these is the thread ready but not running.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 check_t_wait_event`: `wait_counters` counts one submit and its copy as a part of the submit cost, the encode thread's drains with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1` and none without, a submit behind a `g_sub_mu` held 20 ms as one wait of about that, an uncontended one as none, a `g_rmu` wait off the replay thread as another thread's, a `res_commit` behind a held residency commit lock as another thread's wait and an uncontended one as none, and the line's text for given totals.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0, runs C and D).

### Submit-cost probes with one kernel call a reading

- **Symptom:** the submit CPU figure of the `translation` and `translation detail` lines costs time of its own, and part of its kernel time is its own probes.
- **Cause:** a probe scope (`nq_cost_begin` and `nq_cost_end`) runs once per `nvn_queue_submit` and once per replayed command or present node, so in split mode once per GPU event signal. Each reading called `clock_gettime` for the CPU time and `mach_thread_self`, `thread_info` and `mach_port_deallocate` for the kernel time (`thr_sys_ns` in `hle_nvn.c`), about 1.6 µs a scope on an M4 Max. NieR:Automata's 850 nodes a frame before `signal_events node` cost about 1.4 ms a frame of probes.
- **Fix:** every game. A reading is one `THREAD_BASIC_INFO` call, which gives both the CPU time (user plus kernel) and the kernel time, on the thread's port kept per thread (`thr_times`, `pthread_mach_thread_np`). That is about 0.9 µs a scope. The times come in whole microseconds, but each scope takes the difference of two running totals, so the sums lose nothing on average: over 20,000 scopes of 5 to 10 µs the total was within 0.1% of `clock_gettime`'s. The kernel figure is lower than before, because the earlier probes' own port calls ran inside the scope. `DM_NVN_SUBMIT_PROBES=full` reads them as before.
- **Check:** `t_wait_event` `probe_cost` runs 4,000 scopes. It checks that they make one `thread_info` call per reading and no thread port, or with `DM_NVN_SUBMIT_PROBES=full` a port and a `thread_info` call per reading. The CPU time they add up is 85% to 102% of the thread's own clock, and the kernel time is part of it.

### Submissions handed to the GPU in segments

- **Symptom:** the GPU sits idle each frame while the host translates the submission (a game that does not submit frame N+1 until frame N's GPU work is done).
- **Cause:** on the Switch the submit costs nothing; here it is the host's translation, and the GPU got nothing until the whole submission was encoded.
- **Fix:** the replay commits the command buffer encoded so far every 64 draws, between two passes (`sub_cut` in `nvn_mtl.m`, called after `rp_flush` in `OP_SET_RT`; `DM_NVN_CUT=<draws>`, 0 = off). It cuts only with no open encoder and nothing deferred, merged or hoisted, commits the pending upload batch first, and continues on the same command allocator. The fence signal, the completion work (`post` ops, `g_done`) and the allocator's fence stay on the last segment: the queue runs command buffers in commit order, and every encoder starts with a queue barrier.
- **Fix:** the draw count starts with the submission (`sub_cut_begin` in `mtl_sub_begin`). It used to start at the submission's first `sub_cut`, which only a render target change reaches, so the draws a submission makes into the target it inherited, before its first target change, were never counted and the first segment came that many draws late. `t_reorder_chain` (case `inherited_target`, alone with `--inherited-target-only`) draws `cut_draws()` times into an inherited target and checks for one cut at the next target change.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md): a stack sample in the shrine had the main thread in `nvnSyncWait` (the fence of the frame just submitted) for 59% of the time and the render thread in `nvnQueueSubmitCommands` for 40%; the translation was about 3,600 draws and 347 passes, 7 ms, so per frame the GPU ran 13–14 ms and then sat idle 7.7 ms (`DM_NVN_TSV` rows `@gpu_beg_ms` / `@gpu_end_ms` against `@call_ms`). GPU idle per frame went from 7.7 to 3.8 ms; most of the rest is the game's own work between its fence wait and the next submit (about 2.5 ms). The other five games' frame times were unchanged; Metroid Dread's p95 dropped from 19.6 to 16.9 ms.
- **Note:** after this, BotW's docked frame p50 was 16.7 ms (was 20.8 ms before segmenting). Host translation is 8 ms per frame (22,000 instructions per draw), spread over Metal's encoder creation and draw calls, argument encoding, Objective-C retain / release and pool lookups. The Metal 3 per-pass GPU timer (`DM_NVN_PASSGPU`) did not work under Metal 4 and has been removed; `DM_NVN_GPUPROF` times each encoder of the main command buffers from a Metal 4 counter heap. Render-target traffic per frame is in `DM_NVN_TSV`'s `@rt_load_mb` / `@rt_store_mb`.
- **Note:** with the encode thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD`) a segment's commit record is handed to it at once (`rec_publish` in `sub_cut`), not when its block of records fills. Breath of the Wild on an M2 Max (encode thread and snapshots, `DM_FPS_TARGET=120`): the frame's wait on its newest fence 12.5 ms with `DM_NVN_CUT=0`, 5.6 ms with 64, 5.35 ms with 16.
- **Note:** MacNativeGames moved its cut to 30 draws for the first segment and 40 after (`2fa27d7`; uncapped Tears of the Kingdom in a shrine, 10.77 → 10.60 ms a frame). At this repository's 60 fps cap the size changes nothing where the GPU finishes inside the frame: Breath of the Wild outdoors on an M4 Pro (2026-10-26, the outdoor seed of the [BotW Mac routes](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#mac-routes), frames 3,001–5,700, cut 64) waited on its fence 0.02 ms a frame on average (`@sync_ms`), with GPU span 10.8 ms. The default stays 64; a GPU-bound device (an iPhone) is where a smaller cut could show, through `DM_NVN_CUT`.


### A long render pass split while the GPU has nothing

- **Symptom:** the GPU idles in the middle of the frame, before a long render pass (a G-buffer of thousands of draws), while the host translates that pass: segments are committed only between passes, so the whole pass reaches the GPU at its end.
- **Cause:** a cut ([segments](#submissions-handed-to-the-gpu-in-segments)) needs a closed render pass, and a pass closes only at a render-target change.
- **Fix:** `pass_split_try` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game. At a draw, with nothing committed still on the GPU (`g_seg_inflight`), `DM_NVN_PASS_SPLIT` draws (default 256 on the Mac; off on iOS, see below; `0` off) since the last cut and a cut that will commit (`sub_cut_ready`), the pass is ended, the segment committed, and the next draw opens the pass again, loading its attachments. At most `DM_NVN_PASS_SPLIT_MAX` splits (default 2) per NVN pass, as each stores and loads the attachments; merged, ranged, deferred and hoisted passes are not split. The split's pass end stores every attachment without asking the store lookahead, which skips the rest of the pass (its draws set no target) and could otherwise leave unstored an attachment the reopened pass loads. A reopened pass skips the dispatch-hoist scan, which the pass's first draw already ran.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback` (`pass_split_case`): no split while a segment runs or under `DM_NVN_PASS_SPLIT` draws since the last cut, then the pass ends and its segment is committed, at most `DM_NVN_PASS_SPLIT_MAX` times a pass, and a new render pass may be split again.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#found-on-this-game) ([PR #1468](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1468)): 2,700 of its 4,100 draws a frame are one G-buffer pass, and the GPU waited about 2.5 ms of a 9 ms frame for it. On an M4 Max at 120 fps, frame p50 / p90 8.75 / 9.33 → 8.34 / 8.68 ms, attachment traffic within noise. Also seen on [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (scene passes of 500–800 draws; about 0.3 ms a frame of idle on an M1, [PR #2560](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2560)). Open: not measured on an iPhone, where the cost is memory traffic and power. NX 18 therefore leaves it off on iOS (`DM_NVN_PASS_SPLIT` unset there means 0) until a battery A/B on Breath of the Wild and Ocarina of Time with `DM_NVN_PASS_SPLIT=256`; a player build keeps both switches for that A/B.

### The GPU idles with the next command buffer already committed

- **Symptom:** one frame in 50 or so takes 25-40 ms in a scene where the frame normally fits, with the host on time: `@sync_ms` is high, `@sub_wall_ms` is not, and the frame before has a GPU span (`@gpu_end_ms - @gpu_beg_ms`) of 25-40 ms while the summed `@gpu_ms` has not moved. Runs fall into two states, and on the same code one can have 5 long frames and the next 60. A bad run also shows 186 % CPU against 133 %, and the previous frame still running when the next starts (p50 4 ms, p90 10 ms, against about 0 in a good run).
- **Cause, as far as measured:** every command buffer reaches the kernel through one serial dispatch queue, `com.Metal4.SubmissionQueue`. It was active in 896 of 971 samples, mostly in `iokit_user_client_trap`, with about half a core of system time. The GPU stops 5-40 ms in front of a buffer the host committed earlier, and the stalls grow with the number of buffers queued behind running work: about 12 a frame gave 50-150 long frames per 2,700, 30 a frame gave over 200, and 6 gave fewer than 10. A stall leaves a backlog, the backlog queues more buffers, and so a run stays in the state it fell into. What holds the queue inside the kernel is unknown, as the trap could not be timed (interposing does not reach calls inside the shared cache).
- **Ruled out by measurement:** another process's GPU work (WindowServer and the Claude app together used 1-4 ms inside gaps of 15-40 ms); free memory (the same gap rate at 640 MB and at 140 MB free); wired-memory jumps (no more gaps near them than chance); residency set size (10 to 20,000 allocations, the same 0.06 ms from commit to start in a test program); residency commits (654 to 12 per 300 frames, gaps unchanged); a command allocator per segment; `DM_QOS_MAP=1`; the GPU clock (the compute batch's time does not change across a gap); the display's refresh (no phase lock). A buffer queued behind a running one does not stall in a test program by itself, so the load matters too.
- **Fix:** `sub_cut` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game. A cut exists so that the GPU does not sit idle while the host encodes ([segments](#submissions-handed-to-the-gpu-in-segments)), so it commits only when everything committed before it has reported back (`g_seg_inflight`). A GPU that is really idle still gets its cut at the next boundary; a cut for a copy still commits. `DM_NVN_CUT_BUSY=0` commits at every cut. The rule reduces the buffers without explaining the kernel's wait, so it removes the conditions for the stall, not its cause.
- **Timestamps:** a report takes the GPU start or end of its segment, so the game's measurements depended on those commits. Fewer of them left every report the same time, and Ocarina of Time stopped lowering its resolution (GPU 18 ms in its heavy stretch). The boundaries therefore stay every `cut_draws()` draws, each with a GPU timestamp (`ts_stamp`, written at the start of the next encoder), whether or not the host commits there. Two things were tried first. A commit after each report means about 17 more a frame, and the stalls came back. Each report's own true time made the game's controller go to its lowest resolution, with frames of 13 and 52 ms in turn, so what the game reads here is not what hardware gives it. A timestamp written between encoders cost 0.6 ms of GPU a frame, so it waits for the next encoder. With no counter heap every boundary stays a commit; the encode thread writes them as recorded commands.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback` (`cut_boundary_case`: two reports of one command buffer, either side of a boundary, read two times from the boundary's GPU timestamps; the tests' guest clock is their `now_ns`, as the host's is with no pause, and `bp_now`, which most tests give as `now_ns`, is the host's Mach clock, as Metal's timestamps are. With stubs that returned 0 both reports took the time their completion handler wrote them, the same in about half the runs). Ocarina of Time on its walk, settled, per 2,700 frames: over 25 ms 51-75 in the bad state and 3-6 in the rare good one before; 0-8 in all 7 runs after, the last 2 on the landed code; over 33 ms 12-58 before and 0-2 after; frame p95 18.9-21 ms to 17.8-18.9 ms; in a window p95 17.8 ms, 3 over 25 ms. The heavy stretch holds 16.65-16.8 ms by lowering its resolution, as before.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game).

### A segment boundary kept open while the GPU finishes earlier work

- **Symptom:** a game whose frame is a serial chain on the host (the game's thread, then the replay ('NVN queue replay'), then the encode thread ('dm-encode'), then the GPU) loses frame rate when the CPU clock drops while the GPU still has time: Ocarina of Time on a warm iPhone 18 Pro Max (handheld Balanced, CPU capped at about 1.5-1.9 GHz at the same 14 M cycles a frame) ran the replay 8-10 ms and the encode thread about 5 ms a frame, with the GPU only 31-41 % busy and frames at 30-40 fps; no thread was saturated. A Metal System Trace showed 4-5 commit calls a frame (9-10 late), one of them a command buffer of about 13 encoders that took 1.5-2.7 ms from its start to its commit.
- **Cause:** a segment cut ([segments](#submissions-handed-to-the-gpu-in-segments)) commits only when nothing earlier still runs ([the rule above](#the-gpu-idles-with-the-next-command-buffer-already-committed)); otherwise the boundary stays open and the replay goes on encoding into the same command buffer. The next chance comes 64 draws later and only at a pass boundary, so a GPU that finished just after a kept boundary waited for the replay to reach the next one, or the end of a long pass (the long-pass split is off on iOS), and at the replay's end everything since the last commit was left for the GPU: the frame's tail. Whether earlier work still ran was also read from the commit feedback's count (`g_seg_inflight`), which comes 0.08-1.2 ms after the GPU's end, where the encode thread's own event behind each commit is written about 0.04 ms after it.
- **Fix:** `nvn_mtl.m` `sub_cut` and `nvn_rec.h`, for every game on the encode thread (iOS's default), two switches:
  - `DM_NVN_CUT_HOLD` (default on): a boundary whose earlier work still runs is cut all the same, and its command buffer goes to the encode thread marked as holdable (`RO_COMMIT` with `a[5]` 2). The encode thread holds it with the submissions it already holds ([the held submissions below](#a-frames-submissions-queued-behind-its-first-one-while-the-iphones-gpu-clock-is-low)) and commits them together, in order, in one commit call, the moment the GPU runs out of work: at an encoder's end, at the start of each block of records (new: a long pass no longer waits for its encoder's end), or within 50 µs while nothing is recorded. Nothing is committed behind running work, so the kernel stall above keeps its rule. A copy's cut still commits at once. Each held segment keeps its own feedback and part of the commit's GPU time (`DmPartFB`), so the boundaries' report times are read as for held submissions.
  - `DM_NVN_CUT_EVENT` (default on): the cut's test (and the long-pass split's, `pass_split_try`) reads the GPU's event and the commits recorded but not yet on the queue (`rec_gpu_busy`) instead of the feedback count. With `DM_NVN_CUT_HOLD` on it mostly decides whether a cut is held or goes at once, so it matters on its own with `DM_NVN_CUT_HOLD=0` and for the Mac's long-pass split.
  - Cost: a cut costs the replay about 6-10 µs on an M4 Max, most of it the wake of a sleeping encode thread when the cut's records are handed over (a cut that skipped the hand-off saved part of that and lost more in the tail). Ocarina of Time makes at most about 2,300 / 64 boundaries a frame.
  - A `[nvn] segment cuts up to frame N` line every 300 frames: cuts made per frame, those handed to the encode thread to hold, those the event let through while the feedback still counted work, and boundaries kept open. `DM_NVN_CUT_HOLD=0` / `DM_NVN_CUT_EVENT=0` restore the rule before; `ab` alternates with the `DM_AB` phases; both are kept in player builds.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_chain_cut`: a submission of render passes through the production replay (`mtl_exec`) with a spin at every command standing in for translation and a blended fragment loop per draw for the GPU, timed from the submission's start to its fence. M4 Max, 5 alternating runs each: 12 passes of 96 draws, 1.5 µs a command (replay 5.6 ms, the GPU at a low clock about as long): critical path 8.16-8.56 ms (tail after the replay 2.6-3.0 ms, about 4 cuts a frame) off, 7.40-7.53 ms (tail 1.8-1.9 ms, 11 cuts) on; 40 passes of 57 draws (an Ocarina-like shape, replay 11.1 ms): 12.9-13.4 ms off, 12.2-12.6 ms on, worst frame 13.9-14.4 against 12.4-13.3; GPU-bound (12 × 96, 0.5 µs a command): 6.13-6.19 either way; replay-bound (5 µs a command, replay 17.8 ms): 19.1-19.7 off, 19.3-19.4 on, worst frame 20.7-22.0 against 19.8-19.9; 100 passes of 64 draws at 0.3 µs a command (99 cuts a frame against 7): replay 8.1 → 8.7 ms, critical path 11.2 ms either way. `check_t_feedback` (`cut_boundary_case`: a boundary with work running is a held cut, then goes to the GPU once it runs out; run again with both switches off), and the encode-thread runs of the args, texture, query, present and queue tests. Not yet run on the phone.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), Metal System Trace 1 on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (2026-11-21). **Open:** the phone A/B (`DM_NVN_CUT_HOLD=ab`, warm, the same route): frame time and the `segment cuts` line, and whether the replay's extra cuts cost more than the tail saves at the phone's lower clock.

### A frame's submissions queued behind its first one while the iPhone's GPU clock is low

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone at the launcher's 30 fps cap a game falls to 5-18 fps and stays there, in any scene, light ones included, until the app starts again; a restart may fall into it at once. The CPU is nearly idle and resolution does not matter: `NVN submit+present` about 4 ms, `GPU p50` 34-50 ms for work that takes 14-16 ms at the GPU's full clock, whole frames of 55-130 ms, the game's dynamic resolution at its floor with no effect, and the frame step never back at one vsync. In a `DM_NVN_GPUPROF` profile the frame spans 50-80 ms for 33 ms of encoders, every encoder runs about 1.4 times longer than in a run that holds 30 fps, and three frames of four have a gap of 22-37 ms after the frame's first command buffer, with every later submission already committed.
- **Cause, as far as measured** (upstream's measurements on an iPhone 17, [PR #2645](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2645); not measured on NX 18): two things, and the second locks the first in.
  - The iPhone's GPU governor lowers the clock until the GPU's work fills most of the frame. In Ocarina of Time's fairy fountain with Dynamic resolution off, the same 137 encoders took 14.0 ms for the first 40 s and 27.8 ms after, every one about 1.9 times longer, with the frame at 33.3 ms before and after. So "GPU 30 ms" at a 30 fps cap is the governor's doing, in a scene the GPU could draw in 14.
  - Once a frame misses, the game's frame is its GPU time plus what the host adds. Ocarina of Time waits for the frame's last fence before it starts the next frame, and its 22-30 submissions a frame (one at each event signal) reach the queue within 3 ms, all behind the first. The kernel then started the frame's second command buffer 22-37 ms after the first had ended (32 of 44 profiled frames), with Metal's submission thread in `iokit_user_client_trap`. It is the stall of [the Mac's entry above](#the-gpu-idles-with-the-next-command-buffer-already-committed), whose condition is command buffers queued behind running work. With those gaps the GPU read 55-75 % busy, so the governor lowered the clock further and never raised it.
  - Which of the two a load ends in is not decided by the host: of 20 loads of one save with each submission in its own command buffer, 6 began in the slow state and stayed in it; the others held 30 fps for their whole run.
- **Ruled out by measurement** (upstream): more work at night (the passes, draws and dispatches are the same by day); compute dispatch ordering (`DM_NVN_DISPATCH_BARRIER`, `DM_NVN_DISPATCH_OWN`); the present's wait for its drawable as the cause of the long gap; memory; Low Power Mode; the thermal state.
- **Fix:** `nvn_rec.h`, for every game on the encode thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD`, the default on iOS): a submission that ends while the GPU still runs a main command buffer committed earlier is not committed. Its command buffer is ended and held, the next submissions go into command buffers of their own, and the held ones reach the queue in one commit call, in order (`rx_commit`, `[MTL4CommandQueue commit:count:options:]`), each with its own feedback (`DmCommitFB`). They go when the GPU runs out of work (at the end of an encoder), when nothing more is recorded for about half a millisecond, when the submit thread drains the encode thread, at a present of the game's own texture without a snapshot (`rec_flush_held`), and before anything else goes to the queue (an upload batch, a snapshot copy, a one-off command buffer in order, a queue operation, a signal of its own), so each command buffer keeps its place among those exactly as when it was committed on its own; their queue signals and commit notes follow the commit. Each held submission's feedback gets its own part of the commit's GPU time, bounded by the stamp its successor's first encoder writes (`ts_sub_begin`), so a counter report that falls back to its command buffer's start or end (`rep_ticks`) reads what it read before. The submit thread no longer drains the encode thread at the end of each submission ([The GPU idles before each submission while the replay waits for the encode thread](rendering.md#the-gpu-idles-before-each-submission-while-the-replay-waits-for-the-encode-thread)). `DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=0` commits each submission on its own (`ab` alternates with `DM_AB`); NX 18 keeps it in player builds, so `DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=0`, or `DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=ab` with `DM_AB=<frames>`, in `Documents/env.txt` is the iPhone A/B. Ported from upstream b2ae958f2 on top of 1fc17bbcf (`cb_commit`); NX 18's own pieces: the present's upload-batch note (`pup_commit_wait`) stays, and batches recorded before a submission's commit are covered by that submission's note (`g_sub_cmt`) instead of its drain; the encode thread's freeze-detector states and 10 s lines cover the held commits; and a present without a snapshot, whose display command buffer NX 18 commits from the present thread after the frame's note (`sub_commit_wait`), not through the encode thread as upstream does, records a flush (`RO_FLUSH`) so the frame's held submissions go at its place. Without it they waited for the GPU to run out of work and went with the next frame's first ones, the present thread blocked until then, and the display command buffer, which waits for all earlier work on the queue, ran after part of the next frame.
- **When the GPU has run out of work** is read from an event the encode thread signals after each of its commits (`rx_mark`, `rx_busy`: `g_rx_ev` against `g_rx_seq`), upload batches and snapshot copies included, at each encoder's end and every 50 us while nothing is recorded (half a millisecond at most). The GPU writes it as it gets there. Upstream's first version waited for the commit feedback to count the command buffer done instead, which on upstream's M6 came 0.08-0.26 ms after the GPU's end against about 0.04 ms for the event. `DM_NVN_SUB_IDLE_FB=1` reads the feedback's count again (`ab` alternates with `DM_AB`). The event release has not run on a phone, upstream's or NX 18's; NX 18 keeps `DM_NVN_SUB_IDLE_FB` and `DM_NVN_SUB_DRAIN` in player builds too, so upstream's open comparison (`DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=ab:1,2/3 DM_NVN_SUB_IDLE_FB=ab:1/3 DM_AB=900`) runs from `Documents/env.txt`.
- **A first version, wrong** (upstream's): it put the next submissions into the held command buffer itself. An upload batch flushed inside a submission then reached the queue after the draws of that submission recorded before it, which sampled the texture before its upload: pink and dark flashes in play on the iPhone and one GPU error and recovery. Separate command buffers keep each one's place in the queue.
- **Measured** (upstream, not on NX 18): the first version on an iPhone 17 in Ocarina of Time's fairy fountain (`DM_AB=900 DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=ab`, both in one process) in the slow state: frame p50 42-45 ms merged against 54-70 ms, `GPU p50` 37.4-39.8 against 42.1-46.1 ms, 5 main command buffers a frame against 29; in runs that held 30 fps no change in frame time. The fixed version in the owner's play of about 30 minutes: about 17 fps by night in Hyrule Field and Kakariko Village against about 9, no flashes and no GPU error. On an M6 (`save_village`, encode thread on): `NVN submit+present` p50 3.2-3.4 ms against 4.1-5.5 ms, `GPU p50` and the locked 60 fps unchanged.
- **Open:** the merge removes the gaps, not the low clock: in upstream's slow state a frame still took its GPU time plus about 4 ms. What is left of the host's share is the present's wait for its drawable on the game's queue at the display link's frame latency ([Frame latency 2 for the display link in fullscreen](#frame-latency-2-for-the-display-link-in-fullscreen)). Not yet run on the owner's iPhone 18 Pro Max: A `DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=0` against B the default, on battery, judged by frame rate and power. What decides the governor's clock after a load is not known.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), upstream's iPhone 17, at night in Hyrule Field and Kakariko Village and in a fairy fountain.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback` (`merge_case`, in the runs with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`: three submissions recorded behind running work go to the queue in one commit, their notes follow it and each one's report reads its own end; a queue operation recorded inside the next submission, after its first pass, finds the held one committed alone, without that pass; a present's flush sends the held ones and their notes before the next submission's work is encoded. The count checks fail with `DM_NVN_SUB_MERGE=0`; a submission that ends after the GPU's work, before the feedback has reported it, is committed at once, which `DM_NVN_SUB_IDLE_FB=1` holds; skipped with `DM_NVN_SUB_DRAIN=1`). On a device: `DM_NVN_GPUPROF=every:300`, then the gaps before each submission's first encoder in `dm/tools/gpuprof.py --timeline <frame>`, and play where textures stream in.

### The set of CPU-written textures hashed by serial

- **Symptom:** the submit thread's CPU samples half in `__CFBasicHashFindBucket_Linear` under `CFSetContainsValue` (from `tex_mtl_` and `tex_by_id`), a few hundred thousand to over a million instructions of translation per draw, the submission growing with the game's CPU-written textures.
- **Cause:** the set of textures the CPU writes (`tex_dirty_upload`, `mtl_tex_cpu_written`) was a CFSet with no callbacks, which hashes a serial as the number it is; serials come in order, so they filled one run of neighbouring buckets, and each lookup of a texture not in the set (nearly every one) probed to the end of the run. Released textures stayed in it.
- **Fix:** the host's own table (`nvn_hmap`, which mixes the key). `mtl_tex_release` removes live membership and the `packaged textures` log line counts live entries. Recorded commands and registered descriptors can still use a finalized texture, including one written after recording: its CPU-write classification moves to a retired serial-to-pool table and remains until that pool is finalized. Both marking and release lock `g_smu` before `g_cpu_tex_mu`, so a dirty-page poll marking an already finalized snapshot cannot put it back in the live set. Retired metadata can accumulate while a pool remains alive; earlier eviction requires tracking snapshot ownership.
- **Found on:** Batman: Arkham Knight (`0100ACD0163D0000`; profile not present here), reported in [PR #321](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/321). The author's gameplay measurements have not been independently reproduced here.
- **Check:** on an M4 Pro, three runs of one million misses with 6,442 consecutive serials and the same mutex took 1,693–1,707 ms with the callback-free CFSet and 9.6–10.0 ms with `nvn_hmap`. `t_cpu_tex` checks membership, duplicates, release, late dirty-page marking, pool cleanup and lookup cost without a GPU; `--metal` also records a texture-to-buffer copy and descriptor registration, writes before or after recording, finalizes and overwrites the API object, and verifies the pixels on replay. The PR's unconditional deletion failed four pixel checks; preserving retired classification passes them.

### Geometry boundaries submit prepared work early

- **Symptom:** several milliseconds of CPU translation and geometry preparation separate one frame's GPU end from the next frame's GPU start, even though the frame already contains complete geometry passes.
- **Cause:** segmented submission was checked only at the game's render-target changes. Geometry replay creates additional capture/compute/raster encoder boundaries inside one target binding, but their commands stayed on the CPU while the next run was prepared.
- **Fix:** after closing the previous geometry run's encoders and landing deferred updates, check the existing `sub_cut` policy before preparing the next run. Its draw threshold, deferral exclusions, upload flush, final-fence lifetime and segment timestamp handling remain in force. This adds submissions at existing encoder boundaries without adding render passes. `DM_NVN_GEOMETRY_CUT=0` disables the additional checks; `DM_NVN_CUT=0` still disables draw-count cuts overall. GPU dependency and pixel checks pass with either policy.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#library-shadow-sequence-and-geometry-ordering). In an alternating warmed workload of 1,024 geometry/query draws at 1080p, median CPU-encode-to-GPU-completion time falls from 11.605 to 7.800 ms; render passes stay at 17 and all query results agree. GPU execution is essentially unchanged (4.004 versus 4.007 ms), while CPU encoding rises from 6.971 to 7.268 ms: the gain is overlap, not reduced shader work. Gameplay improvement remains unmeasured.

### The mesh stage's argument table set at every mesh draw

- **Symptom:** one `setArgumentTable` record a geometry mesh draw on the encode thread, also for draws gathered into a run.
- **Cause:** `geo_mesh_take` set the mesh stage's table before deciding whether the draw joins a run, whose flush (`geo_mesh_flush_`) sets it itself.
- **Fix:** only a mesh draw that draws alone sets it (`geo_mesh_encode` in `nvn_geometry.inc`).
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game): about 2,500 mesh draws a frame on Layer 1400.

### Reusing upload staging after a slot completes

- **Symptom:** CPU submission spikes during texture updates, with the residency log showing tens of MB of new private staging allocations per frame.
- **Cause:** an upload slot retained a block sized for the first texture in its batch. A small first texture left it at 4 MB, so later textures repeatedly allocated and retired 32 MB overflow blocks even when the same batch shape repeated.
- **Fix:** the slot's next primary block grows to its previous batch demand (capped at 32 MB), and it also retains one 32 MB overflow block. Reuse still follows `up_begin`'s wait for that slot's previous GPU batch. Each retained block is used at most once per batch; larger bursts and individual uploads over 32 MB keep the temporary, deferred-retirement path. Retained capacity is bounded at 64 MB per slot / 192 MB overall, and given back after 600 submissions without a batch that needs it ([Upload staging blocks kept after the uploads stop](memory.md#upload-staging-blocks-kept-after-the-uploads-stop)). `DM_NVN_UPLOAD_REUSE=0` restores the original policy. `t_upload_slots.m` checks distinct GPU-copied bytes across pending batches, an event-gated slot wait, warm allocation reuse, overflow beyond the budget and oversized allocations; both policies pass.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#upload-stability-follow-up). This removes allocation churn in the controlled workload; a gameplay comparison with the updated build is pending.

### Upload batches waiting for one of three slots

- **Symptom:** in a game that makes many new textures a frame, the submit thread waits several milliseconds a frame while the GPU idles; with `DM_NVN_WAITS=1` the `[waits]` line shows the time under `upload slot`.
- **Cause:** upload batches rotate over three slots, each with its own command allocator and staging, and `up_begin` waits until a slot's previous batch has come back before it reuses the slot. A game that makes its new textures in several batches a frame (Breath of the Wild: about 20 first uploads a frame in play, in several segments of a submission) runs out of slots three batches ahead of the GPU.
- **Fix:** per game (`up_slots_pick`, `g_up_nslots` in `nvn_mtl.m`; adapted from MacNativeGames 6ecb27c1 (MIT)). The profile key `upload_slots` sets how many slots rotate, 3 to 8 (3 without it); Breath of the Wild sets 8. The count is fixed for the run in `mtl_init`, before any batch, as a batch's number gives its slot (`up_seq_back`). Every per-slot table (busy flag, completed batch, staging and parameter blocks, idle-trim clocks) is sized for 8, and the idle trims and the stall dumps go over the slots in use. The S2Arm per-slot parameter blocks (`g_up_slot_pblk`) and the staging pool (`DM_NVN_STAGE_POOL_MB`) work per slot as before. Memory: each slot keeps up to a 4 MB block (more, up to 64 MB with its overflow block, while its batches need it, given back after 600 idle submissions), so 8 slots can hold more staging than 3. `DM_NVN_UP_SLOTS=<n>` overrides the profile (3 to 8; kept in player builds, so `=3` is the phone A/B's off side); the log's start says `upload batches: N slots in rotation (…)`.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_up_slot_count`: `up_slots_pick` takes the profile's count, a whole-number override held to 3 to 8, and ignores one that is not a number; with 8 slots, 8 batches held behind a GPU event are all in flight at once with no wait, the ninth waits for the first slot's batch, every batch's bytes land, `up_seq_back` reports each batch once it has come back, and the parameter blocks and staging pool keep working (`check_t_upload_slots`, `check_t_up_params`, `check_t_stage_pool` pass with 3 slots as before).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) by MacNativeGames (1.9.0 on a Mac, 2026-11-17): about 6 ms a frame of slot waits with 3 slots, 0.0-0.2 ms with 8, 50.5 → 54.5 fps in the same state; Tears of the Kingdom 1.4.3 showed no gain (GPU-bound with 3 and with 8), so it keeps 3. Not yet run on NX 18 or an iPhone.

### Small array texture uploads without per-face staging

- **Symptom:** periodically rewritten textures cause CPU submission spikes even with no new staging allocations or shader compilation; a small cube-array image produces thousands of deswizzle dispatches and buffer-to-texture copies.
- **Cause:** the staged upload path encodes a deswizzle dispatch and a copy for every face and mip. Face count can dominate encoding cost even when total pixel data is small.
- **Fix:** mapped, non-sparse RGBA16F 2D/cube arrays with at least 32 faces and dimensions at most 64×64 write all faces in one dispatch per mip. An RGBA16Uint view preserves the exact stored component bits, including half-float NaN payloads, without floating-point conversion. Temporary views live until GPU completion; direct targets participate in dropped-upload recovery. Initial fills retain their original texture and usage contract. Large images, other formats, linear/compressible layouts, multisampling and unaligned sources keep the staged path. `DM_NVN_DIRECT_UPLOAD=0` restores it for all uploads. `t_direct_upload.m` compares all mip/face bits after CPU edits and spare reuse, checks fallback and initial fills, and injects a lost direct upload; Metal validation passes 26,384 checks normally and 26,388 with recovery injection.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#small-light-probe-array-uploads). Its 16×16×768, five-mip RGBA16F array needs five direct dispatches instead of 3,840 staged dispatches and 3,840 copies. In an alternating warmed benchmark on M4 Pro, CPU upload encoding plus commit falls from 3.916 to 0.0275 ms and upload GPU time from 1.802 to 0.0355 ms. Gameplay and terminal-screen improvement remain unmeasured.

### Re-uploading only the written layers of a texture

- **Symptom:** a few textures the game updates in small pieces are re-uploaded whole every frame: the packaged-textures line's "re-uploads on pool content change" and "pages unprotected before host writes" climb by hundreds and by about a million each 300 frames, and the residency set adds tens of GB per 300 frames from the line of `up_big_src` in `nvn_mtl.m`.
- **Cause:** three things. `nvnTextureWriteTexels` unprotected the texture's whole storage, every layer and level, for a region of any size (`write_texels` in `hle_nvn.c`), so the next use found all of it written. A re-upload then deswizzled every level of every layer from the storage. And each staged upload made its no-copy source buffer over the storage twice: once before the GPU path and again after `up_alloc`, whether or not `up_alloc` had flushed the batch that held the first, so both were wired and added to the residency set.
- **Fix:** `WriteTexels` unprotects only the level it writes in each layer the region touches (`write_texels_prepare`, by the span `mirror_prepare` computes; the whole storage when that cannot be computed). A re-upload (`pkg_upload_`) looks at the storage of each level of each layer: one not written since the content it builds on was read is kept and not deswizzled. The re-upload goes into the spare when the GPU is done with it, and the spare holds the storage as read at its own upload (`spare_stamp`). Otherwise it goes into a new texture, which takes the kept levels with a GPU texture-to-texture copy from the texture it replaces, in the upload batch. A re-upload builds on the spare or on the replaced texture only once the upload batch that last wrote it has come back and was not dropped (`up_seq_back`, `g_up_lost`). A batch still running may yet be dropped, and then a copy from it would keep its unwritten texels for good. A batch still open has its copies in the same encoder, with no order among them. Such a re-upload is whole, and the packaged-textures line counts it under "made whole as what they would keep was not known good". The source buffer then ends with the last layer it reads. This applies only to the staged GPU deswizzle of a texture that is never drawn into or written by a shader, and has not been upgraded (no render-target or shader-write usage, and no usage beyond what its description gives: `tex_upgrade` fills its new texture with a blit in the submission, after the upload batch, and image use was missed at first) and does not take the direct path. The GPU also writes copies, clears and render passes into a texture's storage the CPU can read (`gsb_store`, from the upstream merge), which the page tracking does not see. Each such store is logged with its storage range and the command buffer it was encoded in (`gsb_log_note`), and a level of a layer that a store since the content it builds on overlaps counts as written (`gsb_log_since`), whichever texture object over the storage the store went through. Before, a store through another texture object (another format over the same bytes) left that layer kept from the spare or the replaced texture for good. A re-upload into the spare after a store through the texture's own record is whole (`stored`). When the log has overwritten a store it would need, the re-upload is whole. A dropped upload batch is handled per texture. A re-upload that would build on a texture in the record is whole, and so takes the texture it goes into out of the record. The redo of a texture found clean and in the record (`up_lost_take`) is whole and builds on nothing, and the texture it replaces is not used as a spare to build on. A re-upload that would keep every level of every layer is whole too. The record's count used to turn the partial path off for every texture, and it could stay above 0 for the rest of the session. A page that two levels or layers share counts as written for both. `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_PART=0` turns it off. The source buffer is made again only when `up_alloc` began a new batch (`g_up_seq` moved).
- **Check:** `t_texels_layers`, with the encode thread on and off and with `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_PART=0`. It uses an eight-layer, two-level 128×128 RGBA8 array in a pool of its own. A region written into one layer leaves the other layers unwritten. The first re-upload copies 15 levels of layers from the old texture and deswizzles 1. The next one goes into the spare and deswizzles the 3 written since the spare's upload. Each staged re-upload makes one no-copy source buffer. Two re-uploads in one submission make the second one whole. A dropped upload of another texture, freed since, leaves the next re-upload partial and the record's count goes back to 0. A dropped upload of this texture, with no spare, is redone whole. A re-upload in the submission that gave the texture image use (`tex_upgrade`) is whole. A re-upload into a new texture while a shared event holds the batch that wrote the texture it replaces is whole, and the next one after that batch has come back is partial again. A GPU copy into a layer of the texture, then a CPU write into another, makes a whole re-upload into the spare. A GPU copy into a layer through an RGBA8UI texture over the same storage, then a CPU write into another layer, makes a partial re-upload that deswizzles the copied layer, into the spare and then into a new texture. Every readback of both levels of all layers matches what the CPU and the GPU copies wrote, through writes into every layer, a whole-layer rewrite and the two re-uploads in one batch.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#heavy-scenes-one-submission-per-occlusion-query-2026-11-09). In its heavy scenes it writes regions of 28×28 to 244×244 into layers 0 and 1 of a 32 MB RGBA16F and a 16 MB RGBA8 16-layer 512×512 array every frame, about 14 calls a frame. Each call unprotected 133 MB, and the two arrays were re-uploaded whole, through two 48 MB source buffers a frame. On the Mac, on the route in its note, pages unprotected before host writes fell from about 493K to 37K and residency added from 14.7 to 4.4 GB per 300 frames in the heavy windows. In an earlier pair of runs with `DM_NVN_SIGNAL_EVENTS=split`, so with only these changes, they fell from 419K to 22K and from 12.4 to 2.3 GB. About 80% of its re-uploads were partial (33,156 of 41,100 in the run), keeping 77% of their levels of layers; 6 were made whole because the batch they would build on had not come back. Its third texture, a 16×16 cube array with 768 faces, takes the direct path and is still uploaded whole.

### A whole atlas re-uploaded several times a frame for a few written pages

- **Symptom:** in Ocarina of Time a single-level atlas is re-uploaded several times in one frame (one texture 11 times in frame 4117; 176 MB staged where 5% of its pages were written), and about 50 to 70 long frames of a long run fall on such storms (`DM_NVN_REUPLOADS` lines; NX18-X2.0 `oot-alldata-analysis.md`, fix 4).
- **Cause:** two things. The partial re-upload keeps or deswizzles whole images (a layer's level), so a texture of one level and one layer always went up whole.
