And every use of a packaged texture re-uploads it when any of its pages was written since its upload, but the replay runs behind the game: the game's writes for its later submissions (the next glyphs of the atlas, say) were found at each use in the submission being replayed, which re-uploaded each time.
- **Fix:** `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_COALESCE` (on; `=0` restores the old behaviour, both halves). Banded: a re-upload deswizzles only the block-row bands of a written image (8 << the level's block height rows of blocks, contiguous in the storage) whose bytes were written since the content it builds on was read (`band_scan`, `nvn_deswz_gpu_encode_rows_m4`); the other bands stay as the spare it goes into holds them, or are copied by region from the texture it replaces (`up_tcopy_add_at`). 3D levels, one-band levels and images written in every band go up whole as before. Deferred: `nvn_queue_submit` reads the dirty-page clock at the game's submit (`nq_job.written`), the replay hands it to the submission (`mtl_sub_cpu_limit`; after a wait on that queue, at least the clock when the wait passed, `wait_written`, since the game may write and then set the event word the GPU waits for), and a use whose written pages were all first written after that clock keeps its texture (`reup_defer`, `nvn_dirty_needed`): a draw in that submission must see every write the game made before it submitted, which the texture holds, and on the console the GPU may have run it before the later ones. The first use in a submission submitted after them re-uploads them all at once, and within one submission a texture goes up at most once (its stamp is past the limit). A page another upload protected again after a write (another texture object over the same storage, an edge page; `pgen` in `nvn_dirty.c`) or that changed while open counts as written before any limit, since its first write's time is lost. Presents and anything outside a queue submission have no limit. After a deferral the next limit moves the dirty clock (`nvn_dirty_tick`), so the texture-ID and argument caches that skip `tex_mtl_` while it stands still look again. The packaged-textures line counts the deferrals and the bytes they did not read then, and the banded re-uploads with their images and the bytes kept.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_reupload_bands` (the encode thread off and on, `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_COALESCE=0`, `DM_NVN_REUPLOAD_PART=0`): a 1024×1024 RGBA8 and a 2048×2048 BC7 atlas, a three-level RGBA8 array and an R16 cube get rows written in one or two bands, through the texture they replace and through the spare, each read back whole; submissions made with writes between them and replayed after them, a write landing between two lookups of one submission, several writes each before its own submission, and a write whose time another texture object's protect hid (which must re-upload; with `pgen` left out of `nvn_dirty_needed` the test fails). Every texture a lookup returns holds what the game wrote before its submission's submit.
- **Found on:** Ocarina of Time (NX18-X2.0 `oot-alldata-analysis.md`). Not measured on the device yet: the expected gain is the 50 to 70 long frames of the storms, and most of the 176 MB staged a storm frame.

### Mesh draws looked up their pipeline and run twin every draw

- **Symptom:** in a geometry-shader prepass the submit thread's samples in `+[NSValue valueWithPointer:]`, dictionary lookups, `pso_key_fill` and the mesh pipeline table's lock.
- **Cause:** each geometry mesh draw boxed its function pointer into an `NSValue` for the `g_geo_mesh_runfn` lookup and locked/hashed `g_pso_mesh` on every draw, including draws that then drew alone.
- **Fix:** `geo_mesh_pso_for` (`dm/gfx/nvn/nvn_geometry.inc`) returns the last built pipeline when `pso_key` matches, and `geo_mesh_take` caches the last mesh function's run twin until `g_geo_mesh_runfn_gen` advances.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game)'s map ([PR #1847](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1847)): translation 9.7 → 9.0 ms a frame on an M4 MacBook Air.

### Address to memory pool lookups by binary search

- **Symptom:** the submit thread's CPU samples dominated by `mtl_addr` / `mtl_gpu_addr` (vertex buffers in `draw`, uniform buffers in `encode_args`).
- **Cause:** with many memory pools alive, the lookups scanned them all for every buffer binding of every draw.
- **Fix:** they try the last hit, then a binary search over pools sorted by start address (`pool_held`). The index is sorted at its first use and then follows each pool entry registered or removed in place (`pidx_added`, `pidx_removing`), a large pool's chunks made or lengthened too; a large pool itself is not in it, so registering or removing one leaves it alone. It used to be invalidated by every registration or removal and re-sorted whole at the next lookup: the S2Arm project measured that `qsort` at 3.3% of its submit thread's samples (48 of 1,452 in 10 s) in a game that makes or frees a pool or two a frame among about 1,760 (ported from S2Arm 8d2bafd0). When the index was not valid or an entry is not where the order says, it is invalidated and the next lookup sorts it whole as before.
- **Check:** `t_pool_index`: 600 pools registered in a shuffled order over 1,024 slots, then 2,000 random registrations and removals; the index stays valid at every step and every sampled lookup finds the pool the test's own slot table says (about 67,000). A large pool's chunk made and lengthened, and the pool removed, keep it valid too; an index entry corrupted on purpose is found by the next removal and the index re-sorted. Not measured in a game here.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md): about 1,760 memory pools alive; a CPU sample of the submit thread had about 1,000 of its samples under those scans. Host submit time in the shrine went from 21 ms to 7 ms per frame (p50), the median frame from 38 ms to 21 ms.

### Per-draw replay work cut (upstream ports, 2026-11-23)

- **Symptom:** on upstream's Donkey Kong Bananza Layer 1400 run (M5 Pro), the game waits for the replay of an older frame before recording more, so the replay's time per draw is the frame's critical path: frame time p50 18.2 ms above about 10,700 draws.
- **Fix:** six of upstream's seven changes, ported by hand (MIT; `docs/development.md` port table): pool addresses looked up by 64 KB region first (`a359f9e65`, `pc_get` / `pc_put`, `t_pool_region`); the mesh stage's argument table set only for a lone mesh draw (`a7821cfe0`); only the vertex streams the vertex shader reads looked up, bound and laid out (`c1bd080ae`, `t_vbuf`); no retain / release per draw in the draw helpers (`5ccb82adf`: borrowed locals, `enc_r_pso`, `cbuf_slot`'s out-parameter; `prog_get` / `pso_get` were already borrowed here); same-pass feedback views cached with the texture's views (`3a7e53d3c`, `fb_view`; this tree's cache is by texture serial, so only while the texture ID is registered); and the per-draw checks (`fb_depth_rule`, `fb_prepare`, `ubd_conflict_sh`) walking only a shader's texture and buffer bindings (`e49e48145`, `sh_tex_list` / `sh_buf_list`). Not taken: `00f98d5db` (its buffer-image write-back, `post_shadow_wb`, is not in this tree). Upstream measured the six together at 18.2 → 16.7 ms p50 on that route.
- **Check:** `t_pool_region` (51), `t_vbuf` (all runs), `t_feedback`, `t_geometry_mesh`, `t_arena_trim`, `t_args_xpass`, `t_args_partial`, `t_nvn`. Not checked yet: an iPhone run (the replay's CPU per frame in the `translation` line, and CPU power).

### Pool lookups by page missed with thousands of pools

- **Symptom:** the replay thread's samples under `mtl_gpu_addr`'s cache misses: `pc_get`, then `pool_held` and its lock.
- **Cause:** the per-thread cache (`t_pc`) was direct-mapped by 4 KB page with 1,024 slots. A pool of megabytes was found only from pages already seen and still kept, so a game binding from thousands of pools fell through to the global lock and the [binary search](#address-to-memory-pool-lookups-by-binary-search) again and again.
- **Fix:** a second table direct-mapped by 64 KB region is looked at first (`pc_slot_w` in `nvn_mtl.m`), filled and cleared with the page table. Every entry is still checked against its pool's range (`pc_holds`), so only the hit rate changes. It costs about 57 KB more thread-local memory in each thread that looks pools up.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game): about 3,500 pools; the lookups were about 5 % of the replay thread on Layer 1400 (a Metal System Trace's time profile of the replay thread before these changes).

### Argument blocks encoded again when only a uniform buffer moved

- **Symptom:** a submit thread busy in `encode_args` with few blocks reused: the per-frame TSV's `@args_enc` close to `@draws`, though most draws keep their textures and samplers.
- **Cause:** binding a uniform buffer marked the stage's arguments dirty (`g_args_dirty`), and a dirty stage encodes its whole block again: every texture, sampler, image and storage slot, though only the CBUF slots moved. A game that binds a new uniform buffer between most draws paid for a full encode at nearly every draw. An inline update deferred inside a pass that overlaps a bound uniform buffer (`ubd_dirty`: the draws after it read a shadow) did the same.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every NVN game. A change of what a stage's uniform slots point at, a binding (`OP_BIND_UBO`) or a deferred update over one (`ubd_dirty`), takes a uniform generation of its own (`g_args_ugen`, beside `g_args_gen` for any binding: [argument blocks valid by generation](rendering.md#an-argument-block-reused-after-another-shader-of-its-stage-encoded)). When only that generation moved since the block was written, the block of the same pass is still reusable, and the shader reads its uniform buffers only as uniforms (`args_cbuf_only`: an allow-list of binding kinds, decided once per shader; handles or addresses read out of a uniform buffer keep the full encode), the old block is copied to a new arena slot and only its uniform slots and their size table are written again. Both paths take a slot from `cbuf_slot` (the binding, a deferred update's shadow, a [zero-padded copy of a short binding](shaders.md#reads-past-a-uniform-bindings-size), or the zero bank) and the table from `cbsize_table`, so a patched block is the one a full encode would write. The resource check, a pass dump and stage 5 keep the full encode. `@args_patch` counts patched blocks.
- **Note:** the patched copy's address is read when its block is taken (`ag`), before `cbuf_slot` and `cbsize_table` take arena space of their own: when that space came from a new block, `g_cur_arena` named the new block and the copy was bound at the old one's offset there, bytes never written. With a block per submission this took a submission past 4 MB; with blocks shared between submissions ([Per-frame arena blocks made for every submission in flight](memory.md#per-frame-arena-blocks-made-for-every-submission-in-flight)) it hung NieR:Automata's GPU 1-4 times in 160 s. `t_arena_trim` checks it. Since upstream NXNative `1073e98b2` every allocation returns its own addresses (`arena_take`, `arena_at`: the offset with the CPU and GPU addresses as of the allocation), and no caller outside the allocator reads `g_cur_arena`: the argument blocks, the uniform size table, padded uniform copies, push constants, the driver banks, the border block and the 16- and 8-bit index expansions.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game), in the first form (binding changes only, no size table; [PR #1611](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1611)): 7,094 full encodes a frame fell to 1,489 (5,645 patched); submit-thread instructions per draw 14.4k to 13.6k, 60 fps on both (M3 Pro, `scripts/bench.py`, 3 runs each, an early-game route the submit thread does not limit). The reuse was measured first in [issue #1290](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1290). **Open:** the form here, with the size table and the deferred updates, is not measured on Bananza; the deferred updates matter most in Breath of the Wild (221 small updates of a uniform block a frame).

### Argument blocks encoded again when push constants were set to the same bytes

- **Symptom:** an NVN2 game's `[args-stats]` lines (`DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`) count many full encodes under `push`.
- **Cause:** `OP_PUSH_CONST` copied the bytes and marked every stage of its group hard-dirty (`args_dirty_w(AW_PUSH)`) even when they were the bytes the state held already, so the next draw of each stage encoded its block again.
- **Fix:** `state_op` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every NVN2 game (after S2Arm 5442e030f; S2Arm's other half, push and pool bindings on the patch path, NX 18 already had): the same bytes change nothing. `DM_NVN_PUSH_DEDUP=0` copies and marks them as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_stats`: the same push constants again reuse the block, with no full encode counted; with `DM_NVN_PUSH_DEDUP=0` the draw encodes again. **Open:** not measured in a game; S2Arm names the NVN2 games (Ocarina of Time and Xenoblade X Switch 2 Editions, Mario Kart World, Donkey Kong Bananza, Pokopia, Fast Fusion) without numbers.

### Argument blocks encoded again at every pass and program change

- **Symptom:** the replay thread busy in `encode_args` though the draws keep their textures, samplers and uniform buffers: the per-frame TSV's `@args_enc` close to `@draws`, `@args_reuse` low.
- **Cause:** a block was reused only within the render pass that encoded it (`ab_pass`, upstream NXNative `3fe574dfe`), and every new pass and every program change moved every stage's binding generation (`args_dirty_all`). A game that switches programs between most draws, or draws the same materials over many passes, encoded every block again at nearly every draw. The pass rule guarded the arena: a block from another pass was said to be possibly reclaimed and rewritten. It cannot be within its submission: arena blocks are bump-allocated, held in `g_sub_arenas` until the submission's `arena_commit`, and taken again (`arena_new`) or released (`arena_trim`) only once that submission's fence has completed.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every NVN game (`DM_NVN_ARGS_XPASS=0` keeps the pass rule). A new pass and a new program move only the pass rule's generation (`args_dirty_soft`); every other reason (a binding, push constants, a pool base, queue switches, restored state, the host's own encoders, drains, upgrades, registrations, guest writes, replaced textures through `args_sync_dirty`) also moves a hard one per stage (`g_args_hgen`). A vertex or fragment block written for the pass's own encoder is reused in a later pass or after another program while it is of the same submission (`ab_sub`) and its stage's hard generation holds, with the pass rule's other checks (base instance, pool and handle tables, uniform slots, patched when only those moved) and the pass's sample count (c0). A block that holds copies of memory (a padded or deferred-update uniform copy, an aligned copy of a buffer texture: `g_ab_snap`) or handles and addresses read from uniform memory (an allow-list of kinds, `args_xkind`) also needs no write queued or made since (`g_npend`, `g_pend_over`, `g_wr_epoch`, `g_tba_gen`, `g_sbw_gen`); a same-pass feedback snapshot never outlives its draw, and the geometry emulation's buffers, the raw pool table and kinds not on the list keep the pass rule. A block reused so becomes the pass rule's current block, and its stage counts as written, so an update over its uniform slots moves the uniform generation (`ubd_dirty` compares the hard generation). `@args_xpass` counts the reuses and patches only this rule allowed.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_xpass`: draws over passes of one submission into a 16x1 target, with every pixel read back. A later pass and a return after another program reuse; a new texture handle, sampler, registration, memory pool, guest write stamp or storage binding of the stage encodes; a new uniform address patches; an inline update over handles in uniform memory encodes; a block with a padded uniform copy is reused while nothing is written and encoded after a fill elsewhere; a new submission encodes; the switch at 0 encodes at every pass and program change. Its bench, a shader of 16 textures, 8 samplers and 4 uniform slots on an M-series Mac (`-O1`, the Metal API validation layer on): a full encode about 350-410 ns, a cross-pass reuse about 10-12 ns, a reuse within the pass about 9-10 ns.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): an iPhone profile put argument encoding at about 2-3.4 ms a frame of the replay thread (46% of its draw path) for 1,000-2,500 draws a frame. **Open:** not measured in a game yet: how many of those encodes the rule turns into reuses (`@args_xpass` against `@args_enc`) and what that saves on the phone.

### Argument blocks encoded in full when one binding kind moved

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`, most full argument encodes counted under a texture, sampler or storage-buffer rebind, a base instance change or `ubo_unpatchable`, though each such encode walks every binding again.
- **Cause:** any hard change of a stage's bindings (`args_dirty_w`) made every block of the stage encode in full: a new texture in one slot re-resolved all of its textures and samplers with their fix-ups, the uniform slots and their size table, the pool tables, c0 and c2, and the storage buffers. A base instance change did the same for a shader that reads c0. The uniform patch (`args_cbuf_only`) took only shaders whose kinds were on a short list, which left out buffer textures, the dynamic and driver-table handle arrays and pool shaders though none of them reads through a uniform buffer, and shaders with no uniform slot at all.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every NVN game (`DM_NVN_ARGS_PARTIAL=0` restores the full encodes). A texture, sampler or storage binding change still moves the hard generation, but also goes into a history per stage (`g_args_pk`, 8 entries: the kind and, for textures and samplers, the slots that moved, bit slot % 64; when it fills, its two oldest entries are joined, so an older block is patched for more than changed since it, never less); every other hard change empties it and raises its floor, so a block from before it is never patched. A vertex or fragment block that a reuse would take but for those changes (of this pass with no new pass or program since, or kept by the cross-pass rule with its checks; pool and handle tables current) is copied, and the full encode's loop runs over the bindings made from what changed only (`pk_take`): the textures of the slots that moved with their fix-ups, the samplers that moved (every sampler and the border block for a shader with border twins), the handle arrays and c2 after any texture or sampler change, the storage buffers with their storage-write notes and c0 after a storage change, c0 after a base instance change, and the uniform slots with their size table when they moved too. The entries it keeps hold for the block as a reuse's do: same submission, no hard change outside the history. Shaders reading handles or addresses from uniform memory, the raw pool table or the geometry emulation's buffers stay on the full path. `ubd_dirty` no longer skips a stage whose only changes since its last block are in the history: the patch must see an update over the uniform slots. With the switch on the uniform patch also takes buffer textures, TEXD/SAMPD, TEXA/SAMPA and pool shaders, and a shader that reads no uniform keeps its block after a uniform change. The full encode's textures take their type and pixel format from the registration's view record (`tex_fast_rid_tp`, `view_tt` / `view_pf`) instead of two Objective-C messages each, with the switch either way.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_partial`: draws into a 64x1 target whose channels show texture slot 0 through sampler 0, texture slot 1, a storage buffer times a uniform and c0's base instance, every pixel read back, with the switch on and then off in the same run. A texture, sampler, storage or base instance change alone patches; texture and uniform together patch; changes made while another shader of the stage wrote blocks in between are all seen; a later pass patches across; more changes than the history has entries still patch, with the joined kinds and slots; a registration, push constants or an image binding with them, or another submission, encode in full. Bench, a shader of 16 textures, 8 samplers, 4 uniform slots, 2 storage buffers and c0 on the Mac (`-O1`, Metal API validation on, best of 5): full encode 360-410 ns; partial patch of one texture 120-130 ns, one sampler 115-128 ns, a storage buffer 245-265 ns, a base instance 240-265 ns (c0's 2.5 KB bank is made again), a texture and a uniform slot 163-175 ns. `t_args_stats`' bench (16 textures, 8 samplers, 4 uniform slots) gives about 209 ns a full encode with the cached type and format against 256 ns with the messages. `check_t_args_review` draws six cases a review found handled but untested, every pixel read back, with the switches at their defaults and with `DM_NVN_ARGS_PARTIAL=0`, `DM_NVN_ARGS_XPASS=0`, `DM_NVN_ARGS_REGCHK=0` and `DM_NVN_ARGS_DRV=0`: an inline update deferred in the pass over the uniform slot with a texture change (the patch writes both), a pool shader on the widened uniform patch and on partial patches, a border colour twin and c2 on a texture-only patch, a same-pass feedback snapshot with a texture rebind in the pass (the next draw is encoded in full), a cross-pass patch of a block of copies (refused after a buffer write and after a sample count change, c0 then holding the new count), and the vertex stage in a pass and across passes.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` on the iPhone counted about 1,900 full encodes a frame, 29% after a texture rebind, 25% a storage rebind, 10% a base instance change and 10% `ubo_unpatchable`, about 2.0 ms a frame (clock-inflated) of encode time. **Open:** the phone A/B; how many of those encodes become partial patches (`[args-stats] partial patches per frame`), and which kinds keep the rest of `ubo_unpatchable` off the patch (its new per-kind counts).

### Pool table lookups searched again at each full encode

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`, the full encodes' time by kind shows "pool and handle tables" as a large share (Ocarina of Time: 14% of about 2.0 ms a frame), and it is not known which part: the lookups of current tables, the patches and walks of tables a registration or storage change made stale, or the handle tables read from uniform memory.
- **Cause:** each full encode of a pool shader (TEXP / SAMPP) called `pool_gpu`, which ran the read-mark scan's test, two atomics, a search of the 16 tables and the left-out test before returning a current table; no line split the time by how a lookup ended.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m` (`DM_NVN_POOL_MEMO=0` restores the search at every lookup). A lookup is kept per table (sampler or texture, element type, pixel class, base) by `pool_memo_put` when `pool_walk` returns a table its first check would return next (the first check itself, a patch kept or written, a walk), and taken while the registration and storage generations, the submission (`pool_seen_scan` runs once in each; transient entries go at its end), `g_psnap_gen` and, for a table whose walk left textures out for a later frame, the frame hold. With `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` a "levers" line gives the pool table lookups by how they ended (memo, first check, patch kept, patched, standing table, walk) and the handle table lookups (`htab_gpu`: kept for the pass, read again and the same, the same entries, built), each with its time.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_levers`: a repeated lookup is a memo hit with what the first check returns; a registration (the table patched, the new texture in it), a storage change without a note (built again), `g_psnap_gen`, a new submission and, with textures left out, a new frame each go through `pool_walk` again; off, no hit. Mac micro-benchmark (M4 Max, -O1, Metal validation on): about 5 ns a lookup through the search, 3 ns kept. The lookup of a current table was never the cost: the levers line on the phone is what tells whether the share is the patches and walks (registrations and storage changes, needed) or the handle tables.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), from the bindless study's step 0 (NX18-X2.0 bindless-study.md, bindless-plan.md stage 1).

### Pool table patches counted as table work

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`, "pool and handle tables" is a large share of the full encodes' time by kind (Ocarina of Time on 8f17ac43: 13-43% of 1.0-3.3 ms a frame, 22% over the run), and a note read the levers line as showing almost no pool table lookups, so the share looked unexplained.
- **Cause:** the share is the pool tables' patch path, not the lookups of current tables. On that run's busy windows the levers line counts 80-230 pool table lookups a frame: 40-55 memo hits and 50-70 first checks at 0.02-0.09 us each, 2.5-6 patches kept at 1-1.6 us, and 4-7 tables **patched** at 30-180 us each (0.15-0.8 ms a frame, the group's time almost exactly: median 0.33 ms both). The early (loading) windows and Mac test logs do show zeros, which is where the "almost none" reading came from. A patch takes every ID of the registration and storage logs (and the dead and transient lists) again in every table of the pool, and the first lookup of a newly registered ID goes past its cached view (`tex_by_id_ptr`): its view is made, or its texture made and uploaded within the walk's budget, inside the encode's pool binding. That cost belongs to the texture, not the table.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`. Measurement: with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` the time a thread spends in texture lookups past the cached view (`tl_tex_slow`, outermost `tex_by_id_ptr` call) is its own group in the full encodes' split ("texture lookups past the cached view (made, uploaded, checked)", `AG_TEXSLOW`), taken out of whichever binding group asked (textures and samplers as well as pool tables), and the levers line splits the patch path (`g_ppw`): patch paths and the IDs each took again, time collecting them, resolving them (`pool_entry`), texture lookups past the cached view, table copies (`pool_patch_take`), read-mark scans (`pool_seen_scan`) and whole builds. Cheaper entries (`DM_NVN_POOL_ENTRY=0` restores the old order): an ID whose texture type is known (`tt1`) and is not the table's is answered before its storage, budget and stand-in checks, and the type comes from the view record (`tex_fast_rid_tp`), so the other table of the pool (2D and 2D array in Ocarina) skips a lock or three an ID and no longer counts such an ID as left out (a walk the next frame) or dead.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_levers`: a patch bench over two tables of 4,000 IDs (three in four 2D, one in four 2D arrays), 300 registered again a round; every entry of both tables is the ID's texture or the dummy, switch on and off. Mac (M-series, -O1, Metal validation on, no finished spares so every patch copies its table): about 30.7 us a patch (102 ns an ID) with `DM_NVN_POOL_ENTRY=0`, 29.2 us (97 ns) on; split per patch about 0.3 us collecting, 6 us resolving, 14.7 us texture lookups past the view (the IDs' first lookups after their registration), 9.4 us table copies.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), item 2 of NX18-X2.0 open-optimizations.md (the bindless plan's Stage 1). **Open:** the phone's split: how much of the 0.15-0.8 ms is texture lookups past the view (streamed textures made and uploaded for IDs no shader may read yet) against the tables' own work.

### Texture and sampler work a slot table would replace, not measured

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`, "textures and samplers" is the largest share of the full encodes' time by kind (Ocarina of Time on 8f17ac43: 29%), the gate the bindless plan set for its shader-chain stage (NX18-X2.0 bindless-plan.md: at least ~20%), but no line says how much of that work slot tables would take away: how often a table version would still be current at a full encode, how many entries a rebuild would resolve, what the copies would cost.
- **Cause:** the slot path resolves every texture and sampler binding again at each full encode (and each one that moved at a partial patch), however few of them changed; the plan's tables (resource IDs by NVN slot, copy-on-change arena blocks, read through a function-constant path of want-listed fragment shaders) exist only on paper, and the slot path's resolution lived inline in the encode loop.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, host only, no shader or pipeline change (bindless-plan.md stage 1). One resolver for both paths: `tex_resolve` and `samp_resolve` hold the slot path's texture and sampler resolution unchanged (the view with its cached type and format, a same-pass feedback snapshot, a buffer texture read in rows, `tex_fit` / `tex_float_fit` views, the dummy, a buffer texture's aligned copy; a border colour twin). `DM_NVN_BINDLESS=1` (measurement only, off by default) keeps the fragment stage's slot table beside the slot path (`bl_note`): an entry per texture slot (combined and separate, sampled 2D, 2D array, cube, cube array or 3D, no 1D fetch) and sampler slot (SAMP, SAMPT at 128 + its slot; no border twin), holding the slot path's value with the handle, declared type and class and kind it was resolved for. Entries are current within an epoch: the registration, guest-write and storage generations, `g_alt_gen` (a type or class view made again or let go: `tex_fit`, `tex_float_fit`, `img_fit`, `treg_drop_views`) and the submission; a move starts a new one. A version moves with any entry written and is copied into the arena at the encode that finds it moved (`bl_build`, textures then samplers). A shader whose last check saw the version, its stage's texture / sampler binding generation (`g_bl_tgen`: a texture, sampler or image slot, restored state, a queue switch, an upgrade; not push constants, feedback, host encoders, drains or dispatches) and the epoch would take the version without a look. Every fragment encode that resolved textures or samplers checks each converted binding: the slot path's value against the entry (a current entry that differs, or one moved under a version taken without a look, is a mismatch, logged `[RED]`), a same-pass feedback snapshot left out (a draw's own version in stage 2, and the shader looks again at its next encode). With `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` an `[args-stats] slot tables` line gives per frame those encodes and the ones that would take the version without a look, the converted bindings the slot path resolved against the entries the table needed (the share replaced, overall and in full encodes, with the full encodes' texture and sampler time it stands for), slot-bound texture and sampler bindings, feedback snapshots, versions built and their KB, new epochs by what moved, the checks' own time (also a group of the full encodes' split) and the mismatches.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_bindless_slots`: a fragment shader of five bindings (combined slot 0, combined slot 1 declared a 2D array over a 2D texture, separate slot 2, sampler slot 0, slot 0's handle sampler) encoded through `encode_args`, and a hand-written table shader that reads every texture and sampler from the version `bl_build` made, by slot, through typed views of one table of resource IDs: both pixels of each step read back with the texels the bindings name (the first draw, a full encode after push constants taking the version without a look, a rebind into a new block while the earlier draws keep theirs, a registration of a bound ID, a sampler rebind, an empty slot's dummy, a new submission's epoch); the table's entries equal the block's; no mismatch; off, nothing built. `check_t_args_partial`, `check_t_args_xpass`, `check_t_args_review` and `check_t_feedback` also run with `DM_NVN_BINDLESS=1` and fail on any mismatch (feedback snapshots, border twins, partial patches, cross-pass reuse). Mac bench (16 textures, 8 samplers, -O1, Metal validation on): full encode after a hard change 185-198 ns, 245-264 ns with the checks; the check alone 58-61 ns when its version is current (stage 2's reuse would be a compare of three numbers), 185-220 ns to write one entry and copy a version of 131 texture and 129 sampler entries (a separate slot and a combined slot's sampler make the table that long: stage 2 should pack the tables per shader or class).
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (bindless-plan.md stage 1; the stage-2 gate passed at 29%). **Open:** the phone run's slot tables line decides stage 2: the share of full-encode texture and sampler work replaced, the epochs (registrations move the whole table: a per-ID check as `DM_NVN_ARGS_REGCHK`'s would keep it), and the versions' bytes.

### Driver banks made again for every argument block

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`, "driver banks c0/c2" in the full encodes' time by kind (Ocarina of Time: 4%), and partial patches of a texture or storage change in shaders that read c2 or c0 costing about as much as the rest of the patch.
- **Cause:** each block of a shader that reads a driver segment took a 2,560-byte arena block, cleared it and filled it from the stage's bindings: c2 is about 305 handle words (combined, image, separate texture and sampler tables), c0 the storage table, point size, base instance and sample rows. Every shader of the stage that reads the bank gets the same bytes until one of those bindings moves, and a partial patch of one texture made the whole c2 again.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m` `drv_bank` (`DM_NVN_ARGS_DRV=0` restores a bank per block). Each graphics stage keeps its last c0 and c2 bank of the submission (its arena block lives as long as a block's), with the shader's handle form (dyn, pool bases) for c2 and the base instance and sample count for c0. A block takes it while the stage's binding history (`g_args_pk`, `pk_since`) holds every hard change since it was made and none of them is a kind it is made from; when they are all in the history it is copied and only the words they move are written again (texture slot bits name every slot of the bit: combined, separate; a combined slot past 31 keeps the image word it shares; sampler slots; the storage table; the base instance word; the sample rows); any other hard change makes it whole. Compute keeps a bank per block (the dispatch size, and an indirect dispatch writes its counts into the bank). The `[args-stats]` levers line counts the banks reused, patched and made per frame. Filling only the rows a shader reads (the study's lever d) was not done: the build records them (`drv_reads`) only for fragment shaders and only for reads of the form `(*spvDescriptorSet0.fp_cN)._m0[i]`, so a read the scan misses would find another block's bytes; with the banks shared, a bank is made once per binding change anyway.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_levers`: after each change (a storage buffer, a base instance, a sample count, textures in combined slots 0, 33 and 40 and separate slots 5 and 69, samplers 3 and 67, a combined slot past 31 under a bound image, more changes than the history holds) the bank is reused, patched or made as expected and holds the bytes of a bank made whole from the same state; an image binding, push constants, another handle form, another stage and a new submission make it whole. Mac micro-benchmark (M4 Max, -O1, Metal validation on), a shader with 16 textures, 8 samplers, 4 uniform slots, 2 storage buffers, c0 and c2: a texture-only partial patch 505 -> 300 ns, a full encode after a texture or a storage change (partial patches off) 790 -> 465 ns; storage and base instance patches and a full encode after another hard change are unchanged (c0 is about as cheap to make as to copy).
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), from the bindless study's step 0 (NX18-X2.0 bindless-study.md lever d, bindless-plan.md stage 1's c2 bank cache).

### Every argument block encoded in full after a texture registration

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1`, full argument encodes counted under `reg_tex` (a texture or sampler registration since the block), and binding syncs on registrations in the per-frame line, though the registered IDs are rarely the ones the blocks hold (Ocarina of Time: registrations between 0.18% of its draws, about 1% of its full encodes).
- **Cause:** `args_sync_dirty` moved every hard binding generation of every stage when `g_reg_gen` moved, whatever the registration was: every block was encoded in full at its next draw, its partial-patch history emptied. Pool shaders (TEXP / SAMPP) depended on that full encode to take their tables again: `pool_walk` patches a table only when asked.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m` (`DM_NVN_ARGS_REGCHK=0` restores the full encodes). When every move of `g_reg_gen` since the last sync is a texture or sampler registration read back from its note (`args_reg_notes`: none overwritten, no memory pool, which remaps the uniform and storage addresses every block holds) and no guest write or replaced object came with it, the sync stamps each registered ID with a new number of `g_args_seq` and raises `g_args_rck` to it instead. A block from before that (`ab_rck`) is reused or patched only after `args_rck` finds none of the texture, image and sampler IDs its stage's slots name stamped since (the IDs it was made from: its stage's hard generation held, or each change since is one the patch writes again); a shader with a binding kind off its allow-list (`args_rkind`: handles or addresses read from uniform memory, the raw pool table, handle arrays, the geometry buffers) is encoded in full, as before. A pool shader takes its tables again through `pool_gpu` in a partial patch (`PK_POOL`, `ab_pgen` stamped after) after a registration sync, and after another table's build or patch (`g_psnap_gen`), which was a full encode. Guest writes and replaced objects still move every hard generation: what blocks hold besides texture IDs (shadows, copies, packaged textures) follows them. With `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` full encodes are counted under `reg_id` (an ID it holds registered since) and `reg_unchecked` (kinds the check does not take), partial patches under "pool tables", and the levers line gives the syncs taken by ID or by every block and the blocks found current, stale or not checkable.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_args_levers`, pixel readback: after a texture or sampler registered at an ID no slot names the block is reused; after the IDs slot 0 names are registered with other data it is encoded in full and draws the new texels and wrap; a texture change with a registration elsewhere is still a partial patch; a pool shader is patched with its tables taken again and draws the texture registered at the ID it reads; a registration with a replaced object, a change without a note or a memory pool, and a shader with a dynamic handle array encode in full; switch off, every registration encodes in full. Mac micro-benchmark (M4 Max, -O1, Metal validation on), 16 textures, 8 samplers, 4 uniform slots: an encode after a registration elsewhere 245-280 ns in full, 66-72 ns checked and reused (the registration itself 23 ns).
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), from the bindless study (NX18-X2.0 bindless-study.md step 1 levers e and f, as corrected there; step 0 put registrations at about 1% of the full encodes, so this is a small gain there and larger in a game that registers between more of its draws).

### Texture lookups past the cached view after a write elsewhere

- **Symptom:** with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` and the pool tables' patch work split out, "texture lookups past the cached view (made, uploaded, checked)" became the largest group of the full encodes' time by kind on the phone (Ocarina of Time, handheld Balanced: 19-21%, about 0.28-0.33 ms a frame, with about 190 `tex_fit` fix-ups a frame), and the line gave only the total.
- **Cause:** a packaged (or CPU-written) texture ID's view was current only while the dirty clock (`nvn_dirty_now`) stood where its last check left it, and any guest write to any tracked page moves that clock. The same run took about 2,000 write faults a second (pages beside textures that the CPU writes, protected again by each check) across 4,700 memory pools, so between two faults nearly every binding of a packaged texture went through `tex_by_id_ptr` for a check of its pages that found them clean. Also: `tex_fit` kept one type-compatible view per ID, so an ID read as two types made one of them again at each change.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_dirty.c` and `nvn_mtl.m`. Measurement: with `DM_NVN_ARGS_STATS=1` a "texture lookups past the cached view" line counts and times those lookups by outcome (current view, packaged page check found clean, epoch moved, pages written but texture kept, no kept range, texture replaced, first lookup since registration, empty), how new views were had (the texture as is, the view cache, Metal views made), the lookups with an upload inside, which binding group asked in full encodes and what came from elsewhere, and `tex_fit`'s views by why they were made. `DM_NVN_TEX_SEQ` (on; `=0` restores): each tracked range has a write sequence (`wseq`) moved after every write it takes and at its registration and removal, each time to a fresh value of the clock; the page check (`nvn_dirty_since_seq`, which also reads the range's last write first) keeps the range's sequence with the view, and the fast tests take the view while it still reads so, so only writes to a texture's own memory pool send it back to the check. `DM_NVN_TEX_ALT2` (on; `=0` restores): `tex_fit` keeps the view before the current one too. Nothing drawn changes: a lookup the sequence answers is one the page check would have answered clean.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_tex_seq`: 192 CPU-written texture IDs in 96 pools; the same resource IDs with the switch on and off; a write in another pool sends no ID past the cached view on, all 192 a round off; a write beside textures in their pool sends only that pool's two IDs to a clean page check; a write into a texture re-uploads it and only it after any number of writes elsewhere, on and off; a range's removal and registration move its sequence; an ID read as 2D and cube in turn makes 2 views in 20 rounds with `DM_NVN_TEX_ALT2`, 40 without. Mac (M-series, -O1, Metal validation on, 256 KB textures): a write in another pool before each round 13.7 -> 2.0 ns a lookup, beside textures in one of their pools 14.0 -> 2.2 ns. `t_args_stats` checks every lookup is counted under one outcome.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), from the pool patch split above. **Open:** the phone's outcome split, which says how much of the 0.3 ms was these page checks (and the clock reads around each) and how much the lookups the sequence cannot skip: a texture whose own pool the CPU writes beside it (its check takes the whole packaged path and protects its pages again), and IDs registered again (views from the cache or made).

### Main guest thread keeps inherited default QoS

- **Symptom:** gameplay has low CPU clocks, large render submission wall time compared with thread CPU time, and default QoS (`0x15`) on the main guest thread while workers report user-interactive (`0x21`).
- **Cause:** worker initialization applied the guest QoS policy, but `svc_thread_main_init` only recorded the boot pthread's inherited class. On iOS that class can be default. A time-constraint policy may hide this difference until it is disabled for measurement, and does not establish the intended underlying QoS.
- **Fix:** main-thread registration calls the same `qos_apply` used by worker threads, before recording the actual class, and logs the resulting class. With diagnostics enabled, `DM_NVN_CPUPROF=1` measures inclusive CPU/wall time in pass begin/end, pipeline lookup, argument encoding, texture lookup, draw, geometry, and submission indexing. These nested phase times overlap. The thread regression confirms `0x21`; on-device performance remains to be validated.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US 1.0.3](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md), where the slow gameplay captures show 159–160 million host translation instructions per frame, considerable scheduling delay, nominal thermal state, and ample memory headroom.

### The thread policy line read the thread that presents

- **Symptom:** every `thread policy` line of a game says `real-time policy not in effect, current priority 47 (base 47, policy 1)` while the `present thread time-constraint policy (…) set` lines say the policy was set (Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, X1.0: 62 lines in one run, 18 in the other; Super Mario Odyssey on the same build: `in effect, current priority 97 (base 97, policy 2)`).
- **Cause:** the log. `nvnWindowAcquireTexture` sets the time-constraint policy on the thread that acquires the window texture, and `tsummary`, called from `nvnQueuePresentTexture`, read the policy of `pthread_self()`, the thread that presents. Odyssey acquires and presents on one thread. Ocarina of Time acquires on its main thread and presents on 'Presentation Thread' (a thread that sleeps 1–5 ms about once a frame), so the line read a thread that never had the policy; 47 is the base priority of the user-interactive class every guest thread runs at. It is not a demotion seen in the log: XNU's `thread_policy_get` still reports a policy whose thread the fail-safe demoted (from its saved mode), and every declaration was accepted. The declared computation does follow the acquiring thread's CPU time per frame (1.1 times the peak of 120 frames, at most 0.9 of the period), so with this game's bursts on the CPU side it sat at the 15 ms cap in many windows, against a mean of about 2–4.5 ms (the main thread's 115–274 ms/s); whether the main thread's policy was in effect on the phone is not known from these logs.
- **Fix:** logging only (`rt_policy_line`, `hle_nvn.c`): the line reads the thread the policy was set on, through a send right of its own (a thread that ended reads as ended, not as whatever thread reuses its name), names it and the thread presenting with that one's class and priority, tells a policy still set but run as a standard thread (`set, but run as a standard thread (demoted)`, policy 1) from one in effect (policy 2), and counts acquires made on another thread than the one before (`rt_comp_tick` measures the CPU time of whichever thread acquires, so across two threads its declaration means nothing). The policy itself is set as before, for every game: no fix to it is known to be safe without a device run, and a declaration below a thread's peaks stalled Dark Souls Remastered ([Time-constraint computation declared below the thread's peaks](#time-constraint-computation-declared-below-the-threads-peaks)). `DM_FIELD_LOG=0` reads the presenting thread as X1.0 did.
- **What an iPhone A/B should test:** first one run of this build with no switch, to read the line. `in effect, current priority 97` on the main thread ends this lead; the presenting thread's priority is beside it. `set, but run as a standard thread (demoted)` or acquires on several threads would make it one: then the same route with `DM_RT=0` against the default, and with `DM_RT_COMP=6` (a fixed declaration near the thread's p90 instead of its peaks) against the default. Compare the `translation detail` lines' wall clock against CPU and the mean clock, the power lines' share on performance cores, the step ups per window on the `game time` lines, frame p95 and p99, and the `[longframe]` lines for 30–60 ms stalls, the sign of a declaration below the thread's peaks.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 t_nvn && dm/gfx/tests/t_nvn`: a policy set on one thread reads as set there and not on the thread that writes the line (X1.0's reading), keeps that thread's name, and reads as ended once that thread has ended.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0); Super Mario Odyssey for the comparison.

### Time-constraint computation declared below the thread's peaks

- **Symptom:** stalls of 30–60 ms about once a second, at 30 fps and at 60 (99th percentile 63–127 ms); the main thread waits 20–34 ms for the render thread once a second.
- **Cause:** the thread that acquires the window (here the render thread) runs under a time-constraint policy whose computation the host re-declares every 120 frames; it was 1.25 × the 90th percentile of that thread's CPU time per frame. This render thread needs 1.9–2.0 ms a frame with one or two frames in 120 near 4.4 ms, against 2.5 ms declared, and each frame past the declaration stalled the thread. Probes on both threads' waits showed the main thread's waits; adding probes slowed the render thread and the stalls vanished, and `DM_RT=0` or a fixed `DM_RT_COMP=5` / `10` removed them too (99th percentile 18–21 ms) at the same CPU energy.
- **Fix:** the declaration also covers 1.1 × the window's peak (`rt_comp_tick` in `hle_nvn.c`): 5.6 ms here, 60.0 fps, 99th percentile 21 ms.
- **Found on:** [DARK SOULS: REMASTERED](../../dm/games/01004AB00A260000/README.md), in the Asylum cell.

### The replay threads declared no frame deadline

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone the `translation detail` line's mean clock swings between windows on the same route while the work per frame stays the same: Ocarina of Time's run C (X1.0) did 62.6 M instructions a frame at 3.87 GHz (CPU 5.9 ms, wall 8.4 ms, `submit+present` p50 7.7 ms) up to frame 3300 and 64.7 M at 1.72 GHz (CPU 12.8 ms, wall 16.8 ms, p50 16.0 ms) up to frame 3900; every window with a p50 of 14.5 ms or more ran below 2 GHz, at thermal state 0 and 0.8–1.2 W for the whole app.
- **Cause:** most of `submit+present` runs on the host's own threads, 'NVN queue replay' (`nq_worker`, `nvn_queue.inc`) and, with deferred encoding, 'dm-encode' (`rec_thread`, `nvn_rec.h`). Both had QoS user-interactive alone. Only the thread that acquires the window declares a frame deadline (a time-constraint policy, `set_time_constraint` in `hle_nvn.c`), and the performance controller learns the deadline and the work from such a declaration. Whether the slow windows are a clock the whole process was given or these threads placed on efficiency cores (32% of the CPU time on performance cores in one window) is not known; the declaration is the lever the host has for its own threads.
- **Fix:** the profile key `replay_rt on` (`DM_REPLAY_RT=1` / `0` overrides it; `nvn_rt.h`, `nvn_rtw_tick`). Each of the two threads declares its own time-constraint policy: period and constraint the present thread's period (`rt_period_ns`, taken at each acquire whether or not `DM_RT=0`), preemptible, computation half the period at first and then, every 120 frames, the larger of 1.25 × the 90th percentile and 1.1 × the peak of the thread's own CPU time per frame, at least 0.1 of the period (and 1 ms) and at most 0.8 of it, declared again when it moves by more than 15% (as the present thread's, with the peak for [Dark Souls Remastered's stalls](#time-constraint-computation-declared-below-the-threads-peaks)). A frame whose CPU time runs past the declaration raises it at once to 1.1 × that frame, within the cap: the window's peak covers a peak inside a window, not a step up between two (a clock that falls, the end of a pause, a load or a light scene), where waiting for the window's end left up to 119 frames past the declaration; only the window lowers it. A computation is never declared over 50 ms (XNU's largest real-time quantum, which a present period over 62.5 ms would pass), and only a first declaration that fails ends the policy: a later one that fails keeps the one in effect, and the next tick tries again. The replay thread ticks before the first node of each new frame, outside its cost scope; the encode thread after each block, at the replay's frame (`g_cur_frame`). Both sleep between nodes or blocks, which a time-constraint thread may: XNU gives such a thread a new deadline and its whole computation at each wakeup, as Core Audio's I/O threads have between callbacks, and sizing to the whole frame's CPU covers nodes that run back to back. Every other game keeps QoS alone.
- **Log:** `replay threads: 'NVN queue replay' and 'dm-encode' declare a time-constraint policy …` once, `replay threads: '<thread>' time-constraint policy (period …, computation …) set (the thread's first)` and its first 15 changes, and on each `thread policy` line (with `DM_FIELD_LOG` on) each thread's declaration read on that thread's own port: `in effect` (policy 2), `set, but run as a standard thread (demoted)`, `not in effect`, or `unknown (the thread has ended)`, and `N frames past it since the last line`, the frames whose CPU time ran past the computation declared (each one raises it, up to 0.8 of the period; many in a window mean frames above the cap); `no time-constraint policy set yet` before the first window.
- **What an iPhone A/B should test:** the same route with `DM_REPLAY_RT=0` in `Documents/env.txt` and without it. Compare the `translation detail` lines' mean clock and wall clock in the heavy windows (on: 3.5 GHz or more, wall near 8 ms), the `power` line's W, CPU % and share on performance cores, the `timing` line's `submit+present` p50, and the frame step's `held 2` counts; read the `thread policy` lines for `in effect` on both threads and their `frames past it`, alongside the `[longframe]` lines for 30–60 ms stalls. Starved guest threads (longer `frame work`, more `ModuleSystemWorker` yields) or `demoted` would be the costs.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400486024DEC000 t_nvn t_wait_event`: `t_nvn` checks the size (1.1 × the peak, 1.25 × the 90th percentile, the cap, the floor, the start), the switch's values, a thread that ticks 120 frames of 0.3 ms (half the period first, a frame that goes back ignored, then the floor, set and in effect on that thread, read as ended after it ends), then one of 3 ms (raised at once to 1.1 × it and counted once on the line, kept through its window, back to the floor after the next); `t_wait_event` `replay_rt_case` submits three frames once a period is known: the replay thread's policy in effect, the encode thread's with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, neither with `DM_REPLAY_RT=0` (`check_t_wait_event`).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (X1.0, runs C and D). Not yet run on the phone with the key.

### A frame deadline told through a work interval

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone the CPU clock falls from its first burst to about 1.5 GHz, with work moved to the efficiency cores, 10–30 s into heavy play, and stays there whatever the host's own settings: Ocarina of Time's runs with Adaptive Power off, Game Mode on, `DM_REPLAY_RT=0` and `DM_RT=0` all dropped. Thermal state and Low Power Mode do not explain it.
- **Cause (inferred):** the performance controller's documented burst-then-steady behaviour. A time-constraint policy tells the scheduler a period and a computation, not when each frame starts and must end. The only public way to tell it that is a work interval (`os_workgroup_interval_start` / `_finish`), and the only public way for an app to get an interval of its own is `AudioWorkIntervalCreate`, made for audio threads, so using it for frames is off-label. Nothing documented says a deadline keeps the burst clocks, and the steady state is GPU-bound, where a CPU interval has no documented effect: a long shot, measured as a behaviour arm.
- **Arm:** the profile key `frame_wg` (off for every game, Ocarina of Time included); `DM_FRAME_WG=1` in `Documents/env.txt` turns it on for a run, and a player build keeps the line. It needs `DM_RT` on: with `DM_RT=0` it logs `needs DM_RT on: not armed`. `frame_wg.c` makes one interval, `AudioWorkIntervalCreate("nx18-frame", OS_CLOCK_MACH_ABSOLUTE_TIME, NULL)`, once, and never releases it. The thread that acquires the window texture joins it when its time-constraint policy is first set on it (`set_time_constraint_comp`'s block for a new thread). While the arm is on, every thread's first acquire sets the policy on it, so an acquire that moves to another thread with the same period takes the policy and joins there too (without it the moved acquire would only skip until a period change). The 'NVN queue replay' and 'dm-encode' threads at their first declaration (`rtw_declare`, with `replay_rt`). Each thread joins once and keeps its first answer. Each frame (`nvnWindowAcquireTexture`, only when the frame counter moved: an acquire can run more than once a frame) the acquiring thread finishes the open interval just before its pacing wait and starts the next just after it, start `mach_absolute_time()` and deadline the real-time period on, converted to mach ticks. A deadline in nanoseconds would put the deadline about 41 times as far on an iPhone's 24 MHz clock.
- **libdispatch's rules, each an `os_crash` when broken:** start or finish on a thread that is not a member: only a thread whose own join returned 0 calls them, and on any other the frame counts as skipped, once (an acquire before a present period is known, or on a thread whose policy or join failed). With `DM_RT=0` the arm is not armed and nothing is called; under `DM_DETCLOCK` the acquire's paced branch, which holds both calls, does not run, so nothing is called either (the armed line and a 300-frame line of zeros still appear). A second join on a member thread is `EALREADY`, so each thread joins once. Releasing the interval while one is started: it is never released. A member that ends without leaving (`Thread exiting without leaving workgroup`): `frame_wg.c` keeps each thread's join token under a key of its own whose destructor leaves for it, and a key made with `pthread_key_create` is destroyed before libdispatch's own (checked on the Mac). Otherwise a member never leaves. The graphics unit tests link no AudioToolbox: `hle_nvn.c` has weak stand-ins that do nothing, and `t_nvn_framewg` links counting ones. A finish that returns `EINVAL` (no interval started) clears the open mark, so the next begin starts one; one that returns `EBUSY` leaves it, and the next frame's finish tries again.
- **Log:** `frame workgroup (DM_FRAME_WG): …` once when armed; `frame workgroup: interval 'nx18-frame' … made`; `frame workgroup: '<thread>' joined (0)`, or `did not join …` with the error, once per thread; and every 300 frames `frame workgroup up to frame N: intervals started …, finished … (… past the deadline); skipped … frames (…); errors start …, finish … (last …)`. Started and finished near 300 with no errors is the arm working; skips are frames whose acquire ran on a thread that is not a member (its policy or join failed: see that thread's line).
- **What an iPhone A/B should test:** the same route and rules as arm a (nothing in `env.txt`) with `DM_FRAME_WG=1` and `DM_RT` left on. Success: after the burst the clock stays at 2 GHz or more with about 45% of CPU time on performance cores, or recovers within one window; compare fps and the GPU p50 with arm a. Failure: thermal state 1, energy per frame up by more than about 30% with no fps gain, or more audio underruns. The audio interval type may carry auto-join and deferred finish, so real-time threads a member wakes (real audio threads among them) could be pulled in (inferred). The replay and encode threads run a frame behind the interval they are in.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> framewg` (in `run`): on the Mac, `AudioWorkIntervalCreate` makes the interval, a thread with a time-constraint policy joins it (0), a second call keeps that answer where a second `os_workgroup_join` of its own is `EALREADY` (37), the deadline is the period in mach ticks, 8 frames start and finish with no errors or skips and none late, a 0.5 ms deadline with 3 ms of work counts one late, a finish with no interval started (`EINVAL`, 22) clears the open mark and the next begin starts one, a thread that did not join calls neither and counts its frame skipped once, the member then ends without leaving and the process lives on, and one member finishes an interval another started (an acquire that moved). `DM_TEST_FRAMEWG_CRASH=1` also runs a member without the key destructor in a process of its own and expects it to end (a trap on macOS 27.3). On the Mac a thread without a time-constraint policy joined too (0): Apple says only real-time threads join, but neither libdispatch nor this kernel checks, so the arm keeps the `DM_RT` requirement. `t_nvn` checks the switch's values. `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_nvn_framewg` (in `run`) drives `hle_nvn.c`'s side with counting stand-ins, three runs: with `DM_FRAME_WG=1` the acquiring thread joins at its policy, acquires in one frame finish and start once, the start's deadline is the real-time period, an acquire that moves to another thread with the same period sets the policy and joins there, one that comes back calls no join, and a replay thread joins at its first declaration only; with `DM_FRAME_WG=1 DM_RT=0`, and with `DM_FRAME_WG=0`, nothing is called.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#iphone-frame-rate-x10) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max (test 3, arm b). Not yet run on the phone or with the game on the Mac.

### A deadline at 60 at any interval, and a clock rescue that holds interval 1

- **Symptom:** on the iPhone 18 Pro Max, Donkey Kong Bananza at thermal state 0 sits with the GPU clock at about a third (GPU probe 2.75-3.05x) for minutes, flipping present interval 1 ↔ 2 up to about 90 times a minute and settling at 30. `DM_FRAME_WG=1` alone did not hold the clock (device A/B 2026-11-17), and the clock rescue's 3 s were shorter than one clock step: the clock came back only after about 5 s of unbroken 60, and in 3 of 7 rescues the game stayed at interval 2 throughout (NX18-X2.0 gpu-clock-collapse.md).
- **Hypothesis (not settled):** iOS picks the lowest clock that meets the deadline it is told, and at interval 2 NX 18 tells it 33 ms: the present thread's time-constraint period (re-set at each flip) and the frame workgroup's deadline (the real-time period).
- **Experiment 1, `DM_FRAME_WG_PERIOD=<ms>`** (needs the frame workgroup, `DM_FRAME_WG=1`; 8 to 34, where 8, 11, 16 and 33 name 120, 90, 60 and 30 Hz's period, so 16 is 16.667 ms): each frame's workgroup deadline is that period at any interval. The present thread's time-constraint period and the replay threads' follow it, since a policy at 33.3 ms beside a 16.7 ms workgroup would tell the scheduler two deadlines; declaring a shorter deadline than the interval is what `rt_period_ns` already does under a frame step's hold. The pacing stays the game's interval. Log: `frame workgroup deadline (DM_FRAME_WG_PERIOD=16): 16.667 ms a frame at any present interval …` once, and the 300-frame line ends `; deadline 16.667 ms (DM_FRAME_WG_PERIOD), N of the frames at a longer present interval` (its `past the deadline` count is then frames whose work took over 16.7 ms). Without the workgroup it logs `ignored`; a bad value is a red line. Risk: at interval 2 the present thread's computation is capped at 0.9 of 16.7 ms, so a frame needing more CPU runs past its declaration (`DM_POWER_DETAIL`'s thread policy line counts demotions).
- **Experiment 2, `DM_CLOCK_RESCUE_S=<s>`** (3 to 20): the rescue's hold (3 s without it), and while a rescue is under way the game's frame-rate controller is kept at interval 1: `nvn_fctl_next` hands every frame at interval 1 at most interval 1's band, as in a probe's grace, so its steps up to 2 are blocked; at the rescue's end (or a hold, a movie or thermal state 2 ending it) the game's own measure comes back. Only a game whose controller the host gates (`DM_FPS_CTL=host`); under `hold30` the rescue stays off. Unset: as before. Log: the rescue's start line says `for 10 s, the game's controller held at interval 1 for it`, its end line `; interval 1 released: N steps to interval 2 blocked (M frames handed interval 1's band)`, and the rescue's probe line `its frames drawn smaller for 10 s, interval 1 held through it`. A pinned run of 8 s at interval 1 counts as a probe held 8 s, which forgets the failures' hold-off.
- **What the phone should show if it works:** the GPU probe falls from about 2.9x to 1.7x or below within a rescue (or within a window with experiment 1) and stays there at 55-60 fps; far fewer `the game sets present interval` lines. Failure: the probe stays near 2.9x, or energy per frame up more than 30% with no fps gain.
- **Check:** `t_nvn_framewg` (`--period`: the deadline, the policy's and the replay threads' period 16.667 ms at interval 2 and 1; `--period-off`: ignored; unset: the real-time period at interval 2, as before; the parse). Ocarina of Time's frame step already declares 16.667 ms at interval 2 (`rt_period_ns`), so there `DM_FRAME_WG_PERIOD=16` changes nothing, `dm/gfx/tests/t_fctl` (the pin: 300 heavy frames at interval 1, 100 steps blocked, the step up after it), host `t_fctl` (the gate's pin, its counts and release, `game` and `hold30` gate nothing), `t_clock_rescue` (the hold's parse, a 10 s rescue, its back-off).
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md). Not yet run on a device.

### A shader that writes a whole frame through raw global memory

- **Symptom:** a movie or full-screen compute pass far slower than the same work should be, nearly all of it GPU time: `GPU p50` near the frame time with `submit+present` under 1 ms. `[RED] raw global memory: a write log ran out of slots` on older chains. With `DM_NVN_GPUPROF` a relaxed timestamp can put the time before the compute encoder's reported start, so the encoder itself looks short and the gap before it is what is long.
- **Cause:** every store through a loaded pointer searched the pool table in 12 steps, then logged its page in a hash set with compare-and-swap. Atomics from every lane contended even on a page the set already held, and a full frame holds more pages than the set's 1,024 slots.
- **Fix:** [the log became a bitmap of table entries, read before the atomic, and a tagged hint skips the search](shaders.md#global-memory-at-an-address-loaded-from-memory). Ocarina of Time's menu with its movie background, on an M6 Mac with 16 GB: frame time 42 ms → 16.67 ms, GPU 42 → 12.6 ms. The hint alone is 3.5 ms of that (16.1 ms without it).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md); the diagnosis and this design first in [PR 1299](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1299).

### The raw pool table built again for every pool the game makes

- **Symptom:** a frame of an area load or a streaming burst runs long on the replay thread while other threads make memory pools: in Ocarina of Time about 280 frames into the world the game makes 220 pools in six frames, 91 in the worst, while the replay encodes. With `DM_NVN_GMEM_KEEP=0` the log's `raw global memory: pool table` line counts no keeps.
- **Cause:** the raw pool table (`gmem_map`, the pools a shader's raw global memory loads and stores search) followed `g_pool_gen`, which every pool made, every address range given out and every pool taken out moves. Each change built it again at the next draw with such a shader: a 256 KB buffer filled, the pools walked under `g_mu`, the old table to deferred release. The draw-time table never had to be current for the GPU: every commit, whole or segment, points the table words of the blocks encoded so far at a table of the pools as they are then (`gmem_commit_patch`; [Global memory at an address loaded from memory](shaders.md#global-memory-at-an-address-loaded-from-memory)).
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game (`DM_NVN_GMEM_KEEP=0` restores the old rule; kept in player builds). A draw keeps the table built earlier in its submission while pools have only come since; the commit's patch asks for the current table (`gmem_map_at(1)`) and builds it once. A pool taken out (`pool_take_out` stamps `g_pool_drop_gen`) and a new submission build it at the next draw, so no draw takes a table that may name a buffer that is going or one an earlier submission built behind the pools. A pool first addressed since joins the residency set at the commit's build, before the residency commit, as before. Adapted from MacNativeGames b964bd4e (MIT), whose raw table follows the replayed command list's generation and a drop generation in the same way; that commit's other two parts (handle-table captures and their ID sets keyed on the pool generation) have no counterpart here: NX 18's handle tables (`htab_gpu`) follow the registrations (`g_reg_gen`, `g_ser_gen`), not the pool set.
- **Measurement:** not measured on NX 18. MacNativeGames' Mac run of that frame counted 43 table builds (30 ms) before its change, with its handle-table copies the larger part of a 73-131 ms replay.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game), through MacNativeGames b964bd4e.
- **Regression seam:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_gmem_keep`: within a submission a pool made keeps the table at a draw and the commit points the word at one that holds it, built once; a pool taken out builds it at the next draw without that pool; a new submission builds it at its first draw after a change; `--off` builds it at every change. `check_t_gmem_words` (the commit's patch) builds its middle table as a commit does.

### Dynamic resolution that never moves, and draw passes that wait for each other

- **Symptom:** a game with dynamic resolution stays at its full size while the GPU runs far over budget: `GPU p50` above the frame time target, frame times locked to 2 vsyncs, and the same pass sizes frame after frame in `DM_NVN_GPUPROF`.
- **Cause:** the game decides from GPU timestamps. With one time for every report of a submission (the host's default then), so the game measures almost no GPU time and never lowers its resolution. Separately, with the default `DM_NVN_BARRIER=full` each draw pass waits for all earlier GPU work, about 180 full barriers a frame on Ocarina of Time, which NVN does not ask for.
- **Fix:** segment timestamps ([GPU timestamps per submission segment](#gpu-timestamps-per-submission-segment), a profile key then, every game's now) and the profile key `draw_barriers game`. On Ocarina of Time, on the bridge and in the village on an M6 Mac with 16 GB, p50 28 → about 17 ms and attachments moved per frame 300 → about 120 MB; its main pass goes from 1536x864 to about 763x429. Both are what hardware does; `draw_barriers` could become the default after a regression run on the games with no such key.
- **Open:** the main pass at about 763x429 is a low resolution, and the GPU at about 17 ms is still a little over budget, so the frame rate is not locked.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md).

### Draw passes that wait for each other again while images share memory

- **Symptom:** a game with `draw_barriers game` gets no benefit from it: the 300-frame log line `draw-pass entry barriers full N / saved 0`, every draw pass behind a full barrier, and in a Metal System Trace many small idle gaps between consecutive render encoders of one command buffer (Ocarina of Time on an M1 Max: 141 a frame, 2.3 ms in all).
- **Cause:** two things in `enc_r_begin_pass` / `enc_r_end_pass` (`nvn_mtl.m`). The relaxed barriers were turned off whenever image family heaps exist (`g_image_heap_n`), so that the alias flush stays off end barriers, where it costs a command allocator about 100 KB per encoder; Ocarina of Time's dynamic resolution always has such heaps. And any nonzero `nvnCommandBufferBarrier` forced the full barrier, while in NVN only `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` and `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` order earlier work before later vertex or indirect fetch: the game's most common barrier, `0x12` (`ORDER_FRAGMENTS | INVALIDATE_TEXTURE`), asks only for fragment-to-fragment order, which the relaxed end barrier (vertex, task, mesh and fragment before fragment, dispatch and blit) gives. NVN does not order later vertex, task or mesh work after `ORDER_FRAGMENTS` either, so the next pass's primitive work may overlap.
- **Fix:** host-wide, wherever `draw_barriers game` is on: the guard is gone; the relaxed end barrier flushes to the device only, and when aliases exist the alias flush is a start barrier of the next pass before its fragments (`barrierAfterQueueStages:All beforeStages:Fragment`), where it costs no allocator memory and vertex work still overlaps. `g_game_barrier` keeps the bits of the game's barriers since the last pass; only `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` or `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` (`GAME_BARRIER_FULL`) force the full one, and the slice merge still stops at any barrier. The relaxed end barrier now also waits for the task (object) stage, whose writes it did not cover. The draw pass after a MetalFX scaler always takes the full barrier, since MetalFX's own encoders take none: the spatial upscaler (`up_mfx_encode`) cleared the relaxed mark (`g_last_end_relaxed`) for this, but NVN2's DLSS on the temporal scaler (`dlss_eval`) did not, so with `draw_barriers game` the pass after DLSS could start without waiting for the scaler's output. Fixed in X1.0.
- **Measured:** Ocarina of Time, M1 Max on AC, gameplay at frame hold off, two runs each: frame 23.2-23.6 → 21.6-21.9 ms, GPU 22.0-22.3 → 20.2-20.4 ms, `scripts/bench.py` p50 42.5 → 45.8 fps, about 68 of 103 entry barriers a frame saved, same-buffer render gaps 141 → 72 a frame; the picture as before. The gain depends on the GPU. With an earlier version of the same two rules ([PR 1970](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1970), the alias flush still on the end barrier): another M1 Max 46.1 → 48.3 fps (GPU 19.4 → 18.2 ms, three runs each); an iPhone (iPhone19,4) GPU span 29.7 → 27.2 ms, one run each; an M2 Air (five runs a side) and an M4 Pro no change, the M2's encoder-to-encoder gaps (about 4 ms a frame of 17-18 µs each) the same with and without, so there they look like a fixed cost of each encoder boundary rather than the barrier. The M4 Pro had already shown no change with the alias flush on a start barrier alone.
- **Open:** Ocarina of Time is the only profile with `draw_barriers game`; another game reaches this only with `DM_NVN_BARRIER=game`. About 37 full entry barriers a frame remain in Ocarina of Time, from `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` barriers and compute in between. An iPhone run with PR 1970 saw a flickering patch on a wall of the Deku Tree basement's torch room, with no baseline from that room: not reproduced or ruled out. In NX 18 (X1.0) no device has run this yet: an A/B on the iPhone 18 Pro Max in that torch room (`DM_NVN_BARRIER=ab` with `DM_AB`, or one run each with `game` and `full`) is owed, and the Mac's gain is not measured on NX 18 either.
- **Fallback:** `DM_NVN_BARRIER=full` in the game's `env.txt` puts every draw pass back behind the full barrier (as before `draw_barriers game`), for a picture fault suspected of coming from the relaxed order; it overrides the profile key.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_draw_barriers` runs with `DM_NVN_BARRIER=game` and `full`: which second passes skip the full entry barrier (no barrier, `0x12` and a tiled fragment barrier skip; `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` and `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` do not), also while an image family heap exists, a fragment storage write read by the next pass's fragments after `0x12`, and that the draw pass after DLSS through MetalFX's temporal scaler takes the full barrier (2 checks fail without the X1.0 fix); with `full` every pass takes the full barrier. Against the code before this fix, 6 of its checks fail.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game).

### Dynamic resolution at its floor while the GPU has time to spare

- **Symptom:** after a loading screen the game's resolution stays at its lowest (Ocarina of Time: 683x384) for minutes while it holds 60 fps with GPU time well under the frame, and climbs back only slowly or not at all.
- **Cause:** two host errors made the game's frame timer read more GPU time than its frame took. (1) A segment report reads its time from the timestamp heap slot of its boundary. An encoder with no work writes no relaxed timestamp, so the slot kept the ring's value from a frame long ago: Ocarina's frame-start `TIMESTAMP_TOP`, alone in its submission, read 1.7 s early, and its controller saw a frame of 1.7 s. (2) A queue continuation that waited on an event parked until the signalling submission completed on the GPU. About 25 waits a frame each left the GPU idle while the host encoded the next submission, about 2 ms of a 14.5 ms frame, which the frame-start-to-end timestamps count as GPU work.
- **Fix:** (1) `rep_ticks` (`nvn_mtl.m`) drops a heap time from before its submission began encoding (`g_ts_begin`, kept in the segment clock) and takes the segment's feedback time instead. (2) [A wait on a queued WRITE signal is released at encode](nvn-objects.md#gpu-event-waits-order-work-across-queues). The game then read a 13.8 ms span instead of 14.5 and climbed to full resolution in about 25 s instead of staying at its floor.
- **The game's own model:** with the timer right, Ocarina's controller still stayed at its floor. It extrapolates the measured cost to other resolutions as if it grew with the pixel count, which its Switch 2 constants assume. On Apple GPUs this game's cost hardly depends on resolution, so full size was predicted at about five times its real cost. A controller that extrapolates from pixel count can go wrong on any host GPU whose cost has a different shape; Ocarina's was replaced by a climb on the load measured at each level, which reaches full size on an M4 Pro at 60 fps (game note).
- **Ruled out:** the GPU clock. With macOS's `mactop` sampling during play, the GPU held about 1,562 MHz at 94% active on the M4 Pro with and without moving presentation to its own Metal queue, which removed 6 ms of idle per frame but did not change GPU time.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#dynamic-resolution), M4 Pro. Its controller and the switch that holds it at full size are in the game note.

### A full table of running GPU writes ends passes for nothing

- **Symptom:** many passes a frame on the same attachments, with the residency set line showing `passes ended to land them for a read` in the thousands and `refused …: earlier submission running` matching it. Ocarina of Time: 10,463 per 300 frames, each ending the pass over a uniform block no GPU write touched.
- **Cause:** a uniform shadow asks a table of the pages GPU writes of running submissions touch (`gw_live_in`).
