It had 4,096 slots, a colliding slot meaning any page, and a game with this many writes kept all of them live, about 400 as wildcards, so a block whose page hashes onto a wildcard was refused.
- **Fix:** general: 65,536 slots (`GW_N`, about 2 MB). The refusals fell to 291 per 300 frames, and the worst frames from about 45 to about 35 ms.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md).

### The uniform copy thread's hand-off convoys on its mutex

- **Symptom:** the submit (replay) thread spends a large share of its time in `ubc_wait`, under `pthread_mutex_lock` / `pthread_cond_signal`, while the `dm-ub-copy` thread waits for the same mutex far longer than it copies; translation cost jumps several-fold on one host build and not on another with the same code. Ocarina of Time on one build: 192 of the replay thread's 392 busy samples in `ubc_wait`, the copy thread 168 samples waiting for the mutex and 19 copying, 221-333k instructions a draw (9 runs) and 23-57 ms of submit CPU a frame, against 38.9k and 7.1 ms with `DM_NVN_UB_COPY_THREAD=0`. Builds that did not convoy still had 0.3-0.9 % of the replay thread's busy samples there.
- **Cause:** two parts. `ubc_wait` locked `g_ubc_mu` and signalled `g_ubc_cv` on every spin while the copy thread slept, and the thread cleared its flag only once it had the mutex back; the macOS mutex lets the spinning waiter barge in, so whether the woken thread kept losing it depended on the build's timing. And every one of those waits came from `um_overlay`: `ubp_get` queued a shadow's copy, then `um_overlay` waited for that same copy at once to lay a running submission's inline updates over it, so the copy thread saved nothing there (about 35 such shadows a frame in Ocarina of Time).
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_rec.h`, `nvn_mtl.m`):
  - The copy thread sleeps on `g_ubc_head` and a waiter on `g_ubc_done` with `os_sync_wait_on_address`, which compares the word in the kernel, so a change before the sleep is not lost. No mutex. Each sleeper is woken only by the thread that advanced its word, after the store. A waiter spins 4,096 times before it sleeps. The words are on their own 128-byte lines.
  - A waker never clears the sleeper's flag; the copy thread clears it once awake, and waiters are counted. A pusher preempted between its store and its wake call can make that call after the thread has made the op, set its flag and read the word for its next sleep: had the call taken the flag, the thread would sleep with it clear, the next push would not wake it, and every later wait would hang (the waiting thread's commit never made, its fence never signalled, the GPU watchdog firing).
  - `ubp_get` copies a shadow of 512 bytes or more on the submit thread when `um_overlay` will write into it (`um_hits`), instead of queueing it and waiting at once. Other shadows still go to the copy thread. `DM_NVN_UB_COPY_THREAD=0` still copies everything on the submit thread.
- **Check:** `t_ubc_wait` (CPU-only list): waits on a sleeping copy thread finish with the copy made after at most 2 wake calls; a 64 MB copy outlasts the spin; 2,000 pushes with no waiter are each made; 2,000,000 pushes with two waiters arrive in order; a late wake call held on the thread's way to sleep (the `UBC_T_SLEEPING` hook) does not lose the next push. On the mutex hand-off, a wait on the sleeping thread made thousands of signal calls (up to 387,816 for one wait); with a waker that takes the flag, the late-wake case fails every time. `t_ubp_running`: a 1 KB shadow with a running update over it queues no copy and holds the right bytes, one without still goes to the copy thread. On the build that convoyed: no `ubc_wait` samples left in the replay thread, 38.5k instructions a draw and 8.1 ms of submit CPU.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), M4 Pro.

### A host's own store in a fragment shader moves its depth test

- **Symptom:** a game that reads textures through a pool ([Shaders report which textures they read through a pool](memory.md#shaders-report-which-textures-they-read-through-a-pool)) renders far below its maximum dynamic resolution: Ocarina of Time's main pass at 763x429 with the GPU at 17 ms a frame.
- **Cause:** that report is an atomic store in the fragment shader (`dm_pool_mark`), and Metal tests a shader that stores to memory against depth after running it. So every covered pixel was shaded, with no hidden surface removal. It also risks wrong pictures: the late depth differs by an ulp from a prepass's, which fails an `EQUAL` pass along contours ([Early fragment tests from the control section](shaders.md#early-fragment-tests-from-the-control-section)).
- **Fix:** `dmsh_reflect.py` `pool_arrays`: a fragment shader whose only store is the mark gets `[[early_fragment_tests]]`. One that discards, writes depth, stencil or a sample mask, or stores anything itself keeps its test where it was; there the mark costs the shading. A chain change.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/tools/tests/test_dmsh_pool_arrays.py`. On the same route, the game's own controller then held its main pass at 1536x864, four times the pixels, at the same GPU time.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): 56 of the 258 fragment programs of a frame in Kokiri Forest carry the mark, 3 of them with a discard.

### One-off command buffers on the Metal 4 queue

- **Symptom:** with the wait ledger on (`DM_NVN_WAITS=1`), `old-queue command buffer` waits (a ledger site since removed) on the submitting thread: each GPU readback of `nvnTextureReadTexels`, each screenshot, texture dump or frame-capture readback first waited for every upload batch to complete.
- **Cause:** those command buffers went to a classic `MTLCommandQueue` (`g_q`, `q_cb` in `nvn_mtl.m`) beside the Metal 4 queue that runs the frames and the uploads. The two queues are unordered, so on the submitting thread each one flushed the upload batch, drained the encode thread and waited for the batch's GPU completion (`WS_OLDQ`) before the classic queue could read what the uploads wrote.
- **Fix:** with Metal 4 they are one-off Metal 4 command buffers (`once_new` / `once_commit` / `once_wait`, pooled allocators): readbacks, screenshots (`shot`, `grab_present`), `DM_CTL texraw`, `DM_NVN_TEXDUMP` / `TEXRAW` / `SNAPRAW`, the load-skip probe, `DM_NVN_REGLUM`, `DM_UDC_SELFTEST`, the capture's readbacks and the clear of a zero multisample texture (`dummy_tex`). Their order on the one queue replaces the wait: on the submitting thread the command buffer is committed behind the upload batch it flushes and behind the encode thread's records (`rec_commit_upload`), though not behind the replay's open command buffer, which the load-skip probe therefore commits first (`lp_finish`, as `mdi_wait`), so that its readback runs after the pass it checks; from another thread it is a block of its own behind everything published (`rec_own_push`, as the [present snapshot](#present-snapshots-the-games-swapchain-texture-free-once-its-copy-has-run)), or a direct commit without the encode thread. Each encoder starts and ends with a queue barrier, and what is made for one (a readback buffer, an offscreen output) is resident until it completes (`once_use`). The caller waits on the commit feedback (`one-off command buffer` in the wait ledger) or takes it on the feedback queue (`grab_present`, the probe, `DM_NVN_REGLUM`). `mtl_drain` waits for an empty one of them behind all earlier work (`once_drain`), on its commit feedback: a queue signal never comes when the queue runs none of the process's work (revoked GPU access, a submission iOS refuses in the background), and suspend would hang on it. At first the classic queue stayed for a device without Metal 4 (a virtual Mac, and the test switch `DM_TEST_NO_METAL4`, both removed since), where the same calls made classic command buffers through `q_cb`, and for the test fixtures that wrote or read textures on their own. In the Metal 4-only release `g_q`, `q_cb`, `WS_OLDQ` and that switch are deleted: every one-off command buffer is a Metal 4 one (`once_new`), the fixtures use the same ones or a Metal 4 queue of their own (`dm/gfx/tests/t_mtl4.h`), and a device without Metal 4 is refused at start ([GPUs without Metal 4 are refused at start](rendering.md#gpus-without-metal-4-are-refused-at-start)). The wait ledger's sites end with `one-off command buffer` and `post ring`; `old-queue command buffer` is gone.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 check_t_readback check_t_present_crop check_t_nvn check_t_gpu_lost check_t_gfx_suspend` (GPU readbacks on the submitting and on another thread, a released readback buffer, the whole-window grab and screenshots, the drain on suspend; `check_t_load_probe` checked the load-skip probe's readback until the probe was removed with the TBDR mode), and `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01007EF00011E000 run`. Not measured on a game.
- **Found on:** the host's API audit (every NVN game); no game note.

### Pool marks queue on one word when they test it with an atomic load

- **Symptom:** passes that read textures through a pool ([Shaders report which textures they read through a pool](memory.md#shaders-report-which-textures-they-read-through-a-pool)) cost far more GPU time than their texture reads explain, most on an iPad: Ocarina of Time's colour pass about 13 ms on an iPad Pro M5, 6 times the Mac's, while its prepass cost 1.3 times.
- **Cause:** `dm_pool_mark` tested the ID's bit with `atomic_load_explicit` before setting it. An atomic load, even relaxed, is served by the GPU's shared cache, not its core's own. The reads of a pass name a few IDs, so its pixels' loads queue on the same few words. A 1536x864 pass of 16 marks a pixel over 16 IDs (one word) took 2.60 ms, against 0.18 ms with no mark; the same marks over 4,096 IDs spread through the bitmap took 0.33 ms with either load (Apple M6, compute and fragment alike).
- **Fix:** general, in the chain (`dmsh_reflect.py` `POOL_MARK`): the test is a plain load, and only a clear bit takes the atomic OR. The same pass: 0.34 ms. A stale 0 in the core's cache costs an OR that changes nothing. A bit only goes from 0 to 1 while a submission runs: the host clears one (`pool_seen_clear`) only when the ID is registered anew, and Metal makes that store visible to work committed after it. A chain change.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/tools/tests/test_dmsh_pool_arrays.py`.
- **Result on Ocarina of Time, an M6:** no measurable change. Its 4,758 marked pixel shaders were rebuilt with each load over the same library (chain `f9f01e4f9994`), and the game was measured standing on the Kokiri Forest deck, 40 s a run. GPU p50 was 14.38 ms with either load (5 runs plain, 13.99–14.88; 4 atomic, 14.28–15.34). The pictures matched within run-to-run noise. The gain the contributor measured is on an iPad Pro M5 and is not checked here.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), reported with the iPad figures by a contributor in [discussion #1789](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/discussions/1789).

### Archive searches repeated while the build table is full

- **Symptom:** a busy scene runs far below what its GPU time allows, with the main thread busy for most of each second. The `pipelines up to frame` line shows archive lookups that found none in the thousands per 300 frames, at about 3 ms each, many draws with a hybrid pipeline, and `in flight 384`. Ocarina of Time's castle town market: 9,208 searches, 30.5 s in 300 frames, CPU translation 114 ms a frame.
- **Cause:** a draw whose pipeline is not built yet first asks for its base without compiling (`pso_build_ex(…, 0)`), which searches every archive file. It then draws with its pulling stand-in and queues the build (`pso_pend_demand`). With all 384 slots of the build table running, the queue refuses it, so the next draw of that pipeline finds it neither built nor queued and searches every file again. Bases found were kept (`g_pbase`); bases missing were not.
- **Fix:** general, in the host (`nvn_mtl.m`, `g_pbase_miss`): a base the archives lack is remembered under the base lock. `pso4_init` opens the archives once and nothing adds to them in a run, so a miss holds for the process. A later call for that base skips the search: a non-compiling one returns nil, a compiling one goes straight to the compiler. A base once built is in `g_pbase`, looked at first. Nothing is written to disk.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_archive`: 100 asks for one missing base search once, another vertex layout searches on its own, the compile that follows searches no more, and the base it builds is then given without a search.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate), found and measured by a contributor ([PR #1825](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/1825)), confirmed by a second.

### Pulling stand-ins made for predicted keys that are never drawn

- **Symptom:** frames of 50–170 ms on entering an area, in every run again, although the prewarm lists and the pipeline archive hold everything the area draws. The long-frame line shows `new pipelines 0` or `1` with tens of milliseconds, and the `pipelines up to frame` line shows that time under `predictions`, with a hundred or more `archive lookups found` at about 1 ms each and as many `pulling pipelines made`, against one or two `draws with a hybrid pipeline`. Ocarina of Time's castle town: one prediction made 160 stand-ins in 104 ms, three predictions 244 in 191 ms.
- **Cause:** after a submission's first pipeline miss the prediction (`pso_predict_rest_`) walks the rest of its draws. Besides each draw's key it proposes that key without the depth and stencil attachment, for a pass that attaches them on demand. Such a key is rarely drawn, so it never enters the pipeline cache or a prewarm list, and every prediction proposes it again. With hybrid pipelines the prediction made the pulling stand-in of every proposed key, to learn whether its draw would wait: an archive lookup of the pulling base on the submit thread each, outside the budget of two builds. A draw asks for its own pipeline without compiling first, and a Metal 4 base does not depend on the depth and stencil formats, so each of those keys was one specialization of a base already made; the stand-in was never needed.
- **Fix:** general, in the host (`nvn_mtl.m`, `pso_base_made`, `g_pp_easy`): the prediction first asks whether a proposed key's base is made. If it is, the key needs no compile and no stand-in: nothing is made or handed to the background for it, and it is left out of later predictions' candidates, where it used to fill the 160 places for alternatives. Resolved: a proposed key with no base made also got its stand-in made on the submit thread, an archive lookup each; its lookup now goes to a background worker ([Predicted keys' bases looked up on the submit thread](#predicted-keys-bases-looked-up-on-the-submit-thread)).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_depth_stencil`: with a draw's pipeline cached, the prediction hands nothing over for its attachment-free variant, asks no archive, makes no pipeline, and proposes it once only, and its draw then takes it without compiling. On the castle town route the same three predictions took 0.5, 2.0 and 1.4 ms, with 1 pulling pipeline made.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate).

### Predictions replayed every submission of an area's stand-in warm-up

- **Symptom:** while an area's pipelines are still being built and many draws use a pulling stand-in, the submit thread spends 1 ms or more a frame in predictions: the `pipelines up to frame` line shows thousands of `predictions` per 300 frames, against tens of thousands of `draws with a hybrid pipeline`, while `builds admitted` stays low.
- **Cause:** the prediction (`pso_predict_rest_`) replays the rest of a submission after its first pipeline miss, to load the libraries it binds (`shader_prefetch_rest`) and admit the builds of later draws that would wait. A draw that takes a stand-in asks for its own build and keeps missing until that build lands, frames later, so every submission of every frame in the warm-up started a prediction over draws it had already seen. Each one also proposed every key that already had a stand-in and asked whether its base was made (`pso_base_made`, a pipeline descriptor on the submit thread) before `hybrid_get` found the stand-in, though such a key can never be admitted. On Ocarina of Time's village arrival, with only the pair prebuild in the archive, that was 5,600–12,700 predictions in 3,000 frames: 1.4–1.6 s of replay and 0.7–1.1 s for the stand-in keys.
- **Fix:** general, in the host (`nvn_mtl.m`):
  - The prediction runs at a submission's first new miss (`pso_predict_due`): a draw that waits, or a draw that takes a stand-in and asks for its own build now. A draw whose own build is already under way drew in an earlier frame, and so did the submission around it as a rule, so it does not start one. The prediction can only help draws it reaches before their first encode, and new content shows itself by new misses, which still start it with its prefetch.
  - A key with a stand-in (`g_pso_hy`) is no candidate.
  - The `pipelines` line counts the builds the predictions admitted (`builds admitted`).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_pending` (the trigger: a stand-in draw whose build is under way starts nothing, the first new miss starts the submission's one prediction) and `check_t_pso_depth_stencil` (with a stand-in for a draw's key the prediction hands over only its attachment-free variant; fails without the fix). Ocarina of Time v131072, village arrival from `save_ref`, frames 5,100–8,100, M6 Mac, instrumented hosts, two runs a side, against the host before: predictions 12,706 / 5,564 → 269 / 312, their time 3.77 / 2.76 s → 0.65 / 0.75 s (what is left is the first sight of new keys), builds admitted 84 / 85 → 71 / 87, draws that waited 347 / 343 → 352 / 367, submit CPU 7.89 / 7.97 → 5.69 / 6.18 ms a frame. The stand-in check alone: predictions as before, none of the stand-in keys' time.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate), reported with measurements by a contributor ([issue #2438](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2438): the market's warm-up, 1.0–1.5 ms a frame of submit CPU in the prediction after a stand-in draw, which their runs showed admitting nothing; the runs here show it admitting builds, so it is kept at new misses rather than left out).

### Predicted keys' bases looked up on the submit thread

- **Symptom:** on a first visit to an area, on an iPhone, one frame holds for seconds to tens of seconds, and the `pipelines up to frame` line shows that time under `predictions` on the submit thread, with about as many `archive lookups found` at tens of milliseconds each and hundreds of `pulling pipelines made`. Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 18 Pro Max entering a new area: frame 5917 held 26.2 s, and the window up to frame 6000 shows `predictions 398 / 23178.3 ms` against `archive lookups found 470 / 23524.5 ms` (about 50 ms each in the 4.7 GB archive bundled with the app) and 711 pulling pipelines made. Upstream saw the same on an iPhone 17: predictions at 1–7 ms each, up to 3.5 s per 300 frames, at 12–22 fps.
- **Cause:** after a submission's first new miss the prediction (`pso_predict_rest_`) walks the rest of its draws and, with hybrid pipelines, made the pulling stand-in of every proposed key whose base was not made yet, to learn whether its draw would wait (`hybrid_get`): an archive lookup of the pulling base and a specialization each, on the submit thread, for keys drawn later in the submission and for keys never drawn. A lookup's only lasting result is the base, which serves every thread (`g_pbase`, or `g_pbase_miss` for one the archives lack). On the phone a lookup in a large archive costs page faults on the file, so the prediction held the frame for as long as all its lookups took.
- **Fix:** general, in the host (`nvn_mtl.m`, `pso_bg_look`), ported from upstream 9d3a58364 ([PR #2677](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2677)): the prediction hands each proposed key's base lookup to a background worker (one serial queue per performance core, at most four, user-initiated QoS) and makes no stand-in. The worker looks up the base of the key's own pipeline (what its draw tries first), else its pulling base; with neither, the draw would wait, so the worker admits the key's build by NX 18's rule (the first two within the prediction's budget on the global queue, the rest on `pp_bg_queue` as `pso_pend_kick_next` does) and has the pulling base built for the pair's next miss, as a draw does (`hy_base_kick`, which keeps `DM_PSO_HYBRID_KICK`). A base is handed over once per process (`g_pp_looked`, by the base's key, so a key and its variant without depth and stencil share one lookup). The draw then specializes the base made or takes its stand-in from the pulling base made; a draw that comes before its lookup ran does the lookup itself, and one that comes during it waits for its result ([A draw that came during another thread's archive lookup compiled its base](#a-draw-that-came-during-another-threads-archive-lookup-compiled-its-base)). Each prediction owns a budget retained by its workers and spent under the pending-table lock only on an admitted guess. A draw waiting for a running build reserves that slot through completion (`taking`), so a background admission cannot reclaim it before the draw takes its pipeline. Admission, completion and steal counts are sampled together under the same lock. The lookups still happen, so CPU and flash energy stay about the same; what moves is the frame. The `pipelines` line adds `base lookups handed to the background N (found M, T ms)`, `of the archive lookups, on the submit thread N / T ms` and `draws that waited for another thread's archive lookup N / T ms`; `scripts/devlog.py` sums them per log and compares them between two. `DM_NVN_BG_LOOK=0` (kept in player builds) looks them up on the submit thread as before, `ab` alternates, for the phone A/B.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_bg_look`: the prediction makes no stand-in on the submit thread and hands the lookup over once; the worker finds a made pulling base and admits nothing, finds no base and admits the key's build, which can be claimed; a key that shares a base already handed over is not handed over again; the draw then takes its stand-in from the base made with no compile; a blocked archive lookup does not hold the prediction; with `DM_NVN_BG_LOOK=0` the stand-in is made on the submit thread as before. `check_t_pso_pending` covers interleaved prediction budgets and a full-table completion/reclamation race (fails without the reservation: the waiting draw got another key's pipeline) and the in-flight count during concurrent counter updates. Not yet run on the iPhone: the gate is an on-battery Ocarina A/B on the same route, save, archive and prewarm (`DM_NVN_BG_LOOK=0` against the default, a third arm `DM_NVN_PREDICT=0`).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate), the owner's iPhone 18 Pro Max (round 3, entering a new area), and upstream's iPhone 17 play through Hyrule Field, Kakariko Village and Castle Town.

### A draw that came during another thread's archive lookup compiled its base

- **Symptom:** on a first visit, single frames of 0.3–2 s while the pipeline archive holds the area's pulling bases. The `pipelines up to frame` line shows one to eight `draws that waited: pulling base not made yet` with hundreds of milliseconds each, few `draws with a hybrid pipeline`, and its background lookups finding every base they looked for (`base lookups handed to the background 21 (found 21)`). Upstream's Ocarina of Time on an iPhone 17, entering Kakariko Village and Hyrule Castle: 1 / 861 ms, 2 / 1,081 ms, 8 / 1,786 ms in one 300-frame window each, against 1 / 95 ms on the build without the background lookups.
- **Cause:** a call of `pso_build_ex` that does not compile (`compile` 0: a draw asking whether its base or its pulling base is made or archived, a prewarm task) returned nil at once when another thread held that base's job in `g_pbase_pending`, so that it would never wait for the Metal compiler. But a job's owner first only looks in the archives, and a non-compiling owner does nothing else. A draw that came during such a lookup of its pulling base therefore found no stand-in. Its own base was already known missing, so the draw then compiled it on the submit thread: about a second on the phone. The background lookups of the prediction (`pso_bg_look`) make this race happen every time: they look up the bases of the draws that come next in the same submission, a few milliseconds before those draws.
- **Fix:** general, in the host (`nvn_mtl.m`, `pso_build_ex`, `PbaseBuild.looked`), ported from upstream edc7eff9c ([PR #2770](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2770)): the owner of a base's job leaves a second group, `looked`, once it has looked in the archives (or skipped them for a base known missing), with the base it found in `found`. It leaves the group before the compaction note and before any compile. A non-compiling call that finds another thread's job waits on `looked` and takes `found`. If the lookup found nothing it returns nil as before, without waiting for the compile. The wait is one archive lookup at most: on NX 18's iPhone in Ocarina's bundled archive, about 50 ms, not the milliseconds measured on a Mac. The `pipelines` line counts these waits on the submit thread (`draws that waited for another thread's archive lookup`). It is not behind `DM_NVN_BG_LOOK`: with the switch off a draw can still meet a hybrid miss's pulling base build there.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_look_wait`: a non-compiling call during another thread's 200 ms lookup takes the base the lookup found and compiles nothing (fails without the fix: it returned nil at once); during a lookup that finds nothing, followed by a 2 s compile, it returns nil after the lookup, not after the compile; a non-compiling owner still marks a missing base.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate), upstream's play of 2026-11-14 on an iPhone 17 (builds with the prediction's background lookups: 2 fps in Hyrule Castle). Ported with the background lookups so that NX 18 never has the race.

### In-play archive lookups read the GPU code from flash

- **Symptom:** on an iPhone, after the start, each archive lookup that finds its base takes tens of milliseconds: the `pipelines up to frame` line shows `archive lookups found` at about 50 ms each (Ocarina of Time, iPhone 18 Pro Max, round 3: 470 / 23,524.5 ms in the window up to frame 6000) where a Mac with the file in memory takes about 1 ms.
- **Cause:** Metal maps a pipeline archive whole. Its open reads every library of every GPU image (`arch_open_read_on` asks for those ranges first), but a GPU image's code only when a pipeline is first taken from it, one page fault after another from flash. Ocarina's bundled archive is 4.7 GB, so most of its GPU code is not in memory when the game first reaches an area.
- **Fix (off by default, for an A/B):** general, in the host (`nvn_mtl.m`, `ra_note`, `ra_step`): with `DM_PSO_READAHEAD=1` the bundled archive's GPU code ranges (each GPU image's `__TEXT` sections other than `__metallib`, collected while its container is checked) are read into the file cache once the start's pipeline work is over: the first frame presented, the prewarm, the shader-only lines, the prebuild and the crash guard's window done, and no compaction pass scheduled or capturing. One thread at utility QoS reads 8 MB at a time into one 1 MB buffer and then rests at least as long as the read took. Nothing is mapped, wired or pinned: the pages are clean file cache the system can drop, not the app's footprint. It waits while the device is thermally serious or critical, in Low Power Mode or the app is in the background, and stops for good when the headroom to the memory limit (`os_proc_available_memory`) falls under 1 GB or the process ends. One line when it is scheduled (`pipeline archive read-ahead (DM_PSO_READAHEAD=1): … MB of GPU code in … ranges`) and one summary (`pipeline archive read-ahead done|stopped …: X of Y MB of GPU code read in T s (R s reading), held H times`). Not yet measured on the iPhone: it trades flash reads (energy) up front for in-play lookups that would read the same pages later, and only pays off when the game later takes pipelines from much of the archive.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_readahead`: it waits before the first frame, while a compaction pass is scheduled or capturing and while the prewarm runs; waits while hot, in Low Power Mode and in the background (one hold counted while it lasts); reads every range once in 8 MB steps; stops without reading under the headroom, lets go of its file and buffer when the headroom falls while it reads, and stops at the process's end. `DM_PSO_READAHEAD=1 ./t_pso_bundle` shows the ranges found in a real archive. The phone A/B: one build, the same route, save and archive, on battery, `DM_PSO_READAHEAD=1` in `Documents/env.txt` against no line; compare `archive lookups found … ms`, long frames, the power lines and the summary line.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate), the owner's iPhone 18 Pro Max (round 3).

### The power line's GPU time on iOS

- **Symptom:** `[nvn] power up to frame N: … GPU 0.0 ms/s; thermal …` in every window on an iPhone, while the timing line's `GPU p50` is several milliseconds a frame (18–22 ms in Luigi's Mansion 3's heavy rooms). A GPU-bound window cannot be told from an idle one, nor a GPU held back by a power cap at thermal state 0 from a heavier scene.
- **Cause:** the field was `task_power_info_v2`'s `gpu_energy.task_gpu_utilisation`, which iOS leaves at 0 for an app. The Mac fills it.
- **Fix:** diagnostics only. The power line gives the host's own count: `GPU <x> ms/s busy (the host's command buffers, overlaps merged)`, how long per second the Metal 4 queue's command buffers kept the GPU busy. Each counts from its commit feedback's GPU start to its GPU end: the game's submissions and segments, the indirect-count waits, upload batches, one-off command buffers (readbacks, screenshots, drains), display presents and offscreen presents. The intervals are merged so that overlapping ones count once (`gd_busy_add` in `nvn_gpu_diag.h`, `gpu_busy_note` and `mtl_gpu_busy_ns` in `nvn_mtl.m`). The one writer is the queue's serial feedback queue, in the feedback handlers that already ran there; the only command buffer that gets a handler it did not have is the indirect-count wait's, which the submit thread then waits on until the GPU is done (the load-skip probe's commit had one too until the probe was removed with the TBDR mode). Where the system's count moved in the window (the Mac) it follows: `, <y> ms/s by the system's count (task_gpu_utilisation)`. Not counted: the present snapshot copy (one texture copy a frame while present snapshots are on; it has no handler), and the iOS render server's own GPU work over the layer, which is another process's. An interval that ends more than a second after its handler ran, or runs longer than a minute, is a bad time (a failed or lost command buffer), not GPU work, and is left out (`gd_busy_add_checked`): counted, its end would hide every later interval and the line would read `GPU 0.0 ms/s busy` again for the rest of the run. The figure is about 1000 ms/s at most, not exactly: an interval counts in the window its handler ran in, so a long command buffer across a window's edge, or several reported at once after a stall, can put one window a little over 1000. The busy time is an upper bound of the GPU's work: a command buffer's interval includes its waits at queue barriers. A command buffer reported after a later one it did not overlap counts only past that one's end, an undercount, never a double count. The timing line's `GPU` is another measure: per frame, the sum of that frame's game command buffers' intervals, without presents and uploads, overlaps not merged. `scripts/devlog.py` reads both figures (`GPU ms/s busy` and `GPU ms/s, the system's count`); an older log's lone `GPU x ms/s` is the system's count.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> gpu-diag` (overlaps, gaps, one interval inside another, no start time, a late report, a second of overlapping pieces counting a second, a bad end time far in the future or a minute-long interval left out while later intervals still count; a reader thread under ThreadSanitizer) and `dm/tools/tests/test_devlog.py` (`PowerGpu`: the line as `hle_nvn.c` formats it, with and without the system's count, and an older log). **Not run on a device:** the new count.
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md) (62 power lines, all `GPU 0.0 ms/s`) and [Luigi's Mansion 3](../../dm/games/0100DCA0064A6000/README.md) on an iPhone 18 Pro Max. The old fork had the same count (its batch-b, `da1996ad`); this host's Metal 4 queue did not.

### A submission's commit read the whole storage-write page set

- **Symptom:** in a `sample` of the queue's thread (`NVN queue replay`), `mtl_sub_commit` has much time of its own and in `gw_put`, though a submission writes few pages. A game that makes many small submissions a frame pays it each time: Ocarina of Time makes about 30.
- **Cause:** the pages a submission's storage buffers, texture→buffer copies and reports write are kept in an open-addressed set of 16,384 slots tagged with the submission (`g_sbwp`, `sbw_note`), and the commit (`gw_commit`) walked all 16,384 slots to find this submission's pages whenever there was one: 256 KB read per commit.
- **Fix:** for every game, in `nvn_mtl.m` (ported from upstream NXNative `f63ecbd4`). The set lists the slots a submission takes (`g_sbw_list`), and the commit reads only those. Which pages enter the running writes (`g_gw`, `g_gwx`) is unchanged; the order they enter in does not change the tables.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_sbw`: a submission lists each page it noted once, its GPU pages are running writes after the commit and a page only a report wrote is not, and the next submission lists only its own pages.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#frame-rate), upstream: standing on the Kokiri Forest deck (M4 Pro, 640 draws a frame), 42.8–43.9k → 38.7–39.3k submit-thread instructions per draw; on an M4 MacBook Air, `gw_commit` 11.6–12.4 → 2.5–2.7 µs a commit and 0.19–0.27 ms less submit CPU a frame. The frame time did not change on either Mac: the game waits for the GPU there. Not yet measured on an iPhone on battery.

### The write-fault hook scanned every memory pool

- **Symptom:** a game with thousands of memory pools and many CPU writes into protected texture storage spends game-thread time in the page-fault hook: Ocarina of Time takes about 1.28 M write faults a run (about 49 a frame) with about 5,000 pools live (NX18-X2.0 oot-alldata-analysis.md, item 9). Nothing in the log measured it.
- **Cause:** the dirty-page tracker's page-fault hook (`nvn_dirty.c` `on_fault`) found the pools holding the faulting address by reading every registered slot's start and end, and the host pre-write hook (`prep_write`) did the same for each host write into guest memory: about 5 µs a fault at 5,000 pools on an M4 Max, before the `mprotect`.
- **Fix:** for every game (`nvn_dirty.c` `idx_find`, `idx_edit`). An address index lists the live pools sorted by start, each entry with the largest end so far, so a binary search and a short walk back find every pool holding an address, overlapping ones included (neighbours share their rounded first or last 16 KB page). It only names candidates: each is checked as the scan checked it (live, pinned, its own bounds), so the claims are the scan's. Pool registration and retirement rewrite it under the tracker's mutex into the second of two buffers and then point readers at it; readers in the signal handler take no lock, allocate nothing and never wait (a sequence count per buffer, a read made again when it moved). The buffers are reserved once for the table's 262,144 entries and never freed. With more than 32 pools at one address, or no stable read in a few tries, the hook scans as before. `DM_NVN_DIRTY_INDEX=0` scans always (kept in player builds for the A/B). The packaged textures line now gives the hook's cost: `fault hook N/s, M ms/s, T us each (index|scan)`, the hook's time with its `mprotect`.
- **Also:** `find_span`, the containing range the replay's readers ask for (`nvn_dirty_span`, sparse checks, uploads), asks the same index first: of the pools overlapping the span, the lowest slot holding it whole, the scan's answer, taken only when the registry did not change during the read (else the scan). Ported from S2Arm 985c4fae (Apache-2.0), which measured that scan at 4.5% of a phone's replay thread. `t_dirty_index` checks it against the scan (about 80,000 lookups) and times it: 2,685 ns scan, 81 ns index with 5,000 pools on an M4 Max. Not run on the iPhone.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_dirty_index` (with the index and with `DM_NVN_DIRTY_INDEX=0`): about 170,000 lookups among 5,042 pools, nested and equal ones among them, at every pool's edges and at random points and spans, again after 3,000 retirements and registrations, name exactly the pools the scan names; a write to a page two pools share stamps both; a sentinel pool written after each protection while 20,000 registrations rewrite the index around it never misses a write. Benchmark on an M4 Max, 5,000 pools: the lookup alone 5.2 µs by scan, 37 ns by index; real faults, the hook 6.0 → 0.6 µs each and a write with its fault 7.0 → 2.9 µs; an index rewrite 7 µs per registration or retirement. `check_t_dirty_reuse` and `check_t_dirty_race` run both ways; `make tsan-dirty-index` runs the concurrency part under ThreadSanitizer.
- **Expected on the phone:** at Ocarina of Time's ~49 faults a frame about 0.3 ms of game-thread time a frame less at M4 Max speed (more at the phone's clocks); not yet measured on an iPhone. The new log figures tell.

### Spin-counter yields hold a core while the threads they wait for wait for one

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time's main thread spins on yields 2–4 ms a frame once an iPhone 18 Pro Max warms (Metal System Trace 1, handheld Balanced): at thermal Fair, with the CPU clock at about 1.5–1.9 GHz, the main thread (0x1001, which acquires the window texture) spends 0.5–1.2 s of each 300-frame window in tight runs of yields against about 0.14 s cool: 2.1–4.1 ms a frame at 30–39 fps against 0.3–0.6 ms at 60, up to 26,000 yields and short `SleepThread` calls a frame (about 3 yields to each `SleepThread` of 20 µs or less) at about 150 ns a pass. Nearly all of them `moved on` (7,920,655 of 7,926,942 in the worst window; `sched_yield` 6,287). The same windows show 5.6–5.8 of 6 cores busy, the process's threads runnable but waiting for a core 3.3–4.4 s a second (0.7–1.1 cool), the main thread at 230–250 ms/s of CPU (about 100 cool), and the serial main → replay → encode chain CPU-limited (replay plus encode 13–15 ms a frame against about 5).
- **Cause:** the main thread waits for other threads' work (its ModuleSystem workers, as Tears of the Kingdom's does, and the frame chain behind them) in a loop whose callee-saved registers move at every pass: a spin counter. Such a yield counts as progress (`thr_yield`, `moved`) and returns at once with no host call, so the thread keeps its core for the whole wait, at the time-constraint priority the host gives the acquiring thread, the replay threads' own. Its wait is not its work: it lasts until the threads it waits for get a core and finish, and when the clock halves and every core is taken they run later and slower while the spinning main thread is one of the threads taking a core. The wait grows with their delay (5–9× per window), which grows with the spin. A storm sleep (`DM_YIELD_STORM=sleep`, the profile's `yield_storm`) cannot be used on this thread: it sleeps a fixed 50 µs whatever happens, and the acquiring thread is the frame's critical path, so it keeps spinning.
- **Fix (on by default since the phone run below; `DM_YIELD_PARK=0` restores the old path):** `DM_YIELD_PARK` (`park_yield`, `park_progress` in `hle_thread.c`). From 50 µs into a tight run each further yield parks on a progress word (`os_sync_wait_on_address`) for at most 50 µs and wakes as soon as another guest thread makes progress the waiter could be polling for: a thread SVC other than a yield (a signal, a wait that blocks, a lock release, a sleep), a host wait that blocks (a native HLE wait, the acquire's display wait, a queue finish's GPU wait), a yield that continues no tight run (a worker that finished its item, also one back from a blocking wait that ran its job with no SVC), or an exit; a thread SVC bumps again at its return, after the guest words it writes itself (SignalToAddress, ArbitrateUnlock, SignalProcessWideKey's key), which come under G after its entry; the guest's stores come before each of those points. A pass that read the clock never parks (it waits for a time). The waiter takes its snapshot of the word as it leaves each yield, before its loop looks at its condition again, so progress between that look and the park ends the park at once; progress that makes no such point (a store and nothing else) ends it at the cap. Bumps are made only while a thread is registered in a tight run past the start, and a wake call only while one sleeps. Waits under 50 µs (most handoffs between workers) spin as before. A park blocks, so a thread of the same priority waiting for a core runs, as a yield lets it on the Switch. `=<cap µs>:<start µs>` sets both; `0`: the yield path as before.
- **Risk:** a loop that does real work between yields in under 2 µs a pass would wait up to the cap at each pass unless other threads make progress (Breath of the Wild's and Animal Crossing's working loops take 5 µs and more, outside tight runs); and a main thread that parks may look less busy to the performance controller (Tears of the Kingdom's back-off kept it on the efficiency cores), though here it spins on those cores already. It was off by default until the phone run below.
- **Mac:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> build/t_yieldpark` (real threads and clock, M-series Mac): a 1 ms spin-counter wait for another thread's work costs its waiter 83–93 µs of CPU instead of 1,000; the wait ends 3.6–4.7 µs after a signal SVC or a yield after work (0.1 µs spinning) and 34–50 µs after a store alone; waits of 20 µs never park (0.08 µs); the word SignalToAddress type 2 writes with G kept busy by another thread is seen 2.4–5 µs after the SVC's return (48–52 µs, the cap, when only the SVC's entry bumped), and a yield after a blocking wait and 0.9 ms of work 2.4–3.5 µs after the store (49–55 µs when only a yield after a yield bumped); with twice as many spinning waiters as cores, the work they wait for took 58 ms instead of 102–122. Not run in a game on the Mac.
- **What an iPhone A/B should test:** the same warm route (after a save load or 70 s of play) with `DM_YIELD_PARK=1` in `Documents/env.txt` and without it. Compare the `power` line's top yielders (`0x1001 'main thread' T ms in tight runs (… ; parks P, woken W, X ms)`: T should fall, W should be most of P), CPU %, W and fps, the `power detail` line's `threads waited for a core` and the clocks, the `threads` line's main thread ms/s, the `timing` line's frame p50/p95 and `NVN submit+present`, `on-screen … dropped`, and the `thread policy` / `power tick` demotion counts. A higher frame p95 or a lower performance-core share with no fps gain argues against it.
- **Phone (iPhone 18 Pro Max, Ocarina of Time, handheld Balanced, `DM_YIELD_PARK=1` on f5ae72b99 against dd71b2d7 without it):** the main thread's tight-run time fell from 114–678 ms a 300-frame window to about 23 ms; 80–85% of its parks were woken by progress (the rest at the cap); CPU energy 28.8 against 33.0 mJ a frame and 1,035 against 1,288 dropped frames over the same frame count. The second run had a phone cooler on it, so the energy and drop figures are confounded; the tight-run time and the woken share are not. Default on since then, for every game; `DM_YIELD_PARK=0` (or `off`) restores the spinning path.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> build/t_yieldpark` (above), and `build/t_yieldstorm` for the tight-run counting it builds on.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, X2.0 build `a464a49e6`.

### Residency commits paged guest files in holding the lock

- **Symptom:** a frame of 300-600 ms at an area change, with `[RED] slow residency commit: 174.3 ms (adds 374, 201.7 MB; ...) on the commit worker for the replay (DM_NVN_RES_ASYNC)` and, next to it, `[RED] slow wait for the residency commit lock: 178.0 ms ... on thread dm-encode` and `176.9 ms ... on thread NVN queue replay`. Ocarina of Time, iPhone 18 Pro Max, handheld Balanced (a464a49e6; NX18-X2.0 traces/oot-mst-1-findings.md). After the change the footprint stayed at 5.4-5.8 GB, Metal's allocation at 7.0-7.7 GB, and the kernel paged and compressed on about half a core.
- **Cause:** two that feed each other. (1) A residency commit wired its adds' guest-file pages (`mlock`, `fwire_all`, the freeze fix's page-in) holding `g_res_commit_mu`; that page-in was most of the commit (the 300 frames around the 174 ms one: 271.7 of the worker's 340.0 ms of commits, 614 MB wired, mostly the upload sources at `up_big_src`, the game's texture data in its files), and every queue commit and every other commit waited for the lock, needed or not. (2) The page-ins were reads from the disk because memory was short: at the area change the graphics ledger went 2.6 -> 4.3 GB and serial objects 2,673 -> 4,490 (the last area's textures, which the game keeps as live objects), the residency set 4.1 -> 5.7 GB, wired; the footprint went 3.4 -> 5.5 GB while the headroom to the memory limit stayed over 2.5 GB, so the idle-texture release (`DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_MB`, 1536 MB of headroom) never ran; the process read 17 GB of guest-file pages back in.
- **Fix:** for every game. (1) `DM_NVN_RES_PREWIRE` (default on; `nvn_mtl.m` `res_commit_to`, `res_prewire`, `res_commit_take`): each residency change has a number; a commit pages its file ranges in before it takes the lock, in chunks of 64 MB, and takes the lock only for the add / remove calls and `[g_res commit]` of the changes below each chunk's end. The encode thread's guard before a queue commit needs only the changes made before the replay recorded that command buffer (the record keeps the number), and the queue commit itself only the lock, so neither waits for a page-in of later changes; a commit that needs a change being paged in waits for that page-in (`[RED] slow wait for a residency prewire`), as it must: a resource is in the committed set before the command buffer that uses it is committed, as before. `=0` restores the old commit. (2) `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_MB` (default on iOS: two fifths of the device's memory, 4,915 MB on 12 GB; off on the Mac): the idle-texture sweep also runs while the footprint is over the cap and releases the least recently used idle live textures (unbound 3 s, content in guest storage, not GPU-written: the same ones `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_MB` takes) until it is an eighth under (a sweep that frees under 64 MB doubles the time to the next, up to 8 s, said once; under the cap it is a second again); each is made again from guest memory at its next use, so nothing drawn changes. The memory line is followed by `GPU working set up to frame N: GPU allocated A MB of the device's recommended working set W MB (P%); residency set R MB ...; footprint F MB; footprint cap ... (released for it N, M MB in total)` (after S2Arm 27d5d3684).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_res_prewire`: while another thread's commit pages 16 MB in (8 ms per 1 MB call, 148-190 ms on an M4 Max), a commit of the changes before it and a queue commit's take of the lock return in 0.0 ms (with `=0`: 148-192 ms, the whole page-in), with the earlier change in the set and the big buffer not yet; a commit of everything waits and returns with it in the set; in 4 MB chunks a commit that needs the first buffer waits 36-48 ms of a 150-190 ms page-in. A prewire and a commit under the lock in the kernel together each keep their own in-kernel mark (`g_rk[1]`, `g_rk[0]`), so `mtl_res_commit_stuck` (the freeze detector, the suspend's drain) reports the prewire at every look until it is back (223 looks, none empty; with one shared mark 121 of 200 were) and a suspend-style wait for a commit behind it ends on that report at once. `check_t_idle_tex` runs the footprint cap (`T_IDLE_FOOT=1`): under the cap nothing goes, over it the idle CPU-written texture goes and a copy of it made again reads the bytes the CPU wrote; `check_t_file_wire` and `check_t_res_async` as before.
- **Expected on the phone:** no `slow wait for the residency commit lock` behind a `slow residency commit`; a page-in shows as `[RED] slow residency prewire: N ms ...` and only a thread that needs its changes waits (`slow wait for a residency prewire`). The residency line counts `prewired outside the lock N, M MB, T ms; waits for a prewire ...; queue-commit guards G, K of them with their changes committed`. After an area change, `idle live textures released for the footprint cap` lines and a `GPU working set` line with the footprint back under the cap, fewer page-ins in the `[mem] guest file` line, and shorter prewires. Not yet measured on an iPhone.

### A residency page-in faulted its file pages in one call at a time

- **Symptom:** an area load with a hitch of up to 0.4 s and a cluster of `[RED] slow residency prewire: 163.0 ms (116 file ranges, 37.6 MB, ...)` / `181.5 ms (167 file ranges, 63.9 MB, ...)` and `[RED] slow wait for a residency prewire: 150-390 ms ...` lines, the longest on `NVN queue replay`.
- **Cause:** the [page-in outside the lock](#residency-commits-paged-guest-files-in-holding-the-lock) is right that the replay must wait (it needs those changes), but the page-in itself ran at 230-350 MB/s: its `mlock` calls of 8 MB ran one after another on one thread, each faulting a file page in at a time and waiting for its read; the read-ahead (`F_RDADVISE`, `fwire_advise`) runs ahead of them only so far. The phone's storage reads far faster with several reads in flight.
- **Fix:** for every game. `DM_NVN_PREWIRE_JOBS` (4 by default, at most 8; `1`: one call after another, as before; `nvn_mtl.m` `fwire_all`): a page-in of two calls or more cuts its ranges into calls of 8 MB, which up to that many workers (the calling thread among them, on a user-initiated concurrent queue) take in order. Disjoint calls wire disjoint map entries (XNU clips each call's ends and faults with the map unlocked), and each entry stays in transition only for its own call, as before. The wired parts are given back after the commit as before (each call's part its own entry); a refused call stops no other buffer's calls, and one that meets a wired limit stops the calls not yet started. The freeze detector's mark adds the calls' bytes together and shows the oldest call under way.
- **Log:** its own line every 300 frames (it was the end of the residency line, which its caller cuts at 512 bytes, so Ocarina's logs never showed it): `[nvn] residency page-ins up to frame F: file-backed buffers wired before their commit N, M MB, T ms (refused R, P page-ins with up to 4 calls at once, C calls, their times summed), read ahead A MB; prewires in this window N, M MB, T ms (R MB/s; ...)`. T is the calls' time summed; the prewires' figure is their own time, so R is the page-in's speed to compare between runs.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> t_file_wire`: eight 20 ms calls take about 56 ms with four workers on an M4 Max (160 ms one after another), several at once, the buffer wired whole before `[g_res commit]`, given back after it and counted once; every earlier case of `t_file_wire` and `t_res_prewire` runs with one worker.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, build 132a82bda (an area load in Hyrule Field). Not yet run on a device.

### The footprint cap freed nothing in Ocarina of Time

- **Symptom:** `idle live textures for the footprint cap (DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_MB): a sweep freed 0 MB with the footprint at 5069 MB over the cap 4632 MB`, and every `GPU working set up to frame` line `released for it 0, 0 MB in total`, while the footprint went 1.2 -> 5.4 GB, GPU allocated up to 7.9 GB of the 8.2 GB recommended working set, the residency set 5.5-6.3 GB, the process re-read 12.6 GB of guest-file pages by 120 s and prewires waited 180-330 ms. Ocarina of Time, iPhone 18 Pro Max, handheld Balanced, f5ae72b99 (NX18-X2.0 traces/oot-mst-1-findings.md).
- **Cause:** two of the sweep's rules kept every texture. (1) The game's textures have a packaged layout (`nvnTextureBuilderSetPackagedTextureLayout`, its texture files), and the sweep asked `tex_storage_ok`, which is the test for textures *without* a layout and answers no for every packaged one, though a packaged texture is taken from its storage at every use. (2) Ocarina of Time's shaders read most textures through the whole texture pool's table (`pool for handles traced to no slot: texture pool (texture type 2) at base 0, 16640 IDs of which 8598 hold textures`): a pool walk marks each ID it resolves (`tt1`), and the GPU may read any entry of that table in any frame without a binding saying so, so no texture an ID of that table names may go. What the footprint holds beside it: the graphics ledger 4.0-4.9 GB of the 5.0-5.9 GB footprint (the rest anonymous memory, 0.3 GB, and compressed, 0.9-1.0 GB); GPU allocated counts the guest's pages a second time through the pools' zero-copy buffers (memory pools 4,400-4,800, 3,800-4,350 of them resident), which are file pages and not in the footprint, but are wired while resident, so the kernel evicts other file pages and compresses instead. The caches kept for reuse were small beside it (recycled render-target heaps waiting 20-220, 60-240 MB; upload staging blocks; packaged-texture spares 30-35 MB; the pressure trim at frame 18,300 found 574 MB in all, 289 MB of it render targets drawn over).
- **Fix:** for every game. (1) The sweep's line (`tidle_line`): a sweep for the cap that logs (the first 6, then one every 1800 frames) tallies the live entries by kind (render-target or shader-write use, taken from storage, other) and by the first rule each fails, with MB, and the memory pools resident that only texture uploads looked up. (2) `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_PKG` (default on): a packaged texture goes under the same rules as a CPU-written one (unbound 3 s, no pool table names it, nothing else holds or pins it, no render-target or shader-write use), when what the GPU writes into it reaches its storage (a store target: not COMPRESSIBLE, LINEAR, LINEAR_RENDER_TARGET, an uncompressed IMAGE or a 1D array, and a host texture that holds the storage's bytes, `gsb_host_ok`), its package holds no GPU store since its upload and no GPU write its storage never got (`gw_lost`, set for good by `gsb_lost` wherever a store is skipped or missed), the GPU has not written its storage since the CPU did (`gws_owned`, which a CPU write of any page ends, so it is not enough alone) and the command buffer being encoded holds no store into it (`gsb_held`); its next use uploads it again from its storage (`tex_mtl_`'s packaged path), the same bytes. Not one mip drop made without its top levels or whose shape it could drop (a texture made again by another path could come back cut or whole); that holds for CPU-written textures now too. Its sharing entry (`g_pkg_share`) goes with it, so the next use uploads afresh rather than take back the package waiting for the fence. `=0` restores the rules before it exactly (no held-store, store, lost-write or mip-drop test; the sharing entry kept). (3) `DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_TRIM` (default on with the cap): a sweep for the cap that freed less than it wanted asks the next submission's start for a trim of the caches kept for reuse (`mtl_trim_at` level 5, `memory trim (footprint cap)`: render-target heaps waiting, packaged-texture spares, feedback snapshots, arena blocks, render-target shadows), once a minute at most, and a trim that freed under 64 MB doubles the wait, up to 10 minutes (said once; with the cap under what the game needs the caches hold little); off with `DM_MEMORY_TRIM=0` too; not the upload staging blocks (an area change wants them again at once, each a residency commit), the render targets drawn over, the finalized-texture table or frame generation. A pressure warning's trim takes its place. Not done: textures a pool table names. Releasing one leaves the table's entry to a stand-in or the dummy until the GPU's read mark asks for it again, a frame of a different picture (the walk's own over-budget path); the read marks are kept from the first read on, so they say nothing of what has gone unread lately. That is a picture trade for a later round, off by default if made.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_idle_tex` (runs `t_idle_pkg` too, also with `DM_MEMORY_TRIM=0` and `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`): a COMPRESSIBLE packaged 2D array a buffer copy wrote layer 0 of (no store) and whose layer 1 the CPU then rewrote a page of stays, and its layer 0 still reads the copy; one with a store whose page the CPU rewrote (`gws_owned` gone) and one with a lost write stay; a packaged 2D array of 4 levels goes and comes back byte for byte; a CPU-written texture goes in both modes, its sharing entry kept at `=0`. A 16 MB packaged texture a copy reads in the same submission goes at that submission's commit, its package queued for that fence; the copy completes with no GPU error and the bytes it held; the footprint (4 MB plus the texture) is under the 16 MB cap after it; a copy after the fence uploads it again, byte for byte. One a pool table names, one a GPU copy wrote and one bound lately stay, and the tally names each rule. With nothing to take, one cache trim is asked for two sweeps and the next submission's start runs it; a pressure warning replaces it. `=0` keeps the texture (tallied as packaged).
- **Expected on the phone:** a line per logged sweep `idle live textures for the footprint cap (DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_MB): sweep at frame N, footprint F MB over the cap C MB, W MB wanted, X MB freed by K textures (packaged P MB); live entries ..., MB: render-target or shader-write use ..., taken from storage ..., other ...; could go ...; kept, by the first rule: named by a pool table N (M MB), ...; memory pools resident that only texture uploads looked up N, M MB`, and `memory trim (footprint cap): ...` at most once a minute while over the cap. In Ocarina of Time most of the textures' MB should read as `named by a pool table`; the next candidates follow from the line: residency of the pools only texture uploads read (unwired after the upload, made resident again by the next lookup), and the read-mark trade above.

### Upload-only pools kept wired after their uploads

- **Symptom:** a long session on the phone pages the game's files in again and again: the 10 s `[mem] guest file:` line shows `process read 12.60 GB, 967k page-ins` by 120 s, the footprint at 5.0-5.9 GB with GPU allocated at 7.0-7.9 GB, and threads wait 180-330 ms for residency prewires (`[RED] slow wait for a residency prewire`). Ocarina of Time, iPhone 18 Pro Max (12 GB), 4,400-4,800 memory pools of which 3,800-4,350 resident (NX18-X2.0 `traces/oot-mst-1-findings.md`).
- **Cause:** a memory pool in the residency set has its pages pinned by Metal (wired), file pages included, and wired clean pages cannot be dropped, so the kernel compresses dirty memory and evicts other file pages instead (iOS counts dirty and compressed memory against the footprint; clean file pages are free to drop unless wired). Many resident pools are read only by texture uploads (the GPU copy of a pool's bytes into a Metal texture): once the upload batch has run, nothing needs them resident. The census that counted them was also short: an upload's lookup filled the per-thread pool cache, so a later draw's lookup on the same thread hit the cache uncounted, and the pool looked upload-only while draws used it.
- **Fix:** for every game, `DM_NVN_UNWIRE_UP` (`nvn_mtl.m` `up_unwire_sweep`, `pool_res_mark`, `up_back_floor`). (1) The census: an upload's lookup is always counted and never cached (`mtl_addr`, `mtl_gpu_addr`; `pc_put` asserts it), so every per-thread cache entry comes from a counted other use. (2) Each upload notes in its pool the batch that reads it (`up_seq`, raised again when taking the staging flushed the batch partway through the texture, `pool_up_note`, on the entry whose buffer the upload reads, which with overlapping pools need not be the one a lookup of the address alone finds). (3) At a submission's start, before any lookup of it, at most once every 500 ms and 256 pools / 512 MB, the sweep takes out of the residency set a pool that is resident, read by uploads and nothing else, not a large pool's chunk, not sticky, not addressed (a raw pool table, built or to come, may name it: a late first table would put every addressed pool the sweep took out back at once and page them all in before its draw), whose every upload batch has come back (`up_seq` at or below the floor of batches whose feedback has run, over the upload slots), idle for uploads 3 s, and not stored into by the submission being encoded. It bumps the pool generation (every thread's pool cache, the pool table memo and the raw table's keep path), marks the argument blocks dirty and commits the removal. A memory trim runs it too, without the 3 s rule. Nothing is discarded, unlocked by hand, punched or unmapped: dirty pages keep their content and are written back as usual. (4) A later use puts the pool back through the usual paths before any command buffer that uses it is committed: a lookup (`pool_res`: the replay's commit or its hand-off to the worker, the encode thread's guard before the queue commit), or the raw table's build (`gmem_map_at`). A later upload reads it through a buffer of its own batch over the texture's pages only (`up_big_src`), so the pool stays out. A re-add within 10 s of the unwire holds the pool back from the sweep 10 s, then 20, 40 ... up to 120 s, and the third makes it stay for good. The census line and the 10 s line give the resident pools by class: U1 only uploads read it and no raw table can name it (what the sweep takes), U2 only uploads read it and the raw table keeps it (with its MB by how the address was given: the pool's own address or a virtual pool's mapping, buffers only, textures only), U3 other uses, EV out now. `=m` (the default for now) counts what would leave and changes nothing; `=1` on, `=0` off, `=ab` with `DM_AB`.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pool_unwire` (an M4 Max): an upload's lookup then a draw's on the same thread counts both, and the pool stays; with the upload batch's feedback held back the pool stays in the set, and once it is back and 3 s have passed it leaves the committed set; the floor over three slots coming back out of order; a later buffer copy puts the pool back and every queue commit after it finds the re-add committed (encode thread on and off, the residency worker, the prewire on and off, held submissions and segment cuts on and off); an addressed pool stays while no raw table exists; one addressed after it left reads its next upload through a batch buffer, and the table's build puts it back before the queue commit; with a table, an addressed pool stays; with overlapping pools the note of a flushed batch lands on the pool whose buffer is read and the sweep keeps it while that batch is out; back-off 10 s, 20 s, sticky at the third; `=m` changes nothing; 12 uploads byte-exact with a sweep at every submission; all of it under Metal's API and shader validation; three threads (re-adds racing the sweep, the raw table built meanwhile) under ASan and TSan with every resident pool committed after each round. A 256 MB file-backed pool: in the set an invalidate (`msync MS_INVALIDATE`) leaves all its pages resident (pinned); 1.25 s after the sweep's commit none are (Metal lets the pages go a moment after the commit, not at it); a later 1 MB texture upload from it pages in 65 pages (16 KB), 0.4% of the pool.
- **Expected on the phone:** with `DM_NVN_UNWIRE_UP=1`, the `[mem] upload-only unwire` line counts pools and MB unwired and few re-adds; the guest file's line reads fewer GB and page-ins over the same route; fewer `slow wait for a residency prewire`; no `[RED] ... command buffer` or GPU fault line. If U2 (kept by the raw table) is most of the upload-only MB, as expected for Ocarina of Time, the sweep is near inert there, and a raw table that names only the ranges given out is the next lever.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, X2.0.

### Idle reuse-cache memory counted in the footprint

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time on the phone holds a 5-6 GB footprint on a 12 GB iPhone; when the footprint cap (`DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_MB`) asks for its cache trim (`DM_NVN_IDLE_TEX_FOOT_TRIM`), the `memory trim (footprint cap)` line finds about 574 MB sitting in caches kept for reuse: 289 MB of render targets drawn over, 97 MB of render-target heaps waiting, 128 MB of upload staging blocks, 32 MB of packaged-texture spares and 27 MB of feedback snapshots.
- **Cause:** every byte a reuse cache holds counts toward the app's footprint and its memory limit, though nothing reads an idle entry until the cache hands it out again. Apple's "Reducing the memory footprint of Metal apps" marks such memory `MTLPurgeableStateVolatile`: it stays allocated, leaves the footprint, and the system may take it under pressure instead of ending the app. NX 18 did that only going to the background (`DM_NVN_BG_PURGE`).
- **Fix:** for every game, `DM_NVN_CACHE_VOLATILE` (on by default; `nvn_mtl.m` `cv_mark`, `cv_back`). At a submission's start an entry idle in a cache for 250 ms is marked volatile: render-target heaps waiting for reuse (`rh_trim`, under the list's lock; a heap reaches the list only when its texture went after the submissions that used it completed), the upload staging pool's blocks once the pool has gone 250 ms unused (`up_pool_trim`; a block is pooled only by its batch's feedback handler), and same-pass feedback snapshots no submission has taken for 30 submissions, only between submissions and once the fence of the last submission that took them has passed (`fb_vol_sweep`; a snapshot is valid only in the submission that took it). A recycled heap or snapshot heap is marked as a whole (one texture at offset 0). Taken again (`rh_heap`, `up_pool_get`, `fb_snap_shape`, or released by a trim) an entry is made non-volatile first; one the system emptied is dropped, out of the residency set, and a new one made as on a cache miss (its content was never needed: a reused heap's texture is filled or zeroed, a block written whole, a snapshot copied into). Not marked: packaged-texture spares (a partial re-upload builds on a spare's content, and serials that took the old texture over may still draw it), per-frame arena blocks (they cycle every frame, and a caller may keep a block past its submission), render targets drawn over (live textures) and every live texture or buffer. Volatile entries stay in the residency set: Metal's headers state no rule against it, the background purge already keeps its volatile textures there, and taking them out and back in would cost a residency commit at each reuse. The memory line adds `idle cache entries volatile N (M MB now; K marked, E emptied by the system and made again, X MB, in total)`. `DM_NVN_CACHE_VOLATILE=0` keeps them non-volatile, as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_cache_volatile` (an M4 Max, the validation layer, encode thread off and on, and `=0`): for each of the three caches an entry idle past the limit is volatile and still resident, one used just now is not, one taken back is non-volatile before use and reads back what is written into it; one emptied (`setPurgeableState:Empty`, and the test hook `g_cv_test_empty` that reports the next take emptied, as the Mac kernel cannot be made to purge on demand) is dropped from the cache and the residency set and a new one is made that reads back correctly; a snapshot taken in a submission still open, or in one the GPU has not completed (queue held by an event), is never marked, and removing that fence guard fails the test; a render-target heap whose texture a held submission uses never reaches the list; a held batch's staging blocks are never pooled or marked; a kept snapshot emptied while volatile comes back kept with a new texture for its own key, and one taken by a new key of another shape through the rank search does not pass its keep on (the new shape is released when idle as before); the idle release hands a volatile snapshot's heap back non-volatile, or frees it if it was emptied; the footprint cap's trim takes volatile heaps and snapshots and leaves the staging pool's volatile blocks counted; after trims nothing is counted volatile. `t_stage_pool`, `t_mem_trim`, `t_rt_recycle`, `t_feedback`, `t_idle_tex` and the rest of the memory tests pass unchanged.
- **Expected on the phone:** the memory line's `idle cache entries volatile` shows tens to a few hundred MB volatile during play (render-target heaps and staging blocks between bursts), a footprint (`phys_footprint`) lower by about that much, and few or no `emptied by the system and made again` unless the phone is under memory pressure; no `[RED]` GPU fault or command-buffer error line, and no picture fault after a memory warning. Not yet measured on a device: whether iOS leaves a volatile allocation in a committed residency set out of the footprint as it does one outside a set is the open question the phone log answers (compare the footprint with `=0` on the same route).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), iPhone 18 Pro Max, X2.0.

### Host spin-waits kept a core running flat out

- **Symptom:** nothing on screen; CPU energy. The iPhone's performance cores drop to 1.44 GHz while the GPU is busy, so the CPU's energy and the serial chain (the game's frame thread, the NVN queue replay, the encode thread, the GPU) decide the frame rate, and Apple's tuning guide asks not to keep threads busy in `yield` or `sched_yield` loops. The host had four such waits: `ubc_wait` (4,096 `yield`s before it sleeps, `nvn_rec.h`), the uniform copy thread's poll of 2,000 loads after a burst, the same-core handoff's spin of up to 20 µs (`hle_thread.c` `hand_spin`; the iPhone round's logs show 50-2,300 handoffs a second, a third to a half of them ending in the spin) and `thr_free`'s `sched_yield` loop for a wake in flight.
- **Cause:** a `yield` spin runs the core flat out for the whole wait, and its length was set in passes, so it took 1.7 µs on an M4 Max P core, 4-8 µs on its E cores and longer on the phone's capped cores.
- **Fix (on by default where the CPU has FEAT_WFxT; `DM_HOST_SPIN=0` restores the yield spins):** `dm/host/dm_spin.h`. The waits arm the exclusive monitor on the watched word (`LDAXR`) and stop the core with `WFET` until another core writes that cache line, an event comes (the kernel's event stream, about every 1.2 µs) or the deadline passes; xnu lets user code run WFE / WFET (`SCTLR_EL1.nTWE` is required). The spins are bounded in time: `ubc_wait` 4 µs, then it sleeps as before; the copy thread's poll 2 µs; `hand_spin` its 20 µs in slices of at most 2 µs, so the held thread's run state is read again; `thr_free` 20 µs, then `sched_yield` as before. Waits longer than a thread switch still block. The other `sched_yield` sites stay: the guest's own yields (parked or backed off already, [spin-counter yields](#spin-counter-yields-hold-a-core-while-the-threads-they-wait-for-wait-for-one)), `hle_audio.c`'s waits for a render callback in flight (only when a unit stops or restarts), `nvn_dirty.c`'s (the write-fault hook, which must stay async-signal-safe, and a pool's retire) and libvpx's.
- **Mac (M4 Max):** `t_host_spin --bench`: the waiter sees a store 42-83 ns after it with `WFET`, 83 ns with the yield spin and 5.5-6.1 µs blocked; the process's energy per 5 µs wait 32 µJ against 57 for the yield spin and 50 blocked, per 20 µs 127 against 233 and 108. `t_ubc_wait`'s replay-shaped run (30 submissions a frame of 15 copies of 1-64 KB, the commit waiting for them): 85-90% of the waits end within 5 µs and 1.2-1.6 a frame sleep, the copy thread's poll finds the next push 201 times a frame against 133, and the process takes 0.76 mJ a frame against 1.34. `t_handoff_wait`'s lock storm: 9.8 µs a handoff against 10.1-10.7. No wake-latency regression, so the default is on.
- **Measured with it (logging only):**
  - `rec_blk` puts its records on a 128-byte line (each `rec_t` is one line; `r[]` was at offset 16, so every record spanned two). `t_rec_align` (20,000 records a frame, put to drained): 32.2-34.1 CPU cycles a record against 34.7-35.2, 5.6-6.0 ns against 6.0-6.2.
  - The display link line ends with the callback's deadline: `; commits after the callback's deadline (targetTimestamp) L of K, late by p50 … / p95 … / max … ms; the callback blocked for the encode thread's notes (snapshot, upload, submission) on N, p50 … / p95 … / max … ms, for the present allocator on M, p50 … / p95 … / max … ms`. If commits miss the deadline while the callback blocks on the notes, the next step is not to block the callback on the encode thread's commit (repeat the last frame instead).
  - `DM_NVN_PASS_REPEAT=1`: `[pass repeat] up to frame N, per frame: render passes P, X% of them byte-identical to a pass of the previous frame; draws D, Y% in those passes; encode thread time in render passes A ms, B ms of it in the identical ones`, to judge indirect command buffers before re-baking archives for them. Off by default: the hashing costs about 0.07 ms a frame on the M4 Max (`t_pass_repeat`).
  - The threads line (with `DM_POWER_DETAIL`, on by default) ends with `; by core type, CPU on performance / efficiency cores (energy): acquiring (frame) thread P / E ms/s (W mW), replay …, encode …, present …`: each thread samples its own time and energy by core type once a frame (`thread_selfcounts`' per-perf-level kind; `hle_nvn.c` `dm_tcore_sample`). The power detail line splits only the whole process. Its `same-core handoffs` part also gives the time spent spinning (`spinning S ms/s (WFET)`).
- **Expected on the phone:** with the same route and `DM_HOST_SPIN=0` against the default, lower CPU energy in the `power` line at the same frame rate, the same handoff counts, and `uniform copy waits by length` (`DM_NVN_WAITS=1`) mostly under 5 µs.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/host/tests GAME=<id> host-spin handoff-wait`; `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_ubc_wait check_t_rec_align check_t_pass_repeat check_t_chain_lines`.
- **Found on:** not yet measured on a device: the X2.0 CPU queue (Apple's tuning guide and CPU optimization guide, NX18-X2.0 open-optimizations.md), measured on an M4 Max.

### Attachment loads and stores the next operations make unneeded

- **Symptom:** nothing on screen; power. On the iPhone 18 Pro Max the performance cores drop to 1.44 GHz while the GPU is 85-96% busy, and Ocarina of Time's frames are CPU-limited, so GPU memory traffic costs frame rate. Its residency set line reads `attachments per frame load 106-240 MB / store 126-240 MB, load-all store-all passes 39-71` in play (NX18-X2.0 `traces/oot-mst-1-findings.md`).
- **Cause:** on a tile-based GPU every attachment a pass loads is read whole into tile memory and every one it stores is written back whole. Switch games split their frames into many passes (Maxwell renders immediately, where a pass boundary costs nothing), and the replay kept what it could not prove unneeded. The earlier rules cover attachments a pass leaves unwritten (`DM_NVN_UNWRITTEN_DC`, `DM_NVN_UNWRITTEN_LOAD`) and clears a texture already holds (`DM_NVN_SAME_CLEAR`); what was left unmeasured is how much of the rest no later operation needs.
- **Measurement (`DM_NVN_LS_STATS=1`):** every 300 frames `[ls-stats] frames A-B, per frame: P passes; attachments load L MB (cleared whole before any draw wrote them X MB), store S MB: loaded again M MB, cleared or discarded at the next pass on it with no use between U MB (of it registered in the texture pool, which shaders may sample: Q MB), cleared or discarded after a use V MB, not attached again within 2 frames T MB, stored again by another pass O MB; saved by rules: loads of targets cleared whole a MB, depth / stencil stores b MB, multisample stores c MB` and `[ls-stats] by pass kind (W x H, colours, colour 0 / depth Metal format, samples), MB per frame: …` (the 8 kinds with the most traffic: passes, load, store, unread, loaded again). Each stored attachment (root texture, level, layer, plane) waits in a table of 256 for the next pass that attaches it; a use between (any lookup by texture object, a copy, a present: `tu_stamp`) marks it. U - Q is traffic no shader can read; Q is an upper bound (a pool's descriptors are walked whole, so a sample through the pool is not seen per draw). Cost: one flag test per pass and per `tu_stamp` when off.
- **Fix (each its own switch, on by default; `=0` restores the old action):**
  - (a) `DM_NVN_CLEARED_LOAD`: the scan that finds colour attachments no draw of the pass writes (`ldc_scan`) also takes one whose first write is a clear of all four channels over the whole attachment: it loads as DontCare, and the clear triangle, which draws every pixel with no blending, makes it written and stored. A clear found partial at run time ends the pass first and the next one loads it, as for an unforeseen draw. Clears outside a pass were already load-action clears, so this takes only the clears that come into an open pass.
  - (b) `DM_NVN_DS_DEAD_STORE`: a depth or stencil plane the pass wrote is stored DontCare when `ls_dead_scan`, from the op that ended the pass over the rest of the submission's recorded stream, reaches a clear of that plane of the same texture (serial and view) over the whole level, with only state, target and buffer-update operations before it.
